Residue families, singular Yamabe problems and extrinsic conformal Laplacians
Andreas Juhl and Bent Ørsted

Abstract
. Let ( X , g ) (X,g) be a compact manifold with boundary M n M^{n} and σ \sigma a defining function of M M . To these data, we associate natural conformally covariant polynomial one-parameter families of differential operators C ∞ ​ ( X ) → C ∞ ​ ( M ) C^{\infty}(X)\to C^{\infty}(M) . They arise through a residue construction which generalizes an earlier construction in the framework of Poincaré-Einstein metrics and are referred to as residue families. Residue families may be viewed as curved analogs of conformal symmetry breaking differential operators. The main ingredient of the definition of residue families are eigenfunctions of the Laplacian of the singular metric σ − 2 ​ g \sigma^{-2}g . We prove that if σ \sigma is an approximate solution of a singular Yamabe problem, i.e., if σ − 2 ​ g \sigma^{-2}g has constant scalar curvature − n ⁡ ( n + 1 ) -n(n+1) , up to a sufficiently small remainder, these families can be written as compositions of certain degenerate Laplacians (Laplace-Robin operators). This result implies that the notions of extrinsic conformal Laplacians and extrinsic Q Q -curvature introduced in recent works by Gover and Waldron can naturally be rephrased in terms of residue families. This new spectral theoretical perspective allows easy new proofs of several results of Gover and Waldron. Moreover, it enables us to relate the extrinsic conformal Laplacians and the critical extrinsic Q Q -curvature to the scattering operator of the asymptotically hyperbolic metric σ − 2 ​ g \sigma^{-2}g extending the work of Graham and Zworski. The relation to the scattering operators implies that the extrinsic conformal Laplacians are self-adjoint. We describe the asymptotic expansion of the volume of a singular Yamabe metric in terms of Laplace-Robin operators (reproving results of Gover and Waldron). We also derive new local holographic formulas for all extrinsic Q Q -curvatures (critical and sub-critical ones) in terms of renormalized volume coefficients, the scalar curvature of the background metric, and the asymptotic expansions of eigenfunctions of the Laplacian of the singular metric σ − 2 ​ g \sigma^{-2}g . These results naturally extend earlier results in the Poincaré-Einstein case. Furthermore, we prove a new formula for the singular Yamabe obstruction ℬ n {\mathcal{B}}_{n} . The simple structure of these formulas shows the benefit of the systematic use of so-called adapted coordinates. We use the latter formula for ℬ n {\mathcal{B}}_{n} to derive explicit expressions for the obstructions in low-order cases (confirming earlier results). Finally, we relate the obstruction ℬ n {\mathcal{B}}_{n} to the supercritical Q Q -curvature 𝐐 n + 1 {\bf Q}_{n+1} .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 35J30 53B20 53B25 53C18; Secondary 35J70 35Q76 53C25 58J50

August 8, 2026

1.
Introduction

Conformal differential geometry studies natural geometric quantities
associated to Riemannian (and pseudo-Riemannian) manifolds (such as curvature
invariants and natural differential operators) which transform nicely under
conformal changes of the metric. In recent years, it has developed in profound and
surprising ways, which are connected to the spectral theory of Laplace-type operators,
scattering theory, holographic principles (AdS-CFT correspondence), Cartan geometry,
non-linear partial differential equations, and representation theory. Particular
powerful tools are tractor calculus and the Poincaré-Einstein and ambient metrics
in the sense of Fefferman-Graham
[
CS09
,
DGH08
,
FG85
,
FG12
,
BJ10
,
KS15
,
KS18
]
.

A central role in those parts of geometric analysis which are related to conformal differential
geometry play the conformally invariant powers of the Laplacian, which are known as
GJMS-operators
[
GJMS92
]
and the related Branson’s
Q
-curvatures
[
Br95
]
.
The GJMS-operators
P_{2N}(h)
act on the space
C^{\infty}(M)
on any Riemannian
manifold
(M,h)
. They are of the form
\Delta_{h}^{N}+\mbox{lower-order terms}
,
where the lower-order terms are given in terms of the metric and covariant
derivatives of its curvature. We recall that for general
h
, the operators

P_{2N}(h)
exist for all orders
2N
if
n
is odd but only for
2N\leq n
if
n

is even. The GJMS-operators
P_{2N}
generalize the conformal Laplacian (Yamabe
operator)

P_{2}=\Delta-\left(\frac{n}{2}-1\right){\sf J},\quad 2(n-1){\sf J}=\operatorname{scal}

and the Paneitz operator

P_{4}=\Delta^{2}-\delta((n-2){\sf J}-4{\sf P})d+\left(\frac{n}{2}-2\right)\left(\frac{n}{2}{\sf J}^{2}-2|{\sf P}|^{2}-\Delta({\sf J})\right),

where
{\sf P}
is the Schouten tensor of
h
(for the notation, we refer to Section

2
). The scalar curvature quantities

Q_{2}={\sf J}\quad\mbox{and}\quad Q_{4}=\frac{n}{2}{\sf J}^{2}-2|{\sf P}|^{2}-\Delta({\sf J})

are the lowest-order cases of Branson’s
Q
-curvatures. In particular, the quantity
Q_{4}
plays a
central role in geometric analysis
[
C05
,
C18
]
.

In
[
J09
,
J13
]
, one of the authors analyzed the structure of GJMS-operators
and Branson’s
Q
-curvatures of a Riemannian manifold
(M^{n},h)
through a theory of
conformally covariant polynomial one-parameter families of differential operators

C^{\infty}(X)\to C^{\infty}(M)
, where
X^{n+1}
is a manifold of one more dimension.
Such an extrinsic perspective on objects living on
M
is sometimes referred
to as a
holographic
point of view following
[
W98
]
. These families of differential operators
can be regarded as
curved versions
of certain intertwining operators in
representation theory. Following the framework introduced in a series of works by T.
Kobayashi and his coauthors, these intertwining operators are now known as conformal

symmetry breaking operators

C^{\infty}(S^{n+1})\to C^{\infty}(S^{n})
. The notion
is motivated by the fact that they are equivariant only with respect to the subgroup
of the conformal group of
S^{n+1}
consisting of diffeomorphisms that leave the
equatorial subsphere
S^{n}\hookrightarrow S^{n+1}
invariant. Such intertwining
operators acting on functions have analogs acting on sections of homogeneous vector
bundles on spheres as well as in contexts where other groups replace the conformal group
of the sphere. We refer the interested reader to
[
KS15
,
KS18
,
KKP16
,
FJS21
,
FW20
]
.

In the curved case, the conformal covariance property takes the role of the intertwining property,
and the definition of the operators uses a Poincaré-Einstein metric in the sense of Fefferman
and Graham
[
FG12
]
. The curved versions of the symmetry breaking operators then are
defined in terms of the asymptotic expansions of eigenfunctions of the Laplacian of a
Poincaré-Einstein metric on
X
and the resulting so-called renormalized volume coefficients.
The latter quantities are curvature invariants of a metric on
M
, the study of which originally
was motivated by the AdS/CFT-correspondence
[
W98
,
G00
]
. Since the construction of the curved
versions of the symmetry breaking differential operators can be expressed as a
residue construction, these were termed
residue families
in
[
J09
]
. A key
feature of the theory of residue families is that each GJMS-operator
P_{2N}(h)

comes along with a residue family (see (
1.7
)), and one can effectively utilize the
family parameter to study the structure of these special values
[
J09
,
J13
]
.

Another basic perspective on GJMS-operators and
Q
-curvatures of a metric
h
on

M
was developed in
[
GZ03
]
by showing how the scattering operator

\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
of the Laplacian of a Poincaré-Einstein metric on
X
in normal
form relative to
h
naturally encodes both quantities. The scattering operator

\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
is a one-parameter meromorphic family of conformally
covariant pseudo-differential operators, which may be regarded as a curved version of
the Knapp-Stein intertwining operator for spherical principal series
representations. Thus, both
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
and residue families describe
GJMS-operators and
Q
-curvatures. But whereas the former are meromorphic families
of pseudo-differential operators, the latter are polynomial families of differential
operators.

In
[
GW14a
]
, Gover and Waldron developed an alternative perspective on the above
holographic descriptions of GJMS-operators and
Q
-curvatures. In this approach, the
conformal geometry of the metric
h
on
M

and
the associated Poincaré-Einstein metric

g_{+}
on
X
play a central role. In particular, this leads to a deeper understanding of the role
of the Einstein property of
g_{+}
in constructions of GJMS-operators. The conformal tractor
calculus on
X
naturally associates a conformally covariant polynomial one-parameter family
of second-order differential operators on functions on
X
to a metric
g
on
X

and
a
defining function
\sigma
of the hypersurface
\iota^{*}:M\hookrightarrow X
. Later, Gover
and Waldron termed this operator the
Laplace-Robin operator

[
GW21
]
.
1
1

1

In

[
GW14a
]
, the Laplace-Robin operators are regarded as operators acting on densities.
We shall
follow this terminology here. The Laplace-Robin operators degenerate on
M
in the sense that the
coefficients of its leading parts vanish on
M
. The data
(g,\sigma)
induce a
metric
\iota^{*}(g)
on
M
and a singular metric
\sigma^{-2}g
on the complement
of
M
in
X
. It is the latter metric that generalizes the Poincaré-Einstein
metric. Now, if
\sigma^{-2}g
is Einstein, then certain compositions of
Laplace-Robin operators on
X
reduce to GJMS-operators on
M
. Even if the Einstein
condition is violated, analogous compositions of (renormalized) Laplace-Robin
operators still reduce to conformally covariant operators on
M
. We emphasize that the
notion of conformal covariance in the latter statement concerns constructions that
depend on a metric
g
on
X

and
a defining function
\sigma
of the boundary

M
of
X
, i.e., constructions which are invariant under conformal changes of both

g
and
\sigma
:
\hat{g}=e^{2\varphi}g
and
\hat{\sigma}=e^{\varphi}\sigma
.
Note that
\sigma^{-2}g
is invariant under such substitutions. Now a version of
the singular Yamabe problem asks to find (for a given metric
g
on
X
) a defining
function
\sigma
so that the scalar curvature of
\sigma^{-2}g
is a negative
constant. This conformally invariant condition for pairs
(g,\sigma)
actually
determines the defining function
\sigma
by the background metric
g
(at least to
some extent) and the embedding
\iota
. If
\sigma
solves the singular Yamabe
problem, the above constructions of conformally covariant operators on
M

only depend on the metric
g
and the embedding
\iota
. In other words, these
operators live on
M
, their definition depends on the embedding of
M\hookrightarrow X
, and they are conformally covariant with respect to conformal
changes of the background metric
g
on
X
. Gover, Waldron,
and coauthors used this idea in a series of works to develop a theory of conformal invariants of
hypersurfaces. To a large extent, this theory rests on the conformal tractor
calculus
[
GW14b
,
GW14a
,
GW21
,
GW17
,
GW19
,
GGHW19
,
GW21
,
AGW21
]
.

In recent years, Laplace-Robin operators independently also appeared in other
contexts, albeit not under this name. As described above, their original and most
general definition comes from conformal tractor calculus. But already in
[
GW14a
]
,
it was observed that a special case was crucial in
[
GZ03
]
in the setting of
Poincaré-Einstein metrics. Some other special cases were found to be interesting in
representation theory. Clerc
[
C17
]
proved that the symmetry breaking differential
operators
C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})\to C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
introduced in
[
J09
]
also arise
as compositions of some second-order intertwining operator for spherical principal
series representations on functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
. In
[
JO20
]
, an attempt to
generalize the result of Clerc led to a description of the general residue families
of
[
J09
]
as compositions of some second-order operators, which turned out to be
Laplace-Robin operators. In other words, the latter result could be viewed as a
curved version of the quoted result of Clerc. In
[
FOS19
,
FO20
]
, the notions of
spectral
shift operators
and
Bernstein-Sato operators
are used in the
context of symmetry breaking operators on functions, differential forms, and spinors.
These results suggest extensions of the notion of Laplace-Robin operators on forms.
Such extensions on forms using tractor calculus have been developed in the
monograph
[
GLW15
]
.

The present work sheds new light on the above results of Gover and Waldron. We
connect the various strands of development by introducing an extension of the notion
of residue families. Following the principles of
[
J09
]
, we prove a series of new
results, and - as by-products - we confirm and give alternative proofs of some
already known results. The treatment replaces tractor calculus and distributional calculus
by more classical arguments. Among other things, we stress the role of the scattering operator
extending
[
GZ03
]
. We hope that our methods enhance the understanding of the
subject.

We continue with a more detailed description of the main new results.

We consider a compact Riemannian manifold
(X,g)
with boundary
M
. Let
\iota:M\hookrightarrow X
be the canonical embedding. Let
h=\iota^{*}(g)
be the
induced metric on
M
and
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(X)
a boundary defining function,
i.e.,
\sigma\geq 0
on
X
,
\sigma^{-1}(0)=M
and
d\sigma|_{M}\neq 0
.

The data
(g,\sigma)
define a Laplace-Robin operator
[
GW21
]

L(g,\sigma;\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(n+2\lambda-1)(\nabla_{\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)}+\lambda\rho)-\sigma(\Delta_{g}+\lambda{\sf J}),\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}

on
C^{\infty}(X)
. Here
2n{\sf J}=2n{\sf J}^{g}=\operatorname{scal}^{g}
and
(n+1)\rho(g,\sigma)=-\Delta_{g}(\sigma)-\sigma{\sf J}
. The significance of this operator rests on its
conformal covariance property

(1.1)

L(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma};\lambda)\circ e^{\lambda\varphi}=e^{(\lambda-1)\varphi}\circ L(g,\sigma;\lambda),\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}

for all conformal changes
(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma})=(e^{2\varphi}g,e^{\varphi}\sigma)

with
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(X)
. This property implies that all
compositions

(1.2)

L_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}L(g,\sigma;\lambda\!-\!N\!+\!1)\circ\cdots\circ L(g,\sigma;\lambda),\;N\in{\mathbb{N}}

are conformally covariant:
L_{N}(\lambda)
shifts the conformal weight
\lambda

into
\lambda-N
.

The notion of residue families has its origin in a far-reaching generalization of
Gelfand’s distribution
r_{+}^{\lambda}

[
GS64
]
. As the first generalization of

r_{+}^{\lambda}
we consider the distribution

\left\langle M(\lambda),\psi\right\rangle=\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}\psi dvol_{g},\;\Re(\lambda)>-1

with
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
. Later
\sigma
will be a solution of the singular
Yamabe problem, i.e.,
\sigma^{-2}g
has constant scalar curvature

-n(n+1)
.
2
2

2

Then
\sigma
is not necessarily
C^{\infty}
up to the boundary.

Note that
\left\langle M(0),1\right\rangle=\int_{X}dvol_{g}
. The one-parameter
family of distributions
M(\lambda)
admits a meromorphic continuation to
{\mathbb{C}}
with
simple poles in
-{\mathbb{N}}
. In order to describe the residues of
M(\lambda)
, we
introduce coordinates on
X
near its boundary as follows. Let
\mathfrak{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{N}/|\mathcal{N}|^{2}
with
\mathcal{N}=\operatorname{grad}(\sigma)
and define a local diffeomorphism

\eta:I\times M\to X,\;(s,x)\mapsto\Phi^{s}_{\mathfrak{X}}(x),\quad I=(0,\varepsilon)

using the flow
\Phi^{s}_{\mathfrak{X}}
of
\mathfrak{X}
. Such coordinates will be
called
adapted coordinates
. Then
\eta^{*}(\sigma)=s
and
\sigma^{\lambda}

pulls back to
s^{\lambda}
. Moreover, it holds

dvol_{\eta^{*}(g)}=v(s)dsdvol_{h}

with some
v\in C^{\infty}(I\times M)
. Hence studying the residues of
M(\lambda)

reduces to studying the residues of

\left\langle M(\lambda),\psi\right\rangle=\int_{I}\int_{M}s^{\lambda}v(s)\psi(s,x)dsdvol_{h}.

Now Gelfand’s formula

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-N-1}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{\lambda}\psi dr\right)=\frac{\psi^{(N)}(0)}{N!}

shows that

(1.3)

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-N-1}\left\langle M(\lambda),\psi\right\rangle=\frac{1}{N!}\int_{M}(v\psi)^{(N)}(0)dvol_{h}.

In particular, if
\psi=1
near the boundary, then

(1.4)

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-N-1}\left\langle M(\lambda),\psi\right\rangle=\int_{M}v_{N}dvol_{h},

where the expansion
v(s)=\sum_{j\geq 0}s^{j}v_{j}
defines the coefficients

v_{j}(g,\sigma)\in C^{\infty}(M)
. The above definitions immediately imply that the
residue of
\left\langle M(\lambda),1\right\rangle
at
\lambda=-n-1
is a
conformal invariant in the sense that

I(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma})=I(g,\sigma),\quad I(g,\sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\int_{M}v_{n}dvol_{g}.

where
\hat{g}=e^{2\varphi}g
and
\hat{\sigma}=e^{\varphi}\sigma
. Moreover, the formula

\left\langle M(\lambda),1\right\rangle=\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda+n+1}dvol_{\sigma^{-2}g},\;\Re(\lambda)>-1

suggests to regard the finite part of
\left\langle M(\lambda),1\right\rangle
at

\lambda=-n-1
as a
renormalized volume
of the singular metric

\sigma^{-2}g
. In fact, this should be called the
Riesz renormalization
of
the volume of
\sigma^{-2}g

[
A09
]
. In Theorem
10.11
and Theorem

10.13
, we shall return to the issue of renormalized volumes. We shall see that
the residues in (
1.4
) for
N\leq n
determine the singular terms in the
Hadamard renormalization
of the volume of
\sigma^{-2}g
. Similar
renormalization techniques have also been used in the context of Möbius invariant energies of
knots and their generalizations
[
Br99
,
OH03
,
OH20
]
.

Now we continue with a definition of residue families. Here we assume that

|d\sigma|^{2}_{g}=1
on
M
. This assumption implies that the metric
\sigma^{-2}g
is
asymptotically hyperbolic. The data
(g,\sigma)
give rise to a holomorphic
one-parameter family

(1.5)

\lambda\mapsto\langle M_{u}(\lambda),\psi\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{g},\;\Re(\lambda)\gg 0

of distributions
M_{u}(\lambda)
on
X
. Here
\psi
is a test function on
X
with
support up to the boundary, and the additional datum
u
is an eigenfunction of the
Laplacian of the singular metric
\sigma^{-2}g
:

-\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}u=\mu(n-\mu)u,\;\Re(\mu)=n/2,\,\mu\neq n/2

with
boundary value

f\in C^{\infty}(M)
. In particular, it holds
M(\lambda)=M_{1}(\lambda)
since
u=1
is an eigenfunction of
\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}
with
eigenvalue
0
and boundary value
1
. Since
\sigma^{-2}g
is asymptotically
hyperbolic, there is an eigenfunction
u
for any
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
so that in its
asymptotic expansion
f
defines one of the leading terms. Now
M_{u}(\lambda)
admits
a meromorphic continuation to
{\mathbb{C}}
with simple poles in
\{-\mu-N-1,\,|\,N\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
. For
N\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
, we set

(1.6)

{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}N!(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!2N\!+\!1)_{N}\delta_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!N),

where
(a)_{N}=a(a+1)\cdots(a+N-1)
is the Pochhammer symbol and

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-\mu-1-N}(\langle M_{u}(\lambda),\psi\rangle)=\int_{M}f\delta_{N}(g,\sigma;\mu)(\psi)dvol_{h}

for a meromorphic family
\delta_{N}(g,\sigma;\mu)
of differential operators

C^{\infty}(X)\to C^{\infty}(M)
of order
\leq N
.
M_{u}(\lambda)
should be regarded as
a further generalization of Gelfand’s distribution
r_{+}^{\lambda}

[
GS64
]
. The
factor in (
1.6
) guarantees that the
residue family

{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(\lambda)
is polynomial in
\lambda
. Its degree equals
2N
. It easily
follows from these definitions that
{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(\lambda)
satisfies a conformal
transformation law which is analogous to that of
L_{N}(\lambda)
. In view of

M(\lambda)=M_{1}(\lambda)
, the residue formula (
1.3
) can be restated as

\int_{M}{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g,\sigma;0)(\psi)dvol_{h}\sim\int_{M}(v\psi)^{(N)}(0)dvol_{h}

(for
N<n
). The above definition generalizes the notion of residue families
introduced in
[
J09
]
. For the details, we refer to Section
7
.

We also emphasize that while
L_{N}(\lambda)
maps functions on
X
to functions on

X
, residue families map functions on
X
to functions on the boundary
M
.

The families
L_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda)
and
D_{N}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda)
are the main
objects of the present paper. These objects and most related discussions will only depend on
approximations of the data
(g,\sigma)
in a sufficiently small neighborhood of the boundary
M
.

As a preparation for the following discussion, we briefly recall the role of residue
families for Poincaré-Einstein metrics in
[
J09
,
J13
]
. These polynomial families
of differential operators are defined through a residue construction as in
(
1.6
), where
g_{+}=r^{-2}(dr^{2}+h_{r})
is a Poincaré-Einstein
metric on
X=(0,\varepsilon)\times M
in normal form relative to a given metric

h
on
M
. Let
\iota:M\hookrightarrow X
be the embedding
m\mapsto(0,m)
. The
theory in
[
J09
,
J13
]
deals with approximations of Poincaré-Einstein metrics
which are completely
determined
by the metric
h
. In particular, for even

n
, these define even-order residue families
D_{2N}^{res}(h;\lambda)
of order
2N\leq n
. Since

(1.7)

D_{2N}^{res}\left(h;-\frac{n}{2}+N\right)=P_{2N}(h)\iota^{*},

the residue family
D_{2N}^{res}(h;\lambda)
can be regarded as a perturbation of
the GJMS-operator
P_{2N}(h)
. Moreover, residue families satisfy systems of
recursive relations that involve lower-order residue families and GJMS-operators.
These imply recursive relations among GJMS-operators and recursive
relations for
Q
-curvatures. In other words, residue families may be viewed as a
device to study the GJMS-operators and
Q
-curvatures on the boundary
M
.

The above more general construction deals with a general metric
g
on
X
. It
induces a metric
h=\iota^{*}(g)
on
M
, but of course is not determined by
h
. In
that case, the resulting residue families depend on the metric
g

and
a
boundary defining function
\sigma
. To get residue families, which are
determined only by the metric
g
and the embedding
\iota
, we put an extra
condition on
\sigma
. At the same time, we restrict considerations to residue
families for
N\leq n
. Similarly, as in the Poincaré-Einstein case, this restriction means
that it suffices to deal with finite approximations of true eigenfunctions. This
way, we obtain specific conformally covariant polynomial one-parameter families of
differential operators
C^{\infty}(X)\to C^{\infty}(M)
. However, we emphasize that
the problem of describing all such conformally covariant families is more complicated
[
J09
,
GP18
]
.

Now, following
[
GW21
]
, the extra condition which we pose is that
\sigma

solves a singular Yamabe problem. The version of that problem of interest here asks
to find a defining function
\sigma
of
M
so that
\operatorname{scal}(\sigma^{-2}g)=-n(n+1)
.
By
[
AM88
,
ACF92
]
such
\sigma
exist and are unique. However, in
general,
\sigma
is not smooth up to the boundary. More precisely, the existence of
smooth solutions is obstructed by a conformally covariant scalar curvature invariant

{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
called the
singular Yamabe obstruction
. Although this lack of smoothness
will only play a minor role for our main purposes, we also have
some new results for the invariant
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
. By the conformal transformation law of scalar
curvature, the condition that
\sigma
solves the singular Yamabe problem can be restated as

\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}|d\sigma|_{g}^{2}+2\sigma\rho\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}1.

This role of the functional
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)
also implies its conformal invariance:

\mathcal{S}(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma})=\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma).

The main results of the present work only require us to assume that
\sigma
satisfies
the weaker condition

(1.8)

{(\mathcal{SY})}:\hskip 14.22636pt\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)=1+\sigma^{n+1}R_{n+1}\qquad\qquad

with a smooth remainder term
R_{n+1}
. The restriction of the remainder term to

\sigma=0
then defines the singular Yamabe obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
. For more details
and background on the singular Yamabe problems, we refer to Section
3
.

Now we are ready to state the first main result. Let
\iota:M\hookrightarrow X
be
the embedding of
M
into
X
.

Theorem 1
.

Let
{\mathbb{N}}\ni N\leq n
and assume that the condition
{(\mathcal{SY})}
is satisfied. Then

(1.9)

\iota^{*}L_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda)={\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda),\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}.

Some comments on this result are in order. The identity (
1.9
) is to be
interpreted as an identity of operators acting on smooth functions with support in a
sufficiently small neighborhood of
M
. Although the assumptions guarantee that the
operators on both sides of (
1.9
) only depend on the metric
g
and the
embedding
\iota
, we keep the notation of the general case indicating the dependence
of the operators on
g
and
\sigma
. If
g=r^{2}g_{+}
is a Poincaré-Einstein metric
in normal form relative to the metric
h
on
M
, then
the equality (
1.9
) was proven in
[
JO20
]
.
3
3

3

[
JO20
]
uses
a different normalization of residue families.
In particular, if
g
is the
Euclidean metric on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
, then Theorem
1
reduces to the main result
of
[
C17
]
(for more details, we refer to
[
JO20
]
).

Theorem
1
is a consequence of the following identity.

Theorem 2
.

For any boundary defining function
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(X)
, it
holds

L(g,\sigma;\lambda)+\sigma^{\lambda-1}\circ\left(\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}-\lambda(n\!+\!\lambda)\operatorname{Id}\right)\circ\sigma^{-\lambda}=\lambda(n\!+\!\lambda)\sigma^{-1}(\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)-1)\operatorname{Id},\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}.

Again, Theorem
2
is to be interpreted as an identity near the boundary of
X
.

We apply Theorem
2
to prove the existence of a meromorphic continuation of the
family
M_{u}(\lambda)
using a Bernstein-Sato argument. We recall that for any polynomial
p
,
the classical Bernstein-Sato argument
[
B71
]
proves the existence of a meromorphic
distribution-valued function
p^{\lambda}
by using the functional equation

D(\lambda)(p^{\lambda+1})=b(\lambda)p^{\lambda}

for some polynomial
b(\lambda)
and a family
D(\lambda)
of differential operators. Here

\sigma^{\lambda}u
takes the role of
p^{\lambda}
and
L(\lambda)
takes the role of
the family
D(\lambda)
. The Bernstein-Sato argument yields formulas for the residues and
proves Theorem
1
.

The conjugation formula in Theorem
2
easily implies the commutator relations

(1.10)

L_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda)\circ\sigma-\sigma\circ L_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda\!-\!1)=N(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!N)L_{N-1}(g,\sigma;\lambda\!-\!1)

if
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)=1
. The relation (
1.10
) was discovered in
[
GW14a
]
,
where the special case
N=1
is regarded as one commutator relation in a basic

sl(2)
-structure. In turn, it follows that for
2\lambda=-n+N
the operator

L_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda)
reduces to a tangential operator
{\bf P}_{N}(g,\sigma)
on
M
.
By Theorem
1
, we obtain

(1.11)

{\bf P}_{N}(g,\sigma)\iota^{*}={\bf D}_{N}^{res}\left(g,\sigma;\frac{-n+N}{2}\right).

In other words, each operator
{\bf P}_{N}(g,\sigma)
on
M
comes with a
polynomial one-parameter family. The definition of
{\bf P}_{N}
in terms of
L_{N}
is due
to
[
GW14a
]
. The spectral theoretic description (
1.11
) of
{\bf P}_{N}
will be
shown to have a series of significant consequences.

The conformal covariance of the families
L_{N}(\lambda)
implies that the operators

{\bf P}_{N}
on
C^{\infty}(M)
are conformally covariant in the sense that

e^{\frac{n+N}{2}\iota^{*}(\varphi)}\circ{\bf P}_{N}(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma})={\bf P}_{N}(g,\sigma)\circ e^{\frac{n-N}{2}\iota^{*}(\varphi)},\;\varphi\in C^{\infty}(X).

For
N\leq n
, these conformally covariant operators are completely determined by
the metric
g
and the embedding
M\hookrightarrow X
. Following
[
GW21
]
, we
call them
extrinsic
conformal Laplacians. The notion is motivated by the fact
that for even
N
the leading term of
{\bf P}_{N}(g,\sigma)
is given by a constant
multiple of
\Delta_{h}^{N/2}
, where
h=\iota^{*}(g)
(see Theorem
3
). If the
background metric
g
is the conformal compactification
r^{2}g_{+}
of a
Poincaré-Einstein metric in normal form relative to
h
, then these even-order
operators actually are constant multiples of GJMS-operators of
h
. But, in general,
the operators
{\bf P}_{N}(g,\sigma)
depend on the metric
g
in a neighborhood of
M

and the embedding
M\hookrightarrow X
.

The following result makes the leading parts of all extrinsic conformal Laplacians
explicit. Its proof rests on the spectral theoretic interpretation (
1.11
)
of
{\bf P}_{N}
.

Theorem 3
.

It holds

(1.12)

{\bf P}_{2N}=(2N\!-\!1)!!^{2}\Delta^{N}+LOT

for
2N\leq n
and

(1.13)

{\bf P}_{N}=(2N\!-\!2)(N\!-\!1)!\sum_{r=0}^{\frac{N-3}{2}}m_{N}(r)\Delta^{r}\delta(\mathring{L}d)\Delta^{\frac{N-3}{2}-r}+LOT

for odd
N
with
n\geq N\geq 3
with rational coefficients given by
(
9.9
). Here
\Delta
is the Laplacian of
h
, and
\mathring{L}
is the trace-free
part of the second fundamental form
L
. LOT refers to terms of order
2N-2
and

N-3
, respectively.

In the Poincaré-Einstein case,
[
GW14a
, Theorem 4.5]
identifies the operator

{\bf P}_{2N}
with a constant multiple of the GJMS operator
P_{2N}
. An alternative
proof of that result is given in
[
JO20
, Theorem 5.6]
. In the general case,
formula (
1.12
) is also stated in
[
GW21
, Theorem 8.1]
although
the proof only refers to results in the Poincaré-Einstein case in
[
GW14a
]
. Note
also that in the Poincaré-Einstein case, the operators
{\bf P}_{N}
for odd
N
vanish.
Note that the order of
{\bf P}_{2N}
equals
2N
and the order of
{\bf P}_{N}
for odd
N

equals
N-1
in the generic case.

Now we use the operators
{\bf P}_{N}
to define analogs of Branson’s
Q
-curvatures.
Theorem
1
implies that for
N<n
the identity

{\bf P}_{N}(g,\sigma)(1)=\left(\frac{n-N}{2}\right){\bf Q}_{N}(g,\sigma)

defines a function
{\bf Q}_{N}(g,\sigma)
. These functions will be called the
subcritical
extrinsic
Q
-curvatures. The
critical
extrinsic
Q
-curvature

{\bf Q}_{n}(g,\sigma)
can be defined either through a limiting argument from the
subcritical ones or more elegantly by

(1.14)

{\bf Q}_{n}^{res}(g,\sigma)=-\dot{{\bf D}}_{n}^{res}(g,\sigma;0)(1).

We recall that the condition
{(\mathcal{SY})}
guarantees that the quantities in(
1.14
) are determined
by the metric
g
and the embedding
M\hookrightarrow X
. In the Poincaré-Einstein case, these
definitions reduce to constant multiples of Branson’s
Q
-curvatures. If
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)=1
, it follows
from Theorem
1
that the definition (
1.14
) is a special case of
the notion of
Q
-curvature defined in
[
GW17
, Theorem 3.9]
.
4
4

4

The
numbering in the published version differs from that in the arXiv version.
By
differentiating the conformal transformation law of
{\bf D}_{n}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda)

(see Theorem
7.7
) at
\lambda=0
, it follows that

(1.15)

e^{n\iota^{*}(\varphi)}{\bf Q}_{n}(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma})={\bf Q}_{n}(g,\sigma)+{\bf P}_{n}(g,\sigma)(\iota^{*}(\varphi))

for
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(X)
.

As noticed above, the metric
\sigma^{-2}g
is asymptotically hyperbolic if

|d\sigma|^{2}_{g}=1
on
M
. Associated to such metrics, there is a scattering operator

\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
acting on
C^{\infty}(M)
. It describes the asymptotic expansion of
eigenfunctions of the Laplacian of
\sigma^{-2}g
. In
[
GZ03
]
, Graham and Zworski
showed how the GJMS-operators
P_{2N}
and the critical Branson
Q
-curvature
Q_{n}

of a metric
h
on
M
are encoded in the scattering operator of a
Poincaré-Einstein metric with the conformal class of
h
as conformal infinity.
The following result extends these interpretations to the present framework.

Theorem 4
.

Suppose that condition
{(\mathcal{SY})}
is satisfied. Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
2\leq N\leq n
and suppose
that
(n/2)^{2}-(N/2)^{2}
is not in the discrete spectrum of
-\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}
. Then

{\bf P}_{N}=2(-1)^{N}(N-1)!N!\operatorname{Res}_{\frac{n-N}{2}}(\mathscr{S}(\lambda)).

The function
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)(1)
is regular at
\lambda=0
and its value at
\lambda=0
is denoted
by
\mathscr{S}(0)(1)
. Then

{\bf Q}_{n}=2(-1)^{n}(n-1)!n!\mathscr{S}(0)(1).

Note that the scattering operator
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
is a pseudo-differential operator
with principal symbol

2^{2\lambda-n}\frac{\Gamma(\lambda-\frac{n}{2})}{\Gamma(-\lambda+\frac{n}{2})}|\xi|_{h}^{n-2\lambda}.

This shows that the residues of
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
at
\lambda=\frac{n}{2}-N
are caused
by the
\Gamma
-factors and that the residue of its pole at
\lambda=\frac{n}{2}-N
is an operator with principal part given by a constant multiple of
\Delta^{N}
. Moreover,
Theorem
4
yields an identification of this residue. In particular,
these residues are elliptic operators. On the other hand, the above formula yields
no information on the structure of the residues of
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
at
\frac{n-N}{2}

for
odd

N
. This makes the second part of Theorem
3
interesting.

Similarly as in
[
GZ03
]
, the self-adjointness of
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
combined with Theorem
4
implies

Corollary 1
.

Let
{(\mathcal{SY})}
be satisfied. Then the operators
{\bf P}_{N}(g)
are formally self-adjoint
with respect to the scalar product on
C^{\infty}(M)
defined by
h
.

For closed
M
, combining (
1.15
) with the self-adjointness of
{\bf P}_{n}

and
{\bf P}_{n}(1)=0
shows that the integral

\int_{M}{\bf Q}_{n}(g)dvol_{h}

is a global conformal invariant.

Next, we describe a
local
formula for the critical extrinsic
Q
-curvature.
It extends the holographic formula for Branson’s critical
Q
-curvature proved in

[
GJ07
]
. The formulation of that result requires two more ingredients:
renormalized volume coefficients
v_{k}
and solution operators
{\mathcal{T}}_{k}(0)
. These data
are defined in local coordinates. We choose a diffeomorphism
\eta
between the
product
[0,\varepsilon)\times M
with coordinates
(s,x)
and a neighborhood of

M
in
X
.
\eta
is defined by a renormalized gradient flow of
\sigma
. It
satisfies
\eta^{*}(\sigma)=s
. These coordinates will be called
adapted
coordinates
. Then

\eta^{*}(dvol_{\sigma^{-2}g})=s^{-n-1}v(s,x)dsdvol_{h},

and the expansion
v(s,x)=\sum_{k\geq 0}v_{k}(x)s^{k}
defines the coefficients

v_{k}
. Let
(v{\sf J})_{k}
be the coefficients in the analogous expansion of
v\eta^{*}({\sf J})
, where
{\sf J}={\sf J}^{g}=\operatorname{scal}^{g}/2n
. Secondly, the differential operators

{\mathcal{T}}_{k}(0)
describe the asymptotic expansions of harmonic functions of the Laplacian
of
\sigma^{-2}g
. The details of these definitions are given in Sections

6
–
7
.

Theorem 5

(
Holographic formula for
{\bf Q}_{n}
).

Let
n
be even. If
{(\mathcal{SY})}
is satisfied, then it holds

{\bf Q}_{n}(g,\sigma)=(n\!-\!1)!^{2}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{n\!-\!1\!-\!2k}{\mathcal{T}}_{k}^{*}(g,\sigma;0)\left((n\!-\!1)(n\!-\!k)v_{n-k}+2k(v{\sf J})_{n-k-2}\right),

where
{\sf J}={\sf J}^{g}
. For
k=n-1
, the second term in the sum is defined as
0
.

The proof of Theorem
5
generalizes a proof of the holographic
formula for
Q_{n}
for Poincaré-Einstein metrics given in
[
JO20
]
. The arguments
rest on Theorem
1
and an alternative description of residue families in
terms of the coefficients
v_{k}
and the operators
{\mathcal{T}}_{k}(\lambda)
. We conjecture
that the result extends to odd
n
(Conjecture
11.4
). There are analogous
formulas for all subcritical extrinsic
Q
-curvatures which extend
[
J11
]
.

Theorem
5
implies the representation

{\bf Q}_{n}=n!(n\!-\!1)!v_{n}+\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{k}^{*}(0)(\cdots).

Integration of this identity for closed
M
implies the equality

(1.16)

\int_{M}{\bf Q}_{n}dvol_{h}=n!(n\!-\!1)!\int_{M}v_{n}dvol_{h}

of global conformal invariants. This extends a result of
[
GZ03
]
for
Poincaré-Einstein metrics and reproves a special case of
[
GW17
, Theorem
3.9]
. We shall also give a second proof of the identity (
1.16
) which
utilizes residue families and extends to odd
n
. We emphasize again that the
integrands on both sides depend on the embedding of
M
in
X
, i.e., these are
global conformal invariants of the embedding.

The integral on the right-hand side of (
1.16
) appears as a coefficient of
\log\varepsilon
in the asymptotic expansion of the volume

(1.17)

vol_{\sigma^{-2}g}(\{\sigma>\varepsilon\})=\int_{\sigma>\varepsilon}dvol_{\sigma^{-2}g}

of the singular metric
\sigma^{-2}g
for
\varepsilon\to 0
. This implies its
conformal invariance. Through that interpretation of the right-hand side of
(
1.16
), this identity is a special case of
[
GW17
, Theorem 3.9]
. The
singular terms in this expansion of the volume are given by the integrals of the
renormalized volume coefficients
v_{k}
for
k<n
. All these integrals admit the
uniform expressions

(1.18)

\int_{M}v_{k}dvol_{h}=(-1)^{k}\frac{(n\!-\!1\!-\!k)!}{(n-1)!k!}\int_{M}\iota^{*}L_{k}(-n\!+\!k)(1)dvol_{h}

in terms of Laplace-Robin operators. This reproves a special case of
[
GW17
, Proposition 3.5]
.

The above results for the expansion of the volumes (
1.17
) have analogs for general
defining functions
\sigma
. In that case, the operators
L_{k}
are replaced by composition
\tilde{L}_{k}=(L\circ\mathcal{S}^{-1})_{k}
and the coefficient of
\log\varepsilon
is a constant multiple of the integral
of a curvature quantity
\tilde{{\bf Q}}_{n}(g,\sigma)
(Theorem
10.13
, (
10.29
)). The total
integral of
\tilde{{\bf Q}}_{n}
is a conformal invariant of the pair
(g,\sigma)
(Lemma
10.15
).

The integrals in (
1.16
) are generalizations of the Willmore energy of a closed
surface in
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. In fact, for a surface
M^{2}\hookrightarrow X^{3}
with Gauss
curvature
K
, it holds

2v_{2}={\bf Q}_{2}=-K+\frac{1}{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}

(by (
13.9.1
), (
13.4.4
) and
{\sf J}^{h}=K
). But if
X
is the flat
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
,
then
H^{2}-K=|\mathring{L}|^{2}
. Hence for closed
M\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
the integral

\int_{M}v_{2}dvol_{h}
is a linear combination of the Euler characteristic
\chi(M)
and
the Willmore energy

\mathcal{W}=\int_{M}H^{2}dvol_{h}.

The Willmore energy also contributes to the rigid string action introduced in
[
P86
, (4)]
,

[
K86
, (5)]
.

Finally, the usage of adapted coordinates leads to a new formula for the singular Yamabe
obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
(see Section
3
for its definition). In order to state that formula,
we note that the pull-back
\eta^{*}(g)
of
g
takes the form
a^{-1}ds^{2}+h_{s}
with some
coefficient
a\in C^{\infty}((0,\varepsilon)\times M)
and a one-parameter family
h_{s}
of
metrics on
M
. For

\mathring{v}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}dvol_{h_{s}}/dvol_{h},

it holds

\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h_{s}^{\prime}).

Let
{\sf J}
and
\rho
denote the pull-backs of
{\sf J}^{g}
and
\rho(g,\sigma)
by
\eta
, respectively. The
following result restates Theorem
6.6
.

Theorem 6

(
The obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
).

If
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
, then

(1.19)

(n\!+\!1)!{\mathcal{B}}_{n}=-2\partial_{s}^{n}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}+4\sum_{j=1}^{n}j\binom{n}{j}\partial_{s}^{j-1}(\rho)|_{0}\partial_{s}^{n-j}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}-2n\partial_{s}^{n-1}({\sf J})|_{0}

for
n\geq 1
.

For
n=1
, the identity (
1.19
) yields
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}=0
.

Moreover, the Taylor coefficients of
\rho
obey a recursive relation in terms of the
Taylor coefficients of
\mathring{v}^{\prime}/\mathring{v}
and
{\sf J}
(Proposition
6.4
).

This result has several consequences of independent interest. For a flat
background metric, the scalar curvature term on the right-hand side of (
1.19
)
vanishes, and the recursive structure of all terms implies that for even
n

(n\!+\!1)!{\mathcal{B}}_{n}=-2(n\!-\!1)!!/(n\!-\!2)!!\Delta^{\frac{n}{2}}(H)+LOT

(Theorem
6.7
), where LOT refers to terms of lower differential order. Up
to the numerical coefficients, that description of the leading term of
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
was
first formulated in
[
GW14b
]

[
GW21
, Theorem 5.1]
(for general
backgrounds). We also use Theorem
6
to reproduce the known explicit
formulas for
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
(for general backgrounds) and to calculate
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
for
(conformally) flat backgrounds confirming earlier results.

Moreover, Theorem
5
and Theorem
6
play a key role in the proof of the
residue formula

\operatorname{Res}_{n=N-1}({\bf Q}_{N})=(-1)^{n-1}n!(n\!+\!1)!\frac{n}{2}{\mathcal{B}}_{n}

for even
n
(Theorem
11.6
), where we regard
{\bf Q}_{N}
as a function of

n
which is singular at
n=N-1
. This formula relates
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
for even
n
to the
super-critical
Q
-curvature
{\bf Q}_{n+1}
. It proves a conjecture in
[
GW21
, Remark
8.6]
. We conjecture that this result extends to all dimensions.

[
GW21
, Theorem 8.11]
established a coordinate-free formula for the Yamabe obstructions

{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
in terms of tractor calculus constructions. This formula involves an analog of the critical
extrinsic conformal Laplacian acting on the so-called normal tractor of the hypersurface
M
as a
key ingredient.
5
5

5

This result coincides with Theorem 7.7 in
arXiv:150602723v4
.

We finish this section with an outline of the content of the paper. We combine this
review with additional comments on the relations of the new results to the
literature. Section
3
briefly recalls basic results on the version of the
singular Yamabe problem of interest here. In Section
4
, we establish
Theorem
2
and describe first consequences. This result is the basic link that
connects the Laplace-Robin operators of Gover and Waldron with the spectral theory
of the Laplacian of the singular metric
\sigma^{-2}g
. In Section
5
, we
describe some of the representation theoretical aspects of the Laplace-Robin
operators. Section
6
introduces the notion of adapted coordinates. This
notion is basic for the whole work. Adapted coordinates give rise to the definition
of renormalized volume coefficients
v_{k}
, which enables us to prove local formulas
for
Q
-curvatures (Theorem
5
) and to prove the formula for the
obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
in Theorem
6
. In this connection, we show that in
adapted coordinates, the function
\rho
obeys an extremely beneficial ordinary differential
equation in the variable
s
, which implies recursive relations for the Taylor
coefficients of
\rho
. We emphasize that Graham
[
G17
]
utilizes a
different notion of renormalized volume coefficients which are defined in terms of
the normal exponential map. This notion seems to be less appropriate from the
present point of view. Section
7
introduces the notion of residue
families. Here we extend earlier definitions in the context of Poincaré-Einstein
metrics
[
J09
,
J13
,
BJ10
]
. The constructions are built on basic facts in the
spectral theory of asymptotically hyperbolic metrics, which are detailed in

[
GZ03
]
. In particular, we use a version of a Poisson transform and introduce the
scattering operator
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
. We derive a formula for residue families in
terms of the coefficients
v_{k}
and the operators
{\mathcal{T}}_{k}(\lambda)
. Section

8
contains the proof of Theorem
1
. The identity (
1.18
)
is a direct consequence. Here we also prove that these identities can be regarded as
consequences of a beautiful formula for the action of
L_{N}(-1)
on the distribution

\sigma^{*}(\delta)=\delta_{M}
of
M
(Theorem
8.5
). This
distributional formula reproves a basic technical result in
[
GW17
]
. In
Section
9
, we introduce the notion of extrinsic conformal Laplace operators

{\bf P}_{N}
. Here we derive the spectral theoretical interpretation (
1.11
) of

{\bf P}_{N}
, use it to determine the leading terms of
{\bf P}_{N}
(Theorem
3
) and
recognize these operators as residues of the scattering operator (first part of
Theorem
4
). These results extend results of
[
GZ03
]
. Section

10
defines extrinsic
Q
-curvatures and establishes the second part
of Theorem
4
which extends another result of
[
GZ03
]
. Moreover, we
supply two proofs of the equality (
1.16
). The integrated coefficients
v_{k}
are
shown to describe the singular coefficients in the asymptotic expansion of
(
1.17
). These expansions and their generalizations (Theorem

10.13
) reprove results of
[
GZ03
,
GW17
]
. In Section
11
,
we establish extensions of the holographic formulas for
Q
-curvatures in
[
GJ07
,
J11
]
. In
particular, we prove Theorem
5
. Section
12
contains
comments on further perspectives.

In the main body of the text, we usually suppress detailed calculations and the
discussion of examples and special cases. However, the reader can find this material in
Section
13
. It starts with an overview of its own. The results presented
here may be used to gain a deeper understanding of the material. In particular,
this section contains full details on low-order Yamabe obstructions, low-order cases
of conformal Laplacians, extrinsic
Q
-curvatures, and renormalized volume
coefficients. All proofs are independent of the literature.

Hopefully, the attached list of symbols facilitates reading.

Finally, we like to emphasize that, although the present work is deeply inspired by
the pioneering works of Gover and Waldron, the current treatment is fully
independent and self-contained. We also stress again that our perspective is (via
the residue families and their applications) one of a spectral-theoretic nature
exploiting the structure of eigenfunctions of the Laplacian.

After the present work had been posted, the paper
[
CMY21
]
again discussed extrinsic
conformal Laplacians from the perspective of scattering theory. It
defines

extrinsic conformal Laplacians in terms of the scattering operator of the singular
Yamabe metric
\sigma^{-2}g
by mimicking the known relations between
GJMS-operators and the scattering operator of Poincaré-Einstein metrics
[
GZ03
]
.
However, the relations between these definitions and the notions introduced by Gover
and Waldron are established only here.

. The work on this project started during a visit of the first
author at the University of Århus in autumn 2019. A large part of the paper’s
final version was written during a stay of the first author at IHES in early
2020. He is grateful to both organizations for financial support and very
stimulating atmospheres. Finally, we thank the anonymous referee who
provided valuable detailed comments on an earlier version of the manuscript.

2.
General notation

{\mathbb{N}}
is the set of natural numbers, and
{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
is the set of non-negative integers. For
a complex number
a\in{\mathbb{C}}
and an integer
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the Pochhammer symbol
(a)_{N}
is
defined by
(a)_{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}a(a+1)\cdots(a+N-1)
. We set
(a)_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}1
.

All manifolds are smooth. For a manifold
X
,
C^{\infty}(X)
and
C_{c}^{\infty}(X)

denote the respective spaces of smooth functions and smooth functions with compact
support on
X
. If
X
is a manifold with boundary, then
C^{\infty}(X)
is the space of
functions that are smooth up to the boundary.
C^{-\infty}(X)
is the space of distributions
on
X
. Let
\mathfrak{X}(X)
be the space smooth vector fields on
X
. Metrics
on
X
usually are denoted by
g
.
dvol_{g}
is the Riemannian volume element defined by
g
. The
Levi-Civita connection of
g
is denoted by
\nabla_{X}^{g}
or simply
\nabla_{X}
for
X\in\mathfrak{X}(X)
if
g
is understood. In these terms, the curvature tensor
R

of the Riemannian manifold
(X,g)
is defined by
R(X,Y)Z=\nabla_{X}\nabla_{Y}(Z)-\nabla_{Y}\nabla_{X}(Z)-\nabla_{[X.Y]}(Z)
for vector fields
X,Y,Z\in\mathfrak{X}(X)
. We also set
\nabla_{X}(u)=\langle du,X\rangle
for
X\in\mathfrak{X}(X)
and
u\in C^{\infty}(X)
.

For a metric
g
on
X
and
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(X)
, let
\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)
be the
gradient of
\sigma
with respect to
g
, i.e., it holds
g(\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma),V)=\langle d\sigma,V\rangle
for all vector fields
V\in\mathfrak{X}(X)
.
g

defines pointwise scalar products and norms
|\cdot|_{g}
on
\mathfrak{X}(X)
and on
forms
\Omega^{*}(X)
. Then
|\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)|_{g}^{2}=|d\sigma|_{g}^{2}
.
\delta^{g}
is the
divergence operator on differential forms or symmetric bilinear forms. On forms, it
coincides with the negative adjoint
-d^{*}
of the enaböe differential
d
with
respect to the Hodge scalar product defined by
g
. Let
\Delta_{g}=\delta^{g}d
be
the non-positive Laplacian on
C^{\infty}(X)
. On the Euclidean space
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, it
equals
\sum_{i}\partial_{i}^{2}
.

A metric
g
on a manifold
X
with boundary
M
induces a metric
h
on
M
.
Curvature quantities of
g
and
h
have the respective metric as an index if
required by clarity. In particular, the scalar curvature of the metric
g
on
X
is
denoted by
\operatorname{scal}^{g}
or
\operatorname{scal}(g)
.
\operatorname{Ric}^{g}
denote the Ricci tensor of
g
. On a
manifold
(M,g)
of dimension
n
, we set
{\sf J}^{g}=\frac{1}{2(n-1)}\operatorname{scal}^{g}
if
n\geq 2
and define the Schouten tensor
{\sf P}^{g}
of
g
by
(n-2){\sf P}^{g}=\operatorname{Ric}^{g}-{\sf J}^{g}g

if
n\geq 3
.

Let
M
be a hypersurface in
(X,g)
with the induced metric
h
. The second
fundamental form
L
of
M
is defined by
L(X,Y)=-g(\nabla_{X}(Y),N)
for vector
fields
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
and a unit normal vector field
N
. In particular,
if
X
is a manifold with boundary
M
with defining function
\sigma
so that

|d\sigma|_{g}=1
on
M
, then we set
L(X,Y)=-g(\nabla_{X}(Y),\mathcal{N})
with
\mathcal{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)
. With these conventions,
L=g
for the round sphere
S^{n}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
if
\sigma
is the distance function of
S^{n}
. We set
nH=\operatorname{tr}_{h}(L)
if

M
has dimension
n
.
H
is the mean curvature of
M
. Let
\mathring{L}=L-Hh
be the
trace-free part of
L
. We sometimes identify
L
with the shape operator
S
defined by

h(X,S(Y))=L(X,Y)
. For a bilinear form
b
on
T(M)
, we denote its trace-free
part with respect to a metric known by context by
\mathring{b}
or
b_{\circ}
.

\iota
denotes various canonical embeddings such as
\iota:M\hookrightarrow X
.
The symbol
\iota^{*}
will be used for the induced pull-back of functions, forms, and metrics.
For any diffeomorphism
f
, the symbol
f_{*}=(f^{-1})^{*}
denotes the push-forward by
f
.
Differentiation with respect to the variable
\lambda
will often be denoted by

⋅
. In contrast, differentiation with respect to the variables
r
and
s

will usually be denoted by
′
. The symbol
\circ
denotes compositions of
operators. The symbol
\sim
indicates a proportionality.

3.
The singular Yamabe problem

Let
(X,g)
be a compact manifold with boundary
M
of dimension
n
. The problem to
ask for a defining function
\sigma
of
M
so that

(3.1)

\operatorname{scal}(\sigma^{-2}g)=-n(n+1)

is known as (a version of) the singular Yamabe problem
[
LN74
]
. The metric
\sigma^{-2}g

is called a singular Yamabe metric if (
3.1
) is true.
6
6

6

Later we shall often deal with
a weaker condition.
The conformal transformation law of scalar curvature shows that

\operatorname{scal}(\sigma^{-2}g)=-n(n+1)|d\sigma|_{g}^{2}+2n\sigma\Delta_{g}(\sigma)+\sigma^{2}\operatorname{scal}(g).

Following
[
GW14a
]
, we write this equation in the form

\operatorname{scal}(\sigma^{-2}g)=-n(n+1)\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma),

where

\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)=|d\sigma|_{g}^{2}+2\rho\sigma

(see Definition (
4.1
)).
7
7

7

In
[
GW17
, Section 2.2]
, the quantity

\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)
is termed the
\mathcal{S}
-curvature of
(g,\sigma)
.
In these terms,

\sigma
is a solution of (
3.1
) iff
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)=1
. Such
\sigma
exist

[
AM88
]
and are unique
[
ACF92
]
. However, in general,
\sigma
is not smooth up to
the boundary. The smoothness is obstructed by a locally determined conformally
invariant scalar function on
M
, called the singular Yamabe obstruction.
Moreover, the solution is smooth up to the boundary iff the obstruction vanishes

[
ACF92
]
.

In order to describe the structure of
\sigma
more precisely, we follow
[
ACF92
]

and
[
G17
]
. We use geodesic normal coordinates (see Section

6
). Let
r=d_{M}
be the distance function of
M
for the background metric
g
.
Then there are uniquely determined coefficients
\sigma_{(k)}\in C^{\infty}(M)
for
2\leq k\leq n+1
so that the smooth defining function

(3.2)

\sigma_{F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}r+\sigma_{(2)}r^{2}+\dots+\sigma_{(n+1)}r^{n+1}

satisfies

(3.3)

\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma_{F})=1+Rr^{n+1}

with a smooth remainder term
R
. We briefly describe how these coefficients are
recursively determined. In geodesic normal coordinates, the metric
g
takes the form

dr^{2}+h_{r}
with a one-parameter family
h_{r}
of metrics on
M
. The condition
(
3.3
) is equivalent to

|d\sigma_{F}|_{g}^{2}-\frac{2}{n+1}\sigma_{F}\Delta_{g}(\sigma_{F})-\frac{1}{n(n+1)}\sigma_{F}^{2}\operatorname{scal}^{g}=1+Rr^{n+1}.

We write the left-hand side of this equation in the form

\displaystyle\partial_{r}(\sigma_{F})^{2}+h_{r}^{ij}\partial_{i}(\sigma_{F})\partial_{j}(\sigma_{F})

(3.4)

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{n+1}\sigma_{F}\left(\partial_{r}^{2}(\sigma_{F})+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{r}^{-1}h_{r}^{\prime})\partial_{r}(\sigma_{F})+\Delta_{h_{r}}(\sigma_{F})\right)-\frac{1}{n(n+1)}\sigma_{F}^{2}\operatorname{scal}^{g}

and expand this sum into a Taylor series in the variable
r
. Then the vanishing of
the coefficient of
r^{k}
for
k\leq n
is equivalent to an identity of the form

(k-1-n)\sigma_{(k+1)}=LOT,

where
LOT
involves only lower-order Taylor coefficients of
\sigma
. The latter relation
also shows that there is a possible obstruction to the existence of an improved
solution
\sigma_{F}^{\prime}
which contains a term
\sigma_{(n+2)}r^{n+2}
and satisfies

\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma_{F}^{\prime})=1+Rr^{n+2}
. However, by setting

(3.5)

\sigma=\sigma_{F}+{\mathcal{L}}_{n}r^{n+2}\log r

with an appropriate coefficient
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\in C^{\infty}(M)
one may get a solution of

(3.6)

\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)=1+O(r^{n+2}\log r).

The coefficient
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
is determined by the condition that the coefficient of

r^{n+1}
in the expansion of (
3
) vanishes. But that coefficient equals

\left(r^{-n-1}(\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma_{F})-1)\right)|_{r=0}-2\frac{n+2}{n+1}{\mathcal{L}}_{n}.

The first term exists by the construction of
\sigma_{F}
and the second term is
generated by the action of the terms
\partial_{r}(\sigma)^{2}
and
\sigma\partial_{r}^{2}(\sigma)
in (
3
) on the log-term in
\sigma
.
8
8

8

In fact,
\partial_{r}(r^{n+2}\log r)=r^{n+1}+\cdots
and
\partial_{r}^{2}(r^{n+2}\log r)=(2n+3)r^{n}+\cdots
.
Hence for

{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2}\frac{n+1}{n+2}\left(r^{-n-1}(\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma_{F})-1)\right)|_{r=0}

the condition (
3.6
) is satisfied. Following
[
ACF92
]
, we define the

singular Yamabe obstruction
by

(3.7)

{\mathcal{B}}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\left(r^{-n-1}(\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma_{F})-1)\right)|_{r=0}.

In these terms, we see that with

(3.8)

{\mathcal{L}}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{n+1}{n+2}{\mathcal{B}}_{n}

the improved
\sigma
defined in (
3.5
) satisfies (
3.6
). By

[
ACF92
]
, the unique solution
\sigma
of the singular Yamabe problem has an
expansion of the form

\sigma=r+\sigma_{(2)}r^{2}+\dots+\sigma_{(n+1)}r^{n+1}+{\mathcal{L}}_{n}r^{n+2}\log r+\dots.

Graham
[
G17
]
calls
{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
the singular Yamabe obstruction.

Since
\sigma_{F}
is determined by
g
(and the embedding
M\hookrightarrow X
), we regard

{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
as a functional of
g
(and the embedding). It is a key result that
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
is a conformal invariant
of
g
. More precisely, we write
\hat{{\mathcal{B}}}_{n}
for the obstruction defined by
\hat{g}=e^{2\varphi}g

with
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(X)
. Then

Lemma 3.1

(
[
ACF92
,
G17
]
)
.

e^{(n+1)\iota^{*}(\varphi)}\hat{{\mathcal{B}}}_{n}={\mathcal{B}}_{n}
.

Proof.

Let
r
be the distance function of
M
for
g
. Let

S_{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}1+\sum_{j=2}r^{j}\sigma_{(j)}.

Then for
N\leq n+1
the condition

(3.9)

\mathcal{S}(g,S_{N})=1+0r+\cdots+0r^{N-1}+O(r^{N})=1+O(r^{N})

determines the coefficient
\sigma_{(N)}
in terms of lower-order coefficients
\sigma_{(2)},\dots,\sigma_{(N-1)}
.
In that case, we also write
S_{N}=S_{N}(g)
. Moreover, we recall that the coefficient of
r^{k}
(
k\leq N-1
) in the
expansion of
\mathcal{S}(g,S_{N})
depends only on
\sigma_{(2)},\dots,\sigma_{(k+1)}
.

Now we have the obvious relation

\mathcal{S}(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma})=\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)

for any
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(X)
and
\hat{\sigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}e^{\varphi}\sigma
.

We write the expansion (
3.9
) (for
N\leq n+1
) as an expansion in terms of
the distance function
\hat{r}
of
M
for
\hat{g}
. Hence

(3.10)

\mathcal{S}(\hat{g},e^{\varphi}S_{N}(g))=1+O(\hat{r}^{N}).

This expansion determines the coefficients
\hat{\sigma}_{(2)},\dots,\hat{\sigma}_{(N)}
in the expansion

e^{\varphi}S_{N}(g)=\hat{r}+\hat{r}^{2}\hat{\sigma}_{(2)}+\dots+\hat{r}^{N}\hat{\sigma}_{(N)}+\cdots=S_{N}(\hat{g})+\mbox{higher-order terms}.

In general, this expansion involves higher-order terms, i.e.,
e^{\varphi}S_{N}(g)\neq S_{N}(\hat{g})
.
In particular, the remainder term in (
3.10
) depends on
\hat{\sigma}_{N+1}
. However,
for
N=n+1
, the coefficient of
\hat{r}^{n+1}
in (
3.10
) does
not
depend
on
\hat{\sigma}_{(n+2)}
since it appears with a prefactor
0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle(\hat{r}^{-n-1}\mathcal{S}(\hat{g},S_{n+1}(\hat{g})))|_{\hat{r}=0}

\displaystyle=(\hat{r}^{-n-1}\mathcal{S}(\hat{g},e^{\varphi}S_{n+1}(g)))|_{\hat{r}=0}

\displaystyle=e^{-(n+1)\iota^{*}(\varphi)}(r^{-n-1}\mathcal{S}(g,S_{n+1}(g)))|_{r=0}.

This relation implies the assertion.
∎

In
[
GW21
]
, Gover and Waldron describe an elegant algorithm that recursively
determines the solution
\sigma
of the singular Yamabe problem as a power series of some
boundary defining function
\sigma_{0}
(like the distance function
\sigma_{0}=d_{M}
). Note that
these power series are not Taylor series: their coefficients still live on the ambient space
X
.
This algorithm rests on the interpretation of the quantity
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)
as the squared length of
the scale tractor associated to
\sigma
.

For our purposes, it will be enough to consider smooth
approximate
solutions
of the singular Yamabe problem. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 3.2
.

The defining function
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(X)
of
M
is said to
satisfy the condition
{(\mathcal{SY})}
iff

\sigma=r+\sigma_{(2)}r^{2}+\dots+\sigma_{(n+1)}r^{n+1}+O(r^{n+2})

and

\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)=1+R_{n+1}r^{n+1}

for a smooth remainder term
R_{n+1}
. Equivalently, it holds

\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)=1+R_{n+1}\sigma^{n+1}

for another smooth remainder term
R_{n+1}
. The restriction of either remainder
terms to
M
is the singular Yamabe obstruction:
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}=\iota^{*}R_{n+1}
.

We recall that the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
satisfies

(3.11)

e^{(n+1)\iota^{*}(\varphi)}{\mathcal{B}}_{n}(\hat{g})={\mathcal{B}}_{n}(g).

Graham
[
G17
]
determined the first two non-trivial coefficients
\sigma_{(2)}

and
\sigma_{(3)}
in the expansion of
\sigma
(Lemma
13.3.2
). An explicit formula
for the next coefficient
\sigma_{(4)}
is given in
[
GG19
, (2.18)]
. We reproduce these results
in a slightly different form in Section
13.3
and also display a formula for
\sigma_{(5)}
.

Graham
[
G17
]
shows that the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
vanishes. The obstruction
for surfaces in a three-manifold is given by the formula

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}(\delta^{h}\delta^{h}(\mathring{L})+H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+(\mathring{L},\iota^{*}({\sf P}^{g})))

(3.12)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}(\Delta_{h}(H)+\delta^{h}(\operatorname{Ric}^{g}(\mathcal{N},\cdot))+H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+(\mathring{L},\iota^{*}({\sf P}^{g})))

(
[
GW21
, Corollary 6.10]
). The equivalence of both expressions follows from the
Codazzi-Mainardi equation. For the details of that argument and a derivation of these formulas from
Theorem
6
, we refer to Section
13.6
. The first formula reproduces a result
in
[
ACF92
, Theorem 1.3]
.

Explicit formulas for
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
were first derived in
[
Ha15
]
and
[
GGHW19
]
from a general universal tractor
formula found in
[
GW21
]
. In particular, it was proved that for a conformally flat background metric
g

the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
is given by the closed formula

(3.13)

6{\mathcal{B}}_{3}=3(\delta^{h}\delta^{h}+({\sf P}^{h},\cdot))((\mathring{L}^{2})_{\circ})+|\mathring{L}|^{4},

where
b_{\circ}
denotes the trace-free part of the symmetric bilinear form
b
. For general background
metrics
g
, the formula for
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
contains additional terms defined by the Weyl tensor of
g
. For full details,
we refer to
[
GGHW19
]
. In the more recent work
[
JO22
]
, these formulas for
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
were derived without
utilizing tractor calculus. In Section
13.7
, we shall deduce (
3.13
) from the
general formula in Theorem
6
. Formula (
3.13
) manifestly implies the conformal
invariance
e^{4\varphi}\hat{{\mathcal{B}}}_{3}={\mathcal{B}}_{3}
since the operator
b\mapsto\delta\delta(b)+({\sf P},b)
is
conformally covariant on trace-free symmetric bilinear forms on
M
.

Up to constant multiples,
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
are the respective variations of the functionals

\int_{M}|\mathring{L}|_{h}^{2}dvol_{h}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\int_{M}(\mathring{L},\mathcal{F})_{h}dvol_{h}

(for the definition of the Fialkov tensor
\mathcal{F}
we refer to Section
13.1.1
).
These are special cases of the variation formulas of the functional
{\mathcal{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\int_{M}v_{n}dvol_{h}

(with respect to a one-parameter family of hypersurfaces
M\hookrightarrow X
) which were proved
in
[
G17
, Theorem 3.1]
and
[
GW17
, Section 5]
. They state that the
variation of
{\mathcal{A}}
is proportional to the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
. The equivalence of both results follows
from (
3.8
). This result may be regarded as an analog of the result that the metric
variation of the total critical
Q
-curvature of an even-dimensional closed manifold is given by the
corresponding Fefferman-Graham obstruction tensor
[
GH05
]
.

Poincaré-Einstein metrics are an important special class of singular Yamabe
metrics. In fact, if
g_{+}=r^{-2}(dr^{2}+h_{r})
is a Poincaré-Einstein metric in
normal form relative to
h=h_{0}

[
FG12
]
, then
\operatorname{scal}(g_{+})=-n(n+1)
, the
background metric is
dr^{2}+h_{r}
and the corresponding defining function is

\sigma=r
. In this case, the singular Yamabe obstruction vanishes. We shall refer to this
case as the Poincaré-Einstein case.

4.
The conjugation formula

In this section, we introduce Laplace-Robin operators (or degenerate Laplacians)
following
[
GW21
]
. We relate them to the spectral theory of the Laplacian of
singular metrics
\sigma^{-2}g
and use this relation to prove basic properties of the
Laplace-Robin operators.

Let
X
be a manifold of dimension
n+1
.

Definition 4.1

(
Laplace-Robin operators).

For any pair
(g,\sigma)
consisting of a metric
g
on
X
and
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(X)
, the one-parameter family

(4.1)

L(g,\sigma;\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(n\!+\!2\lambda\!-\!1)(\nabla_{\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)}+\lambda\rho)-\sigma(\Delta_{g}+\lambda{\sf J}):C^{\infty}(X)\to C^{\infty}(X)

of differential operators is called the Laplace-Robin operator of the pair
(g,\sigma)
. Here

\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
,

2n{\sf J}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\operatorname{scal}(g)\quad\mbox{and}\quad(n+1)\rho(g,\sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}-\Delta_{g}(\sigma)-\sigma{\sf J}.

Moreover, we set

(4.2)

\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}|d\sigma|_{g}^{2}+2\sigma\rho.

Similarly, we define
L(g,\sigma;\lambda)
for a manifold
X
with boundary
M
. In
this case,
g
and
\sigma
are assumed to be smooth up to the boundary. Then

L(\lambda)
acts on the space
C^{\infty}(X)
of smooth functions up to the boundary
and on the space
C^{\infty}(X^{\circ})
of smooth functions on the open interior

X^{\circ}=X\setminus M
of
X
.

From now on, we assume that
(X,g)
is a compact manifold with boundary
M
and

\sigma
is a defining function of
M
. We recall that
\sigma
is a defining
function of
M
if
\sigma^{-1}(0)=M
,
\sigma>0
on
X^{\circ}
and
d\sigma|_{M}\neq 0
. Let
\iota:M\hookrightarrow X
be the embedding and set
h\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\iota^{*}(g)
.

Then the operator
\iota^{*}L(g,\sigma;\lambda)
degenerates to the first-order operator

(4.3)

C^{\infty}(X)\ni u\mapsto(n\!+\!2\lambda\!-\!1)\iota^{*}\left[\nabla_{\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)}(u)-\frac{\lambda}{n\!+\!1}\Delta_{g}(\sigma)u\right]\in C^{\infty}(M).

Since certain linear combinations of Dirichlet and Neumann boundary values are also known
as Robin boundary values, this naturally motivates the above notion of a Laplace-Robin operator.

If
\sigma^{-2}g
has constant scalar curvature
-n(n+1)
, the boundary
operator (
4.3
) reduces to the conformally covariant boundary operator

u\mapsto(n\!+\!2\lambda\!-\!1)\iota^{*}(\nabla_{\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)}-\lambda H)u,

where
H
is the mean curvature of
M
(
[
G10
, Section 3.1]
,
[
GW17
, Lemma 2.3]
).

If
g_{+}=r^{-2}g
is Poincaré-Einstein in the sense that
\operatorname{Ric}(g_{+})=-ng_{+}
, then

L(g,r;\lambda-n+1)
equals the shift operator
S(g_{+};\lambda)
of
[
JO20
]
.

Theorem 4.2

(
Conjugation formula).

Assume that
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(X)
is a defining function of the boundary
M
of
X
.
Then it holds

(4.4)

L(g,\sigma;\lambda)+\sigma^{\lambda-1}\circ\left(\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}-\lambda(n\!+\!\lambda)\operatorname{Id}\right)\circ\sigma^{-\lambda}=\lambda(n\!+\!\lambda)\sigma^{-1}(\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)-1)\operatorname{Id}

as an identity of operators acting on
C^{\infty}(X^{\circ})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}(g,\sigma;\lambda)
denote the operator

\sigma^{\lambda-1}\circ\left(\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}-\lambda(n\!+\!\lambda)\operatorname{Id}\right)\circ\sigma^{-\lambda}.

The relation

\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}=\sigma^{2}\Delta_{g}-(n\!-\!1)\sigma\nabla_{\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)}

shows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(g,\sigma;\lambda)(u)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\lambda+1}\Delta_{g}(\sigma^{-\lambda}u)-(n\!-\!1)\sigma^{\lambda}\nabla_{\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)}(\sigma^{-\lambda}u)-\lambda(n\!+\!\lambda)\sigma^{-1}u

\displaystyle=\sigma\Delta_{g}(u)-(n\!+\!2\lambda\!-\!1)\nabla_{\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)}(u)+\mathcal{C}(g,\sigma;\lambda)(1)u

for
u\in C^{\infty}(X^{\circ})
. Thus, it only remains to calculate the constant term
\operatorname{CT}(\mathcal{C})(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{C}(\lambda)(1)\in C^{\infty}(X^{\circ})
of
\mathcal{C}(\lambda)
. Note that

\displaystyle\operatorname{CT}(\mathcal{C})(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\lambda-1}(\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}-\lambda(n\!+\!\lambda)\operatorname{Id})(\sigma^{-\lambda})

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\lambda+1}\Delta_{g}(\sigma^{-\lambda})+\lambda(n\!-\!1)\sigma^{-1}|\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)|^{2}-\lambda(n\!+\!\lambda)\sigma^{-1}

shows that
\operatorname{CT}(\mathcal{C})(\lambda)
is a quadratic polynomial in
\lambda
. It is
obvious that
\operatorname{CT}(\mathcal{C})(0)=0
. Next, we determine the leading coefficient of that
polynomial. We choose orthonormal bases
\left\{\partial_{i}\right\}
on the
tangent spaces of the level hypersurfaces
\sigma^{-1}(c)
of
\sigma
. The sets

\sigma^{-1}(c)
are smooth manifolds if
c
is sufficiently small.
\mathcal{N}
is
perpendicular to these hypersurfaces. Let
\left\{\alpha,dx^{i}\right\}
be the
dual basis of
\left\{\mathcal{N},\partial_{i}\right\}
. Then

(4.5)

\Delta_{g}(u)=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{N}|^{2}}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(u)+\Delta_{g_{\sigma}}(u)+\frac{1}{|\mathcal{N}|}H_{\sigma}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(u)-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{N}|^{2}}\langle du,\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathcal{N})\rangle,

where
-H_{\sigma}=\langle\alpha,\nabla_{\partial_{i}}(\partial_{i})\rangle
and

\Delta_{g_{\sigma}}
denotes the tangential Laplacians for the induced metrics on
the leaves
\sigma^{-1}(c)
; for more details, see Section
13.1.3
.
Since
\Delta_{g_{\sigma}}(\sigma^{-\lambda})=0
, it follows that the coefficient of
\lambda^{2}

in
\sigma^{\lambda+1}\Delta_{g}(\sigma^{-\lambda})
is given by

\sigma^{-1}|\mathcal{N}|^{-2}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\sigma)^{2}=\sigma^{-1}|\mathcal{N}|^{2}
.
Hence the leading coefficient of the quadratic polynomial
\operatorname{CT}(\mathcal{C})(\lambda)
equals

\sigma^{-1}|\mathcal{N}|^{2}-\sigma^{-1}.

Finally, we calculate

\operatorname{CT}(\mathcal{C})(-1)=\Delta_{g}(\sigma)-(n-1)\sigma^{-1}|\mathcal{N}|^{2}+(n-1)\sigma^{-1}.

These arguments prove that

(4.6)

\operatorname{CT}(\mathcal{C})(\lambda)=\lambda\left((n\!+\!\lambda)\sigma^{-1}(|\mathcal{N}|^{2}-1)-\Delta_{g}(\sigma)\right).

Hence

\displaystyle\operatorname{CT}(L)(\lambda)+\operatorname{CT}(\mathcal{C})(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\lambda(n\!+\!2\lambda\!-\!1)\rho-\lambda\sigma{\sf J}+\lambda\left((n\!+\!\lambda)\sigma^{-1}(|\mathcal{N}|^{2}-1)-\Delta_{g}(\sigma)\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda(n\!+\!\lambda)\sigma^{-1}(2\rho\sigma+|\mathcal{N}|^{2}-1)

\displaystyle=\lambda(n\!+\!\lambda)\sigma^{-1}(\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)-1)

by the definition of
\rho
. This completes the proof.
∎

The above proof shows the identity (
4.4
) only for functions with support near the boundary
M
.
This will be enough for all later applications. For simplicity, we shall interpret (
4.4
) and similar identities in this
way without further mentioning.

Remark 4.3
.

Let
g_{+}=r^{-2}(dr^{2}+h_{r})
be a Poincaré-Einstein metric in normal form
relative to the metric
h
on
M
. It lives on the space
(0,\varepsilon)\times M

and satisfies
\operatorname{Ric}(g_{+})=-ng_{+}
. Hence
\operatorname{scal}(g_{+})=-n(n+1)
. Then

\mathcal{S}(dr^{2}+h_{r},r;\lambda)=\mathcal{S}(g_{+},1;\lambda)=1
, and the conjugation formula reads

-L(dr^{2}+h_{r},r;\lambda)=r^{\lambda-1}\circ(\Delta_{g_{+}}-\lambda(n+\lambda)\operatorname{Id})\circ r^{-\lambda}.

We refer to
[
JO20
, Section 3]
for the discussion of the relation between this conjugation formula
and a formula in
[
GZ03
]
.

Remark 4.4
.

The conjugation formula is equivalent to the identity

(4.7)

L(g,\sigma;\lambda)+\sigma^{\lambda-1}\circ\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}\circ\sigma^{-\lambda}=\lambda(n\!+\!\lambda)\sigma^{-1}\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)\operatorname{Id}

of operators acting on
C^{\infty}(X^{\circ})
.

The conformal covariance of the Laplace-Robin operator is an immediate consequence of these identities.
More precisely, we have

Corollary 4.5
.

The Laplace-Robin operator satisfies

(4.8)

L(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma};\lambda)\circ e^{\lambda\varphi}=e^{(\lambda-1)\varphi}\circ L(g,\sigma;\lambda),\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}

for all conformal changes
(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma})=(e^{2\varphi}g,e^{\varphi}\sigma)
,
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(X)
.

Proof.

It suffices to note that
\hat{\sigma}^{-2}\hat{g}=\sigma^{-2}g
and

\mathcal{S}(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma})=\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)
.
∎

Strictly speaking, the above arguments prove the conformal covariance of the
operator
L(g,\sigma;\lambda)
for boundary defining
\sigma
when acting on

C^{\infty}(X^{\circ})
. In
[
GW14a
]
, the conformal covariance of the operator

L(g,\sigma;\lambda)
for
any
pair
(g,\sigma)
acting on
C^{\infty}(X)
follows from
its interpretation in terms of tractor calculus. For a direct proof, see
[
JO20
, Proposition 2.2]
.

The following consequence of the conjugation formula will be of central significance
in the rest of the paper. We continue to assume that
\sigma
is a boundary defining function
and statements are valid near
M
.

Corollary 4.6
.

It holds

(4.9)

-L(g,\sigma;\lambda)=\sigma^{\lambda-1}\circ(\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}-\lambda(n+\lambda)\operatorname{Id})\circ\sigma^{-\lambda}

for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
iff
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)=1
. More generally, it holds

-L(g,\sigma;\lambda)=\sigma^{\lambda-1}\circ(\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}-\lambda(n+\lambda)\operatorname{Id})\circ\sigma^{-\lambda}+O(\sigma^{n})

if
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. These identities are identities of operators acting on
C^{\infty}(X^{\circ})
.

For
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we define

(4.10)

L_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}L(g,\sigma;\lambda\!-\!N\!+\!1)\circ\cdots\circ L(g,\sigma;\lambda).

In these terms, iterated application of (
4.9
) implies

Corollary 4.7
.

Assume that
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)=1
. Then it holds

\sigma^{-\frac{n}{2}-N}\circ\prod_{j=0}^{2N-1}\left(\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}+\left(\frac{n}{2}\!+\!N\!-\!j\right)\left(\frac{n}{2}\!-\!N\!+\!j\right)\operatorname{Id}\right)\circ\sigma^{\frac{n}{2}-N}=L_{2N}\left(g,\sigma;-\frac{n}{2}\!+\!N\right)

for
2N\leq n
. In particular, we have

(4.11)

\sigma^{-n}\circ\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\left(\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}+(n\!-\!j)j\operatorname{Id}\right)=L_{n}(g,\sigma;0)

The conjugation formula also sheds new light on the fact that the formal adjoint of a
Laplace-Robin operator is another Laplace-Robin operator.

Corollary 4.8
.

The Laplace-Robin operator satisfies

(4.12)

L(g,\sigma;\lambda)^{*}=L(g,\sigma;-\lambda\!-\!n),\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}},

where
∗
denotes the adjoint operator with respect to the Riemannian volume of

g
. More precisely, it holds

(4.13)

\int_{X}L(g,\sigma;\lambda)(\varphi)\psi dvol_{g}=\int_{X}\varphi L(g,\sigma;-\lambda\!-\!n)(\psi)dvol_{g}

for
\varphi,\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X^{\circ})
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi,\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X^{\circ})
. The identity (
4.7
) yields

\displaystyle\int_{X}L(g,\sigma;\lambda)(\varphi)\psi dvol_{g}

\displaystyle=-\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda-1}\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}(\sigma^{-\lambda}\varphi)\psi dvol_{g}+\lambda(n\!+\!\lambda)\int_{X}\sigma^{-1}\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)\varphi\psi dvol_{g}.

Note that
\lambda(n+\lambda)
is invariant under the substitution
\lambda\mapsto-\lambda-n
. We rewrite the first integral in terms of volumes with respect to the
metric
\sigma^{-2}g
and apply the self-adjointness of
\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}
with
respect to the volume of the metric
\sigma^{-2}g
. Using
dvol_{\sigma^{-2}g}=\sigma^{-n-1}dvol_{g}
, we find

-\int_{X}\sigma^{-\lambda}\varphi\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}(\sigma^{\lambda+n}\psi)dvol_{\sigma^{-2}g}=-\int_{X}\varphi\sigma^{-\lambda-n-1}\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}(\sigma^{\lambda+n}\psi)dvol_{g}.

Now another application of (
4.7
) implies the assertion (
4.13
).
∎

For later applications, we need an extension of Corollary
4.8
to another class of test functions.
The proof of the following result will be given in Section
6
.

Proposition 4.9
.

The identity (
4.13
) continues to be true for
\psi\in C^{\infty}(X)
and

\varphi\in C^{2}(X)
so that
\iota^{*}(\varphi)=0
.

The proof of this result actually shows that

\displaystyle\int_{X}L(g,\sigma;\lambda)(\varphi)\psi dvol_{g}-\int_{X}\varphi L(g,\sigma;-\lambda\!-\!n)(\psi)dvol_{g}

(4.14)

\displaystyle=(n\!+\!2\lambda)\int_{M}\iota^{*}(\varphi\psi|\mathcal{N}|)dvol_{h}

for
\varphi,\psi\in C^{2}(X)
. The proof of this identity rests on a calculation of the left-hand side
(see also
[
GW17
, Theorem 2.2]
).

Finally, we derive some basic commutator relations.

Corollary 4.10
.

For any
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, it holds

L(g,\sigma;\lambda\!+\!N)\circ\sigma^{N}-\sigma^{N}\circ L(g,\sigma;\lambda)=N(n\!+\!2\lambda\!+\!N)\sigma^{N-1}\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)\operatorname{Id}.

In particular, it holds

(4.15)

L(g,\sigma;\lambda\!+\!1)\circ\sigma-\sigma\circ L(g,\sigma;\lambda)=(n\!+\!2\lambda\!+\!1)\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)\operatorname{Id}.

Hence

\displaystyle L_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda)\circ\sigma-\sigma\circ L_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda\!-\!1)=N(n\!+\!2\lambda\!-\!N)L_{N-1}(g,\sigma;\lambda-1)

(4.16)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{N}(n\!+\!2\lambda\!-\!2j\!+\!1)\underbrace{L(g,\sigma;\lambda\!-\!N\!+\!1)\circ\cdots\circ(\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)-1)\circ\cdots\circ L(g,\sigma;\lambda\!-\!1)}_{Nfactors}.

Moreover, if
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)
is nowhere zero, then for any
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
it holds

(4.17)

\tilde{L}_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda\!+\!1)\circ\sigma-\sigma\circ\tilde{L}_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda)=N(n\!+\!2\lambda\!-\!N\!+\!2)\tilde{L}_{N-1}(g,\sigma;\lambda),

where

(4.18)

{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\tilde{L}(g,\sigma)$}}}\tilde{L}(g,\sigma;\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}L(g,\sigma;\lambda)\circ\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)^{-1}

and

(4.19)

{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\tilde{L}_{N}(g,\sigma)$}}}\tilde{L}_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\tilde{L}(g,\sigma;\lambda\!-\!N\!+\!1)\circ\cdots\circ\tilde{L}(g,\sigma;\lambda).

Proof.

The identity (
4.7
) implies

\displaystyle L(g,\sigma;\lambda)\circ\sigma^{N}-\sigma^{N}\circ L(g,\sigma;\lambda-N)

\displaystyle=\lambda(n\!+\!\lambda)\sigma^{N-1}\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)\operatorname{Id}-(\lambda\!-\!N)(n\!+\!\lambda\!-\!N)\sigma^{N-1}\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)\operatorname{Id}

\displaystyle=N(n\!+\!2\lambda\!-\!N)\sigma^{N-1}\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)\operatorname{Id}.

This proves the first commutator relation. The remaining claims are
consequences. This completes the proof.
∎

The above commutator relations substantially simplify if
\mathcal{S}=1
.

Although we proved the identities in Corollary
4.10
as identities of operators acting on
C^{\infty}(X^{\circ})
,
they are also valid for operators acting on
C^{\infty}(X)
(
[
GW14a
, Section 3]
).

5.
Symmetry breaking operators

In the present section, we discuss some representation theoretical aspects of the
results in Section
4
.

The simplest special case of the Laplace-Robin operator
L
appears for the
hyperplane
M={\mathbb{R}}^{n}
in
X={\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
with the flat Euclidean metric
g_{0}
. Let
M
be
given by the zero locus of the defining function
\sigma_{0}=x_{n+1}
. We shall also
write
\sigma_{0}=r
and
g_{+}=r^{-2}g_{0}
. Then
{\sf J}=\rho=0
and we obtain

(5.1)

L(g_{0},\sigma_{0};\lambda)=(n+2\lambda-1)\partial_{n+1}-x_{n+1}\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}}:C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})\to C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}).

An easy calculation shows the conjugation formula

L(g_{0},r;\lambda)=r^{\lambda-1}\circ(-\Delta_{g_{+}}+\lambda(n+\lambda))\circ r^{-\lambda}.

It implies that the operator
L(g_{0},r;\lambda)
is an intertwining operator for
spherical principal series representations. Indeed, let
\gamma\in SO(1,n+1)
be an
isometry of the hyperbolic metric
g_{+}=r^{-2}g_{0}
acting on the upper-half space
r>0
. Then we calculate

\displaystyle L(g_{0},r;\lambda)\left(\left(\frac{\gamma_{*}(r)}{r}\right)^{-\lambda}\gamma_{*}(u)\right)

\displaystyle=r^{\lambda-1}(-\Delta_{g_{+}}+\lambda(n+\lambda))\left(r^{-\lambda}\left(\frac{\gamma_{*}(r)}{r}\right)^{-\lambda}\gamma_{*}(u)\right)

\displaystyle=r^{\lambda-1}(-\Delta_{g_{+}}+\lambda(n+\lambda))(\gamma_{*}(r^{-\lambda}u))

\displaystyle=r^{\lambda-1}\gamma_{*}(-\Delta_{g_{+}}+\lambda(n+\lambda))(r^{-\lambda}u))

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\gamma_{*}(r)}{r}\right)^{-\lambda+1}\gamma_{*}(r^{\lambda-1}(-\Delta_{g_{+}}+\lambda(n+\lambda))(r^{-\lambda}u)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\gamma_{*}(r)}{r}\right)^{-\lambda+1}\gamma_{*}L(g_{0},r;\lambda)(u).

In other words, it holds

(5.2)

L(g_{0},r;\lambda)\circ\pi^{0}_{-\lambda}(\gamma)=\pi^{0}_{-\lambda+1}(\gamma)\circ L(g_{0},r;\lambda)

with

\pi^{0}_{\lambda}(\gamma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\left(\frac{\gamma_{*}(r)}{r}\right)^{\lambda}\gamma_{*}.

Note that

\frac{\gamma_{*}(r)}{r}=e^{\Phi_{\gamma}},

where
\Phi_{\gamma}
is the conformal factor of the conformal transformation induced
by
\gamma
with respect to the Euclidean metric, i.e.,
\gamma_{*}(g_{0})=e^{2\Phi_{\gamma}}g_{0}
. The representation
\pi_{\lambda}^{0}(\gamma)
is actually
well-defined for all
\gamma\in SO(1,n+2)
acting on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
(viewed as the
boundary of hyperbolic space of dimension
n+2
). However, the intertwining property
(
5.2
) holds true only for the subgroup of
SO(1,n+1)
leaving the
boundary
r=0
