of the upper half-space invariant. The fact that
L(g_{0},r;\lambda)

is an intertwining operator for a subgroup of the conformal group of the Euclidean
metric on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
connects it with the theory of symmetry breaking operators. In
fact, it follows from the above that the compositions

D_{N}(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\iota^{*}L(\lambda-N+1)\circ\cdots\circ L(\lambda):C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})\to C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}),\;N\in{\mathbb{N}}

satisfy

D_{N}(\lambda)\circ\pi_{-\lambda}^{0}(\gamma)=\pi_{-\lambda+N}^{0\prime}(\gamma)\circ D_{N}(\lambda),\;\gamma\in SO(1,n+1)

and Clerc
[
C17
]
proved that
D_{N}(\lambda)
coincides with the symmetry breaking
operator introduced in
[
J09
, Chapter 5]
.
9
9

9

\pi_{\lambda}^{0\prime}

denotes the analogous representation on functions on the subspace
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Similarly, let
M=S^{n}
be an equatorial subsphere of
X=S^{n+1}
with the round
metric
g
. Let
M
be defined as the zero locus of the height function
\sigma=\mathcal{H}

being defined as the restriction of
x_{n+2}
to
S^{n+1}
. Then
{\sf J}=\frac{n+1}{2}
and

(n+1)\rho=-\Delta_{S^{n+1}}\mathcal{H}+{\sf J}\mathcal{H}=-(n+1)\mathcal{H}+\frac{n+1}{2}\mathcal{H}=-\frac{n+1}{2}\mathcal{H}

using the fact that
\mathcal{H}
is an eigenfunction of the Laplacian on the sphere

S^{n+1}
. Thus,
\rho=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}
and we obtain

(5.3)

L(g,\mathcal{H};\lambda)=(n+2\lambda-1)\nabla_{\operatorname{grad}(\mathcal{H})}-\mathcal{H}\Delta_{S^{n+1}}+\lambda(\lambda+1)\mathcal{H}:C^{\infty}(S^{n+1})\to C^{\infty}(S^{n+1}).

A calculation shows that

L(g,\mathcal{H};\lambda)=\mathcal{H}^{\lambda-1}\circ(-\Delta_{\mathcal{H}^{-2}g}+\lambda(n+\lambda))\circ\mathcal{H}^{-\lambda}.

Again, the operator
L(g,\mathcal{H};\lambda)
is an intertwining operator for spherical
principal series representations. Indeed, it holds

L(g,\mathcal{H};\lambda)\circ\pi_{-\lambda}(\gamma)=\pi_{-\lambda+1}(\gamma)\circ L(g,\mathcal{H};\lambda)

for all
\gamma\in SO(1,n+1)
acting on the upper hemisphere
\mathcal{H}>0
of

S^{n+1}
. Here

\pi_{\lambda}(\gamma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\left(\frac{\gamma_{*}(\mathcal{H})}{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{\lambda}\gamma_{*}.

The latter representations are well-defined for
\gamma\in SO(1,n+2)

acting on
S^{n+1}
. Note that

\frac{\gamma_{*}(\mathcal{H})}{\mathcal{H}}=e^{\Phi_{\gamma}},

where
\Phi_{\gamma}
is the conformal factor of the conformal transformation induced
by
\gamma
with respect to the round metric
g
, i.e.,
\gamma_{*}(g)=e^{2\Phi_{\gamma}}g
. We also note that the operator (
5.3
) is equivalent
to the intertwining operator displayed in
[
C17
, Proposition 7.9]
. We omit the
details of that calculation.

Finally, we observe that the above two models of the Laplace-Robin operator are conformally equivalent.
In fact, let
\kappa:S^{n+1}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
be the stereographic projection. Then

\kappa^{*}(\mathcal{H})=\Phi x_{n+1}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\kappa^{*}(g)=\Phi^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}dx_{i}^{2}=\Phi^{2}g_{0}

with
\Phi=2/(1+|x|^{2})

[
J09
, Section 2.2]
. Hence

\displaystyle\kappa^{*}L(g,\mathcal{H};\lambda)\kappa_{*}

\displaystyle=L(\kappa^{*}(g),\kappa^{*}(\mathcal{H});\lambda)

\displaystyle=L(\Phi^{2}g_{0},\Phi x_{n+1};\lambda)

\displaystyle=\Phi^{\lambda-1}L(g_{0},x_{n+1};\lambda)\Phi^{-\lambda}

using a very special case of the conformal invariance of the Laplace-Robin operator (Corollary
4.5
).
By combining this conjugation formula with the results in the later sections, it follows that the equivariant
families
D_{2N}^{c}(\lambda):C^{\infty}(S^{n+1})\to C^{\infty}(S^{n})
constructed in
[
J09
, Section 5.2]

can be regarded as residue families
{\bf D}_{2N}^{res}(g,\mathcal{H};\lambda)
(as defined in Section
7
).

6.
Adapted coordinates, renormalized volume coefficients and a formula for
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}

Let
X
be compact with closed boundary
M
and let
\sigma
be a defining function of

M
, i.e.,
\sigma^{-1}(0)=M
,
\sigma>0
on
X\setminus M
and
d\sigma|_{M}\neq 0
. Let

\iota:M\hookrightarrow X
be the embedding and
h=\iota^{*}(g)
.

We start with the definition of two different types of local coordinates of
X
near the boundary:

geodesic normal
coordinates and
adapted
coordinates.

Geodesic normal coordinates are defined by the normal geodesic flow of the hypersurface
M
,
i.e., we consider a diffeomorphism of
I\times M
(with a small interval
I=[0,\varepsilon)
)
onto a neighborhood of
M
in
X
, which is defined by

\kappa:I\times M\ni(r,x)\mapsto\Phi^{r}(x)\in X,

where
\Phi^{r}
is the geodesic flow with initial speed given by a unit normal field on
M
. Then
\kappa^{*}(g)

has the form
dr^{2}+h_{r}
for a one-parameter family
h_{r}
on
M
. Let

(6.1)

u(r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}dvol_{h_{r}}/dvol_{h},\quad u(r)=\sum_{j\geq 0}r^{j}u_{j},\quad u_{j}\in C^{\infty}(M).

Now, if
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)=1
, then the volume form of the singular metric
\sigma^{-2}g
has the form

\displaystyle dvol_{\kappa^{*}(\sigma^{-2}g)}

\displaystyle=\sigma(r)^{-n-1}u(r)drdvol_{h}

(6.2)

\displaystyle=r^{-n-1}w(r)drdvol_{h}.

for
\sigma(r)=\kappa^{*}(\sigma)
and some
w\in C^{\infty}(I\times M)
. Moreover, we have expansions

(6.3)

w(r)=1+\sum_{j\geq 1}r^{j}w_{j}

with
w_{j}\in C^{\infty}(M)
and

dvol_{\kappa^{*}(\sigma^{-2}g)}=\sum_{j\geq 0}r^{-n-1+j}w_{j}drdvol_{h}.

Following
[
G17
]
, the coefficients
w_{j}\in C^{\infty}(M)
for
j\leq n
are called
singular Yamabe

renormalized volume coefficients. Note that the definition of the coefficients
w_{j}\in C^{\infty}(M)
involves the Taylor
expansion of
\sigma(r)
in
r
. Special interest deserves the critical coefficient
w_{n}
since for closed
M
, the
total integral

\int_{M}w_{n}dvol_{h}

is conformally invariant
[
G17
, Proposition 2.1]
.

Similarly,
adapted coordinates
are associated to the data
(g,\sigma)
through a diffeomorphism

\eta:I\times M\ni(s,x)\mapsto\Phi_{\mathfrak{X}}^{s}(x)\in X

onto a open neighborhood of
M
in
X
(with some small interval
I=[0,\varepsilon)
), where

\Phi_{\mathfrak{X}}^{s}
denotes the flow of the vector field

(6.4)

\mathfrak{X}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{N}/|\mathcal{N}|^{2},\quad\mathcal{N}=\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)

with
\Phi^{0}_{\mathfrak{X}}=\operatorname{Id}
. We shall also use the notation

\mathfrak{X}_{\sigma}
in cases where the dependence on
\sigma
is important. Note that
|\mathfrak{X}|=1/|\mathcal{N}|
. Then

(d/ds)(\sigma\circ\eta)=\langle d\sigma,\mathfrak{X}\rangle=\langle d\sigma,\mathcal{N}\rangle/|\mathcal{N}|^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}1

and the differential of
\eta
maps the vector field
\partial_{s}
to the vector field

\mathfrak{X}
(see Section
2
). This implies the important relation

(6.5)

\eta^{*}(\sigma)=s

and the intertwining property

(6.6)

\eta^{*}\circ\mathfrak{X}=\partial_{s}\circ\eta^{*},

where
\partial_{s}
and
\mathfrak{X}
are viewed as first-order differential operators. Therefore,

\eta^{*}\circ\mathfrak{X}^{k}=\partial_{s}^{k}\circ\eta^{*},

and by composition with
\iota^{*}
, we obtain

(6.7)

\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k}\circ\eta^{*}=\iota^{*}\mathfrak{X}^{k}=\iota^{*}\left(|\mathcal{N}|^{-2}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}\right)^{k}.

Now if
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)=1
, i.e., if
|\mathcal{N}|^{2}=1-2\sigma\rho
, then it follows from
(
6.7
) that the Taylor coefficients in the variable
s
of any function

\eta^{*}(u)
with
u\in C^{\infty}(X)
can be written as linear combinations of
iterated gradients
\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{k}(u)
with coefficients that are polynomials
in the quantities
\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{k}(\rho)\in C^{\infty}(M)
. In particular, it
holds

(6.8)

\iota^{*}\partial_{s}\circ\eta^{*}=\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\iota^{*}\partial^{2}_{s}\circ\eta^{*}=\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}-2H\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}.

For more details, we refer to Section
13.1.4
. If
\sigma
satisfies only the weaker
condition
{(\mathcal{SY})}
, then
|\mathcal{N}|^{2}=1-2\sigma\rho+O(\sigma^{n+1})
and the same
conclusions are true for sufficiently small
k
.

Note that the metric
\eta^{*}(g)
has the form

(6.9)

\eta^{*}(|\mathcal{N}|^{-2})ds^{2}+h_{s}

with a one-parameter family
h_{s}
of metrics on
M
so that
h_{0}=h=\iota^{*}(g)
. We shall refer to
(
6.9
) as the
normal form
of
g
in adapted coordinates. We expand

h_{s}=\sum_{j\geq 0}h_{(j)}s^{j}
. It follows from (
6.7
) that

\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k}(\eta^{*}(|\mathcal{N}|^{-2}))=\iota^{*}(\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(|\mathcal{N}|^{-2}))^{k}.

Thus, if
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
, the Taylor coefficients of the coefficient

\eta^{*}(|\mathcal{N}|^{-2})
in the variable
s
are polynomials in the quantities

\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{k}(\rho)
.

In later calculations in adapted coordinates, we shall often use the same notation for quantities like

\rho
and
{\sf J}
and their pull-backs by
\eta
without further mentioning.

Now (
6.9
) implies

dvol_{\eta^{*}(g)}=\eta^{*}(|\mathcal{N}|)^{-1}dsdvol_{h_{s}}=v(s)dsdvol_{h}

for some
v(s)\in C^{\infty}(I\times M)
. Since the condition
{(\mathcal{SY})}

implies
|\mathcal{N}|=1
on
M
, we get
v(0,x)=1
, and we have an expansion

(6.10)

v(s)=1+\sum_{j\geq 1}s^{j}v_{j}\quad\mbox{with $v_{j}\in C^{\infty}(M)$}.

The coefficients
v_{j}
for
j\leq n
also will be called
singular Yamabe
renormalized
volume coefficients. They describe the volume of the singular metric
\sigma^{-2}g
through the expansion

dvol_{\eta^{*}(\sigma^{-2}g)}=s^{-n-1}v(s)dsdvol_{h}=\sum_{j\geq 0}s^{-n-1+j}v_{j}dsdvol_{h}.

Again, special interest deserves the
critical
coefficient
v_{n}
since

\int_{M}v_{n}dvol_{h}

is conformally invariant for closed
M
. This follows from the equality

(6.11)

\int_{M}v_{n}dvol_{h}=\int_{M}w_{n}dvol_{h}

which can be proved by the following argument of
[
G17
]
. The identity

|d\sigma|^{2}_{\tilde{g}}=1
for
\tilde{g}=|d\sigma|_{g}^{2}g
shows that
\sigma
can be viewed
as the distance function
d_{M}^{\tilde{g}}
of
M
in the metric
\tilde{g}
. Hence it holds

vol_{\sigma^{-2}g}(\{\sigma>\varepsilon\})=vol_{\sigma^{-2}g}(\{d_{M}^{\tilde{g}}>\varepsilon\})=vol_{\tilde{\sigma}^{-2}\tilde{g}}(\{d_{M}^{\tilde{g}}>\varepsilon\})

with
\tilde{\sigma}=|d\sigma|_{g}\sigma
. By comparing the coefficients of
\log\varepsilon
in the
expansions of both sides, we find

\int_{M}v_{n}(g)dvol_{\iota^{*}(g)}=\int_{M}w_{n}(\tilde{g})dvol_{\iota^{*}(\tilde{g})}.

Now the conformal invariance of the latter integral implies the equality (
6.11
). Following

[
G17
]
, the integral

(6.12)

{\mathcal{A}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\int_{M^{n}}v_{n}dvol_{h}

for a closed
M
is called the
singular Yamabe energy
of
M
. The quantity
{\mathcal{A}}
appears
in the asymptotic expansion of the volume of the singular metric
\sigma^{-2}g
(Theorem
10.11
).

Now we continue with the

Proof of Proposition
4.9
.

We use adapted coordinates. It suffices to prove
that the operator
\mathbf{L}(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}L(\eta^{*}(g),s;\lambda)
satisfies

\int_{X}\mathbf{L}(\lambda)(\varphi)\psi dvol_{\eta^{*}(g)}=\int_{X}\varphi\mathbf{L}(-\lambda-n)(\psi)dvol_{\eta^{*}(g)}

if
X=[0,\varepsilon)\times M
,
\psi\in C_{c}^{2}([0,\varepsilon)\times M)
and

\varphi\in C^{2}([0,\varepsilon)\times M)
so that
\varphi(0,x)=0
. Now, by definition

\mathbf{L}(\lambda)=(n\!+\!2\lambda\!-\!1)(\eta^{*}(|\mathcal{N}|^{2})\partial_{s}+\lambda\eta^{*}(\rho))-s(\Delta_{\eta^{*}(g)}+\lambda\eta^{*}({\sf J})).

In the following, we simplify the notation by writing
g
,
\rho
, and
{\sf J}
instead of the pull-backs of these
quantities by
\eta
. Then

(6.13)

\mathbf{L}(\lambda)=(n\!+\!2\lambda\!-\!1)(a\partial_{s}+\lambda\rho)-s(\Delta_{g}+\lambda{\sf J}),

where
a\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}|\mathcal{N}|^{2}
. In these terms, the background metric reads
g=a^{-1}ds^{2}+h_{s}

and we obtain
dvol_{g}=a^{-1/2}dsdvol_{h_{s}}
. Hence

v=a^{-1/2}(\det(h_{s})/\det(h))^{1/2}

and

(6.14)

\frac{v^{\prime}}{v}=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{s}),

where
′
denotes the derivative in the variable
s
. An easy calculation shows that

(6.15)

\Delta_{g}=a\partial_{s}^{2}+\frac{a}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{s})\partial_{s}+\frac{1}{2}a^{\prime}\partial_{s}-\frac{1}{2}(d\log a,d\cdot)_{h_{s}}+\Delta_{h_{s}}.

Now we observe that

\displaystyle\int_{X}a\varphi^{\prime}\psi dvol_{g}=\int_{X}a\varphi^{\prime}\psi vdsdvol_{h}

\displaystyle=-\int_{X}\varphi\left[a\psi^{\prime}+a\frac{v^{\prime}}{v}\psi+a^{\prime}\psi\right]vdsdvol_{h}=-\int_{X}\varphi\left[a\psi^{\prime}+a\frac{v^{\prime}}{v}\psi+a^{\prime}\psi\right]dvol_{g}

and

\int_{X}s\Delta_{g}(\varphi)\psi dvol_{g}=\int_{X}\varphi\Delta_{g}(s\psi)dvol_{g}

using Green’s formula and the assumptions. The expression (
6.15
) shows that

\Delta_{g}(s\psi)=s\Delta_{g}(\psi)+2a\psi^{\prime}+\frac{a}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{s})\psi+\frac{1}{2}a^{\prime}\psi.

Hence

\displaystyle\int_{X}\mathbf{L}(\lambda)(\varphi)\psi dvol_{g}

\displaystyle=-(n\!+\!2\lambda\!-\!1)\int_{X}\varphi\left(a\psi^{\prime}+a\frac{v^{\prime}}{v}\psi+a^{\prime}\psi\right)dvol_{g}

\displaystyle-\int_{X}\varphi\left(s\Delta_{g}(\psi)+2a\psi^{\prime}+\frac{a}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{s})\psi+\frac{1}{2}a^{\prime}\psi\right)dvol_{g}

\displaystyle+\int_{X}\varphi\psi(\lambda(n\!+\!2\lambda\!-\!1)\rho-\lambda s{\sf J})dvol_{g}.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\int_{X}\varphi\mathbf{L}(-\lambda\!-\!n)(\psi)dvol_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\varphi\left(-(n\!+\!2\lambda\!+\!1)a\psi^{\prime}-s\Delta_{g}(\psi)+(n\!+\!2\lambda\!+\!1)(\lambda\!+\!n)\rho\psi+(\lambda\!+\!n)s{\sf J}\psi\right)dvol_{g}.

It follows that the assertion is equivalent to the identity

\displaystyle-(n\!+\!2\lambda\!-\!1)a\frac{v^{\prime}}{v}-(n\!+\!2\lambda\!-\!1)a^{\prime}-\frac{a}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{s})-\frac{1}{2}a^{\prime}+\lambda(n\!+\!2\lambda\!-\!1)\rho-\lambda s{\sf J}

\displaystyle=(n\!+\!2\lambda\!+\!1)(\lambda\!+\!n)\rho+(\lambda\!+\!n)s{\sf J}.

By (
6.14
), this identity is equivalent to

-a\frac{v^{\prime}}{v}-a^{\prime}=(n+1)\rho+s{\sf J}.

The identities (
6.14
) and (
6.15
) also show that

(6.16)

\Delta_{g}(s)=\frac{a}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{s})+\frac{1}{2}a^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}a\frac{v^{\prime}}{v}+a^{\prime}.

Thus, we have reduced the assertion to the identity

-\Delta_{g}(s)=(n+1)\rho+s{\sf J}.

But this is just the definition of
\rho
. The proof is complete.

The above arguments also prove the relation (
4
). In fact, partial integration and
Green’s formula yield the additional terms

(n\!+\!2\lambda\!-\!1)\int_{M}\iota^{*}(a\varphi\psi v)dvol_{h}+\int_{M}\iota^{*}(\varphi\psi|\mathcal{N}|)dvol_{h}=(n+2\lambda)\int_{M}\iota^{*}(\varphi\psi|\mathcal{N}|)dvol_{h}

since the unit normal field on
M
is
|\mathcal{N}|\partial_{s}
and
v_{0}=|\mathcal{N}|^{-1}

∎

Note that equation (
6.16
) can be written in the form

(6.17)

v(s)\Delta_{g}(s)=\partial_{s}(v(s)a);

we recall that
a=\eta^{*}(|\mathcal{N}|^{2})
. As a corollary of this formula, we obtain a useful
formula for
v(s)
in terms of
\rho
and
{\sf J}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

If
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
, then it holds

(6.18)

\frac{v^{\prime}}{v}=\frac{-(n-1)\rho+2s\rho^{\prime}-s{\sf J}}{1-2s\rho}+O(s^{n}).

Here
\rho
and
{\sf J}
are identified with their pull-backs by
\eta
. If
\rho=0
, then
it holds
v^{\prime}/v=-s{\sf J}+O(s^{n})
. The latter case contains the Poincaré-Einstein case.

Proof.

We write (
6.17
) in the form

v(s)(-(n+1)\rho-s{\sf J})=\partial_{s}(v(s)a).

Now the assumption implies
a=\mathcal{S}-2s\rho=1-2s\rho+O(s^{n+1})
. Hence

\displaystyle v(s)(-(n+1)\rho-s{\sf J})

\displaystyle=\partial_{s}(v(s)(1-2s\rho+O(s^{n+1})))

\displaystyle=v^{\prime}(s)(1-2s\rho)-2v(s)\rho-2v(s)s\rho^{\prime}+vO(s^{n}).

Now simplification proves the claim. In the Poincaré-Einstein case, one easily shows that
\rho=0

and
v^{\prime}/v=-s{\sf J}
(see Example
6.5
).
∎

Lemma
6.1
can be used to derive formulas for the coefficients
v_{k}
with
k\leq n
in terms
of the Taylor coefficients of
\rho
and
{\sf J}
. In particular, we obtain

Corollary 6.2
.

For
{\mathbb{N}}\ni k\leq n
, we have

v_{k}=-(n\!-\!2k\!+\!1)\iota^{*}\frac{1}{k!}\partial_{s}^{k-1}(\rho)+LOT,

where LOT refers to terms with lower-order derivatives of
\rho
and
{\sf J}
.

Proof.

We consider the coefficient of
s^{k-1}
in the expansion of
v^{\prime}/v
.
On the one hand, it equals
kv_{k}
. On the other hand, (
6.18
) yields the expression

\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k-1}(\rho)\left(-\frac{n-1}{(k-1)!}+\frac{2}{(k-2)!}\right)

for this coefficient. This implies the assertion.
∎

In particular, the critical coefficient
v_{n}
involves the quantity
\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}(\rho)
.

Conversely, a version of Lemma
6.1
implies a recursive formula for the Taylor coefficients
of
\rho
. For the discussion of that formula, we introduce the notation

(6.19)

\mathring{v}(s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}dvol_{h_{s}}/dvol_{h}.

Then

\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h_{s}^{\prime}).

We also recall that
a=1-2s\rho+O(s^{n+1})
if
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. The recursive formula
for the Taylor coefficients of
\rho
will be a consequence of a first-order differential equation.

Lemma 6.3

(
Differential equation for
\rho
).

If
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
, then
\rho
solves the differential equation

(6.20)

-s\rho^{\prime}+n\rho+a\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}+s{\sf J}=O(s^{n})

with the initial condition
\rho(0)=-H
.

Proof.

The identity (
6.14
) implies

(6.21)

\frac{v^{\prime}}{v}=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}+\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}.

We use this decomposition on the left-hand side of (
6.18
) and multiply the resulting identity
with
a
. Then

(n-1)\rho-2s\rho^{\prime}+s{\sf J}-\frac{1}{2}a^{\prime}+a\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}=O(s^{n}).

By
a^{\prime}=-2\rho-2s\rho^{\prime}+O(s^{n})
, this identity simplifies to (
6.20
). By restriction
of (
6.20
) to
s=0
, we obtain
n\rho(0)+\operatorname{tr}(L)=0
using
h_{(1)}=2L

(see (
13.2.3
)). Hence
\rho(0)=-H
.
∎

Another proof of
\rho(0)=-H
will be given in Lemma
13.5.1
.

By repeated differentiation of the identity (
6.20
) in the variable
s
, it follows that the
Taylor coefficients of
\rho
can be determined recursively using the Taylor coefficients of
h_{s}

and
{\sf J}
. More precisely, we obtain

Proposition 6.4

(
Recursive formula for Taylor coefficients of
\rho
).

Assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then

(6.22)

(n-k)\partial_{s}^{k}(\rho)|_{0}=-\partial_{s}^{k}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}+2\sum_{j=1}^{k}j\binom{k}{j}\partial_{s}^{j-1}(\rho)|_{0}\partial_{s}^{k-j}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}-k\partial_{s}^{k-1}({\sf J})|_{0}

for
1\leq k\leq n-1
.

For a discussion of more details of such types of formulas in low-order cases, we refer to Section
13.5
.
In particular, we use (
6.22
) to derive explicit formulas for the first two derivatives of
\rho

in
s
at
s=0
.

Example 6.5
.

Let
g_{+}=s^{-2}(ds^{2}+h_{s})
be a Poincaré-Einstein metric. Assume that
g=ds^{2}+h_{s}
is smooth
up to the boundary. In particular, the obstruction tensor vanishes. In that case, adapted coordinates coincide
with geodesic normal coordinates. Now it holds
\rho=0
. We show that the vanishing of the Taylor coefficients
of
\rho
(in the variable
s
) up to order
n-1
recursively follows from (
6.22
). In fact, assume
that we know that
\partial_{s}^{j}(\rho)|_{0}=0
for
j=0,\dots,k-1
. Then the right-hand side of (
6.22
)
simplifies to

-\partial_{s}^{k}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}-k\partial_{s}^{k-1}({\sf J})|_{0}.

But

k\partial_{s}^{k-1}({\sf J})|_{0}=\partial_{s}^{k}(s{\sf J})|_{0}=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{s}^{k}(\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h_{s}^{\prime})|_{0}=-\partial_{s}^{k}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}

by

(6.23)

{\sf J}=-\frac{1}{2s}\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h_{s}^{\prime})

(which follows by combining the Einstein condition with the conformal transformation law for scalar curvature
- for the details see
[
J09
, (6.11.8)]
). Hence (
6.22
) implies
\partial_{s}^{k}(\rho)|_{0}=0
.
Alternatively, we could note that the relation (
6.23
) transforms the differential equation
(
6.20
) into

-s\rho^{\prime}+n\rho-2s\rho\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}=O(s^{n})

with the initial condition
\rho(0)=0
. Then
\rho=0
is the unique solution of this initial value problem.

For
k=n
, the coefficient on the left-hand side of (
6.22
) vanishes. This suggests the following
formula for the singular Yamabe obstruction.

Theorem 6.6

(
The obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
).

Assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then

(6.24)

(n\!+\!1)!{\mathcal{B}}_{n}=-2\partial_{s}^{n}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}+4\sum_{j=1}^{n}j\binom{n}{j}\partial_{s}^{j-1}(\rho)|_{0}\partial_{s}^{n-j}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}-2n\partial_{s}^{n-1}({\sf J})|_{0}.

Proof.

We start with general data
(g,\sigma)
. As before, we identify
|\mathcal{N}|^{2}
and
\mathcal{S}
with their respective
pull-backs by
\eta
. Then
\mathcal{S}=|\mathcal{N}|^{2}+2s\rho=a+2s\rho
. In these terms, the identity
(
6.17
) reads

v(-(n+1)\rho-s{\sf J})=v^{\prime}a+va^{\prime}.

Hence

\displaystyle a\frac{v^{\prime}}{v}

\displaystyle=-(n+1)\rho-s{\sf J}-\partial_{s}(a)

\displaystyle=-(n+1)\rho-s{\sf J}+2\partial_{s}(s\rho)-\partial_{s}(\mathcal{S}-1)

\displaystyle=-(n-1)\rho-s{\sf J}+2s\rho^{\prime}-\partial_{s}(\mathcal{S}-1).

We decompose the left-hand side using (
6.21
) and reorder. This gives

a\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}-\frac{1}{2}a^{\prime}+(n-1)\rho+s{\sf J}-2s\rho^{\prime}+\partial_{s}(\mathcal{S}-1)=0.

Now, using
a=(1-2s\rho)+(\mathcal{S}-1)
, we obtain the relation

(6.25)

-s\rho^{\prime}+n\rho+a\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}+s{\sf J}=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{s}(\mathcal{S}-1)

which improves (
6.20
). Now, assuming that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
, differentiate
(
6.25
)
n
times in
s
. By
\partial_{s}^{j}(a)|_{0}=-2j\partial_{s}^{j-1}(\rho)|_{0}

for
1\leq j\leq n
and

\partial_{s}^{n+1}(\mathcal{S}-1)|_{0}=(n+1)!{\mathcal{B}}_{n},

this proves the assertion.
∎

The basic relation (
6.25
) will be confirmed in a number of special cases with

\mathcal{S}=1
in Examples
13.2.3
–
13.2.5
.

Proposition
6.4
and Theorem
6.6
should be compared with

[
GW21
, Proposition 6.4]
. The latter result establishes formulas for the restrictions of normal derivatives

\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{k}(\rho)
of
\rho
to
M
and for the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
in terms of lower-order normal derivatives
of
\rho
and additional terms. The above results clarify the structure of all such additional terms.
Here it is crucial to work in adapted coordinates.

Note that the formula (
6.24
) shows that the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}

involves the Taylor coefficients
h_{(k)}
of
h_{s}
(in the normal form
(
6.9
) of
g
in adapted coordinates) for
k\leq n+1
.

In Section
13.6
, we shall derive the classical formula for
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
(see
(
3
) and
[
ACF92
]
) from (
6.24
). Similarly, in Section

13.7
we evaluate the formula (
6.24
) for the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}

in case of a (conformally) flat background.

Finally, we apply the above results to determine the leading term of the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}

for an embedding
M^{n}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
if
n
is even.

First, we note that Theorem
6.6
and Proposition
6.4
show
that
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
is a functional of the second fundamental form
L
.

Theorem 6.7
.

For a flat background metric and even
n
, it holds

(6.26)

(n+1)!{\mathcal{B}}_{n}=c_{n}\Delta^{\frac{n}{2}}(H)+nl

with

c_{n}=-2\frac{(n-1)!!}{(n-2)!!}

and a non-linear functional
nl
of
L
. For odd
n
, the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
is non-linear in
L
.

The non-linear part in (
6.26
) can also be described as a term of lower
differential order. In fact, we can write
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
as a sum of terms that are
homogeneous in
L
. In each such term, the sum of the number of derivatives and the
homogeneous degree in
L
is
n+1
. But the non-linear terms in (
6.26
)
consist of homogeneous terms of degree at least
2
. One should compare that version
of the structural result for
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
with
[
GW21
, Theorem 5.1]
.

Theorem
6.7
extends the following observations. By the second formula in
(
3
),
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
is the sum of a constant multiple of
\Delta(H)
being linear in

L
and a term that is cubic in
L
and does not contain derivatives. Similarly,
the first three terms in (
13.7.2
) are homogeneous of degree
2
in
L
and
each such term involves
2
derivatives.

Proof.

Let
n
be even. We extract from the formula

(n\!+\!1)!{\mathcal{B}}_{n}=-2\partial_{s}^{n}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}+4\sum_{j=1}^{n}j\binom{n}{j}\partial_{s}^{j-1}(\rho)|_{0}\partial_{s}^{n-j}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}

the contributions which are
linear
in
L
. In the following, the symbol
nl

indicates non-linear terms. First, we ignore in this sum all products with at least
two factors. Hence

(n+1)!{\mathcal{B}}_{n}=-2\partial_{s}^{n}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}+nl.

Moreover, the expansion

\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h_{s}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\geq 1}(k\operatorname{tr}(h_{(k)})+nl)s^{k-1}

implies

\partial_{s}^{n}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}=\frac{1}{2}(n+1)!\operatorname{tr}(h_{(n+1)})+nl.

In order to evaluate
h_{(n+1)}
, we
(n-1)
-times differentiate in
s
the determining relation
(
13.7
) for
h_{s}
. Then

\frac{1}{2}\partial_{s}^{n+1}(h_{s})|_{0}=-\operatorname{Hess}(\partial_{s}^{n-1}(s\rho))|_{0})+nl

using
g^{00}=a=1-2s\rho
. Hence

\frac{1}{2}(n+1)!h_{(n+1)}=-(n-1)\operatorname{Hess}(\partial_{s}^{n-2}(\rho)|_{0})+nl.

These results imply

\displaystyle(n+1)!{\mathcal{B}}_{n}

\displaystyle=-(n+1)!\operatorname{tr}(h_{(n+1)})+nl

(6.27)

\displaystyle=2(n-1)\Delta(\partial_{s}^{n-2}(\rho)|_{0})+nl.

Now Proposition
6.4
shows that

\displaystyle(n-k)\partial_{s}^{k}(\rho)|_{0}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{s}^{k}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}+nl

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(k+1)!\operatorname{tr}(h_{(k+1)})+nl.

But
(k-1)
-times differentiating in
s
the determining relation for
h_{s}
, shows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{s}^{k+1}(h_{s})|_{0}

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{Hess}(\partial_{s}^{k-1}(s\rho)|_{0})+nl

\displaystyle=-(k-1)\operatorname{Hess}(\partial_{s}^{k-2}(\rho)|_{0})+nl.

Hence

(6.28)

(n-k)\partial_{s}^{k}(\rho)|_{0}=(k-1)\Delta(\partial_{s}^{k-2}(\rho)|_{0})+nl.

Combining (
6
) and (
6.28
) gives

\displaystyle(n+1)!{\mathcal{B}}_{n}

\displaystyle=2(n-1)\Delta(\partial_{s}^{n-2}(\rho)|_{0})+nl

\displaystyle=(n-1)(n-3)\Delta^{2}(\partial_{s}^{n-4}(\rho)|_{0})+nl

\displaystyle=\cdots=2(n-1)!!/(n-2)!!\Delta^{\frac{n}{2}}(\rho|_{0})+nl.

This implies the assertion using
\rho|_{0}=-H
(Lemma
13.5.1
). For odd
n
, the same
arguments show that
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
is a constant multiple of
\Delta^{\frac{n-1}{2}}(\partial_{s}(\rho)|_{0})+nl
.
Since
\partial_{s}(\rho)|_{0}
is a constant multiple of
|\mathring{L}|^{2}
(Lemma
13.5.1
), this completes the proof.
∎

Example 6.8
.

For
n=2
and
n=4
, we find

3!{\mathcal{B}}_{2}=-2\Delta(H)+nl\quad\mbox{and}\quad 5!{\mathcal{B}}_{4}=-3\Delta^{2}(H)+nl,

respectively. The first decomposition fits with (
3
).

Remark 6.9
.

For a flat background and odd
n
, the proof of Theorem
6.7
shows that one contribution
to
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
is a constant multiple of
\Delta^{\frac{n-1}{2}}(|\mathring{L}|^{2})
. But
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
has further contributions
of the same differential order, which are quadratic in
L
. For instance, (
13.7.2
) shows that in
addition to
\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})
,
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
contains the contributions
|dH|^{2}
and
(\mathring{L},\operatorname{Hess}(H))
of differential
order
2
.

We finish this section with a representation theoretical argument proving the vanishing of the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}

for the equatorial subsphere
S^{n}\hookrightarrow S^{n+1}
. In the following, we use the notation of
Section
5
.

First, assume that
M^{n}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
(with the flat metric
g_{0}
). Let
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{M}(g_{0})
be the singular
Yamabe obstruction of
M
. Let
\gamma\in SO(1,n+2)
be a conformal diffeomorphism of
g_{0}
, i.e.,

\gamma_{*}(g_{0})=e^{2\Phi_{\gamma}}g_{0}
. Then

e^{(n+1)\iota^{*}\Phi_{\gamma}}{\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{M}(\gamma_{*}(g_{0}))={\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{M}(g_{0})

by Lemma
3.1
. This relation is equivalent to

(6.29)

e^{(n+1)\iota^{*}\Phi_{\gamma}}\gamma_{*}({\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{\gamma(M)}(g_{0}))={\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{M}(g_{0}),\;\gamma\in SO(1,n+2).

In particular, all
\gamma\in SO(1,n+1)\hookrightarrow SO(1,n+2)
leave invariant
the hypersurface
M^{n}={\mathbb{R}}^{n}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
, and it holds

\iota^{*}\left(\frac{\gamma_{*}(r)}{r}\right)^{n+1}\gamma_{*}({\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{M}(g_{0}))={\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{M}(g_{0}),

i.e.,

\pi_{n+1}^{0}(\gamma)({\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{M}(g_{0}))={\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{M}(g_{0}).

But since the identical representation is not a subrepresentation of
\pi_{n+1}^{0}
, it follows that
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}^{M}(g_{0})=0
.
A similar argument proves the vanishing of
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
for the equatorial subsphere
S^{n}\hookrightarrow S^{n+1}
.
By the analog of (
6.29
) for hypersurfaces of
S^{n+1}
, the obstruction of any
\gamma(S^{n})
vanishes, too.

7.
Residue families

In the present section, we associate residue families to any pair
(g,\sigma)
which satisfies the
condition
{(\mathcal{SY})}
(see Section
3
). Residue families are defined in terms of the residues
of one-parameter families

\lambda\mapsto\langle M_{u}(\lambda),\psi\rangle=\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{g},\;\Re(\lambda)\gg 0

of distributions on
X
, where
u
are eigenfunctions of the Laplacian

\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}
of the singular metric
\sigma^{-2}g
. These residues
express the obstruction extending
u
as a distribution up to the boundary of
X
.

The restriction of
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)
to the boundary
M
equals
|\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)|_{g}^{2}=|d\sigma|^{2}_{g}
. Therefore, the condition
{(\mathcal{SY})}
implies that

|d\sigma|_{g}^{2}=1
on
M
. That property is equivalent to the property that the
sectional curvatures of
\sigma^{-2}g
tend to
-1
at the boundary
M
, i.e., the
metric
\sigma^{-2}g
is asymptotically hyperbolic. Next, we recall
some basic results in the spectral theory of the Laplacian of asymptotically hyperbolic
metrics. For more details, we refer to
[
GZ03
, Section 3]
. The spectrum of

-\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}
is the union of a finite pure
point spectrum
\sigma_{pp}\subset(0,(n/2)^{2})
and an absolutely continuous
spectrum
\sigma_{ac}=[(n/2)^{2},\infty)
of infinite multiplicity. The generalized
eigenfunctions with smooth functions on
M
as boundary values are described
by a
Poisson operator
. This operator is a far-reaching
generalization of the well-known Poisson transform of Helgason
[
He84
]
which
relates generalized eigenfunctions of the commutative algebra of invariant
differential operators on a symmetric space of the non-compact type to
hyperfunctions on a naturally associated boundary. It is defined by an integral
transform. In the present situation, the family
\mathcal{P}(\lambda)
of Poisson operators
is meromorphic for
\Re(\lambda)<n/2
,
\lambda\neq n/2
with poles in
\lambda
iff

\lambda(n-\lambda)\in\sigma_{pp}
such that

(\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}+\lambda(n-\lambda))\mathcal{P}(\lambda)(f)=0

for any
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
. In contrast to Helgason’s definition of a Poisson operator by
an integral transform, it is defined in terms of the resolvent of the Laplacian. In both theories,
the argument
f
is seen in the leading terms of the asymptotic expansion of the
eigenfunction
\mathcal{P}(\lambda)(f)
. To describe the asymptotic expansion of
eigenfunctions in the range of the Poisson operator, we choose coordinates on
X

near the boundary. Indeed, there is a unique defining
function
\theta
and a diffeomorphism
\tau
mapping
[0,\varepsilon]\times M

with coordinates
(t,x)
to a neighborhood of
M
in
X
so that

\tau^{*}(\sigma^{-2}g)=t^{-2}(dt^{2}+h_{t})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\;\iota^{*}(\theta^{2}\sigma^{-2}g)=h_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}h,\;\tau^{*}(\theta)=t.

This is the normal form of an asymptotically hyperbolic metric with prescribed conformal infinity
as used in
[
GZ03
]
. The function
\theta
is a solution of the eikonal equation
|d\theta|_{\theta^{2}\sigma^{-2}g}=1
near
M
and the gradient flow of
\theta
with respect to the
metric
\theta^{2}\sigma^{-2}g
defines the diffeomorphism
\tau
. Note that

\iota^{*}(\theta^{2}\sigma^{-2})=1
. In these terms, the eigenfunctions

\mathcal{P}(\lambda)(f)
have the following properties.

(i)

\tau^{*}\mathcal{P}(\lambda)(f)=t^{\lambda}f+t^{n-\lambda}g+O(t^{n/2+1})

for some
g\in C^{\infty}(M)
if
\Re(\lambda)=n/2
,
\lambda\neq n/2
.
10
10

10

Of course, the
function
g
should not be confused with the metric
g
.

(ii)

\tau^{*}\mathcal{P}(\lambda)(f)=t^{\lambda}F+t^{n-\lambda}G
with smooth
F
and

G
on
[0,\varepsilon)\times M
so that
\iota^{*}(F)=f
if
\Re(\lambda)\leq n/2
with

\lambda\not\in\{n/2-N/2\,|\,N\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}\}
and
\lambda(n-\lambda)\notin\sigma_{pp}
.

The function
f
is called the
boundary value
of
u=\mathcal{P}(\lambda)(f)
. The
function
F
in (ii) depends on
\lambda
and has poles in
\lambda\in\{n/2-N/2\,|\,N\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
.
But these poles cancel against poles of the second term
G=G(\lambda)
in the decomposition of
\mathcal{P}(\lambda)
.
If
\lambda
is as in (ii), we define

(7.1)

\mathscr{S}(\lambda)(f)=\iota^{*}(G).

The operator
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
is called the
scattering operator
of the asymptotically
hyperbolic metric
\sigma^{-2}g
.
11
11

11

The substitution

\lambda\mapsto n-\lambda
maps the operator
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
to the scattering operator in
[
GZ03
]
.

It is a family of pseudo-differential operators with principal
symbol being a constant multiple of
|\xi|^{n-2\lambda}
. It is meromorphic in
\Re(\lambda)<n/2

with poles in the set
\{\frac{n-N}{2}\,|\,N\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
and if
\lambda(n-\lambda)\in\sigma_{pp}
.
The poles in
\lambda=\frac{n-N}{2}
are sometimes referred to as its
trivial
poles. Its nontrivial poles
in
\Re(\lambda)>n/2
will not be of interest here. Now let
u=\mathcal{P}(\mu)(f)
be a solution of

-\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}u=\mu(n-\mu)u,\quad\Re(\mu)=n/2,\;\mu\neq n/2

with
boundary value

f\in C^{\infty}(M)
. Instead of the asymptotic expansions
of
u
as above, we will consider asymptotic expansion in terms of powers of

\sigma
. The following formal arguments describe the expansion of
u
using the
Laplace-Robin operators
L(g,\sigma;\mu)
.
12
12

12

The arguments are only formal
since the products
\sigma^{j}f_{j}
are not functions on
X
without a specification of
coordinates.
In the Poincaré-Einstein case, the following algorithm is contained
in the proof of
[
GZ03
, Proposition 4.2]
. We start with
f_{0}=f
and define
f_{N}\in C^{\infty}(M)
recursively by

N(n-2\mu-N)f_{N}=\iota^{*}\left(\sigma^{-N+1}L(-\mu)\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\sigma^{j}f_{j}\right)\right).

But the definition of
L(\lambda)
implies

L(\lambda)(\sigma^{j}f_{j})=j(n+2\lambda-j)\sigma^{j-1}f_{j}+O(\sigma^{j}),\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}.

Hence

L(-\mu)\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N}\sigma^{j}f_{j}\right)=O(\sigma^{N})

and the conjugation formula yields

-(\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}+\mu(n-\mu))\left(\sigma^{\mu}\sum_{j=0}^{N}\sigma^{j}f_{j}\right)=\sigma^{\mu+1}L(-\mu)\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N}\sigma^{j}f_{j}\right)=\sigma^{\mu}O(\sigma^{N+1}).

The coefficients
f_{j}
are given by differential operators
f\mapsto{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\mu)(f)
. The construction shows that
the operator
{\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\mu)
has simple poles in the set

\left\{\frac{n-j}{2}\;|\,j\leq N\right\}.

These are the poles that appeared above in (ii). Note that, using
\iota^{*}(\rho)=-H
, it easily follows
that
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\mu)=-\mu H
(see also Lemma
13.11.1
). We also observe that
L(\mu)(1)\in C^{\infty}(X)

is a multiple of
\mu
. By an easy induction, this implies that

(7.2)

{\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\mu)(1)=\frac{\mu}{N!(n\!-\!2\mu\!-\!1)\cdots(n\!-\!2\mu\!-\!N)}{\bf Q}_{N}(\mu)

for some polynomial
{\bf Q}_{N}(\mu)\in C^{\infty}(M)
. In particular, the function
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\mu)(1)

is regular at
\mu=0
.

Now, in order to justify the above arguments, we use adapted coordinates. Let
u
be as above. Then

\tau^{*}(u)
has the form
t^{\mu}F+t^{n-\mu}G
with
\iota^{*}(F)=f
and
\iota^{*}(G)=\mathcal{S}(\mu)(f)
.
Let
\zeta\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\tau^{-1}\circ\eta
. Then

\zeta^{*}(t)=\eta^{*}\tau_{*}(t)=\eta^{*}\left(\sigma\frac{\tau_{*}(t)}{\sigma}\right)=s\eta^{*}\left(\frac{\tau_{*}(t)}{\sigma}\right)

by
\eta^{*}(\sigma)=s
. It follows that the pull-back by
\zeta
of
\tau^{*}(u)
equals

\eta^{*}(u)=s^{\mu}\Omega^{\mu}\zeta^{*}(F)+s^{n-\mu}\Omega^{n-\mu}\zeta^{*}(G)

with
\Omega\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\tau_{*}(t)/\sigma
. But
\iota^{*}\zeta^{*}(F)=\iota^{*}(F)
,
\iota^{*}\zeta^{*}(G)=\iota^{*}(G)

and

(7.3)

\iota^{*}(\Omega)=1

imply that the leading terms in the expansion of
\eta^{*}(u)
are
\iota^{*}(F)=f
and
\iota^{*}(G)=\mathscr{S}(\mu)(f)
.
In order to prove the restriction property (
7.3
), we recall that

\tau_{*}(t^{-2}(dt^{2}+h_{t}))=\sigma^{-2}g.

Hence

\Omega^{2}g=dt^{2}+h_{t}.

In particular,
\iota^{*}(\Omega^{2})h=h_{0}
. But in the construction of
(\tau,\theta)
we required that
h_{0}=h
.
This proves (
7.3
). Thus, the asymptotic expansion of the eigenfunction
\eta^{*}(u)
of

\Delta_{\eta^{*}(\sigma^{-2}g)}
takes the form

(7.4)

\sum_{j\geq 0}s^{\mu+j}{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\mu)(f)(x)+\sum_{j\geq 0}s^{n-\mu+j}{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(n\!-\!\mu)\mathscr{S}(\mu)(f)(x),\quad s\to 0,

where
{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\mu)
are families of differential operators on
M
; we shall refer to these operators as

solution operators
. One easily find that the order of
{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\mu)
is
\leq 2[\frac{j}{2}]
. The above
formal arguments show that the families
{\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\mu)
are rational in
\mu
with simple poles in the set

\left\{\frac{n-j}{2}\;|\,j\leq N\right\}.

Of course, the coefficients
{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\mu)
can be determined recursively in terms of the Laplace-Robin operator
in adapted coordinates. In the following, it will often suffice to work with a finite version of the expansion
(
7.4
).

Remark 7.1
.

The solution operators
{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\lambda):C^{\infty}(M)\to C^{\infty}(M)
describe
formal asymptotic expansions of eigenfunctions (with smooth boundary value) of the Laplacian of asymptotically
hyperbolic metrics
\sigma^{-2}g
. Another type of asymptotic expansions of eigenfunctions appears in

[
GW14a
, Section 5]
. In an even more general setting, these are expansions, say in powers of a defining function,
the coefficients of which are functions on the space
X
but not on its boundary
M
. Comparing both types of
expansions would require additional expansions of the coefficients.

Later, we shall use the fact that the scattering operator
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}

is formally selfadjoint with respect to the scalar product on
C^{\infty}(M)
defined by
h
.
For the convenience of the reader, we include a proof that directly derives this property from
the expansion (
7.4
) (without invoking the definition of
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
in terms of
expansions in power series of
t
) (compare with
[
GZ03
, Proposition 3.3]
).

Lemma 7.2
.

\mathscr{S}(\lambda)^{*}=\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\lambda<n/2

such that
\lambda\notin\left\{\frac{n-N}{2}\,|\,N\in{\mathbb{N}}\right\}
and
\lambda(n-\lambda)\notin\sigma_{pp}
.

Note that the assumptions guarantee that the ladders
\lambda+{\mathbb{N}}
and
n-\lambda+{\mathbb{N}}
are disjoint.

Proof.

We recall Green’s formula

(7.5)

\int_{X}(du,dv)_{g}dvol_{g}+\int_{X}u\Delta_{g}(v)dvol_{g}=\int_{\partial X}u\star_{g}dv=\int_{\partial X}ui_{N(v)N}(dvol_{g})

on a compact Riemannian manifold
(X,g)
with boundary
\partial X
. Here
N
denotes a unit
normal field. Now let
{\mathbb{R}}\ni\lambda<n/2
be as above. Let

u_{1}
and
u_{2}
be real solutions of

-\Delta_{\eta^{*}(\sigma^{-2}g)}u=\lambda(n-\lambda)u

on
(0,\varepsilon)\times M
of the form
u=s^{\lambda}F+s^{n-\lambda}G
with smooth

F,G
. These are defined by the Poisson transforms of smooth boundary functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
. Let

k\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\eta^{*}(\sigma^{-2}g)=s^{-2}(a^{-1}ds^{2}+h_{s}).

Then
dvol_{k}=s^{-n-1}a^{-1/2}dsdvol_{h_{s}}
and the restriction of

\nu=s\sqrt{a}\partial_{s}
to the boundary
s=\varepsilon

defines a unit normal field. By (
7.5
), we find

\displaystyle\int_{s>\varepsilon}((du_{1},du_{2})_{k}-\lambda(n-\lambda)u_{1}u_{2})dvol_{k}

(7.6)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{-n}\int_{s=\varepsilon}u_{1}\partial_{\nu}(u_{2})dvol_{h_{\varepsilon}}=\varepsilon^{-n+1}\int_{s=\varepsilon}u_{1}\partial_{s}(u_{2})\sqrt{a}dvol_{h_{\varepsilon}}.

The finite part of the expansion of the last integral in
\varepsilon
is the coefficient of

\varepsilon^{n-1}
in the expansion of

\int_{s=\varepsilon}u_{1}\partial_{s}(u_{2})\sqrt{a}dvol_{h_{\varepsilon}}.

By plugging in the expansions of
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
,
\sqrt{a}
and

dvol_{h_{\varepsilon}}
, we obtain the expression

\int_{M}\iota^{*}(\lambda F_{2}G_{1}+(n-\lambda)F_{1}G_{2})dvol_{h}

for this coefficient. Since the left-hand side of (
7
) is symmetric in

u_{1}
and
u_{2}
, the latter result equals

\int_{M}\iota^{*}(\lambda F_{1}G_{2}+(n-\lambda)F_{2}G_{1})dvol_{h}.

It follows that

\int_{M}\iota^{*}(F_{1}G_{2})dvol_{h}=\int_{M}\iota^{*}(F_{2}G_{1})dvol_{h}.

In terms of the expansion (
7.4
), this means that

\int_{M}f_{1}\mathcal{S}(\lambda)(f_{2})dvol_{h}=\int_{M}f_{2}\mathcal{S}(\lambda)(f_{1})dvol_{h},

i.e.,
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)^{*}=\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
.
∎

Now we are ready to define residue families.

Let
{\mathbb{N}}\ni N\leq n
and assume that the condition
{(\mathcal{SY})}
is satisfied. We consider an eigenfunction
u

of
\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}}g
with boundary value
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
satisfying

(7.7)

\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}u+\mu(n-\mu)u=0\quad\mbox{for $\Re(\mu)=n/2$, $\mu\neq n/2$}.

Such eigenfunctions have asymptotic expansions (as above) near the boundary. We consider the integral

(7.8)

\left\langle M_{u}(\lambda),\psi\right\rangle=\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{g}

with
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
and
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
. The function

\lambda\mapsto\left\langle M_{u}(\lambda),\psi\right\rangle

is holomorphic if
\Re(\lambda)\gg 0
and we regard
M_{u}(\lambda)
as a holomorphic family of distributions on
X
.
If
\operatorname{supp}(\psi)\cap M=\emptyset
, then
M_{u}(\lambda)
admits a holomorphic continuation to
{\mathbb{C}}
.
M_{u}(\lambda)

generalizes the meromorphic family of distributions
M(\lambda)=M_{1}(\lambda)
discussed in Section
1
. Likewise
as
M(\lambda)
, the family
M_{u}(\lambda)
admits a meromorphic continuation with simple poles in
\{-\mu-N-1\}
.
The proof of this fact is similar as for
u=1
and follows by expanding the integrand near the boundary. In addition to these
poles,
M_{u}(\lambda)
has simple poles in the set
\{\mu+n-N-1\}
. However, this second ladder of poles will be
ignored in the following. The details are given in the proof of Theorem
7.4
. This proof also shows that the
residues have the form

(7.9)

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-\mu-1-N}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{g}\right)=\int_{M}f\delta_{N}(g,\sigma;\mu)(\eta^{*}(\psi))dvol_{h}

with some meromorphic families
\delta_{N}(g,\sigma;\mu)
of differential operators
C^{\infty}([0,\varepsilon)\times M)\to C^{\infty}(M)
of order
\leq N
. The residues of
M_{u}(\lambda)
are distributions on
X
with support on the
boundary
M
of
X
. They may be regarded as obstructions to extending
M_{u}(\lambda)
as a distribution to
X
.

Definition 7.3

(
Residue families).

Let
{\mathbb{N}}\ni N\leq n
and assume that the condition
{(\mathcal{SY})}
is satisfied.
Then the one-parameter family

(7.10)

{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda)=N!(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!2N\!+\!1)_{N}\delta_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!N),\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}

is called the residue family of order
N
. The family
{\bf D}_{n}^{res}(\lambda)
will be called the critical residue family.

Some comments are in order.
The families
\delta_{N}(\mu)
are defined for
\Re(\mu)=\frac{n}{2}
(with
\mu\neq\frac{n}{2}
).
Hence
{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(\lambda)
is defined for
\Re(\lambda)=-\frac{n}{2}+N
(with
\lambda\neq-\frac{n}{2}+N
).
But the normalizing coefficient in (
7.10
) is chosen so that
{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda)
actually extends to
a
polynomial

family of order
N
and degree
2N
in
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
. This fact will follow from Theorem
8.1
. The
proof of the latter result actually contains a second proof of the existence of the meromorphic continuation of
\langle M_{u}(\lambda),\psi\rangle
. The method is a version of a Bernstein-Sato-type argument. It provides explicit knowledge
of the position of poles of
M_{u}(\lambda)
and formulas for the residues. However, the following result gives a
more direct description of the operator
\delta_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda)
in terms of solution
operators. The equivalence of both descriptions of residues will have interesting consequences.

Theorem 7.4
.

Let
{\mathbb{N}}\ni N\leq n
. Then

(7.11)

\displaystyle{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda)

\displaystyle=N!(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!2N\!+\!1)_{N}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{j=0}^{N}\frac{1}{j!}\left[{\mathcal{T}}_{N-j}^{*}(g,\sigma;\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!N)v_{0}+\cdots+{\mathcal{T}}_{0}^{*}(g,\sigma;\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!N)v_{N-j}\right]\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{j}.

Proof.

The relation (
6.5
) implies

(7.12)

\langle M_{u}(\lambda),\psi\rangle=\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{g}=\int_{(0,\varepsilon)\times M}s^{\lambda}\eta^{*}(u)\eta^{*}(\psi)dvol_{\eta^{*}(g)},\;\Re(\lambda)\gg 0.

Here
\eta^{*}(u)
is an eigenfunction of the Laplacian of the metric
s^{-2}\eta^{*}(g)
. In order to simplify the notation, we write the latter integral as

\int_{[0,\varepsilon)\times M}s^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{\eta^{*}(g)}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{M}s^{\lambda}u\psi vdsdvol_{h}

with an appropriate eigenfunction
u
and test functions
\psi
on the space

[0,\varepsilon)\times M
. Now we expand
v
according to (
6.10
) and
u

according to (
7.4
). The classical formula
[
GS64
]

(7.13)

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-N-1}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{\lambda}\psi(s)ds\right)=\frac{\psi^{(N)}(0)}{N!},\;N\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}

for test functions
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\overline{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}})
shows that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-\mu-1-N}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{g}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-\mu-1-N}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\sum_{a=0}^{N}\sum_{j+k=a}\int_{M}s^{\lambda+\mu+a}{\mathcal{T}}_{k}(\mu)(f)v_{j}\psi dvol_{h}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{a=0}^{N}\frac{1}{(N-a)!}\sum_{j+k=a}\int_{M}{\mathcal{T}}_{k}(\mu)(f)v_{j}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{N-a}(\psi)dvol_{h}.

Since
f
is arbitrary, taking adjoints proves the assertion.
∎

Remark 7.5
.

Definition
7.3
generalizes the notion of
residue families
D_{N}^{res}(h;\lambda)
introduced in
[
J09
]
. In that case,

g=r^{2}g_{+}
is the conformal compactification of a Poincaré-Einstein metric

g_{+}
. However, the definitions in
[
J09
,
J13
,
JO20
]
use a different normalizing
coefficient. That choice is motivated by the fact that in these references, the expansion of

g
involves only even powers of
r
. More precisely, if
g_{+}
is in normal form
relative to
h
, i.e.,
\iota^{*}(r^{2}g_{+})=h
, then it holds

\displaystyle{\bf D}^{res}_{2N}(g,r;\lambda)

\displaystyle=(-2N)_{N}\left(\lambda\!+\!\frac{n}{2}\!-\!2N\!+\!\frac{1}{2}\right)_{N}D^{res}_{2N}(h;\lambda),

\displaystyle{\bf D}^{res}_{2N+1}(g,r;\lambda)

\displaystyle=-2(-2N\!-\!1)_{N+1}\left(\lambda\!+\!\frac{n}{2}\!-\!2N\!-\!\frac{1}{2}\right)_{N+1}D^{res}_{2N+1}(h;\lambda).

If
\lambda
is a zero of the prefactors in these factorization identities, then the family
{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g,r;\lambda)

vanishes and therefore hides the non-trivial operator
D_{N}^{res}(h;\lambda)
. In particular, if
2N+1=n
, then

{\bf D}_{n}^{res}(g,r;0)=0.

However, for general
g
and
\sigma
satisfying
{(\mathcal{SY})}
, the critical value

{\bf D}_{n}^{res}(g,\sigma;0)
for odd
n
need not vanish. For the case
n=3
, we refer to
Section
13.10
.

The degrees of
D_{2N}^{res}(h;\lambda)
and
D_{2N+1}^{res}(h;\lambda)
both equal

N
. Hence the above relations show that the respective degrees of

{\bf D}_{2N}^{res}(g,r;\lambda)
and
{\bf D}_{2N+1}^{res}(g,r,\lambda)
are
2N
and
2N+1
.
Since in the generic case
{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(\lambda)
has degree
2N
, it follows that in
the Poincaré-Einstein case the degrees fall on half. This drop in degree reflects
the vanishing of curvature invariants in the Poincaré-Einstein case. For instance,
(
13.11
) and Lemma
13.11.3
show that
{\bf D}_{1}^{res}(g,r;\lambda)
and

{\bf D}_{2}^{res}(g,r;\lambda)
have respective degrees
1
and
2
. In these cases, the
vanishing of
H
,
\mathring{L}
, and
{\sf P}_{00}
are responsible for the drop in degree.

The following result implies that residue families of order
N\leq n
are completely
determined by the metric
g
and the embedding
M\hookrightarrow X
.

Proposition 7.6
.

Let
{\mathbb{N}}\ni N\leq n
. Then
{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda)
is determined only by the
coefficients
\sigma_{(j)}
for
j\leq N+1
in the expansion of
\sigma
in geodesic
normal coordinates.

Proof.

The claim follows by evaluating the residue definition of residue families in terms
of geodesic normal coordinates. We use the diffeomorphism
\kappa
to write

(7.14)

\langle M_{u}(\lambda),\psi\rangle=\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{g}=\int_{[0,\varepsilon)}\int_{M}\left(\frac{\kappa^{*}(\sigma)}{r}\right)^{\lambda}r^{\lambda}\kappa^{*}(u)\kappa^{*}(\psi)drdvol_{h_{r}}

for
\Re(\lambda)\gg 0
and test functions
\psi
with sufficiently small support.
The eigenfunction
\kappa^{*}(u)
of
\Delta_{\kappa^{*}(\sigma)^{-2}g}
has an
asymptotic expansion in
r
of the form

\sum_{j\geq 0}r^{\mu+j}{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\mu)(f)+\cdots,

where the dots indicate an asymptotic expansion with exponents
n-\mu+j
. In that
expansion, the operators
{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\mu)
are determined by recursive relations. An
induction argument using the formula

\Delta_{\kappa^{*}(\sigma)^{-2}g}=(\kappa^{*}(\sigma))^{2}\Delta_{dr^{2}+h_{r}}-(n-1)\kappa^{*}(\sigma)\nabla_{\operatorname{grad}(\kappa^{*}(\sigma))}

shows that
{\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\mu)
is determined only by the coefficients of
r^{j}
for
j\leq N+1
in the expansion of
\kappa^{*}(\sigma)
, i.e., by
\sigma_{(j)}
for
j\leq N+1
. Now the residue of the left-hand side of (
7.14
)
at
\lambda=-\mu-1-N
is determined by
the coefficient of
r^{\lambda+\mu+N}
in the expansion of the integrand. That
coefficient involves the operators
{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\mu)
with
j\leq N
and the coefficients
of
r^{j}
for
j\leq N
in the expansion of
\kappa^{*}(\sigma)/r
. The latter are
determined by
\sigma_{(j)}
for
j\leq N+1
. All other ingredients of the integrand
do not depend on
\sigma
. The proof is complete.
∎

Since the coefficients
\sigma_{(j)}
for
j\leq n+1
are determined by the metric
g
and
the embedding
\iota
, the residue families
{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda)
for
N\leq n

are completely determined by the metric
g
and the embedding
\iota
, and it is justified
to use the simplified notation to
{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g;\lambda)
.

The definition of residue families can be extended to a wider setting. Let

\sigma\in C^{\infty}(X)
be a boundary defining function so that
|d\sigma|_{g}^{2}=1

on the boundary
M
. Then the singular metric
\sigma^{-2}g
is asymptotically
hyperbolic. This implies the existence of a Poisson operator and the existence of an
eigenfunction
u
of
\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}
with eigenvalue
-\mu(n-\mu)
and
arbitrary given boundary value
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
. The asymptotic expansion of
u

in terms of adapted coordinates can be stated as an asymptotic expansion in powers
of
\sigma
. For
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we define an operator

\delta_{N}(g,\sigma;\mu):C^{\infty}(X)\to C^{\infty}(M)

by

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-\mu-1-N}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{g}\right)=\int_{M}f\delta_{N}(g,\sigma;\mu)(\psi)dvol_{\iota^{*}(g)}

and let

{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}N!(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!2N\!+\!1)_{N}\delta_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!N).

These general residue families are conformally covariant in the following sense.

Theorem 7.7
.

The residue family
{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda)
is
conformally covariant in the sense that

{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma};\lambda)\circ e^{\lambda\varphi}=e^{(\lambda-N)\iota^{*}(\varphi)}\circ{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda)

for all conformal changes
(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma})=(e^{2\varphi}g,e^{\varphi}\sigma)
,
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(X)
.

Proof.

Let
u\in\ker(\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}+\mu(n-\mu))
be an eigenfunction with leading
term
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
in its expansion into powers of
\sigma
. We calculate the
residue

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-\mu-1-N}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{g}\right)

in two ways. On the one hand, it equals

\int_{M}f\delta_{N}(g,\sigma;\mu)(\psi)dvol_{\iota^{*}(g)}.

On the other hand, for
\Re(\lambda)\gg 0
, we have

\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{g}=\int_{X}\hat{\sigma}^{\lambda}u(e^{(-\lambda-n-1)\varphi}\psi)dvol_{\hat{g}}

and the leading term in the
\hat{\sigma}
-expansion of
u
equals
e^{-\mu\iota^{*}(\varphi)}f
. Hence the residue equals

\int_{M}e^{-\mu\iota^{*}(\varphi)}f\delta_{N}(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma};\mu)(e^{(\mu-n+N)\varphi}\psi)dvol_{\iota^{*}(\hat{g})}.

But
dvol_{\iota^{*}(\hat{g})}=e^{n\iota^{*}\varphi}dvol_{\iota^{*}(g)}
. Since
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
is arbitrary, we find

\delta_{N}(g,\sigma;\mu)=e^{(-\mu+n)\iota^{*}(\varphi)}\circ\delta_{N}(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma};\mu)\circ e^{(\mu+N-n)\varphi}.

This result implies the assertion.
∎

As a special case, it follows that the compositions of residue families of order

N\leq n
in the sense of Definition
7.3
with
\eta^{*}
(and
\sigma
being a solution of
the Yamabe problem) are conformally covariant. Whereas the general residue families in
Theorem
7.7
depend on
g
and
\sigma
, Proposition
7.6
shows
that the special cases in Definition
7.3
only depend on
g
(and the embedding
\iota
).

Although Definition
7.3
breaks the conformal covariance (by omitting
\eta^{*}
), for
those values of
\lambda
for which residue families are tangential, the resulting operators on
M

are still conformally covariant. This observation will play a central role in Section
9
.
In the following sections, it will always be clear from the context which notion of residue families is
being used.

8.
Residue families as compositions of
L
-operators

In the present section, we show that the composition of residue families as defined in Definition
7.3

with
\eta^{*}
(defining adapted coordinates) can be identified with compositions of Laplace-Robin operators and
the restriction operator
\iota^{*}
.

We recall the notation
L_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}L(g,\sigma;\lambda\!-\!N\!+\!1)\circ\cdots\circ L(g,\sigma;\lambda)

and set
L_{0}=\operatorname{Id}
(see (
4.10
)).

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
{\mathbb{N}}\ni N\leq n
and assume that
\sigma
satisfies the condition
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then

{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda)\circ\eta^{*}=\iota^{*}L_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda).

Proof.

It suffices to prove that

(8.1)

\delta_{N}(g,\sigma;\lambda)\circ\eta^{*}=\frac{1}{N!(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!1)_{N}}\iota^{*}L(g,\sigma;\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!1)\circ\cdots\circ L(g,\sigma;\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!N).

Let
u
be an eigenfunction with boundary value
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
satisfying
(
7.7
) with
\Re(\mu)=n/2
,
\mu\neq n/2
. In the following, it will be
convenient to use the notation

A((\lambda)_{N})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}A(\lambda)\circ A(\lambda+1)\circ\cdots\circ A(\lambda+N-1)

for any
\lambda
-dependent family
A(\lambda)
of operators. Then
L_{N}(\lambda)=L((\lambda-N+1)_{N})
. On the one hand, (
7.9
) states that

(8.2)

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-\mu-1-N}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{g}\right)=\int_{M}f\delta_{N}(g,\sigma;\mu)(\eta^{*}(\psi))dvol_{h}.

Now we first assume that
\sigma
satisfies the stronger assumption
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)=1
. We apply Corollary
4.6
to calculate

(8.3)

\displaystyle-L(g,\sigma;\lambda+1)(\sigma^{\lambda+1}u)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\lambda}\left(\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}-(\lambda\!+\!1)(n\!+\!\lambda\!+\!1)\operatorname{Id}\right)u

(8.4)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\lambda}(-\mu(n\!-\!\mu)-(\lambda\!+\!1)(n\!+\!\lambda\!+\!1))u

(8.5)

\displaystyle=-(\lambda\!+\!\mu\!+\!1)(\lambda\!-\!\mu\!+\!n\!+\!1)\sigma^{\lambda}u.

We regard this relation as a Bernstein-Sato-type functional equation. Hence for
\Re(\lambda)\notin-\frac{n}{2}-{\mathbb{N}}

we obtain

\displaystyle\sigma^{\lambda}u

\displaystyle=\frac{L(g,\sigma;\lambda\!+\!1)(\sigma^{\lambda+1}u)}{(\lambda\!+\!\mu\!+\!1)(\lambda\!-\!\mu\!+\!n\!+\!1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{L(g,\sigma;\lambda\!+\!1)L(g,\sigma;\lambda\!+\!2)(\sigma^{\lambda+2}u)}{(\lambda\!+\!\mu\!+\!1)(\lambda\!+\!\mu\!+\!2)(\lambda\!-\!\mu\!+\!n\!+\!1)(\lambda\!-\!\mu\!+\!n\!+\!2)}

\displaystyle=\dots=\frac{L(g,\sigma;(\lambda\!+\!1)_{N})(\sigma^{\lambda+N}u)}{(\lambda\!+\!\mu\!+\!1)_{N}(\lambda\!-\!\mu\!+\!n\!+\!1)_{N}}.

It follows that the integral

\lambda\mapsto\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{g},\;\Re(\lambda)\gg 0

admits a meromorphic continuation to
{\mathbb{C}}
with simple
poles in the set

-\mu-1-{\mathbb{N}}_{0}\cup\mu-n-1-{\mathbb{N}}_{0}.

More precisely, we get

(8.6)

\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{g}=\frac{1}{(\lambda\!+\!\mu\!+\!1)_{N}(\lambda\!-\!\mu\!+\!n\!+\!1)_{N}}\int_{X}L(g,\sigma;(\lambda\!+\!1)_{N})(\sigma^{\lambda+N}u)\psi dvol_{g}

for
\Re(\lambda)>-\frac{n}{2}-1
and
N\geq 1
. In the following, it will be convenient to choose
\lambda
so
that
\Re(\lambda)>-\frac{n}{2}+1
. Now we note that a function in
C^{\infty}(X^{\circ})
with an asymptotic
expansion of the form
\sum_{j\geq 0}\sigma^{\nu+j}a_{j}
with
\Re(\nu)>2
and
a_{j}\in C^{\infty}(M)

satisfies the assumptions in Proposition
4.9
. Thus, by a repeated application of Proposition

4.9
, the right-hand side of (
8.6
) equals

\frac{1}{(\lambda\!+\!\mu\!+\!1)_{N}(\lambda\!-\!\mu\!+\!n\!+\!1)_{N}}\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda+N}uL(g,\sigma;(-\lambda\!-\!n\!-\!N)_{N})(\psi)dvol_{g}.

By the assumptions, the zeros of the product

\mu\mapsto(\lambda\!+\!\mu\!+\!1)_{N}(\lambda\!-\!\mu\!+\!n\!+\!1)_{N}

are simple for
\Re(\lambda)>-\frac{n}{2}+1
. Thus, using the residue formula

(8.7)

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-\mu-1}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{g}\right)=\int_{M}f\iota^{*}(\psi)dvol_{h},

we find

\displaystyle\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-\mu-1-N}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{g}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{N}}{(-N)_{N}(2\mu\!-\!n\!+\!1)_{N}}\int_{M}f\iota^{*}L(g,\sigma;(\mu\!-\!n\!+\!1)_{N})(\psi)dvol_{h}

(8.8)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N!(2\mu\!-\!n\!+\!1)_{N}}\int_{M}f\iota^{*}L_{N}(g,\sigma;\mu\!-\!n\!+\!N)(\psi)dvol_{h}

for
N\geq 1
. Comparing this result with (
8.2
), completes the proof of
(
8.1
) for
\Re(\mu)=n/2
,
\mu\neq n/2
. The assertion then follows by
meromorphic continuation. If
\sigma
satisfies only the assumption
{(\mathcal{SY})}
, analogous
arguments show that the right-hand side of (
8.6
) contains an additional integral

\int_{X}u\psi R_{n+1}\sigma^{\lambda+n+1}dvol_{g}.

Since
R_{n+1}
is smooth up to the boundary and
N\leq n
, this integral is regular
at
\lambda=-\mu-1-N
, i.e., does not contribute to the residue. The proof is
complete.
∎

Corollary 8.2
.

Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
N\leq n
and assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then

{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda)={\bf D}_{N-1}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda-1)\circ L(g,\sigma;\lambda).

Remark 8.3
.

Theorem
8.1
identifies the composition of residue families with

\eta^{*}
with compositions of
L
-operators if
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. By Theorem

7.4
, residue families are linear combinations of compositions of
tangential operators and iterated normal derivatives
\iota^{*}\partial^{k}_{s}
. We may use
formula (
6.7
) to write their composition with
\eta^{*}
in
terms of iterated gradients
\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{k}
. This yields a formula for the composition
of residue families with
\eta^{*}
in terms of iterated gradients and tangential
operators.

For closed
M
, Theorem
8.1
implies formulas for integrated
renormalized volume coefficients in terms of compositions of Laplace-Robin
operators. First, we observe that, for
N\leq n-1
, Theorem
7.4
implies

\displaystyle\int_{M}{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g,\sigma;-n\!+\!N)(1)dvol_{h}

\displaystyle=N!(-n\!+\!1)_{N}\sum_{j=0}^{N}\int_{M}{\mathcal{T}}_{N-j}^{*}(g,\sigma;0)(v_{j})dvol_{h}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{N}\frac{(n\!-\!1)!N!}{(n\!-\!1\!-\!N)!}\sum_{j=0}^{N}\int_{M}v_{j}{\mathcal{T}}_{N-j}(g,\sigma;0)(1)dvol_{h}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{N}\frac{(n\!-\!1)!N!}{(n\!-\!1\!-\!N)!}\int_{M}v_{N}dvol_{h}.

In the last equality, we used the fact that all coefficients except the leading one
in the expansion of the harmonic function
u=1
vanish. Combining this identity with
Theorem
8.1
we obtain

Corollary 8.4
.

Let
{\mathbb{N}}\ni N\leq n-1
and assume that
\sigma
satisfies the condition
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then

(8.9)

\int_{M}v_{N}dvol_{h}=(-1)^{N}\frac{(n\!-\!1\!-\!N)!}{(n-1)!N!}\int_{M}\iota^{*}L_{N}(g,\sigma;-n\!+\!N)(1)dvol_{h}.

We shall see later in Theorem
10.11
that this reproves the special case
\tau=1
of

[
GW17
, Proposition 3.5]
. The critical case
N=n
will be discussed in Section
10
.

These identities for integrated renormalized volume coefficients admit a natural interpretation as special
cases of an interesting identity for distributions. In order to describe that point of view, we smoothly extend

g
and
\sigma
to a sufficiently small neighborhood
\tilde{X}
of
M
so that
|\mathcal{N}|\neq 0
on
\tilde{X}
(this
is always possible
[
PV16
]
). It will be convenient to assume that
\tilde{X}=\eta(I\times M)
with a sufficiently small interval

I=(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
around
0
. For any
u\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
, the pull-back
\sigma^{*}(u)\in C^{\infty}(X)

defines a current by

\left\langle\sigma^{*}(u),\psi dvol_{g}\right\rangle=\int_{\tilde{X}}\sigma^{*}(u)\psi dvol_{g},\;\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\tilde{X}).

By approximating the delta distribution
\delta
at
0
by test functions, we obtain a current
\sigma^{*}(\delta)
. We recall that
the pull-back
\sigma^{*}(u)
of a distribution
u
on the real line by
\sigma
exists since the differential of
\sigma
is
surjective. The pull-back operation itself then is continuous on distributions (and currents)
[
Hö83
, Theorem 6.1.2]
.
Since
|\mathcal{N}|=1
on
M
, it holds

(8.10)

\left\langle\sigma^{*}(\delta),\psi dvol_{g}\right\rangle=\int_{M}\iota^{*}(\psi)dvol_{h}

by an extension of
[
Hö83
, Theorem 6.1.5]
. We also use the notation
\delta_{M}
for the latter distribution and call it the
delta distribution of
M
. We define the action of a differential operator
D

on currents
u
on
\tilde{X}
by

\langle D(u),\psi dvol_{g}\rangle=\langle u,D^{*}(\psi)dvol_{g}\rangle,\;\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\tilde{X}).

Here the formal adjoint
D^{*}
of
D
is determined by the relation

(8.11)

\int_{\tilde{X}}D(\varphi)\psi dvol_{g}=\int_{\tilde{X}}\varphi D^{*}(\psi)dvol_{g},\;\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\tilde{X}),\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\tilde{X}).

Theorem 8.5
.

Assume that
N\leq n-1
and assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then

(8.12)

L(g,\sigma;-N)\circ\cdots\circ L(g,\sigma;-1)(\sigma^{*}(\delta))=a_{N}\mathfrak{X}^{N}(\sigma^{*}(\delta)),

where
a_{N}=(n\!-\!1)!/(n\!-\!1\!-\!N)!
and
\mathfrak{X}=\mathcal{N}/|\mathcal{N}|^{2}
is defined in Section
6
.

Proof.

Corollary
4.8
implies that
L_{N}(\lambda)
acts on
\sigma^{*}(\delta)
by

(8.13)

\langle L_{N}(\lambda)(\sigma^{*}(\delta)),\psi dvol_{g}\rangle=\langle\sigma^{*}(\delta),L_{N}(-n\!-\!\lambda\!+\!N\!-\!1)(\psi)dvol_{g}\rangle.

Hence

\langle L_{N}(\lambda)(\sigma^{*}(\delta)),\psi dvol_{g}\rangle=\int_{M}\iota^{*}L_{N}(-n\!-\!\lambda\!+\!N\!-\!1)(\psi)dvol_{h}.

Thus, Theorem
8.1
yields

\langle L_{N}(\lambda)(\sigma^{*}(\delta)),\psi dvol_{g}\rangle=\int_{M}{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(-\lambda\!-\!n+\!N\!-\!1)(\eta^{*}(\psi))dvol_{h}.

Note that this formula implies that
\langle L_{N}(\lambda)(\sigma^{*}(\delta)),\psi dvol_{g}\rangle

only depends on the first
N
terms in the expansion of
\sigma
. Now, by Theorem

7.4
, the latter integral equals

\displaystyle N!(-2\lambda\!-\!n\!-\!1)_{N}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{j=0}^{N}\frac{1}{(N\!-\!j)!}\int_{M}[{\mathcal{T}}_{j}^{*}(-\lambda\!-\!1)\circ v_{0}+\cdots+{\mathcal{T}}_{0}^{*}(-\lambda\!-\!1)\circ v_{j}](\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{N-j}(\eta^{*}(\psi)))dvol_{h}.

Hence using partial integration, we obtain

\displaystyle\langle L_{N}(\lambda)(\sigma^{*}(\delta)),\psi dvol_{g}\rangle=N!(-2\lambda\!-\!n\!-\!1)_{N}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{j=0}^{N}\frac{1}{(N\!-\!j)!}\int_{M}[{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(-\lambda\!-\!1)(1)+\cdots+{\mathcal{T}}_{0}(-\lambda\!-\!1)(1)v_{j}]\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{N-j}(\eta^{*}(\psi))dvol_{h}.

Now let
\lambda=-1
. Since
{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(0)(1)=0
for
j\geq 1
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{0}=\operatorname{Id}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\langle L_{N}(-1)(\sigma^{*}(\delta)),\psi dvol_{g}\rangle

\displaystyle=N!(-n\!+\!1)_{N}\sum_{j=0}^{N}\frac{1}{(N\!-\!j)!}\int_{M}v_{j}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{N-j}(\eta^{*}(\psi))dvol_{h}

(8.14)

\displaystyle=(-n\!+\!1)_{N}\int_{M}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{N}(v\eta^{*}(\psi))dvol_{h}.

On the other hand, partial integration shows

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{X}^{N}(u),\psi dvol_{g}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\tilde{X}}\mathfrak{X}^{N}(u)\psi dvol_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{I\times M}\eta^{*}\mathfrak{X}^{N}(u)\eta^{*}(\psi)vdsdvol_{h}

\displaystyle=\int_{I\times M}\partial_{s}^{N}\eta^{*}(u)\eta^{*}(\psi)vdsdvol_{h}

(by (
6.6
))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{N}\int_{I\times M}\eta^{*}(u)v^{-1}\partial_{s}^{N}(\eta^{*}(\psi)v)vdsdvol_{h}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{N}\int_{\tilde{X}}u\eta_{*}(v^{-1}\partial_{s}^{N}(v\eta^{*}(\psi))dvol_{g}

for
u\in C^{\infty}(\tilde{X})
and
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\tilde{X})
. Hence
(\mathfrak{X}^{N})^{*}(\psi)=(-1)^{N}\eta_{*}(v^{-1}\partial_{s}^{N}(v\eta^{*}(\psi))
and

(8.15)

\langle\mathfrak{X}^{N}(\sigma^{*}(\delta)),\psi dvol_{g}\rangle=(-1)^{N}\int_{M}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{N}(v\eta^{*}(\psi))dvol_{h}.

The proof is complete.
∎

Note that, for
\psi=1
near
M
, the arguments in the above proof show that

\displaystyle\int_{M}\iota^{*}L_{N}(-n\!+\!N)(1)dvol_{h}

\displaystyle=\frac{(n\!-\!1)!}{(n\!-\!1\!-\!N)!}\langle\mathfrak{X}^{N}(\sigma^{*}(\delta)),dvol_{g}\rangle

\displaystyle=(-1)^{N}\frac{(n\!-\!1)!}{(n\!-\!1\!-\!N)!}\int_{M}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{N}(v)dvol_{h}

for
N\leq n-1
. This proves that the identity (
8.9
) is a special case of Theorem
8.5
.

Remark 8.6
.

Theorem
8.5
results from our attempt to understand the distributional formula
in
[
GW17
, Proposition 3.2]
. In
[
GW17
]
, this distributional identity is the key to prove
formulas like (
8.9
). Here we follow a reverse logic.

The following shift-property of residue families either follows by combining Theorem

8.1
with Corollary
4.10
or directly from the residue
definition of residue families.

Lemma 8.7
.

Let
1\leq N\leq n
and assume that
\sigma
satisfies the condition
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then

{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda)\circ s=N(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!N){\bf D}_{N-1}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda\!-\!1).

Proof.

We use the residue definition of residue families (Definition
7.3
).
In particular,
u
is an eigenfunction of the Laplacian of
\sigma^{-2}g
for the
eigenvalue
-\mu(n-\mu)
. Now the calculation

\displaystyle\int_{M}f\delta_{N}(\mu)(\eta^{*}(\sigma\psi))dvol_{h}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-\mu-1-N}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}u(\sigma\psi)dvol_{g}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-\mu-1-N}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda+1}u\psi dvol_{g}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-\mu-N}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{g}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{M}f\delta_{N-1}(\mu)(\eta^{*}(\psi))dvol_{h}

shows that
\delta_{N}(\mu)\circ\eta^{*}\circ\sigma=\delta_{N-1}(\mu)\circ\eta^{*}
, i.e.,
\delta_{N}(\mu)\circ s=\delta_{N-1}(\mu)
. The claim is a direct
consequence. Finally, we note that the relation
\delta_{N}(\mu)\circ s=\delta_{N-1}(\mu)
also is an immediate consequence of Theorem
7.4
.
∎

9.
Extrinsic conformal Laplacians

If
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)\neq 0
and
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the commutator relation (
4.17
) in Corollary

4.10
shows that the composition

\tilde{L}_{N}(g,\sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\tilde{L}_{N}\left(g,\sigma;\frac{-n\!+\!N}{2}\right)=\tilde{L}\left(g,\sigma;\frac{-n\!-\!N}{2}\!+\!1\right)\circ\cdots\circ\tilde{L}\left(g,\sigma;\frac{-n\!+\!N}{2}\right)

is a tangential operator, i.e., it holds
\tilde{L}_{N}\circ\sigma=\sigma\circ\tilde{L}^{\prime}_{N}
for
some operator
\tilde{L}^{\prime}_{N}
. Thus, the operator
\tilde{L}_{N}(g,\sigma)
on
C^{\infty}(X)

induces an operator
\tilde{{\bf P}}_{N}(g,\sigma)
on
C^{\infty}(M)
according to

(9.1)

\iota^{*}\tilde{L}_{N}(g,\sigma)=\tilde{{\bf P}}_{N}(g,\sigma)\iota^{*}.

This observation is a special case of
[
GW14a
, Theorem 4.1]
. If additionally
\sigma
satisfies
the condition
{(\mathcal{SY})}
and
N\leq n
, then the resulting operator on
C^{\infty}(M)
will be denoted by

{\bf P}_{N}(g,\sigma)
. In the latter case, one may also directly apply (
4.10
).

Now, following Gover and Waldron, we define

Definition 9.1

(
Extrinsic conformal Laplacians).

Let
{\mathbb{N}}\ni N\leq n
. Assume that
\sigma
satisfies the condition
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then the
operators
{\bf P}_{N}(g,\sigma)
are called extrinsic conformal Laplacians. The operator
{\bf P}_{n}(g,\sigma)

is called the critical extrinsic conformal Laplacian.

The notion is justified by the fact that for even
N
these operators generalize the
GJMS-operators
P_{2N}
which are of the form
\Delta^{N}+LOT
. More precisely, let

g_{+}
be a Poincaré-Einstein metric in normal form relative to
h
. Then

(9.2)

{\bf P}_{2N}(r^{2}g_{+},r)=(2N\!-\!1)!!^{2}P_{2N}(h).

In the general case, the operator
{\bf P}_{2N}(g,\sigma)
is of the form

(2N\!-\!1)!!^{2}\Delta^{N}_{M}+LOT.

Here the lower order terms depend on the embedding
M\hookrightarrow X

(Proposition
9.4
). For odd
N
, the leading part of
{\bf P}_{N}(g)
is not
given by a power of the Laplacian but involves
\mathring{L}
(Proposition
9.10
).
Note that
{\bf D}_{1}^{res}(\frac{-n+1}{2})=0
shows that
{\bf P}_{1}=0
. Note also that the
vanishing

L(g,\sigma;0)(1)=0

implies

(9.3)

{\bf P}_{n}(g,\sigma)(1)=0.

Theorem 9.2
.

Let
{\mathbb{N}}\ni N\leq n
. Assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
.
Then

(9.4)

{\bf D}_{N}^{res}\left(g,\sigma;\frac{-n\!+\!N}{2}\right)={\bf P}_{N}(g,\sigma)\iota^{*}.

More generally, the factorization identities

{\bf D}_{N}^{res}\left(g,\sigma;\frac{-n\!-\!k}{2}\!+\!N\right)={\bf P}_{k}(g,\sigma)\circ{\bf D}_{N-k}^{res}\left(g,\sigma;\frac{-n\!-\!k}{2}\!+\!N\right)

for
1\leq k\leq N
hold true.

Proof.

Theorem
8.1
implies

{\bf D}_{N}^{res}\left(\frac{-n\!-\!k}{2}\!+\!N\right)=\iota^{*}L\left(\frac{-n\!-\!k}{2}\!+\!1\right)\circ\cdots\circ L\left(\frac{-n\!-\!k}{2}\!+\!N\right).

We decompose this product as

\left(\iota^{*}L\left(\frac{-n\!-\!k}{2}\!+\!1\right)\circ\cdots\circ L\left(\frac{-n\!+\!k}{2}\right)\right)\circ\left(L\left(\frac{-n\!+\!k}{2}+1\right)\circ\cdots\circ L\left(\frac{-n\!-\!k}{2}\!+\!N\right)\right).

By the definition of
{\bf P}_{k}
and Theorem
8.1
, this composition equals

{\bf P}_{k}\circ{\bf D}_{N-k}^{res}\left(\frac{-n\!-\!k}{2}\!+\!N\right).

The proof is complete.
∎

Note that
{\bf P}_{1}=0
implies the vanishing property

{\bf D}_{N}^{res}\left(\frac{-n-1}{2}\!+\!N\right)=0
. The trivial zero of

D_{N}^{res}(\lambda)
at
\lambda=-\frac{n+1}{2}\!+\!N
is actually one of the zeros
in the prefactor in the definition (
7.10
). It appears here as a trivial
zero since the solution operator
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\lambda)
is regular (Lemma
13.11.1
).

Theorem
9.2
extends factorization formulas in the setting of
Poincaré-Einstein metrics. In that case, the left factors are GJMS-operators on
the boundary. For details, we refer to
[
J09
,
J13
]
.

Finally, we notice an interesting direct formula for the critical extrinsic conformal Laplacian

{\bf P}_{n}(g,\sigma)
in terms of the Laplacian of the singular Yamabe metric
\sigma^{-2}g
.
By composition with
\iota^{*}
, Corollary
4.7
shows that

{\bf P}_{n}(g,\sigma)\iota^{*}(f)=\iota^{*}\left(\sigma^{-n}\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\left(\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}+(n\!-\!j)j\right)\right)(f)

for any
f\in C^{\infty}(X)
. We omit the analogous formulas in the subcritical cases.

Next, we provide a spectral theoretical description of the extrinsic conformal Laplacians.

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
2\leq N\leq n
. Assume that
\sigma
satisfies the condition

{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then

{\bf P}_{N}=(-1)^{N-1}2(N\!-\!1)!N!\operatorname{Res}_{\frac{n-N}{2}}({\mathcal{T}}_{N}^{*}(\lambda)).

Proof.

On the right-hand side of formula (
7.11
) in Theorem
7.4
, all
solution operators except
{\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)
are regular at
\lambda=\frac{n-N}{2}
.
The result follows by combining that observation with the identity
(
9.4
) in Theorem
9.2
.
∎

We use the spectral theoretical interpretation of the operators
{\bf P}_{N}
to separate
their leading parts.

Proposition 9.4
.

Let
{\mathbb{N}}\ni N\leq n
. Assume that
\sigma
satisfied
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then

\operatorname{LT}(\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=\frac{n}{2}-N}({\mathcal{T}}_{2N}(\lambda))=-\frac{1}{2^{2N}(N-1)!N!}\Delta^{N}_{h}.

Hence

\operatorname{LT}({\bf P}_{2N})=(2N-1)!!^{2}\Delta^{N}_{h}.

The remaining terms are of order
\leq N-2
.

As a preparation for the proof, we observe that formula (
6.15
) and the identity

\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}=\sigma^{2}\Delta_{g}-(n\!-\!1)\sigma\nabla_{\operatorname{grad}(\sigma)}

imply that the Laplacian of the metric
\eta^{*}(\sigma^{-2}g)=s^{-2}\eta^{*}(g)
takes the form

(9.5)

\displaystyle s^{2}a\partial_{s}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}s^{2}a\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{s})\partial_{s}+\frac{1}{2}s^{2}a^{\prime}\partial_{s}-(n\!-\!1)sa\partial_{s}-\frac{1}{2}s^{2}(d\log a,d\cdot)_{h_{s}}+s^{2}\Delta_{h_{s}},

where
a=\eta^{*}(|\mathcal{N}|^{2})
.

Remark 9.5
.

In the special case
|\mathcal{N}|=1
, the formula (
9.5
) simplifies to

s^{2}\partial_{s}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}s^{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{s})\partial_{s}-(n\!-\!1)s\partial_{s}+s^{2}\Delta_{h_{s}}.

In particular, this reproduces the formula for the Laplacian of a Poincaré-Einstein metric
[
JO20
, (2.18)]
.

Remark 9.6
.

By combining the formula (
9.5
) with (
6.13
), we can give another proof of the conjugation
formula (
4.4
). Here the identities (
6.14
) and (
6.16
) are useful. We omit the details.

The solution operators
{\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)
are determined by the ansatz

\sum_{N\geq 0}s^{\lambda+N}{\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)(f),\;{\mathcal{T}}_{0}=\operatorname{Id}

for an approximate solution
u
of the equation
-\Delta_{s^{-2}\eta^{*}(g)}u=\lambda(n\!-\!\lambda)u
with boundary value
f
. By formula (
9.5
), this means that the sum

\displaystyle a\sum_{N\geq 0}(\lambda\!+\!N)(\lambda\!+\!N\!-\!1){\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)s^{\lambda+N}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{a}{2}\sum_{N\geq 0}(\lambda\!+\!N)\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{s}){\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)s^{\lambda+N+1}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{a^{\prime}}{2}\sum_{N\geq 0}(\lambda\!+\!N){\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)s^{\lambda+N+1}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle(n\!-\!1)a\sum_{N\geq 0}(\lambda\!+\!N){\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)s^{\lambda+N}

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{N\geq 0}(d\log a,d{\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda))_{h_{s}}s^{\lambda+N+2}

(9.6)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{N\geq 0}\Delta_{h_{s}}{\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)s^{\lambda+N+2}

coincides with the sum

(9.7)

-\lambda(n\!-\!\lambda)\sum_{N\geq 0}{\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)s^{\lambda+N}.

In order to compare coefficients of powers of
s
in (
9
) and
(
9.7
), we also insert the expansions of
a
and of
h_{s}
. Note that the
equality of the coefficients of
s^{\lambda}
in (
9
) and (
9.7
)
is trivially satisfied using
\iota^{*}a=1
. The equality of coefficients of
s^{\lambda+N}
in
(
9
) and (
9.7
) yields a recursive formula for

{\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)
. For the coefficient
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\lambda)
, we find
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\lambda)=-H\operatorname{Id}
(Lemma
13.11.1
). An easy induction shows that the order of

{\mathcal{T}}_{2N}(\lambda)
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{2N+1}(\lambda)
is
2N
.

Now we give the proof of Proposition
9.4
.

Proof of Proposition
9.4
.

Let
N
be even. Then (
9
) implies that

N(2\lambda-n+N)\operatorname{LT}({\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda))+\Delta_{h}\operatorname{LT}({\mathcal{T}}_{N-2}(\lambda))=0.

It follows that the leading term of
{\mathcal{T}}_{2N}(\lambda)
is given by

(9.8)

\prod_{j=1}^{N}\frac{1}{2j(n-2\lambda-2j)}\Delta_{h}^{N}=\frac{1}{N!2^{2N}}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{n}{2}-\lambda-N)}{\Gamma(\frac{n}{2}-\lambda)}\Delta_{h}^{N}.

Note that this observation fits with
[
GZ03
, Equation (4.7)]
. Hence

\operatorname{LT}(\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=\frac{n}{2}-N}({\mathcal{T}}_{2N}(\lambda))=-\frac{1}{2^{2N}(N-1)!N!}\Delta_{h}^{N}.

The second claim follows from that result by combining it with Theorem
9.4
.
∎

For odd
N
, the operator
{\bf P}_{N}
is of order
N-1
for general metrics. In the
following result, we separate from the residue

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=\frac{n-N}{2}}({\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda))
and from
{\bf P}_{N}
respective
self-adjoint
leading terms

\operatorname{LT}(\cdot)
so that the remaining terms are of
order
N-3
for general metrics.

Proposition 9.7
.

Let
3\leq N\in{\mathbb{N}}
be odd. Assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then

\operatorname{LT}(\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=\frac{n-N}{2}}({\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda))=\frac{1}{N(N\!-\!2)!}\sum_{r=0}^{\frac{N-3}{2}}m_{N}(r)\Delta^{r}\delta(\mathring{L}d)\Delta^{\frac{N-3}{2}-r}

with

(9.9)

m_{N}(r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\binom{N-1}{r}\prod_{j=1}^{\frac{n-3}{2}}(N-j)\prod_{j=1}^{r}\frac{1}{(N-2j)}\prod_{j=1}^{\frac{n-3}{2}-r}\frac{1}{(N-2j)}.

Hence

(9.10)

\operatorname{LT}({\bf P}_{N})=(2N\!-\!2)(N\!-\!1)!\sum_{r=0}^{\frac{N-3}{2}}m_{N}(r)\Delta^{r}\delta(\mathring{L}d)\Delta^{\frac{N-3}{2}-r}.

Proof.

Let
N
be odd. Note that
a=1+2Hs+\cdots
and

\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{s})=2\operatorname{tr}(L)+\cdots
by Lemma
13.5.1
and (
13.2.3
).
Comparing the coefficients of
s^{\lambda+N}
in (
9
) and (
9.7
) yields the relation

\displaystyle N(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!N){\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)

\displaystyle+2(\lambda\!+\!N\!-\!1)(\lambda\!+\!N\!-\!2)H{\mathcal{T}}_{N-1}(\lambda)+(\lambda\!+\!N\!-\!1)\operatorname{tr}(L){\mathcal{T}}_{N-1}(\lambda)

\displaystyle+(\lambda\!+\!N\!-\!1)H{\mathcal{T}}_{N-1}(\lambda)-2(n\!-\!1)(\lambda\!+\!N\!-\!1){\mathcal{T}}_{N-1}(\lambda)

\displaystyle-(dH,d{\mathcal{T}}_{N-3}(\lambda))_{h}

\displaystyle+\Delta_{h}{\mathcal{T}}_{N-2}(\lambda)+\Delta_{h}^{\prime}{\mathcal{T}}_{N-3}(\lambda)=0,

up to operators of order
\leq N-3
. Here
\Delta_{h_{s}}=\Delta_{h}+s\Delta_{h}^{\prime}+\cdots
. Simplification shows that

\displaystyle N(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!N){\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)+(\lambda\!+\!N\!-\!1)(2\lambda\!+\!2N\!-\!n\!-\!1)H{\mathcal{T}}_{N-1}(\lambda)-(dH,d{\mathcal{T}}_{N-3}(\lambda))_{h}

(9.11)
