\displaystyle+\Delta{\mathcal{T}}_{N-2}(\lambda)+\Delta^{\prime}{\mathcal{T}}_{N-3}(\lambda)=0,

up to operators of order
\leq N-3
.
13
13

13

From now on,
\Delta
is the Laplacian of
h
.

The leading terms of the solution operators
{\mathcal{T}}_{N-1}(\lambda)
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{N-3}(\lambda)

are multiplies of powers of
\Delta
(see (
9.8
)). Moreover, we recall the variation formula

(9.12)

(d/dt)|_{0}(\Delta_{g+sh})=-(\operatorname{Hess}_{g}(\cdot),h)_{g}-(\delta^{g}(h),d\cdot)_{g}+\frac{1}{2}(d\operatorname{tr}_{g}(h),d\cdot)_{g}

(
[
B87
, Proposition 1.184]
). Hence

\displaystyle\Delta^{\prime}_{h}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(d/dt)|_{0}(\Delta_{h+2sL})

\displaystyle=-(\operatorname{Hess}_{h}(\cdot),2L)_{h}-2(\delta^{h}(L),d\cdot)_{h}+n(dH,d\cdot)_{h}

\displaystyle=-2\delta^{h}(Ld)+n(dH,d\cdot)_{h}

(9.13)

\displaystyle=-2H\Delta_{h}+(n-2)(dH,d\cdot)_{h}-2\delta^{h}(\mathring{L}d).

In other words, the first variation
\Delta_{h}^{\prime}
of the Laplacian with respect to the variation
h_{s}
of
h

is given by

(9.14)

-2H\Delta+(n-2)(dH,d\cdot)_{h}-2\delta(\mathring{L}d).

It follows that
N(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!N){\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)
is a linear combination of terms of the form

(9.15)

H\Delta^{\frac{N-1}{2}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad(dH,d\Delta^{\frac{N-3}{2}}),

terms

(9.16)

\Delta^{r}\delta(\mathring{L}d)\Delta^{\frac{N-3}{2}-r},\;r=0,\dots,\tfrac{N-3}{2}

and terms of order
\leq N-3
. In order to determine the coefficients of the terms
(
9.16
) in
{\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)
, we let
\mathring{{\mathcal{T}}}_{N}(\lambda)
be the sum of
these contributions to
{\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)
. Then (
9
) implies

(9.17)

a_{N}(\lambda)\mathring{{\mathcal{T}}}_{N}(\lambda)+\Delta\mathring{{\mathcal{T}}}_{N-2}(\lambda)-2(-1)^{\frac{N-3}{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{\frac{N-3}{2}}\frac{1}{a_{2j}(\lambda)}\delta(\mathring{L}d)\Delta^{\frac{N-3}{2}}=0,

where
a_{N}(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}N(2\lambda-n+N)
. That recursive relation is solved by

(-1)^{\frac{N-3}{2}}a_{N}(\lambda)\mathring{{\mathcal{T}}}_{N}(\lambda)=2\sum_{r=0}^{\frac{N-3}{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{\frac{N-3}{2}-r}\frac{1}{a_{2j}(\lambda)}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\frac{1}{a_{N-2j}(\lambda)}\Delta^{r}\delta(\mathring{L}d)\Delta^{\frac{N-3}{2}-r}.

Hence

\displaystyle N\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=\frac{n-N}{2}}(\mathring{{\mathcal{T}}}_{N}(\lambda))

\displaystyle=2^{-\frac{N-3}{2}}\sum_{r=0}^{\frac{N-3}{2}}\frac{1}{(\frac{N-3}{2}\!-\!r)!r!}\prod_{j=1}^{\frac{N-3}{2}-r}\frac{1}{(N-2j)}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\frac{1}{(N-2j)}\Delta^{r}\delta(\mathring{L}d)\Delta^{\frac{N-3}{2}-r}.

Now the inverse of the coefficient of the term for
r=0
equals

2^{\frac{N-3}{2}}\left(\frac{N-3}{2}\right)!\prod_{j=1}^{\frac{N-3}{2}}(N-2j)=(N-2)!.

Therefore, we obtain

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=\frac{n-N}{2}}\left({\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)\right)=\frac{1}{N(N-2)!}\sum_{r=0}^{\frac{N-3}{2}}m_{N}(r)\Delta^{r}\delta(\mathring{L}d)\Delta^{\frac{N-3}{2}-r},

up to contributions by the terms in (
9.15
) (containing
H
) and lower-order
terms. However, the terms in (
9.15
) do not contribute. This is a consequence of
the conformal covariance of
{\bf P}_{N}
. The proof is complete.
∎

The formula (
9.10
) also makes clear that, if
\mathring{L}
vanishes, the operator

{\bf P}_{N}
is of order
<N-2
. For a discussion of the special cases of
{\bf P}_{3}
and

{\bf P}_{5}
, we refer to Lemma
13.11.6
and Lemma
13.11.7
. In particular, the
proof of Lemma
13.11.6
confirms the vanishing of the terms
H\Delta
and

(dH,d\cdot)
.

Next, we relate the operators
{\bf P}_{N}
to the scattering operator
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)

generalizing a result of
[
GZ03
]
.

Theorem 9.8
.

Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
2\leq N\leq n
. Assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
and
that
(n/2)^{2}-(N/2)^{2}\notin\sigma_{pp}
. Then

(9.18)

{\bf P}_{N}=(-1)^{N}2(N-1)!N!\operatorname{Res}_{\frac{n-N}{2}}(\mathscr{S}(\lambda)).

Proof.

The assumptions guarantee that the scattering operator is well-defined and that

\lambda(n-\lambda)\notin\sigma_{pp}
for
\lambda=\frac{n-N}{2}
.
If
\Re(\lambda)<\frac{n}{2}
so that

\lambda(n-\lambda)\notin\sigma_{pp}
and
\lambda\notin\frac{n}{2}-{\mathbb{N}}
,
the Poisson transform
\mathcal{P}(\lambda)(f)
yields an eigenfunction
u
of the Laplacian
of the metric
\eta^{*}(\sigma^{-2}g)=s^{-2}\eta^{*}(g)
with boundary value

f\in C^{\infty}(M)
and with an asymptotic expansion of the form

(9.19)

\sum_{j\geq 0}s^{\lambda+j}{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(\lambda)(f)+\sum_{j\geq 0}s^{n-\lambda+j}{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(n-\lambda)\mathscr{S}(\lambda)(f).

Although the families
{\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)
and
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
have simple poles at

\lambda=\frac{n-N}{2}
, the Poisson transform
\mathcal{P}(\lambda)(f)
is holomorphic at

\lambda=\frac{n-N}{2}

[
GZ03
, Section 3]
. That means that

\frac{\operatorname{Res}_{\frac{n-N}{2}}({\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda))s^{\lambda+N}}{\lambda-\frac{n-N}{2}}+\frac{\operatorname{Res}_{{\frac{n-N}{2}}}(\mathscr{S}(\lambda))s^{n-\lambda}}{\lambda-\frac{n-N}{2}}

is regular at
\lambda=\frac{n-N}{2}
. Hence

\operatorname{Res}_{\frac{n-N}{2}}({\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda))+\operatorname{Res}_{{\frac{n-N}{2}}}(\mathscr{S}(\lambda))=0

and the asymptotic expansion of
u
involves a
\log
-term

2\operatorname{Res}_{\frac{n-N}{2}}({\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda))(f)s^{\frac{n+N}{2}}\log(s).

Now the claim follows from Theorem
9.3
.
∎

Remark 9.9
.

In
[
GZ03
]
, the scattering operator is defined as
\mathscr{S}(n-\lambda)
and the
GJMS-operators
P_{2N}
have leading part
(-\Delta)^{N}
. Now

\displaystyle P_{2N}(h)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2N\!-\!1)!!^{2}}{\bf P}_{2N}(r^{2}g_{+},r)

(by (
9.2
))

\displaystyle=\frac{2(2N\!-\!1)!(2N)!}{(2N\!-\!1)!!^{2}}\operatorname{Res}_{\frac{n}{2}-N}(\mathscr{S}(\lambda))

(by Theorem
9.8
)

\displaystyle=2^{2N}N!(N\!-\!1)!\operatorname{Res}_{\frac{n}{2}-N}(\mathscr{S}(\lambda)).

This shows that Theorem
9.8
extends
[
GZ03
, Theorem 1]
.

Corollary 9.10
.

Let
{\mathbb{N}}\ni N\leq n
. Assume that
\sigma
satisfied
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then the operators

{\bf P}_{N}(g,\sigma)
are formally self-adjoint as operators on
C^{\infty}(M)
with respect to the scalar product defined by
h
.

Proof.

Lemma
7.2
shows that
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
is self-adjoint on

{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\left\{\frac{n-N}{2}\,|\,N\in{\mathbb{Z}}\right\}
. Since
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
is meromorphic, its
residue at
\lambda=\frac{n-N}{2}
is self-adjoint, too. Then Theorem
9.8
proves the assertion.
∎

10.
Extrinsic
Q
-curvatures and renormalized volume coefficients

The zeroth-order terms of the GJMS-operators
P_{2N}
led to the notion of
Branson’s
Q
-curvature
[
Br95
]
. In the present section, we use residue families to
extend the notion of Branson’s
Q
-curvatures to the framework of the singular Yamabe
problem. This extends the discussion of
Q
-curvatures in
[
J09
]
. The resulting curvature
quantities will be called extrinsic
Q
-curvatures.
14
14

14

In
[
GW17
]
, the critical extrinsic

Q
-curvature is called the
extrinsically coupled
Q
-curvature
.
We relate the integrated
critical renormalized volume coefficient
v_{n}
to the integrated critical extrinsic
Q
-curvature.
Moreover, we discuss the Hadamard renormalization of the volume of singular
Yamabe metrics. Here the total critical extrinsic
Q
-curvature plays an important role.
Although the treatment is inspired by
[
GW14a
,
GW17
]
, our arguments differ and may continue to
illuminate these topics.

By Theorem
9.2
, it holds
{\bf P}_{n}(g,\sigma)\iota^{*}={\bf D}_{n}^{res}(g,\sigma;0)
if
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Now, following the philosophy
of
[
J09
]
, it is natural to consider the pair
({\bf D}_{n}^{res}(g,\sigma;0),\dot{{\bf D}}_{n}^{res}(g,\sigma;0)(1))
.

Definition 10.1

(
Critical extrinsic
Q
-curvature).

Assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then the function

(10.1)

{\bf Q}_{n}(g,\sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}-\dot{{\bf D}}_{n}^{res}(g,\sigma;0)(1)\in C^{\infty}(M)

is called the critical extrinsic
Q
-curvature of
g
.

Since
L(g,\sigma;0)(1)=0
, the identification of residue families with
products of
L
-operators (Theorem
8.1
) implies that

(10.2)

{\bf Q}_{n}(g,\sigma)=-\iota^{*}L(g,\sigma;-n\!+\!1)\circ\cdots\circ L(g,\sigma;-1)\circ\dot{L}(g,\sigma;0)(1).

Moreover, the definition of
L
yields

(10.3)

\dot{L}(g,\sigma;0)(1)=(n-1)\rho-\sigma{\sf J}.

Therefore, we obtain

(10.4)

{\bf Q}_{n}(g,\sigma)=-\iota^{*}L(g,\sigma;-n\!+\!1)\circ\cdots\circ L(g,\sigma;-1)((n-1)\rho-\sigma{\sf J}).

Next, we define subcritical versions of
{\bf Q}_{n}
. Let
N<n
. The definition of
L

implies that

(10.5)

L\left(g,\sigma;\frac{-n+N}{2}\right)(1)=\left(\frac{n-N}{2}\right)(-(N\!-\!1)\rho+\sigma{\sf J}).

Hence the function
{\bf P}_{N}(g,\sigma)(1)
is of the form

\left(\frac{n-N}{2}\right){\bf Q}_{N}(g,\sigma)

with a scalar curvature quantity
{\bf Q}_{N}(g,\sigma)\in C^{\infty}(M)
. It follows that

(10.6)

{\bf Q}_{N}(g,\sigma)=-\iota^{*}L\left(g,\sigma;\frac{-n\!-\!N}{2}\!+\!1\right)\circ\cdots\circ L\left(g,\sigma;\frac{-n\!+\!N}{2}\!-\!1\right)((N\!-\!1)\rho-\sigma{\sf J})

We shall call these quantities
subcritical extrinsic
Q
-curvatures
. In terms
of residue families, these definitions are equivalent to the following definition.

Definition 10.2

(
Subcritical extrinsic
Q
-curvature).

Let
{\mathbb{N}}\ni N<n
. Assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. The functions

{\bf Q}_{N}(g,\sigma)\in C^{\infty}(M)
which are determined by the equation

(10.7)

{\bf D}_{N}^{res}\left(g,\sigma;\frac{-n+N}{2}\right)(1)=\left(\frac{n-N}{2}\right){\bf Q}_{N}(g,\sigma)

are called subcritical extrinsic
Q
-curvatures.

Since the residue families
{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda)
for
N\leq n
are
completely determined by
g
(and
\iota
) (Proposition
7.6
), the quantities

{\bf Q}_{N}(g,\sigma)
for
N\leq n
are also completely determined by
g
(and
\iota
).
Therefore, we shall also use the notation
{\bf Q}_{N}(g)
.

The identities (
10.4
) and (
10.6
) show that the critical extrinsic

Q
-curvature is a limiting case
\lim_{n\to N}
of the subcritical extrinsic

Q
-curvatures (continuation in dimension).

The subcritical
Q
-curvature
{\bf Q}_{N}
is directly linked to the solution operator
{\mathcal{T}}_{N}(\lambda)

through the relation

(10.8)

{\bf Q}_{N}=(-1)^{N}{\bf Q}_{N}\left(\frac{n-N}{2}\right),

where the polynomial
{\bf Q}_{N}(\lambda)
is defined in (
7.2
). In fact, (
7.2
) implies

\operatorname{Res}_{\frac{n-N}{2}}({\mathcal{T}}_{N})(1)=-\frac{\frac{n-N}{2}}{2(N-1)!N!}{\bf Q}_{N}\left(\frac{n-N}{2}\right).

On the other hand, Theorem
9.3
shows that

\operatorname{Res}_{\frac{n-N}{2}}({\mathcal{T}}_{N})(1)=-(-1)^{N}\frac{1}{2(N-1)!N!}{\bf P}_{N}(1)=-(-1)^{N}\frac{\frac{n-N}{2}}{2(N-1)!N!}{\bf Q}_{N}.

The identity (
10.8
) follows by combining both relations. Continuation in
n
also gives the relation

(10.9)

{\bf Q}_{n}=(-1)^{n}{\bf Q}_{n}(0)

in the critical case.

Remark 10.3
.

The extrinsic
Q
-curvatures
{\bf Q}_{N}
are related to Branson’s
Q
-curvatures

Q_{2N}
as follows. We use the convention that
Q_{2N}
for even
n
and
2N<n
is
defined by

P_{2N}(g)(1)=\left({\frac{n}{2}}-N\right)(-1)^{N}Q_{2N}(g),

where
P_{2N}=\Delta^{N}+LOT
is the GJMS-operator of order
2N
with
\Delta

denoting the non-positive Laplacian. Now let
g_{+}
be a Poincaré-Einstein metric
in normal form relative to
h
. Then (
9.2
) implies

{\bf Q}_{2N}(r^{2}g_{+},r)=(-1)^{N}(2N-1)!!^{2}Q_{2N}(h).

Remark 10.4
.

Formula (
10.2
) for the critical extrinsic
Q
-curvature
{\bf Q}_{n}
is closely related to the
definition of the critical extrinsic
Q
-curvature used in
[
GW17
]
. In fact, under the
assumption
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)=1
,
[
GW17
, (3.14)]
defines
{\bf Q}_{n}(g,\sigma)
by

(-1)^{n}\iota^{*}L(g,\sigma;-n+1)\circ\cdots\circ L(g,\sigma;-1)\circ L_{\log}(g,\sigma;1)(\log(1)).

Here
L_{\log}(g,\sigma;\lambda)
is a version of
L
which acts on log-densities
by
15
15

15

For the definition of the notion of log-densities, we refer to
[
GW14a
]
.

L_{\log}(g,\sigma;\lambda)(u)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(n-1)(\nabla_{\operatorname{grad}(\sigma)}(u)+\lambda\rho)-\sigma(\Delta_{g}(u)+\lambda{\sf J}).

In particular, we have
L_{\log}(g,\sigma;\lambda)(\log(1))=\lambda((n-1)\rho-\sigma{\sf J})
and hence

L_{\log}(g,\sigma;1)(\log(1))=(n-1)\rho-\sigma{\sf J}=\dot{L}(g,\sigma;0)(1)

using (
10.3
).

Differentiation of the conformal transformation law for the critical residue family

{\bf D}_{n}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda)
at
\lambda=0
yields the following result.

Theorem 10.5
.

Assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then

(10.10)

e^{n\iota^{*}(\varphi)}{\bf Q}_{n}(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma})={\bf Q}_{n}(g,\sigma)+{\bf P}_{n}(g,\sigma)(\iota^{*}(\varphi))

for all conformal changes
(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma})=(e^{2\varphi}g,e^{\varphi}\sigma)
,

\varphi\in C^{\infty}(X)
.

Proof.

Theorem
7.7
implies

e^{-(\lambda-n)\iota^{*}(\varphi)}\circ{\bf D}_{n}^{res}(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma};\lambda)={\bf D}_{n}^{res}(g,\sigma;\lambda)\circ e^{-\lambda\varphi}.

By differentiating this identity with respect to
\lambda
at
\lambda=0
, we obtain

-e^{n\iota^{*}(\varphi)}\iota^{*}(\varphi)\circ{\bf D}_{n}^{res}(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma};0)+e^{n\iota^{*}(\varphi)}\circ\dot{{\bf D}}_{n}^{res}(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma};0)=\dot{{\bf D}}_{n}^{res}(g,\sigma;0)-{\bf D}_{n}^{res}(g,\sigma;0)\circ\varphi.

Hence

-\iota^{*}(\varphi){\bf D}_{n}^{res}(g,\sigma;0)(1)+e^{n\iota^{*}(\varphi)}\dot{{\bf D}}_{n}^{res}(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma};0)(1)=\dot{{\bf D}}_{n}^{res}(g,\sigma;0)(1)-{\bf D}_{n}^{res}(g,\sigma;0)(\varphi).

Now
{\bf D}_{n}^{res}(g,\sigma;0)={\bf P}_{n}(g,\sigma)\iota^{*}
and (
9.3
) imply the
assertion.
∎

For closed
M^{n}
, integration of(
10.10
) implies

\int_{M}{\bf Q}_{n}(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma})dvol_{\hat{h}}=\int_{M}{\bf Q}_{n}(g,\sigma)dvol_{h}+\int_{M}{\bf P}_{n}(g,\sigma)(\iota^{*}(\varphi))dvol_{h}.

Later we shall prove that
{\bf P}_{n}
is self-adjoint. Hence the second integral on the
right-hand side equals
\int_{M}{\bf P}_{n}(1)\iota^{*}(\varphi)dvol_{h}
. By
(
9.3
), this integral vanishes. This shows

Corollary 10.6
.

Let
M^{n}
be closed. Assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then the integral

\int_{M}{\bf Q}_{n}(g)dvol_{h}

is conformally invariant as a functional of
g
and the embedding
M\hookrightarrow X
.

An alternative argument proving this invariance will be given in Theorem
10.9
.

A generalization of Corollary
10.6
to general
\sigma
will be discussed in Lemma
10.15
.

Next, we recall that Theorem
9.8
relates the operators
{\bf P}_{N}
to the
scattering operator
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)
. In particular, it holds

{\bf P}_{n}=(-1)^{n}2(n-1)!n!\operatorname{Res}_{0}(\mathscr{S}(\lambda)).

Since
{\bf P}_{n}(1)=0
, it follows that the function
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)(1)
is regular at

\lambda=0
. Its value at
\lambda=0
will be denoted by
\mathscr{S}(0)(1)
.

Theorem 10.7
.

Assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then

{\bf Q}_{n}=2(-1)^{n}(n-1)!n!\mathscr{S}(0)(1).

Proof.

We consider the coefficient of
s^{n}
in the asymptotic expansion (
7.4
) of
the eigenfunction
\mathcal{P}(\lambda)(1)
for
\lambda=0
. Since
\mathcal{P}(0)(1)=1
, that
coefficient vanishes. On the other hand, it is given by

{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(0)(1)+\mathscr{S}(0)(1);

we recall that the function
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\lambda)(1)
is regular at
\lambda=0
. Hence

{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(0)(1)=-\mathscr{S}(0)(1)
. But

(10.11)

{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(0)(1)=-\frac{1}{2(n-1)!n!}{\bf Q}_{n}(0)=-(-1)^{n}\frac{1}{2(n-1)!n!}{\bf Q}_{n}

using (
7.2
) and (
10.9
). This implies the assertion.
∎

Remark 10.8
.

Theorem
10.7
extends
[
GZ03
, Theorem 2]
for the scattering operator of Poincaré-Einstein
metrics. In fact,

\displaystyle Q_{n}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\frac{n}{2}}\frac{1}{(n-1)!!^{2}}{\bf Q}_{n}

(by Remark
10.3
)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\frac{n}{2}}\frac{2(n-1)!n!}{(n-1)!!^{2}}\mathscr{S}(0)(1)

(by Theorem
10.7
)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\frac{n}{2}}2^{n}\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)!\left(\frac{n}{2}-1\right)!\mathscr{S}(0)(1).

In that case, the crucial relation between the values of
\mathscr{S}(\lambda)(1)

and
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\lambda)(1)
at
\lambda=0
is provided by
[
GZ03
, Proposition 3.7]
.

The following result (
[
GW17
, Theorem 3.9]
for
\tau=1
and
\mathcal{S}=1
) is an analog of the
identity (
8.9
) in the critical case.

Theorem 10.9
.

Let
M
be closed and assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Let
n\geq 2
be even. Then it holds the equality

(10.12)

{\mathcal{A}}=\int_{M}v_{n}(g)dvol_{h}=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(n-1)!n!}\int_{M}{\bf Q}_{n}(g)dvol_{h}

of conformal invariants.

The quantity
{\mathcal{A}}
is sometimes referred to as an anomaly. This is motivated by the fact
that, in the Poincaré-Einstein case, the function
v_{n}
is the infinitesimal conformal anomaly of the
renormalized volume
[
G00
]
.

We shall give two proofs of that result. The arguments in the first proof will also play a role in Section

11
. The second proof resembles the proof of the analogous result in the subcritical cases.

Proof.

We work in adapted coordinates. In particular, the notation will not distinguish
between objects on
X
and their pull-backs by
\eta
. First, we note that

(10.13)

\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}(v\dot{L}(0)(1))=\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n}(v).

Since
\dot{L}(0)(1)=(n-1)\rho-s{\sf J}
(see (
10.3
)), this local identity is a special case
of the local identity (
10.22
). The current assumption implies
|\mathcal{N}|^{2}=1-2s\rho+O(s^{n+1})

and the same arguments as in the proof of Theorem
10.13
yield the assertion. Now we integrate (
10.13
).
It holds

\int_{M}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n}(v)dvol_{h}=n!\int_{M}v_{n}dvol_{h}

and the integral of the left-hand side of (
10.13
) equals

\displaystyle(-1)^{n-1}\langle\mathfrak{X}^{n-1}(\sigma^{*}(\delta)),\dot{L}(0)(1)dvol_{g}\rangle

(by (
8.15
))

\displaystyle=c_{n}\langle L(-n\!+\!1)\circ\cdots\circ L(-1)(\sigma^{*}(\delta)),\dot{L}(0)(1)dvol_{g}\rangle

(by Theorem
8.5
)

\displaystyle=c_{n}\langle\sigma^{*}(\delta),L(-n\!+\!1)\circ\cdots\circ L(-1)\circ\dot{L}(0)(1)dvol_{g}\rangle

(by (
8.13
))

\displaystyle=c_{n}\int_{M}\iota^{*}L(-n\!+\!1)\circ\cdots\circ L(-1)\circ\dot{L}(0)(1)dvol_{h}

(by (
8.10
))

\displaystyle=-c_{n}\int_{M}{\bf Q}_{n}dvol_{h}

(by (
10.2
))

with
c_{n}=(-1)^{n-1}/(n\!-\!1)!
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 10.10
.

In the Poincaré-Einstein case, it holds
v^{\prime}=v\dot{L}(0)(1)
. This immediately proves (
10.13
).

The proof of Theorem
10.9
rests on the local identity (
10.13
). This identity will also play a role
in Section
11
. We continue with a

Second proof of Theorem
10.9
.

Theorem
7.4
yields the identity

(10.14)

\int_{M}{\bf D}_{n}^{res}(\lambda)(1)dvol_{h}=n!(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!1)_{n}\sum_{j=0}^{n}\int_{M}v_{j}{\mathcal{T}}_{n-j}(\lambda)(1)dvol_{h}.

We split the sum on the right-hand side as

\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}\int_{M}v_{j}{\mathcal{T}}_{n-j}(\lambda)(1)dvol_{h}+\int_{M}v_{n}dvol_{h}+\int_{M}{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\lambda)(1)dvol_{h}.

Now we differentiate (
10.14
) at
\lambda=0
. Since,
{\mathcal{T}}_{j}(0)(1)=0
for
j=1,\dots,n-1
, we find

\int_{M}\dot{{\bf D}}_{n}^{res}(0)(1)dvol_{h}=2(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!n!\left(\int_{M}v_{n}dvol_{h}+\int_{M}{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(0)(1)dvol_{h}\right)

using that
{\mathcal{T}}_{n}(\lambda)(1)
is regular at
\lambda=0
. Now (
10.1
) and (
10.11
) imply

-2\int_{M}{\bf Q}_{n}dvol_{h}=2(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!n!\int_{M}v_{n}dvol_{h}.

This implies (
10.12
).
∎

We finish this section with a discussion of renormalized volumes of singular metrics
\sigma^{-2}g
.
First, we combine the above results to prove

Theorem 10.11
.

Let
M
be closed and assume that
\sigma
satisfied
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then the volume

vol_{\sigma^{-2}g}(\{\sigma>\varepsilon\})=\int_{\sigma>\varepsilon}dvol_{\sigma^{-2}g}

admits the expansion

\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{c_{k}}{n-k}\varepsilon^{-n+k}-{\mathcal{A}}\log\varepsilon+V+o(1),\;\varepsilon\to 0,

where

c_{k}=\int_{M}v_{k}(g)dvol_{h}\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\mathcal{A}}=\int_{M}v_{n}(g)dvol_{h}.

V
is called the renormalized volume. The coefficients in the expansion are natural functionals of the
metric background
g
, which can be written in the form

c_{k}=(-1)^{k}\frac{(n\!-\!1\!-\!k)!}{(n\!-\!1)!k!}\int_{M}\iota^{*}L_{k}(-n\!+\!k)(1)dvol_{h}

for
k=0,\dots,n-1
and

(10.15)

{\mathcal{A}}=(-1)^{n-1}\frac{1}{(n\!-\!1)!n!}\int_{M}\iota^{*}\dot{L}_{n}(0)(1)dvol_{h}.

Proof.

Using
\eta^{*}(dvol_{\sigma^{-2}g})=dvol_{s^{-2}\eta^{*}(g)}=s^{-n-1}v(s)dsdvol_{h}
,
we obtain the asymptotic expansion

\int_{\sigma>\varepsilon}dvol_{\sigma^{-2}g}=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{n-k}\varepsilon^{-n+k}\int_{M}v_{k}dvol_{h}-\log\varepsilon\int_{M}v_{n}dvol_{h}+V+o(1).

Now the expressions for the coefficients in terms of Laplace-Robin operators follow from Corollary

8.4
and Theorem
10.9
.
∎

The above definition of the renormalized volume is also known as the Hadamard renormalization
[
A09
]
.

In the proof of Theorem
10.11
, we deduced the formulas for the coefficients
c_{k}
from the relation between
residue families and iterated Laplace-Robin operators (Theorem
8.1
). We recall that this relation
requires assuming that
\sigma
solves the singular Yamabe problem. However, the only consequences of Theorem

8.1
which are relevant in this context already follow from a study of the residues of the meromorphic
continuation of the integral

(10.16)

\lambda\mapsto\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}dvol_{g},\;\Re(\lambda)>-1.

Therefore it is of interest to include a discussion of an extension of Theorem
10.11
to general
\sigma
which only
rests on the study of that integral. First, we note that the coefficients
c_{k}
in Theorem
8.1
are related
to the residues of (
10.16
):
c_{k}=\int_{M}v_{k}dvol_{h}
. Moreover, the Hadamard renormalization
V
of the
volume of
\sigma^{-2}g
is related to the Riesz renormalization of the volume of
\sigma^{-2}g
which is defined
as the constant term in the Laurent series of (
10.16
) at
\lambda=-n-1

[
A09
]
.

We first observe that Remark
4.4
implies

\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)^{-1}\circ L(g,\sigma;\lambda)+\sigma^{\lambda-1}\circ\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)^{-1}\circ\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}\circ\sigma^{-\lambda}=\lambda(n\!+\!\lambda)\sigma^{-1}\operatorname{Id}

in a sufficiently small neighborhood of the boundary, where
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)\neq 0
. Hence

(10.17)

\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)^{-1}L(g,\sigma;\lambda)(\sigma^{\lambda})=\lambda(n\!+\!\lambda)\sigma^{\lambda-1}.

This is a Bernstein-Sato-type functional equation. Let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
be a cut-off function of
the boundary
M
so that
\mathcal{S}\neq 0
on the support of
\chi
. In the following, we shall simplify the notation by
suppressing the arguments
(g,\sigma)
of
L
and
\mathcal{S}
. The second integral on the right-hand side of the
decomposition

\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}dvol_{g}=\int_{X}\chi\sigma^{\lambda}dvol_{g}+\int_{X}(1-\chi)\sigma^{\lambda}dvol_{g}

is holomorphic on
{\mathbb{C}}
. Now (
10.17
) implies

\int_{X}\chi\sigma^{\lambda}dvol_{g}=\frac{1}{(\lambda\!+\!1)(n\!+\!\lambda\!+\!1)}\int_{X}\chi\mathcal{S}^{-1}L(\lambda\!+\!1)(\sigma^{\lambda+1})dvol_{g}

for
\Re(\lambda)>-1
. Partial integration using Proposition
4.9
shows that for
\Re(\lambda)\gg 0
the
latter integral equals

\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda+1}L(-\lambda\!-\!n\!-\!1)(\chi\mathcal{S}^{-1})dvol_{g}.

Next, we note that
v_{0}=\iota^{*}|\mathcal{N}|^{-1}
implies the residue formula

(10.18)

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-1}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}\psi dvol_{g}\right)=\int_{M}\iota^{*}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{N}|}\psi dvol_{h}.

Now combining the above result with the residue formula (
10.18
) yields

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-2}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}dvol_{g}\right)=-\frac{1}{n\!-\!1}\int_{M}\iota^{*}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{N}|}L(-n\!+\!1)(\mathcal{S}^{-1})dvol_{h}.

More generally, arguments as in the proof of Theorem
8.1
resting on a repeated application
of the functional equation (
10.17
) and Proposition
4.9
show that

(10.19)

\int_{X}\chi\sigma^{\lambda}dvol_{g}=\frac{1}{(\lambda\!+\!1)_{k}(\lambda\!+\!n\!+\!1)_{k}}\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda+k}\tilde{L}_{k}(-\lambda\!-\!n\!-\!1)(\chi)dvol_{g},

for
\Re(\lambda)\gg 0
, and it follows that

(10.20)

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-k-1}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}dvol_{g}\right)=\frac{1}{(-n\!+\!1)_{k}k!}\int_{M}\iota^{*}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{N}|}\tilde{L}_{k}(-n\!+\!k)(1)dvol_{h}

for
k<n
using the relation
\iota^{*}\tilde{L}_{k}(\lambda)(\chi)=\iota^{*}\tilde{L}_{k}(\lambda)(1)
.
The assumption
k<n
guarantees that the prefactor on the right-hand side of (
10.19
) is regular
at
\lambda=-k-1
. Here we use the notation

\tilde{L}(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}L(\lambda)\circ\mathcal{S}^{-1}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\tilde{L}_{k}(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\tilde{L}(\lambda\!-\!k\!+\!1)\circ\cdots\circ\tilde{L}(\lambda)

(see (
4.18
) and (
4.19
)).

Example 10.12
.

If
\mathcal{S}=1
, then
\iota^{*}\mathcal{N}=1
and

\displaystyle\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-2}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}dvol_{g}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{n\!-\!1}\int_{M}\iota^{*}L(-n\!+\!1)(1)dvol_{h}

\displaystyle=-(n-1)\int_{M}\iota^{*}\rho dvol_{h}=(n-1)\int_{M}Hdvol_{h}

using
\iota^{*}\rho=-H
(Lemma
13.5.1
). This fits with the fact that
v_{1}=(n-1)H
(Example
13.4.2
).
Similarly, if
\sigma=d_{M}
is the distance function of
M
, then
\mathcal{S}=1+2\sigma\rho
implies

\iota^{*}L(-n+1)(\mathcal{S}^{-1})=(n-1)^{2}\iota^{*}\rho+2(n-1)\iota^{*}\rho=(n-1)(n+1)\iota^{*}\rho
. Hence

\displaystyle\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-2}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}dvol_{g}\right)=\int_{M}\iota^{*}\Delta_{g}(\sigma)dvol_{h}=n\int_{M}Hdvol_{h}.

These results prove the first part of the following theorem.

Theorem 10.13
.

Let
M
be closed and
\sigma
be a defining function of
M
. Then the volume

vol_{\sigma^{-2}g}(\{\sigma>\varepsilon\})=\int_{\sigma>\varepsilon}dvol_{\sigma^{-2}g}

admits the expansion

\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{c_{k}}{n-k}\varepsilon^{-n+k}-{\mathcal{A}}\log\varepsilon+V+o(1),\;\varepsilon\to 0,

where

c_{k}=\int_{M}v_{k}dvol_{h}=(-1)^{k}\frac{(n\!-\!1\!-\!k)!}{(n\!-\!1)!k!}\int_{M}\iota^{*}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{N}|}\tilde{L}_{k}(-n\!+\!k)(1)dvol_{h}

for
k=0,\dots,n-1
. Moreover, it holds

(10.21)

{\mathcal{A}}=(-1)^{n-1}\frac{1}{(n\!-\!1)!n!}\int_{M}\iota^{*}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{N}|}\tilde{L}_{n-1}(-1)\left(\dot{L}(0)(1)\mathcal{S}^{-1}+(d\mathcal{S}^{-1},d\sigma)_{g}\right)dvol_{h}.

Theorem
10.13
is due to
[
GW17
]
. For a discussion of the relations between the current arguments and the
proofs in these references, we refer to Remark
10.14
.

Proof.

We work in adapted coordinates. The form of the expansion follows as in the proof of Theorem

10.11
. Since
c_{k}=\int_{M}v_{k}dvol_{h}
is the residue of
\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}dvol_{g}
at
\lambda=-k-1
,
the asserted formula for
c_{k}
follows by a calculation of these residues using the functional equation (
10.17
).
It only remains to prove the formula for the anomaly
{\mathcal{A}}
. Note that the proof for
c_{k}
with
k\leq n-1
does not
extend to the present case since for
k=n
the right-hand side of (
10.19
) has a double pole at
\lambda=-n-1
.
To bypass this difficulty, we first prove the local relation
16
16

16

This relation can be interpreted as a local version
of the global relation in
[
GW17
, Lemma 3.8]
.

(10.22)

\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}\left(v\left[((n-1)\rho-s{\sf J})\mathcal{S}^{-1}+|\mathcal{N}|^{2}\partial_{s}(\mathcal{S}^{-1})\right]\right)=\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n}(v)

near
M
. Let

I\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}(v\left[(n-1)\rho-s{\sf J}\right]\mathcal{S}^{-1}).

The definition
\rho=-\frac{1}{n+1}(\Delta(s)+s{\sf J})
implies that

(10.23)

I=-\frac{n-1}{n+1}\left(\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}(v\Delta(s)\mathcal{S}^{-1})+2n\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-2}(v{\sf J}\mathcal{S}^{-1})\right).

Now we consider the first term on the right-hand side of (
10.23
). By (6.17), it holds

v\Delta(s)=\partial_{s}(v|\mathcal{N}|^{2}).

Hence we obtain

\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}(v\Delta(s)\mathcal{S}^{-1})=\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n}(v|\mathcal{N}|^{2}\mathcal{S}^{-1})-\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}(v|\mathcal{N}|^{2}\partial_{s}(\mathcal{S}^{-1})).

But
\mathcal{S}=|\mathcal{N}|^{2}+2s\rho
implies
|\mathcal{N}|^{2}=\mathcal{S}-2s\rho
. Hence

\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}(v\Delta(s)\mathcal{S}^{-1})=\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n}(v)-2n\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}(v\rho\mathcal{S}^{-1})-\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}(v|\mathcal{N}|^{2}\partial_{s}(\mathcal{S}^{-1})).

Now another application of the definition of
\rho
shows that the middle term on the right-hand side of the last equation
equals

\frac{2n}{n+1}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}((v\Delta(s)+vs{\sf J})\mathcal{S}^{-1}).

Simplification of the resulting equation yields

\displaystyle-\frac{n-1}{n+1}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}(v\Delta(s)\mathcal{S}^{-1})

(10.24)

\displaystyle=\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n}(v)+\frac{2n(n-1)}{n+1}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-2}(v{\sf J})-\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}(v|\mathcal{N}|^{2}\partial_{s}(\mathcal{S}^{-1})).

By combining this result with (
10.23
), we have proved

I=\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n}(v)-\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}(v|\mathcal{N}|^{2}\partial_{s}(\mathcal{S}^{-1})).

This implies (
10.22
). Now the relation (
10.22
) shows that

\displaystyle\int_{M}v_{n}dvol_{h}=\frac{1}{n!}\int_{M}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n}(v)dvol_{h}=\frac{1}{n!}\int_{M}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}\left(v{\mathcal{E}}\right)dvol_{h},

where

(10.25)

{\mathcal{E}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}((n-1)\rho-s{\sf J})\mathcal{S}^{-1}+|\mathcal{N}|^{2}\partial_{s}(\mathcal{S}^{-1}).

Hence

\int_{M}v_{n}dvol_{h}=\frac{(n-1)!}{n!}\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-n}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}{\mathcal{E}}dvol_{g}\right)=\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-n}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}{\mathcal{E}}dvol_{g}\right).

Now we apply the functional equation (
10.17
). We assume that
\Re(\lambda)\gg 0
and let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X)
be a cut-off function as in the discussion following (
10.17
). First, the relation

\sigma^{\lambda}=\mathcal{S}^{-1}\frac{1}{(\lambda\!+\!1)(n\!+\!\lambda\!+\!1)}L(\lambda\!+\!1)(\sigma^{\lambda+1})

implies

\displaystyle\int_{X}\chi\sigma^{\lambda}{\mathcal{E}}dvol_{g}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\lambda\!+\!1)(n\!+\!\lambda\!+\!1)}\int_{X}\chi\mathcal{S}^{-1}L(\lambda\!+1\!)(\sigma^{\lambda+1}){\mathcal{E}}dvol_{g}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\lambda\!+\!1)(n\!+\!\lambda\!+\!1)}\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda+1}\tilde{L}(-n\!-\!\lambda\!-\!1)(\chi{\mathcal{E}})dvol_{g}

by partial integration using Proposition
4.9
. We continue to apply this argument and find

\int_{X}\chi\sigma^{\lambda}{\mathcal{E}}dvol_{g}=\frac{1}{(\lambda\!+\!1)_{n-1}(n\!+\!\lambda\!+\!1)_{n-1}}\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda+n-1}\tilde{L}_{n-1}(-n\!-\!\lambda\!-\!1)(\chi{\mathcal{E}})dvol_{g}.

Hence the residue formula (
10.18
) yields

\displaystyle\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-n}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}{\mathcal{E}}dvol_{g}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(-n\!+\!1)\dots(-1)(n\!-\!1)!}\int_{M}\iota^{*}\tilde{L}_{n-1}(-1)(\chi{\mathcal{E}})dvol_{h}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n-1}\frac{1}{(n\!-\!1)!(n\!-\!1)!}\int_{M}\iota^{*}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{N}|}\tilde{L}_{n-1}(-1)({\mathcal{E}})dvol_{h}.

Thus, we find

\int_{M}v_{n}dvol_{h}=(-1)^{n-1}\frac{1}{n!(n-1)!}\int_{M}\iota^{*}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{N}|}\tilde{L}_{n-1}(-1)({\mathcal{E}})dvol_{h}.

This proves the formula for the anomaly using
\dot{L}(0)(1)=(n-1)\rho-s{\sf J}
(see (
10.3
)) and the fact that

\operatorname{grad}(\sigma)
in adapted coordinates equals
|\mathcal{N}|^{2}\partial_{s}
(see (
6.6
)).
∎

Remark 10.14
.

The formulas for the coefficients
c_{k}
in Theorem
10.11
and
Theorem
10.13
are special cases of
[
GW17
, Proposition 3.5]
(for
\tau=1
). Similarly, the formulas
for
{\mathcal{A}}
are special cases of
[
GW17
, Theorem 3.9]
. But note that in
[
GW17
]
there are no
local coefficients
v_{k}
(defined in adapted coordinates). The present proofs differ from those in
[
GW17
]
.
Whereas the above proofs rest on the conjugation formula and a Bernstein-Sato-type argument, the latter
rest on a certain distributional calculus (see also Remark
8.6
). Since the expansion in Theorem

10.13
can be written in the form

\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{n-k}\left\langle\sigma^{*}(\delta^{(k)}),dvol_{g}\right\rangle\varepsilon^{-n+k}-(-1)^{n-1}\left\langle\sigma^{*}(\delta^{(n)}),dvol_{g}\right\rangle\log\varepsilon+V+o(1)

using the formula

(10.26)

\left\langle\sigma^{*}(\delta^{(k)}),dvol_{g}\right\rangle=(-1)^{k}k!\int_{M}v_{k}dvol_{h}

for the currents
\sigma^{*}(\delta^{(k)})
, this proves the equivalence of Theorem
10.13
and

[
GW17
, Theorem 3.1]
combined with
[
GW17
, Proposition 3.5]
(for
\tau=1
).
We continue with a proof of the relation (
10.26
). First, we extend
g
and
\sigma
smoothly
to a sufficiently small neighborhood
\tilde{X}
of
M
as in the discussion after Corollary
8.4
.
Then
\sigma^{*}(\delta^{(k)})
is defined as a continuous functional on smooth volume forms on
\tilde{X}
with
compact support.
17
17

17

Since
\sigma^{*}(\delta)
has compact support, we can pair it with any smooth volume form.

Next, we note the commutation rule

(10.27)

\sigma^{*}(u^{\prime})=\mathfrak{X}_{\sigma}(\sigma^{*}(u)),\;u\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})

where

\mathfrak{X}(\tilde{X})\ni\mathfrak{X}_{\sigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{N}/|\mathcal{N}|^{2},\;\mathcal{N}=\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma).

Hence

(10.28)

\sigma^{*}(u^{(k)})=\mathfrak{X}^{k}_{\sigma}(\sigma^{*}(u))

for
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Now any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(\tilde{X})
defines a current on
\tilde{X}
by

\langle\varphi,\psi dvol_{g}\rangle=\int_{\tilde{X}}\varphi\psi dvol_{g},\;\psi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\tilde{X}).

An extension of (
10.28
) to
u=\delta
yields an analogous relation for currents; it suffices to approximate
\delta
by
test functions in the weak topology. The relation (
10.28
) for
u=\delta
implies

\displaystyle\left\langle\sigma^{*}(\delta^{(k)}),\psi dvol_{g}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathfrak{X}_{\sigma}^{k}(\sigma^{*}(\delta)),\psi dvol_{g}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sigma^{*}(\delta),(\mathfrak{X}_{\sigma}^{*})^{k}(\psi)dvol_{g}\right\rangle,

where the adjoint operator
\mathfrak{X}^{*}_{\sigma}
is defined by

\int_{\tilde{X}}\mathfrak{X}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\psi dvol_{g}=\int_{\tilde{X}}\varphi\mathfrak{X}_{\sigma}^{*}(\psi)dvol_{g},\;\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\tilde{X}).

Now we calculate (using adapted coordinates and partial integration)

\displaystyle\int_{\tilde{X}}\mathfrak{X}^{k}_{\sigma}(\varphi)\psi dvol_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{I\times M}\eta^{*}(\mathfrak{X}_{\sigma}^{k}(\varphi))\eta^{*}(\psi)vdsdvol_{h}

\displaystyle=\int_{I\times M}\partial_{s}^{k}(\eta^{*}(\varphi))\eta^{*}(\psi)vdsdvol_{h}\qquad\mbox{(by \eqref{intertwine})}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}\int_{I\times M}\eta^{*}(\varphi)v^{-1}\partial_{s}^{k}(\eta^{*}(\psi)v)vdsdvol_{h}\qquad\mbox{(by partial integration)}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}\int_{X}\varphi\eta_{*}(v^{-1}\partial_{s}^{k}(v\eta^{*}(\psi)))dvol_{g}.

This shows that
\mathfrak{X}_{\sigma}^{*}(\psi)=\eta_{*}(v^{-1}\partial_{s}^{k}(v\eta^{*}(\psi)))
. Next, we observe that

\left\langle\sigma^{*}(\delta),\psi dvol_{g}\right\rangle=\int_{M}\iota^{*}\frac{\psi}{|\mathcal{N}|}dvol_{h}.

This formula is an extension of
[
Hö83
, Theorem 6.1.5]
(for a flat background).
18
18

18

With appropriate interpretation,
this identity coincides with
[
GW17
, (2.16)]
. The current reference to Hörmander replaces the reference for this result
which has been used in
[
GW17
]
. This reference actually only utilizes formal arguments for domains in flat space
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

By

\iota^{*}(v)=v_{0}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{N}|}
, these results imply

\displaystyle\left\langle\sigma^{*}(\delta^{(k)}),\psi dvol_{g}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}\int_{M}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k}(v\eta^{*}(\psi))dvol_{h}.

In particular, for
\psi=1
we find (
10.26
).

By (
10.21
), the anomaly
{\mathcal{A}}
is proportional to

\int_{M}\iota^{*}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{S}}}\tilde{L}_{n-1}(-1)({\mathcal{E}})dvol_{h}

with
{\mathcal{E}}=\dot{L}(0)(1)\mathcal{S}^{-1}+(d\mathcal{S}^{-1},d\sigma)_{g}
(see (
10.25
)). In the singular Yamabe case,
this integral reduces to

\int_{M}\iota^{*}L_{n-1}(-1)\dot{L}(0)(1)dvol_{h}=\int_{M}\iota^{*}\dot{L}_{n}(0)(1)dvol_{h}=-\int_{M}{\bf Q}_{n}dvol_{h}.

This motivates Gover and Waldron
[
GW17
]
to regard the function

(10.29)

\tilde{{\bf Q}}_{n}(g,\sigma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\iota^{*}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{S}}}\tilde{L}_{n-1}(g,\sigma;-1)({\mathcal{E}})

as a
generalized
critical extrinsic
Q
-curvature. We show that this quantity shares basic properties with

{\bf Q}_{n}
. In this context, we consider conformal changes
(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma})=(e^{2\varphi}g,e^{\varphi}\sigma)

and we let
L=L(g,\sigma)
and
\hat{L}=L(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma})
. Similarly
\hat{\cdot}
also denotes other functionals
of
(g,\sigma)
for such conformal changes. Now differentiating the conformal transformation law

e^{-(\lambda-1)\varphi}\circ\hat{L}(\lambda)=L(\lambda)\circ e^{-\lambda\varphi}

at
\lambda=0
, gives

e^{\varphi}\dot{\hat{L}}(0)(1)=\dot{L}(0)(1)-L(0)(\varphi)

using
L(0)(1)=0
. Hence

e^{\varphi}\hat{{\mathcal{E}}}={\mathcal{E}}-\mathcal{S}^{-1}L(0)(\varphi)+\sigma(d\mathcal{S}^{-1},d\varphi),

and the conformal covariance of
\tilde{L}(\lambda)
implies

\displaystyle e^{n\iota^{*}(\varphi)}\hat{\tilde{{\bf Q}}}_{n}

\displaystyle=\iota^{*}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{S}}}e^{n\varphi}\hat{\tilde{L}}_{n-1}(-1)(\hat{{\mathcal{E}}})=\iota^{*}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{S}}}\tilde{L}_{n-1}(-1)(e^{\varphi}\hat{{\mathcal{E}}})

\displaystyle=\iota^{*}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{S}}}\tilde{L}_{n-1}(-1)({\mathcal{E}}-\mathcal{S}^{-1}L(0)(\varphi)+\sigma(d\mathcal{S}^{-1},d\varphi))

\displaystyle=\tilde{{\bf Q}}_{n}-\iota^{*}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{S}}}\tilde{L}_{n-1}(-1)(\mathcal{S}^{-1}L(0)(\varphi)-\sigma(d\mathcal{S}^{-1},d\varphi)).

This proves the conformal transformation law

(10.30)

e^{n\iota^{*}(\varphi)}\hat{\tilde{{\bf Q}}}_{n}=\tilde{{\bf Q}}_{n}-\iota^{*}\tilde{{\bf P}}_{n}(\varphi)

with

\tilde{{\bf P}}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\mathcal{S}}}\tilde{L}_{n-1}(-1)(\mathcal{S}^{-1}L(0)(\cdot)-\sigma(d\mathcal{S}^{-1},d\cdot)).

Note that the latter operator again is conformally covariant:
e^{n\varphi}\tilde{{\bf P}}_{n}(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma})=\tilde{{\bf P}}_{n}(g,\sigma)
. This immediately follows from the conformal covariance of
L
and the invariance of
\mathcal{S}
.
The following result generalizes Corollary
10.6
and clarifies the content of
[
GW17
, Proposition 3.11]
.

Lemma 10.15
.

For closed
M^{n}
, the integral

\int_{M}\tilde{{\bf Q}}_{n}(g,\sigma)dvol_{h}

is invariant under conformal changes of the pair
(g,\sigma)
.

Proof.

We give two proofs. The first proof uses an argument of
[
G17
]
. Since the integral
of
\tilde{{\bf Q}}_{n}
is proportional to the anomaly
{\mathcal{A}}
in Theorem
10.13
, it suffices to prove that
{\mathcal{A}}(\hat{g},\hat{\sigma})={\mathcal{A}}(g,\sigma)
.
We recall that
\hat{\sigma}^{-2}\hat{g}=\sigma^{-2}g
and prove that the expansion of the difference

\int_{\hat{\sigma}>\varepsilon}dvol_{\sigma^{-2}g}-\int_{\sigma>\varepsilon}dvol_{\sigma^{-2}g}

does not contain a
\log\varepsilon
term. Note that
\{\hat{\sigma}>\varepsilon\}=\{\sigma>\varepsilon e^{-\varphi}\}
. Now, using adapted coordinates, we find that the above difference equals

\displaystyle\int_{\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon e^{-\varphi}}s^{-n-1}\left(\int_{M}v(s)dvol_{h}\right)ds

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\varepsilon^{-n+k}\int_{M}\frac{v_{k}}{n-k}\left(1-e^{(n-k)\varphi}\right)dvol_{h}-\int_{M}\varphi v_{n}dvol_{h}+o(1).

This expansion does not contain a
\log\varepsilon
term.

The second proof rests on the conformal transformation law (
10.30
). It shows that it suffices to prove
that
\int_{M}\iota^{*}\tilde{{\bf P}}_{n}(\varphi)dvol_{h}=0
for all
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(X)
. Now the
generalization
19
19

19

With appropriate interpretations as in Remark
10.14
, the
relation (
10.31
) follows from
[
GW17
, Proposition 3.3]
.

(10.31)

\int_{M}\iota^{*}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{N}|}\tilde{L}_{n-1}(-1)(\psi)dvol_{h}\sim\int_{M}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}(v\psi)dvol_{h}

of

\displaystyle\langle\sigma^{*}(\delta),L_{n-1}(-1)(\psi)dvol_{g}\rangle\sim\int_{M}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}(v\psi)dvol_{h}

(see (
8
)) shows that the assertion is equivalent to

(10.32)

\displaystyle\int_{M}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}\left(v\left[\mathcal{S}^{-1}L(0)(\varphi)-s(d\mathcal{S}^{-1},d\varphi)_{g}\right]\right)dvol_{h}=0.

We recall that
L(0)(\varphi)=(n-1)a\partial_{s}(\varphi)-s\Delta_{g}(\varphi)
with
a=|\mathcal{N}|^{2}
and calculate

\displaystyle\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}\left(vfL(0)(\varphi)\right)

\displaystyle=(n-1)\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}(vfa\varphi^{\prime})-(n-1)\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-2}(vf\Delta_{g}(\varphi))

\displaystyle=(n-1)\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-2}(v^{\prime}fa\varphi^{\prime}+vf^{\prime}a\varphi^{\prime}+vfa^{\prime}\varphi^{\prime}+vfa\varphi^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle-(n-1)\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-2}\left(vf\left(a\varphi^{\prime\prime}+a\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h_{s}^{\prime})\varphi^{\prime}+\frac{1}{2}a^{\prime}\varphi^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}(d\log a,d\varphi)_{h_{s}}+\Delta_{h_{s}}(\varphi)\right)\right)

using (
6.15
). Simplification of that result gives

(n-1)\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-2}\left(vaf^{\prime}\varphi^{\prime}+vf\frac{1}{2}(d\log a,d\varphi)_{h_{s}}-vf\Delta_{h_{s}}(\varphi)\right).

Here we used that
v=a^{-\frac{1}{2}}dvol_{h_{s}}/dvol_{h}=a^{-\frac{1}{2}}\mathring{v}
implies

\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h_{s}^{\prime})=\frac{v^{\prime}}{v}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}

(see (
6.14
)). Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{M}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}\left(vfL(0)(\varphi)\right)dvol_{h}

\displaystyle=(n-1)\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-2}\left(\int_{M}v\left(af^{\prime}\varphi^{\prime}+f\frac{1}{2}(d\log a,d\varphi)_{h_{s}}-f\Delta_{h_{s}}(\varphi)\right)dvol_{h}\right).

Now partial integration on
M
yields

\displaystyle\int_{M}vf\Delta_{h_{s}}(\varphi)dvol_{h}

\displaystyle=\int_{M}a^{-\frac{1}{2}}f\Delta_{h_{s}}(\varphi)dvol_{h_{s}}=-\int_{M}(d(a^{-\frac{1}{2}}f,d\varphi)_{h_{s}}dvol_{h_{s}}

\displaystyle=-\int_{M}va^{\frac{1}{2}}(d(a^{-\frac{1}{2}}f),d\varphi)_{h_{s}}dvol_{h}

\displaystyle=-\int_{M}v(df,d\varphi)_{h_{s}}dvol_{h}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}vf(d\log a,d\varphi)_{h_{s}}dvol_{h}

using
a^{\frac{1}{2}}(d(a^{-\frac{1}{2}}f),d\varphi)_{h_{s}}=-\frac{1}{2}f(d\log a,d\varphi)_{h_{s}}+(df,d\varphi)_{h_{s}}
. Differentiating
this relation by
s
implies

\displaystyle\int_{M}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1}\left(vfL(0)(\varphi)\right)dvol_{h}

\displaystyle=(n-1)\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-2}\left(\int_{M}vaf^{\prime}\varphi^{\prime}+v(df,d\varphi)_{h_{s}}dvol_{h}\right)

\displaystyle=(n-1)\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-2}\left(\int_{M}v(df,d\varphi)_{g}dvol_{h}\right).

For
f=\mathcal{S}^{-1}
, this identity implies the vanishing of (
10.32
).
∎

Note that similar arguments show that

L(-n)(f\sigma^{*}(\delta^{(n-1)}))=L(0)(f)\sigma^{*}(\delta^{(n-1)})

for
f\in C^{\infty}(X)
(see
[
GW17
, Proposition 3.4]
). We omit the details.

Example 10.16
.

A calculation shows that in adapted coordinates

(10.33)

\tilde{{\bf Q}}_{2}(g,\sigma)=\iota^{*}(|\mathcal{N}|^{-5}(\rho^{2}-\rho\mathcal{S}^{\prime}-2(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})^{2})+|\mathcal{N}|^{-3}({\sf J}-\rho^{\prime}+\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime})).

In particular, if
\iota^{*}\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and
\iota^{*}\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime}
vanish (singular Yamabe case), then
{\bf Q}_{2}=\iota^{*}(\rho^{2}-\rho^{\prime}+{\sf J})
. By
\iota^{*}(\rho)=-H
and
\iota^{*}(\rho^{\prime})={\sf P}_{00}+|\mathring{L}|^{2}

(Lemma
13.5.1
), we get
{\bf Q}_{2}=-{\sf P}_{00}-|\mathring{L}|^{2}+\iota^{*}({\sf J})
. Now the Gauss equation

\iota^{*}{\sf J}={\sf J}^{h}+{\sf P}_{00}+\frac{1}{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-H^{2}
implies

{\bf Q}_{2}={\sf J}^{h}-\frac{1}{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}.

This is the known formula for the critical extrinsic
Q
-curvature in dimension
n=2
(Example
13.9.1
).

If
\sigma=d_{M}
is the distance function, then
|\mathcal{N}|=1
and geodesic normal coordinates are adapted coordinates.
Now
\mathcal{S}=1+2s\rho
implies
\iota^{*}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})=2\iota^{*}(\rho)
and
\iota^{*}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime\prime})=4\iota^{*}(\rho^{\prime})
. Thus, we get

\tilde{{\bf Q}}_{2}=\iota^{*}({\sf J}+3\rho^{\prime}-9\rho^{2}).

But
\iota^{*}(\rho)=-\frac{2}{3}H
and
3\iota^{*}(\rho^{\prime})=-\iota^{*}\partial_{s}(\Delta_{g}(s)+s{\sf J})=-\iota^{*}(2H^{\prime}+{\sf J})=\iota^{*}(\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-{\sf J})+|L|^{2}={\sf P}_{00}+|L|^{2}
(using
2H^{\prime}=-|L|^{2}-\operatorname{Ric}_{00}

- see (
13.8
)) show that
\tilde{{\bf Q}}_{2}=\operatorname{Ric}_{00}+|\mathring{L}|^{2}-2H^{2}
. This confirms the formula

-2{\mathcal{A}}=\int_{M}u_{2}dvol_{h}
(with
u_{2}
as in (
13.3.4
)).

Finally, we use similar arguments as above to establish analogous formulas for the integrated renormalized
volume coefficients
w_{k}
, which are defined in terms of geodesic normal coordinates. Let
d_{M}
be the
distance function of
M
.

Theorem 10.17
.

Let
M
be closed. Assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then

\int_{M}w_{k}dvol_{h}=(-1)^{k}\frac{(n\!-\!1\!-\!k)!}{(n\!-\!1)!k!}\int_{M}\iota^{*}L_{k}(g,\sigma;-n\!+\!k)\left(\left(\frac{d_{M}}{\sigma}\right)^{n-k}\right)dvol_{h}

for
0\leq k\leq n-1
and

\int_{M}w_{n}dvol_{h}=\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{(n\!-\!1)!n!}\int_{M}\iota^{*}\dot{L}_{n}(g,\sigma;0)(1)dvol_{h}.

Proof.

We compare two different calculations of the residues of the family

\lambda\mapsto\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}\psi dvol_{g},\;\Re(\lambda)>-1

for appropriate test functions
\psi
. On the one hand, arguments as above using the functional equation
(
10.17
) prove the relation

(10.34)

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-k-1}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}\psi dvol_{g}\right)=\frac{1}{k!(-n\!+\!1)_{k}}\int_{M}\iota^{*}L_{k}(g,\sigma;-n\!+\!k)(\psi)dvol_{h}.

On the other hand, we use geodesic normal coordinates and asymptotic expansions of the resulting
integrand. By
\kappa^{*}(d_{M})=r
and

\kappa^{*}(dvol_{g})=\left(\frac{\kappa^{*}(\sigma)}{r}\right)^{n+1}w(r)drdvol_{h}

(see (
6
)), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}\psi dvol_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{X}d_{M}^{\lambda}\left(\frac{\sigma}{d_{M}}\right)^{\lambda}\psi dvol_{g}

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,\varepsilon)}\int_{M}r^{\lambda}\left(\frac{\kappa^{*}(\sigma)}{r}\right)^{\lambda+n+1}\kappa^{*}(\psi)w(r)drdvol_{h}

for test functions
\psi
with appropriate support. The classical formula (
7.13
)
implies that

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-k-1}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}\psi dvol_{g}\right)=\frac{1}{k!}\int_{M}\left(\left(\frac{\kappa^{*}(\sigma)}{r}\right)^{-k+n}\kappa^{*}(\psi)w(r)\right)^{(k)}(0)dvol_{h}.

For the test function
\psi_{k}=(d_{M}/\sigma)^{n-k}\chi
(with an appropriate
cut-off function
\chi
), the latter result yields

(10.35)

\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=-k-1}\left(\int_{X}\sigma^{\lambda}\psi_{k}dvol_{g}\right)=\int_{M}w_{k}dvol_{h}.

Now comparing (
10.34
) and (
10.35
) proves the first assertion. The assertion in the critical case
follows from
\int_{M}w_{n}dvol_{h}=\int_{M}v_{n}dvol_{h}
(see (
6.11
)) and Theorem
10.11
.
∎

The first part of Theorem
10.17
is a special case of
[
GW17
, Proposition 3.5]
.

11.
Holographic formulæ for extrinsic
Q
-curvatures

We work in adapted coordinates. In particular,
{\sf J}={\sf J}^{g}
is identified with
\eta^{*}({\sf J})
.

The following result is a local version of Theorem
10.9
.

Theorem 11.1
.

Let
n
be even and assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then

(11.1)

{\bf Q}_{n}(g)=(-1)^{n}(n\!-\!1)!^{2}\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{n\!-\!1\!-\!2k}{\mathcal{T}}_{k}^{*}(g;0)\left((n\!-\!1)(n\!-\!k)v_{n-k}+2k(v{\sf J})_{n-k-2}\right).

For
k=n-1
, the second term on the right-hand side is defined as
0
.

The assumption that
n
is even guarantees that the fractions on the right-hand side
are well-defined. For odd
n
, we refer to Conjecture
11.4
.

Remark 11.2
.

Assume that
g_{+}=r^{-2}(dr^{2}+h_{r})
is an even Poincaré-Einstein metric in normal
form relative to
h
and let
g=r^{2}g_{+}=dr^{2}+h_{r}
. Then

{\sf J}^{g}=-\frac{1}{2r}\operatorname{tr}(h_{r}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{r})=-\frac{1}{r}\frac{v^{\prime}}{v}(r)

by
[
J09
, (6.11.8)]
or Lemma
6.1
, and the second term on the right-hand side
of (
11.1
) yields
-2k(n-k)v_{n-k}
. Therefore, the sum simplifies to

\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}(n-k){\mathcal{T}}_{k}^{*}(0)(v_{n-k})

and the assertion reduces to the main result of
[
GJ07
]
by noting that it only
contains contributions for even
k
.

Note that (
11.1
) can be written in the form

{\bf Q}_{n}=(-1)^{n}n!(n\!-\!1)!v_{n}+\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{k}^{*}(0)(\cdots).

Since
{\mathcal{T}}_{k}(0)(1)=0
for
k\geq 1
, integration of this identity (for closed
M
)
reproduces Theorem
10.9
, and the holographic formula provides a formula for the
lower-order terms.

There is a generalization of Theorem
11.1
to subcritical
Q
-curvatures.

Theorem 11.3
.

Let
n
be even and
{\mathbb{N}}\ni N<n
. Assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then

\displaystyle{\bf Q}_{N}(g)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{N}(N\!-\!1)!^{2}\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{(2N\!-\!n\!-\!1\!-\!2k)}

(11.2)

\displaystyle\times{\mathcal{T}}_{k}^{*}\left(g;\frac{n-N}{2}\right)\left((N\!-\!1)(N\!-\!k)v_{N-k}+(n\!-\!N\!+\!2k)(v{\sf J})_{N-k-2}\right).

For
k=N-1
, the second term on the right-hand side is defined as
0
.

Again, the assumption that
n
is even guarantees that the fractions on the right-hand side are well-defined.
For odd
n
, we refer to Conjecture
11.4
.

In the even Poincaré-Einstein case, the formula is non-trivial only for even
N
. In that case,
the solution operators in (
11.3
) act on

(N\!-\!1)(N\!-\!k)v_{N-k}-(n\!-\!N\!+\!2k)(N\!-\!k)v_{N-k}=(2N\!-\!n\!-\!1\!-\!2k)(N\!-\!k)v_{N-k}

and the sum simplifies to

\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{k}^{*}\left(g;\frac{n-N}{2}\right)(N-k)v_{N-k}.

Here only terms with even
k
contribute. Thus the formula reduces to the main result of
[
J11
]
.

The following conjecture implies that for odd
n
the respective terms with the singular
fractions in the sums (
11.1
) and (
11.3
) do not contribute.

Conjecture 11.4
.

For odd
n\geq 3
, it holds

(11.3)

\tfrac{n+1}{2}v_{\frac{n+1}{2}}+(v{\sf J})_{\frac{n-3}{2}}=0.

Moreover, the holographic formulas (
11.1
) and (
11.3
)
are valid also for odd
n
.

Remark 11.5
.

For
n=3
and
n=5
, the respective relations (
11.3
) read

\displaystyle 2v_{2}+{\sf J}_{0}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle 3v_{3}+{\sf J}^{\prime}_{0}+v_{1}{\sf J}_{0}

\displaystyle=0.

For details, we refer to the discussion in Examples
13.4.2
–
13.4.4
. These identities are
consequences of the relation (
6.18
). More generally, the relation (
6.18
) implies that any
coefficient
v_{N}
can be written as a linear combination of products of derivatives of
\rho
and

{\sf J}
at
s=0
. The calculations of the resulting formulas for odd
n\leq 11
confirm the relation
(
11.3
) in these special cases. For the discussion of the holographic formula for
{\bf Q}_{3}
in
general dimensions, we refer to Example
13.9.2
.

Now we present the proof of Theorem
11.1
.

Proof.

We evaluate the quantity
\dot{{\bf D}}_{n}^{res}(0)(1)
using the factorization formula

{\bf D}_{n}^{res}(\lambda)={\bf D}_{n-1}^{res}(\lambda-1)L(\lambda)

(Corollary
8.2
). In view of
L(0)(1)=0
, it follows that

(11.4)

\dot{{\bf D}}_{n}^{res}(0)(1)={\bf D}_{n-1}^{res}(-1)\dot{L}(0)(1).

Now we apply the representation formula

{\bf D}_{n-1}^{res}(-1)=(-1)^{n-1}(n\!-\!1)!^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{(n\!-\!1\!-\!j)!}\left[{\mathcal{T}}_{j}^{*}(0)v_{0}+\cdots+{\mathcal{T}}_{0}^{*}(0)v_{j}\right]\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1-j}

(Theorem
7.4
). We find

\dot{{\bf D}}_{n}^{res}(0)(1)=(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{(n\!-\!1\!-\!j)!}\left[{\mathcal{T}}_{j}^{*}(0)v_{0}+\cdots+{\mathcal{T}}_{0}^{*}(0)v_{j}\right]\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1-j}(\dot{L}(0)(1)).

In the latter sum, the operator
{\mathcal{T}}_{k}^{*}(0)
acts on the sum

\left(v_{0}\frac{1}{(n\!-\!1\!-\!k)!}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1-k}+\cdots+v_{n-1-k}\iota^{*}\right)(\dot{L}(0)(1)).

But this quantity equals the
(n\!-\!1\!-\!k)
’th
Taylor coefficient
(v\dot{L}(0)(1))_{n-1-k}
of
v\dot{L}(0)(1)
. Now the
identity

(11.5)

\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k}(v\dot{L}(0)(1))=-\frac{n\!-\!1}{(n\!-\!1\!-\!2k)}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k+1}(v)-\frac{2k(n\!-\!1\!-\!k)}{(n\!-\!1\!-\!2k)}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k-1}(v{\sf J})

for
0\leq k\leq n-1
(which extends (
10.13
))
20
20

20

For
k=0
, the second term on the
right-hand side is defined as
0
.
yields

\displaystyle(v\dot{L}(0)(1))_{n-1-k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(n\!-\!1\!-\!k)!}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-1-k}(v\dot{L}(0)(1))

\displaystyle=\frac{n\!-\!1}{(n\!-\!1\!-\!2k)(n\!-\!1\!-\!k)!}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-k}(v)+\frac{2k(n\!-\!1\!-\!k)}{(n\!-\!1\!-\!2k)(n\!-\!1\!-\!k)!}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{n-2-k}(v{\sf J})

\displaystyle=\frac{(n\!-\!1)(n\!-\!k)}{(n\!-\!1\!-\!2k)}v_{n-k}+\frac{2k}{(n\!-\!1\!-\!2k)}(v{\sf J})_{n-2-k}

for
0\leq k\leq n-1
. This implies the claim. It only remains to prove the identity (
11.5
).
Note that
\dot{L}(0)(1)=(n-1)\rho-s{\sf J}
. Using the definition of
\rho
, we obtain

(11.6)

\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k}(v\dot{L}(0)(1))=-\frac{n-1}{n+1}\left(\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k}(v\Delta(s))+\frac{2kn}{n-1}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k-1}(v{\sf J})\right).

Now the relation (
6.17
) implies

\displaystyle\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k}(v\Delta(s))

\displaystyle=\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k+1}(v|\mathcal{N}|^{2})

\displaystyle=\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k+1}(v)-2(k+1)\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k}(v\rho)

\displaystyle=\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k+1}(v)+\frac{2(k+1)}{n+1}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k}(v\Delta(s)+sv{\sf J})

for
0\leq k\leq n-1
using
|\mathcal{N}|^{2}=1-2s\rho+O(s^{n+1})
. We rewrite this identity in the form

\frac{n-2k-1}{n+1}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k}(v\Delta(s))=\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k+1}(v)+\frac{2k(k+1)}{n+1}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k-1}(v{\sf J}).

By substituting this result into (
11.6
), we obtain (
11.5
). Note
that the above arguments extend those in the proof of Theorem
10.9
.
∎

Finally, we sketch a
proof of Theorem
11.3
. For
N<n
, we have

{\bf D}_{N}^{res}\left(\frac{-n+N}{2}\right)(1)=\left(\frac{n-N}{2}\right){\bf Q}_{N}

by (
10.7
). Hence

\displaystyle{\bf Q}_{N}

\displaystyle={\bf D}_{N}^{res}\left(\frac{-n+N}{2}\right)(1)\left(\frac{n-N}{2}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle={\bf D}_{N-1}^{res}\left(\frac{-n+N}{2}-1\right)L\left(\frac{-n+N}{2}\right)(1)\left(\frac{n-N}{2}\right)^{-1}

(by Corollary
8.2
)

\displaystyle={\bf D}_{N-1}^{res}\left(\frac{-n+N}{2}-1\right)(-(N-1)\rho+s{\sf J})

\displaystyle\mbox{(by \eqref{aa})}.

This formula generalizes (
11.4
). Now we proceed as in the proof of
Theorem
10.9
. In particular, Theorem
7.4
and the above identity imply
that

\displaystyle{\bf Q}_{N}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{N}(N\!-\!1)!^{2}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{j!}\left[{\mathcal{T}}_{N-1-j}^{*}\left(\frac{n\!-\!N}{2}\right)v_{0}+\cdots+{\mathcal{T}}_{0}^{*}\left(\frac{n\!-\!N}{2}\right)v_{j}\right]\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{j}((N\!-\!1)\rho-s{\sf J}).

But in the latter double sum the operator
{\mathcal{T}}_{k}^{*}\left(\frac{n-N}{2}\right)
acts on
the
(N-1-k)
’th Taylor coefficient of
((N-1)\rho-s{\sf J})v
. A calculation using the definition of
\rho

yields the extension

(11.7)

\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k}(v((N\!-\!1)\rho-s{\sf J}))=-\frac{N\!-\!1}{(n\!-\!1\!-\!2k)}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k+1}(v)-\frac{k(n\!-\!2k\!+\!N\!-\!2)}{(n\!-\!1\!-\!2k)}\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k-1}(v{\sf J})

of (
11.5
) for
k=0,\dots,N-1
.
21
21

21

For
k=0
, the second term on the right-hand side
is defined as
0
.
Hence

(v((N\!-\!1)\rho-s{\sf J}))_{N-1-k}=-\frac{(N\!-\!1)(N\!-\!k)}{(n\!-\!2N\!+\!2k\!+\!1)}v_{N-k}-\frac{n\!-\!N\!+\!2k}{(n\!-\!2N\!+\!2k\!+\!1)}(v{\sf J})_{N-2-k}

for
k=0,\dots,N-1
. This implies the assertion.
\square

We finish this section with an application to the singular Yamabe obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}

(see Section
3
). We recall that our discussion of extrinsic conformal Laplacians

{\bf P}_{N}
and extrinsic
Q
-curvatures
{\bf Q}_{N}
is restricted to the range
N\leq n
.
Already the first super-critical
{\bf Q}
-curvature
{\bf Q}_{n+1}
is not well-defined.
Calculations in low-order cases point to the origin of its non-existence:
{\bf Q}_{N}

has a pole in
n=N-1
. The following result interprets its residue.

Theorem 11.6
.

Let
n
be even and
N\geq 3
. Then it holds

(11.8)

\operatorname{Res}_{n=N-1}({\bf Q}_{N})=(-1)^{n-1}n!(n\!+\!1)!\frac{n}{2}{\mathcal{B}}_{n}.

Proof.

Assume that
N\leq n
. Theorem
11.1
and
Theorem
11.3
imply that

{\bf Q}_{N}=(-1)^{N}(N\!-\!1)!N!\frac{N\!-\!1}{2N\!-\!n\!-\!1}v_{N}+\cdots,

where the hidden terms are regular at
n=N-1
. However, the term
v_{N}
has a
simple pole at
n=N-1
. More precisely, it follows from Corollary
6.2
that

v_{N}=-(n\!+\!1\!-\!2N)\frac{1}{N!}\partial^{N-1}_{s}(\rho)|_{0}+\cdots,

where the hidden terms are regular at
n=N-1
, and Proposition
6.4
explains the origin
of the pole of
\partial^{N-1}_{s}(\rho)|_{0}
. But a comparison of Proposition
6.4
and
Theorem
6.6
shows that
22
22

22

See also
[
GW21
, Proposition 6.4]
.

(11.9)

{\mathcal{B}}_{n}=\frac{2}{(n+1)!}\operatorname{Res}_{n=N-1}(\partial_{s}^{N-1}(\rho))|_{0}.

The result follows from these facts.
∎

For a detailed discussion of the relation between
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
and
{\bf Q}_{3}
, we refer to Section
13.9
.

Combining Theorem
11.6
with
\operatorname{Res}_{n=N-1}({\bf P}_{N})\sim\operatorname{Res}_{n=N-1}({\bf Q}_{N})
allows us to derive the conformal invariance
e^{(n+1)\iota^{*}(\varphi)}\hat{{\mathcal{B}}}_{n}={\mathcal{B}}_{n}
of the obstruction from the conformal covariance of
{\bf P}_{N}
.

The above observations resemble the result that the residues

\operatorname{Res}_{n=4}(P_{6})=-16(\delta({\mathcal{B}}d)-({\mathcal{B}},{\sf P}))

and

\operatorname{Res}_{n=6}(P_{8})=-48(\delta({\mathcal{O}}_{6}d)-({\mathcal{O}}_{6},{\sf P}))

of super-critical GJMS-operators
P_{6}
and
P_{8}
are conformally covariant. Here

{\mathcal{B}}
is the Bach tensor, and
{\mathcal{O}}_{6}
is the Fefferman-Graham obstruction tensor in
dimension
n=6
. For more details, we refer to
[
J13
, Section 11.3]
.

12.
Comments on further developments

We recall that
{\bf P}_{n}\iota^{*}={\bf D}_{n}^{res}(0)
. For even
n
, this critical extrinsic conformal Laplacian
is elliptic. For odd
n
, the leading part of this operator is determined by
\mathring{L}
. The lower-order terms are
not known in general. For
n=3
, the explicit formula in Proposition
13.10.1
shows that
{\bf P}_{3}
vanishes iff

\mathring{L}=0
, i.e., iff
M
is totally umbilic. The vanishing of
{\bf P}_{n}
is a conformally invariant condition. Now
if
{\bf P}_{n}(g)=0
, we define

(12.1)

{\bf P}_{n}^{\prime}(g)=\dot{{\bf D}}_{n}^{res}(g;0)\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\bf Q}_{n}^{\prime}(g)=\frac{1}{2}\ddot{{\bf D}}_{n}^{res}(g;0).

Then
{\bf P}_{n}^{\prime}:C^{\infty}(X)\to C^{\infty}(M)
is a conformally covariant operator

e^{n\iota^{*}(\varphi)}{\bf P}_{n}^{\prime}(\hat{g})={\bf P}_{n}^{\prime}(g),\;\varphi\in C^{\infty}(X)

such that the pair

({\bf P}_{n}^{\prime},{\bf Q}_{n}^{\prime})

satisfies the fundamental identity

(12.2)

e^{n\iota^{*}(\varphi)}{\bf Q}_{n}^{\prime}(\hat{g})={\bf Q}_{n}^{\prime}(g)-{\bf P}_{n}^{\prime}(g)(\varphi)+\iota^{*}(\varphi){\bf P}_{n}^{\prime}(g)(1)

for all
\varphi\in C^{\infty}(X)
. This identity follows by twice differentiation of the conformal
transformation law of the critical residue family
{\bf D}_{n}^{res}(\lambda)
at
\lambda=0
. Note that

{\bf P}_{n}^{\prime}(g)(1)=-{\bf Q}_{n}(g)
. It is interesting
to analyze this construction further. First of all, it is a question of independent interest to characterize
the vanishing of
{\bf P}_{n}(g)
for odd
n
. Is it true that
{\bf Q}_{n}(g)(1)=0
? The operator
{\bf P}_{n}
may be regarded
as a boundary operator for a conformally covariant boundary value problem for the critical GJMS-operator

P_{n+1}
on
X
. For
n=3
, such boundary value problems were recently analyzed in
[
C18
]
. A
conformally covariant boundary operator of third order together with a
Q
-curvature like scalar curvature quantity
was discovered in
[
CQ97
]
in connection with the study of Polyakov formulas on four-dimensional manifolds with
boundary. Later it was studied from various perspectives. For details, we refer to
[
G03
,
J09
,
C18
,
GP18
]
and
the references in these works.

It would be interesting to develop an extension of the present theory in higher
codimension situations again using solutions of singular Yamabe problems. We briefly
describe some aspects of a special case of such a theory. Let
S^{m}\hookrightarrow S^{n}
be an equatorial subsphere of
S^{n}
,
m\leq n-1
. It is well-known that the
complement of
S^{m}
in
S^{n}
with the round metric
g
is conformally equivalent to
the product
\mathbb{H}^{m+1}\times S^{n-m-1}
with the respective hyperbolic and
round metric of constant scalar curvature
-(m+1)(m+2)
and
(n-m-1)(n-m)
on the
factors. The conformal factor
\sigma\in C^{\infty}(S^{n}\setminus S^{m})
can be defined
in terms of a Knapp-Stein intertwining operator on
S^{n}
applied to the
delta-distribution of
S^{m}

[
J09
, Section 2.4]
. More explicitly, assume that

S^{m}
is defined by the equations
x_{m+2}=\cdots=x_{n+1}=0
. Then
\sigma
is
the restriction of
(\sum_{j=m+2}^{n+1}x_{j}^{2})^{1/2}
to
S^{n}
. A stereographic
projection yields an isometry of
\sigma^{-2}g
and

(x_{m+1}^{2}+\cdots+x_{n}^{2})^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}dx_{i}^{2}=r^{-2}\sum_{i=1}^{m+1}dx_{i}^{2}+g_{S^{n-m-1}},

where
r^{2}=\sum_{i=m+1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}
. The above
\sigma
is a generalization of the
height function
\mathcal{H}
in Section
5
(the case
m=n-1
). Now, for any
eigenfunction
\tau
of the Laplacian on
S^{n-m-1}
(spherical harmonics), there is
an intertwining operator

D_{N,\tau}(\lambda):C^{\infty}(S^{n})\to C^{\infty}(S^{m})

for the subgroup
SO(1,m+1)\subset SO(1,n+1)
leaving
S^{m}
invariant (symmetry breaking operator).
These families can be constructed in terms of the residues of the family

\lambda\mapsto\int_{S^{n}}\sigma^{\lambda}u\psi dvol_{S^{n}},

where
u
is an eigenfunction of
\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}
on the complement of
S^{m}

which is compatible with
\tau
. The conformal factor
\sigma
is a solution of the
singular Yamabe problem on the complement of
S^{m}
. In fact, the scalar curvature of

\sigma^{-2}g
is
-(n+1)(2m-n+2)
. It is negative iff
m>(n-2)/2
, i.e., iff the
dimension of the hyperbolic space exceeds the dimension of the sphere. This is a
special case of
[
LN74
]
, where it is proved that if
M\subset S^{n}
is a smooth
submanifold of dimension
m
, a solution of the singular Yamabe problem with
negative scalar curvature exists iff
m>(n-2)/2
. The analogous result for
S^{n}

replaced by an arbitrary
X
is in
[
AM88
]
. For a description of the asymptotic
expansions of solutions of the singular Yamabe problem in the negative case, we refer
to
[
M91
]
. Related representation theoretical aspects of the case
S^{m}\hookrightarrow S^{n}
are studied in
[
MO15
]
. Here the spectral decomposition of
spherical principal series representations of
O(1,n+1)
under restriction to

O(1,m+1)\times O(n-m)
is made fully explicit.

The conformal invariance of the integral

\int_{M}{\bf Q}_{n}(g)dvol_{h}

for closed
M
(see (
1.16
)) generalizes the conformal invariance of the total Branson

Q
-curvature

\int_{M}Q_{n}(h)dvol_{h}.

The classification of scalar Riemannian curvature quantities of a manifold
(M,h)

which - like
Q_{n}
- upon integration give rise to a conformal invariant has been the subject
of the Deser-Schwimmer conjecture
[
DS93
]
. S. Alexakis has achieved this classification in a series of
works (see
[
A12
]
and its references). The present context suggests asking
for an analogous classification of scalar Riemannian invariants of a manifold
(X,g)
which, upon
integration over closed submanifolds, yield conformal invariants of
g
. For first results in that direction,
we refer to
[
MN18
]
. For related results around
{\bf Q}_{4}
, we refer to
[
J21
]
and
[
BGW21
]
.

13.
Calculations and further results

In the present section, we collect formulas and computational details used in the
main body of the text. In addition, we illustrate various aspects of the general theory
by low-order examples. This often gives additional insight into the situation’s nature and
complexity. The results may also serve as material for future research. Moreover, we
add a few further results.

For the reader’s convenience, we start with an outline of the content of this section.

In Section
13.1
, we recall basic forms of the hypersurface Gauss equations,
recall the transformation laws of the second fundamental form and of some derived constructions
under conformal changes of the metric, derive a basic formula for the Laplacian, which plays a
key role in the proof of the conjugation formula and clarify the relation between
high-order iterated normal derivatives
\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{k}
and their analogs in adapted coordinates.

In Section
13.2
, we determine the first three terms in the expansion of a
general metric
g
in geodesic normal and adapted coordinates. These results are
fundamental for the later calculations. We also illustrate the results in several
model cases with constant curvature and vanishing trace-free
part of
L
. These model cases may serve as valuable test examples of identities of the general theory.

In Section
13.3
, we first determine the first four terms in the asymptotic expansion of
solutions of the singular Yamabe problem in geodesic normal coordinates. Then, we use these results to
calculate the obstructions
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
in these terms. Finally, we prove a formula for the obstructions
in terms of a formal residue of the super-critical coefficient
\sigma_{(n+2)}
. The explicit
form of the first few coefficients
\sigma_{(k)}
enables us to confirm this formula in low-order cases.

In Section
13.4
, we derive explicit formulas for the first three renormalized volume
coefficients
v_{k}
(in adapted coordinates).

In Section
13.5
, we derive explicit formulas for the first two normal derivatives of
\rho
.
The discussion illustrates the efficiency of the recursive relation for the Taylor coefficients of
\rho

expressed in Proposition
6.4
. We further use these results in Section
13.9
for a
detailed discussion of
{\bf Q}_{2}
and
{\bf Q}_{3}
.

In Section
13.6
, we show that the well-known formula for the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}

naturally follows from Theorem
6
. Moreover, in Section
13.7
, we evaluate
the special case
n=3
of Theorem
6
for a flat background metric. We find that the
formula coincides with the one derived in Section

13.3
as well with a formula of Gover and Waldron. In addition, we verify that in the
conformally flat case, the result fits with the formula for
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
established in
[
GGHW19
]
.

In Section
13.8
, we illustrate the role of the obstruction in the variational formula for
the singular Yamabe energy
[
G17
]
,
[
GW17
]
in low-order cases. In
[
GW17
]

and
[
GGHW19
]
, the authors developed a new variational calculus. In contrast
to these references, here we only use classical style arguments as in
[
W93
,
CM11
,
HP99
,
G17
]
,
for instance.

In Section
13.10
, we provide direct proofs of the conformal covariance of
{\bf P}_{3}

and the fundamental conformal transformation law of
{\bf Q}_{3}
.

Section
13.11
is devoted to a derivation of explicit formulas for the first two
solution operators
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\lambda)
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\lambda)
and the resulting first two
residue families
{\bf D}_{1}^{res}(\lambda)
and
{\bf D}_{2}^{res}(\lambda)
. In addition, we
determine the leading term of
{\bf P}_{3}
through the leading term of the third solution
operator
{\mathcal{T}}_{3}(\lambda)
.

In the last section, we describe low-order renormalized volume coefficients
w_{k}
in terms of
Laplace-Robin operators. These results imply low-order cases of Theorem
10.17
.

Throughout this section, we apply some additional conventions. We use
indices
i,j
for tensorial objects on
M
and
0
for
\mathcal{N}
when viewed as a normal
vector of
M
. In particular,
h_{ij}
are the components of the metric
h
on the boundary
and
{\sf P}_{00}={\sf P}_{X}(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{N})
is the restriction to
M
of the Schouten tensor of

g
for the normal vector
\mathcal{N}
. Similar conventions are used in adapted coordinates.

Sometimes, it will be convenient to distinguish curvature quantities of
g
and
h
not
by superscripts but by adding a bar to those of
g
and leaving those of
h
unbared.
Then
\bar{R}
,
\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}
and
\overline{\operatorname{scal}}
are the curvature tensor, the Ricci
tensor and the scalar curvature of
g
, respectively. For instance,
\operatorname{Ric}^{g}(\mathcal{N},\cdot)

and
\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{0}
are the same
1
-forms on
M
.

As before, we often use the same notation for a function on
X
and its pull-back
by
\kappa
or
\eta
without mentioning it. A prime denotes derivatives in the variable
r
and
s
.
The restriction of a function
f(s)
to
s=0
is also denoted by
f_{0}
.
This notation often replaces
\iota^{*}(f)
. For instance, we write
\rho^{\prime}_{0}
for the restriction

\iota^{*}(\partial_{s}(\rho))
of
\partial_{s}(\rho)
to
s=0
. If confusion is excluded, we
sometimes even omit the symbols indicating restriction.

13.1.
Some basic identities

13.1.1.
Gauss equations

Let
M^{n}
be a hypersurface in
(X^{n+1},g)
with the induced metric
h=\iota^{*}(g)
.
Then it holds

(13.1.1)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ric}^{g}_{ij}-\operatorname{Ric}^{h}_{ij}

\displaystyle=R^{g}_{0ij0}+L^{2}_{ij}-nHL_{ij},

(13.1.2)

\displaystyle\operatorname{scal}^{g}-\operatorname{scal}^{h}

\displaystyle=2\operatorname{Ric}^{g}_{00}+|L|^{2}-n^{2}H^{2}

on
M
. In the bar-notation, (
13.1.2
) reads

\iota^{*}\overline{\operatorname{scal}}-\operatorname{scal}=2\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00}+|L|^{2}-n^{2}H^{2}.

The following result follows from the Gauss equation (
13.1.2
).

Lemma 13.1.1
.

For
n\geq 2
, it holds

(13.1.3)

\iota^{*}{\sf J}^{g}-{\sf J}^{h}={\sf P}^{g}_{00}+\frac{1}{2(n\!-\!1)}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-\frac{n}{2}H^{2}.

Proof.

The Gauss equation (
13.1.2
) is equivalent to

2n\iota^{*}{\sf J}^{g}-2(n\!-\!1){\sf J}^{h}=2((n\!-\!1){\sf P}^{g}_{00}+{\sf J}^{g})+|L|^{2}-n^{2}H^{2}.

Hence

2(n\!-\!1)\iota^{*}{\sf J}^{g}-2(n\!-\!1){\sf J}^{h}=2(n\!-\!1){\sf P}^{g}_{00}+|\mathring{L}|^{2}-n(n-1)H^{2}.

This implies the assertion.
∎

Next, let

(13.1.4)

\mathcal{F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\iota^{*}({\sf P}^{g})-{\sf P}^{h}+H\mathring{L}+\frac{1}{2}H^{2}h.

\mathcal{F}
is a conformally invariant symmetric bilinear form, i.e., it holds

\hat{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{F}
(
[
J09
, Theorem 6.22.11]
.
23
23

23

In
[
GW21
,
GW17
]
, the tensor

\mathcal{F}
is called the Fialkow tensor following
[
V13
]
(in turn being inspired by
[
J09
]
). For more details on
the relation to Fialkow’s classical work, we refer to
[
J09
, Section 6.23]
.
Moreover, it satisfies the
fundamental relation

\displaystyle(n\!-\!2)\mathcal{F}_{ij}

\displaystyle=(n\!-\!2)\left(\iota^{*}{\sf P}^{g}_{ij}-{\sf P}^{h}_{ij}+H\mathring{L}_{ij}+\frac{1}{2}H^{2}h_{ij}\right)

(13.1.5)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}W_{0ij0}+\mathring{L}^{2}_{ij}-\frac{|\mathring{L}|^{2}}{2(n\!-\!1)}h_{ij}

(
[
J09
, Lemma 6.23.3]
), where
W
is the Weyl tensor of
g
. We recall that

(13.1.6)

R=W-{\sf P}\owedge g,

where the Kulkarni-Nomizu product of the bilinear forms
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
is defined by

\displaystyle(b_{1}\owedge b_{2})(X,Y,Z,W)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}b_{1}(X,Z)b_{2}(Y,W)-b_{1}(Y,Z)b_{2}(X,W)+b_{1}(Y,W)b_{2}(X,Z)-b_{1}(X,W)b_{2}(Y.Z).

13.1.2.
Conformal change and the second fundamental form

Let
N
be a fixed unit normal field of
M
defining
L
. Let
\hat{L}
denote the second fundamental form
of
M
with respect to the metric
\hat{g}=e^{2\varphi}g
. Then it holds

e^{-\varphi}\hat{L}=L+\nabla_{N}(\varphi)h.

As a consequence, we find

e^{\varphi}\hat{H}=H+\nabla_{n}(\varphi).

Both relations combine into the conformal invariance property

(13.1.7)

e^{-\varphi}\hat{\mathring{L}}=\mathring{L}

of the trace-free part
\mathring{L}=L-Hh
of
L
. It follows that
\hat{\mathring{L}}^{2}=\mathring{L}^{2}
, where

(\mathring{L}^{2})_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}h^{ab}\mathring{L}_{ia}\mathring{L}_{jb}
. For
|L|^{2}=\operatorname{tr}_{h}(L^{2})=h^{ij}(L^{2})_{ij}=h^{ij}h^{ab}L_{ia}L_{jb}
, we find

e^{2\varphi}|\hat{\mathring{L}}|^{2}=|\mathring{L}|^{2}
.
24
24

24

Of course, the norm on the left-hand side is taken with respect to
\hat{h}
.

Hence

(13.1.8)

(\hat{\mathring{L}}^{2})_{\circ}=(\mathring{L}^{2})_{\circ}.

13.1.3.
Some formulas for the Laplacian

Here we discuss some useful formulas for the Laplacian. In particular, we prove the identity
(
4.5
).

Lemma 13.1.2
.

Let
\sigma
be a defining function of
M
and
\partial_{0}=\mathcal{N}=\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)
.
We assume that
|\partial_{0}|_{g}=1
on
M
. Then it holds

(13.1.9)

\Delta_{g}(u)=\partial_{0}^{2}(u)+\Delta_{h}(u)+nH\partial_{0}(u)-\langle du,\nabla_{\partial_{0}}(\partial_{0})\rangle

on
M
.

Proof.

Let
\left\{\partial_{i}\right\}
be an orthonormal basis of the tangent spaces of
the level surfaces
\sigma^{-1}(c)
(for small
c
) and let
\partial_{0}=\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)
. By assumption, these form an orthonormal basis on
M
. Let

\left\{dx^{i}\right\}
together with
\left\{dx^{0}\right\}
be the dual basis. We
calculate
\iota^{*}\Delta_{g}(f)
using
\Delta=\operatorname{tr}(\nabla^{2})
. First of all, we
have
\nabla(u)=\partial_{i}(u)dx^{i}+\partial_{0}(u)dx^{0}
. Hence
25
25

25

As
usual, we sum over repeated indices.

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}(u)

\displaystyle=\partial_{ij}(u)dx^{i}\otimes dx^{j}+\partial_{i}(u)\nabla_{\partial_{j}}(dx^{i})\otimes dx^{j}

\displaystyle+\partial_{i0}(u)dx^{i}\otimes dx^{0}+\partial_{i}(u)\nabla_{\partial_{0}}(dx^{i})\otimes dx^{0}

\displaystyle+\partial_{0j}(u)dx^{0}\otimes dx^{j}+\partial_{0}(u)\nabla_{\partial_{j}}(dx^{0})\otimes dx^{j}

\displaystyle+\partial_{00}(u)dx^{0}\otimes dx^{0}+\partial_{0}(u)\nabla_{\partial_{0}}(dx^{0})\otimes dx^{0}.

Now taking traces gives

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}(\nabla^{2})(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\partial_{ii}(u)+\partial_{i}(u)\langle\nabla_{\partial_{j}}(dx^{i}),\partial_{j}\rangle+\partial_{i}(u)\langle\nabla_{\partial_{0}}(dx^{i}),\partial_{0}\rangle+\partial_{0}(u)\langle\nabla_{\partial_{j}}(dx^{0}),\partial_{j}\rangle

\displaystyle+\partial_{0}^{2}(u)+\partial_{0}(u)\langle\nabla_{\partial_{0}}(dx^{0}),\partial_{0}\rangle

on
M
. Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}(\nabla^{2})(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\partial_{ii}(u)+\partial_{i}(u)\langle\nabla_{\partial_{j}}(dx^{i}),\partial_{j}\rangle-\partial_{i}(u)\langle dx^{i},\nabla_{\partial_{0}}(\partial_{0})\rangle-\partial_{0}(u)\langle dx^{0},\nabla_{\partial_{j}}(\partial_{j})\rangle

\displaystyle+\partial_{0}^{2}(u)-\partial_{0}(u)\langle dx^{0},\nabla_{\partial_{0}}(\partial_{0})\rangle.

But since
\nabla^{g}_{\partial_{i}}(\partial_{j})=\nabla^{h}_{\partial_{i}}(\partial_{j})
on

M
, the last display simplifies to

\displaystyle\Delta_{g}(u)

\displaystyle=\partial_{0}^{2}(u)+\Delta_{h}(u)-\partial_{0}(u)\langle dx^{0},\nabla_{\partial_{j}}(\partial_{j})\rangle

\displaystyle-\partial_{i}(u)\langle dx^{i},\nabla_{\partial_{0}}(\partial_{0})\rangle-\partial_{0}(u)\langle dx^{0},\nabla_{\partial_{0}}(\partial_{0})\rangle

on
M
. By
L(\partial_{i},\partial_{j})=-\langle\nabla_{\partial_{i}}(\partial_{j}),dx^{0}\rangle
, we obtain

\Delta_{g}(u)=\partial_{0}^{2}(u)+\Delta_{h}(u)+nH\partial_{0}(u)-\langle du,\nabla_{\partial_{0}}(\partial_{0})\rangle

on
M
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 13.1.3
.

Without the assumption that
|\partial_{0}|=1
on
M
, an extension
of the above arguments shows that

\Delta_{g}(u)=\frac{1}{|\partial_{0}|^{2}}\partial_{0}^{2}(u)+\Delta_{h}(u)+nH\frac{1}{|\partial_{0}|}\partial_{0}(u)-\frac{1}{|\partial_{0}|^{2}}\langle du,\nabla_{\partial_{0}}(\partial_{0})\rangle

on
M
. Similar arguments prove (
4.5
).

Remark 13.1.4
.

In the situation of Lemma
13.1.2
, assume that
\sigma
is the distance function of
M
. Then

\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathcal{N})=0
and we recover the well-known formula

\Delta_{g}=\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}+\Delta_{h}+nH\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}

on
M
.

The following result reproves
[
GW21
, Lemma B2]
.

Corollary 13.1.5
.

If
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
, then

(13.1.10)

\Delta_{g}=\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}+\Delta_{h}+(n-1)H\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}

on
M
.

Proof.

By Lemma
13.1.2
, it suffices to prove that
\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathcal{N})=H\mathcal{N}
on
M
.
For any
X\in\mathfrak{X}(X)
, we calculate

\langle\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\mathcal{N}),X\rangle=\operatorname{Hess}_{g}(\sigma)(\mathcal{N},X)=\operatorname{Hess}_{g}(\sigma)(X,\mathcal{N})=\langle\nabla_{X}(\mathcal{N}),\mathcal{N}\rangle=1/2\langle d(|\mathcal{N}|^{2}),X\rangle.

Now the assertion follows from
{(\mathcal{SY})}
using
\rho=-H
on
M
(Lemma
13.5.1
).
∎

13.1.4.
Iterated normal derivatives

The identity

\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k}\circ\eta^{*}=\iota^{*}(|\mathcal{N}|^{-2}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}})^{k}

(see (
6.7
)) relates iterated normal derivatives
\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{k}\circ\eta^{*}
with respect to
s
to iterated weighted gradients
\iota^{*}(|\mathcal{N}|^{-2}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}})^{k}
of
\sigma
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)=1
, i.e., if

|\mathcal{N}|^{2}=1-2\sigma\rho
, any iterated weighted gradient
\iota^{*}(|\mathcal{N}|^{-2}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}})^{k}
can be written as a composition with
\iota^{*}
of a linear
combination of iterated gradients
\nabla^{j}_{\mathcal{N}}
for
j\leq k
and polynomials in
the curvature quantities
\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{j}(\rho)\in C^{\infty}(M)
for
j\leq k-1
. This follows by an easy induction using
\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\sigma)=|\mathcal{N}|^{2}
. In
particular, we obtain the following low-order formulas.

Example 13.1.6
.

If
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)=1
, then it holds

\iota^{*}\partial_{s}\eta^{*}=\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{2}\eta^{*}=\iota^{*}(\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}+2\rho\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}).

Proof.

We calculate

\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{2}\eta^{*}=\iota^{*}(|\mathcal{N}|^{-2}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}})^{2}=\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(1+2\sigma\rho)\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}=\iota^{*}(\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}+2\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\sigma)\rho\nabla_{\mathcal{N}})

using
|\mathcal{N}|^{2}=1-2\sigma\rho
. Now
\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\sigma)=\iota^{*}(|\mathcal{N}|^{2})=1

implies the second identity.
∎

These formulas can easily be inverted to express iterated normal gradients in terms of iterated
normal derivatives with respect to
s
. In particular, we obtain the following identities.

Example 13.1.7
.

If
\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma)=1
, then it holds

\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}=\iota^{*}\partial_{s}\eta^{*}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}=(\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{2}-2\rho_{0}\iota^{*}\partial_{s})\eta^{*}.

The above discussion obviously generalizes to the case that
\sigma
only satisfies the condition
{(\mathcal{SY})}

with a non-trivial remainder.

13.2.
Expansions of the metric. Model cases

We start by discussing the normal forms of a given metric
g
in geodesic normal coordinates
and in adapted coordinates (as defined in Section
6
). The formulas for these normal forms
contain respective families
h_{r}
and
h_{s}
of metrics on
M
. In order to simplify notation,
we shall use the same notation for the coefficients of their Taylor series in
r
and
s
. It always will
be clear from the context which coefficients are meant. We derive formulas for the first few Taylor
coefficients of
h_{r}
and
h_{s}
. A series of geometrically intuitive examples follow the discussion.
