Proposition 13.2.1
.

We expand the family
h_{r}
in the normal form
dr^{2}+h_{r}
of
g
in geodesic normal
coordinates as

h_{r}=h+h_{(1)}r+h_{(2)}r^{2}+\cdots.

The coefficients
h_{(k)}
can be expressed in terms of the curvature of the metric
g
, its
covariant derivatives, and the second fundamental form
L
. The expansion starts
with

(13.2.1)

(h_{r})_{ij}=h_{ij}+2L_{ij}r+((L^{2})_{ij}-R_{0ij0})r^{2}+(h_{(3)})_{ij}r^{3}+\cdots

with

(13.2.2)

3(h_{(3)})_{ij}=-\nabla_{\partial_{r}}(R)_{0ij0}-2L_{i}^{k}R_{0jk0}-2L_{j}^{k}R_{0ik0}.

Here
(L^{2})_{ij}=L_{ik}L^{k}_{j}=L_{ik}L_{js}h^{ks}
.

Next, assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. We expand the family
h_{s}
in the
normal form (
6.9
) as

h_{s}=h+h_{(1)}s+h_{(2)}s^{2}+\cdots.

The coefficients
h_{(k)}
can be expressed in terms of the curvature of the metric

g
, its covariant derivatives, and the second fundamental form
L
. The expansion
starts with

(13.2.3)

(h_{s})_{ij}=h_{ij}+2L_{ij}s+((L\mathring{L})_{ij}-R_{0ij0})s^{2}+(h_{(3)})_{ij}s^{3}+\cdots

with

\displaystyle 3(h_{(3)})_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{\partial_{s}}(R)_{0ij0}-2\mathring{L}_{i}^{k}R_{0jk0}-2\mathring{L}_{j}^{k}R_{0ik0}

(13.2.4)

\displaystyle+\operatorname{Hess}_{ij}(H)-HR_{0ij0}-3H(L\mathring{L})_{ij}+2L_{ij}\rho_{0}^{\prime}.

Here
(L\mathring{L})_{ij}=L_{ik}\mathring{L}_{js}h^{ks}
and
\rho_{0}^{\prime}={\sf P}_{00}+|\mathring{L}|^{2}/(n-1)
.

The notation
\rho_{0}^{\prime}
will be justified in Lemma
13.5.1
.

Proof.

We recall the standard formulas

R^{s}_{ijk}=\Gamma_{jk}^{l}\Gamma_{il}^{s}-\Gamma_{ik}^{l}\Gamma_{jl}^{s}+\partial_{i}(\Gamma_{jk}^{s})-\partial_{j}(\Gamma_{ik}^{s}),\quad R_{ijkl}=R_{ijk}^{s}g_{sl}

and

\Gamma_{ij}^{m}=\frac{1}{2}g^{km}(\partial_{i}(g_{jk})+\partial_{j}(g_{ik})-\partial_{k}(g_{ij}))

for the components of the curvature tensor of a metric
g
.
26
26

26

As usual, we
sum over repeated indices and denote derivatives by a lower index.
For the metric

g=dr^{2}+h_{r}
, we find
\Gamma_{ij}^{0}=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{r}(h_{r})_{ij}
. Hence

L_{ij}=-\Gamma_{ij}^{0}=\frac{1}{2}(h_{(1)})_{ij}
on
M
. This proves
h_{(1)}=2L
. In order to verify the formula for
h_{(2)}
in (
13.2.1
), we
calculate the components
R_{0ij0}
of the curvature tensor of the metric
dr^{2}+h_{r}
. We decompose
R_{0jk}^{0}
as

R_{0jk}^{0}=\left(\Gamma_{jk}^{l}\Gamma_{0l}^{0}-\Gamma_{0k}^{l}\Gamma_{jl}^{0}+\partial_{r}(\Gamma_{jk}^{0})-\partial_{j}(\Gamma_{0k}^{0})\right)+\Gamma_{jk}^{0}\Gamma_{00}^{0}-\Gamma_{0k}^{0}\Gamma_{j0}^{0},

where the summations run only over tangential indices. Now, for the metric
g=dr^{2}+h_{r}
, we
find the Christoffel symbols

\Gamma_{ij}^{0}=-\frac{1}{2}g_{ij}^{\prime},\quad\Gamma_{0j}^{0}=0,\quad\Gamma_{00}^{0}=0,\quad\Gamma_{0k}^{l}=\frac{1}{2}g^{rl}g_{kr}^{\prime},

where
′
denotes
\partial_{r}
. It follows that

(13.2.5)

R_{0jk}^{0}=\frac{1}{4}g^{rl}g_{kr}^{\prime}g_{jl}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}g_{jk}^{\prime\prime}.

We evaluate this formula at
r=0
. Using
g_{jk}^{\prime}=2L_{jk}
, we find

R_{0jk0}=R_{0jk}^{0}=h^{rl}L_{kr}L_{jl}-(h_{(2)})_{jk}=(L^{2})_{jk}-(h_{(2)})_{jk}.

This implies the formula for
h_{(2)}
in (
13.2.1
). Next, we prove the
formula for the cubic term. First, we note that

\nabla_{\partial_{r}}(R)_{0jk0}=\partial_{r}(R_{0jk0})-R(\partial_{r},\nabla_{\partial_{r}}(\partial_{j}),\partial_{k},\partial_{r})-R(\partial_{r},\partial_{j},\nabla_{\partial_{r}}(\partial_{k}),\partial_{r})

using
\nabla_{\partial_{r}}(\partial_{r})=0
. Now (
13.2.5
) implies

\displaystyle\partial_{r}(R_{0jk0})=\partial_{r}(R_{0jk}^{0}g_{00})=\partial_{r}(R_{0jk}^{0})=\frac{1}{4}(g^{rl})^{\prime}g_{kr}^{\prime}g_{jl}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{4}g^{rl}g_{kr}^{\prime\prime}g_{jl}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{4}g^{rl}g_{kr}^{\prime}g_{jl}^{\prime\prime}-\frac{1}{2}g_{jk}^{\prime\prime\prime}.

Evaluation of this formula for
r=0
yields

\displaystyle\partial_{r}(R_{0jk0})

\displaystyle=-2L^{rl}L_{kr}L_{jl}+h^{rl}(h_{(2)})_{kr}L_{jl}+h^{rl}L_{kr}(h_{(2)})_{jl}-3(h_{(3)})_{jk}

\displaystyle=-L_{j}^{r}R_{0kr0}-L_{k}^{l}R_{0jl0}-3(h_{(3)})_{jk}.

The two remaining terms in the formula for
\partial_{r}(R_{0jk0})
for
r=0
are

-R_{0jl0}L_{k}^{l}-R_{0lk0}L_{j}^{l}.

Thus, we find

\nabla_{\partial_{r}}(R)_{0jk0}=-2L_{j}^{r}R_{0kr0}-2L_{k}^{l}R_{0jl0}-3(h_{(3)})_{jk}.

This proves (
13.2.2
).

Similarly, the formula for
h_{s}
in (
13.2.3
) and (
13.2.1
)
follows from a calculation of the Christoffel symbols and the components
R_{0ij0}=R_{0ij}^{0}g_{00}
of the curvature tensor of the metric

\eta^{*}(g)=\eta^{*}(|\mathcal{N}|^{2})^{-1}ds^{2}+h_{s}=a^{-1}ds^{2}+h_{s}

with
a=\eta^{*}(|\mathcal{N}|^{2})
. In order to simplify the notation, we shall write
g

instead of
\eta^{*}(g)
and
\rho
instead of
\eta^{*}(\rho)
. For the above metric,
we find the Christoffel symbols
27
27

27

As usual the prime means derivative in
s
.

\Gamma_{ij}^{0}=-\frac{1}{2}g^{00}g_{ij}^{\prime},\quad\Gamma_{0j}^{0}=\frac{1}{2}g^{00}\partial_{j}(g_{00}),\quad\Gamma_{00}^{0}=\frac{1}{2}g^{00}g_{00}^{\prime},\quad\Gamma_{0k}^{l}=\frac{1}{2}g^{rl}g_{kr}^{\prime}.

Here
g^{00}=a=1-2s\rho
(by assumption) and
g_{00}=a^{-1}=1+2s\rho+\cdots
. In particular,
g^{00}=1
,
g_{00}^{\prime}=-2H
and
(g^{00})^{\prime}=2H
on
M
.
Here we used that
\rho=-H
on
M
(Lemma
13.5.1
). Hence
L_{ij}=-\Gamma_{ij}^{0}=\frac{1}{2}(h_{(1)})_{ij}
. The components
R_{0jk0}
of the
curvature tensor are given by

R_{0jk0}=R_{0jk}^{0}g_{00}

with

\displaystyle R_{0jk}^{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Gamma_{jk}^{l}g^{00}\partial_{l}(g_{00})+\frac{1}{4}g^{00}g^{rl}g_{kr}^{\prime}g_{jl}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}((g^{00})^{\prime}g_{jk}^{\prime}+g^{00}g_{jk}^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{j}(g^{00})\partial_{k}(g_{00})+g^{00}\partial^{2}_{kj}(g_{00}))

(13.2.6)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}(g^{00})^{2}g_{jk}^{\prime}g_{00}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{4}(g^{00})^{2}\partial_{j}(g_{00})\partial_{k}(g_{00}).

Evaluation of this formula for
s=0
gives

R_{0jk0}=h^{rl}L_{kr}L_{jl}-(h_{(2)})_{jk}-2HL_{jk}+HL_{jk}=(L^{2})_{jk}-HL_{jk}-(h_{(2)})_{jk}.

But
L^{2}-HL=L(L-Hh)=L\mathring{L}
. Hence
R_{0jk0}=(L\mathring{L})_{jk}-(h_{(2)})_{jk}
. This implies the formula for
h_{(2)}
in (
13.2.3
).
Finally, we prove the formula for the cubic term. Here we utilize the expansions

g_{00}=(1-2\rho s)^{-1}=1+2\rho s+4\rho^{2}s^{2}+\cdots=1+2\rho_{0}s+(2\rho_{0}^{\prime}+4\rho_{0}^{2})s^{2}+\cdots

and

g^{00}=1-2\rho s=1-2\rho_{0}s-2\rho_{0}^{\prime}s^{2}+\cdots.

We proceed as above and calculate
\nabla_{\partial_{s}}(R)_{0jk0}
for
s=0
. In the
present case, it holds

\displaystyle\nabla_{\partial_{s}}(R)_{0jk0}

\displaystyle=\partial_{s}(R_{0jk0})-R(\partial_{s},\nabla_{\partial_{s}}(\partial_{j}),\partial_{k},\partial_{s})-R(\partial_{s},\partial_{j},\nabla_{\partial_{s}}(\partial_{k}),\partial_{s})

\displaystyle-R(\nabla_{\partial_{s}}(\partial_{s}),\partial_{j},\partial_{k},\partial_{s})-R(\partial_{s},\partial_{j},\partial_{k},\nabla_{\partial_{s}}(\partial_{s}));

note that
\nabla_{\partial_{s}}(\partial_{s})
does not vanish in general. First, we
calculate the term
\partial_{s}(R_{0jk0})
for
s=0
using (
13.2
). We
obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{s}(R_{0jk0})

\displaystyle=\partial_{s}(R_{0jk}^{0})-2HR_{0jk}^{0}

\displaystyle=-\Gamma_{jk}^{l}\partial_{l}(H)+\frac{1}{2}Hh^{rl}g_{kr}^{\prime}g_{jl}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{4}(g^{rl})^{\prime}g_{kr}^{\prime}g_{jl}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{4}h^{rl}g_{kr}^{\prime\prime}g_{jl}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{4}h^{rl}g_{kr}^{\prime}g_{jl}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle+4\rho_{0}^{\prime}L_{jk}-2Hg_{jk}^{\prime\prime}-\frac{1}{2}g_{jk}^{\prime\prime\prime}+\partial^{2}_{kj}(H)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}Hg_{jk}^{\prime\prime}-\rho_{0}^{\prime}g_{jk}^{\prime}-2HR_{0jk0}

using the expansions of
g_{00}
and
g^{00}
. We further simplify that sum by using
the known formula for the first derivative of
h_{s}
and
\operatorname{Hess}_{jk}=\partial^{2}_{jk}-\Gamma_{jk}^{l}\partial_{l}
. Then

\displaystyle\partial_{s}(R_{0jk0})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hess}_{jk}(H)+2HL_{k}^{l}L_{jl}-2L^{rl}L_{kr}L_{jl}+L_{j}^{r}(h_{(2)})_{kr}+L_{k}^{l}(h_{(2)})_{jl}

\displaystyle+4L_{jk}\rho_{0}^{\prime}-4H(h_{(2)})_{jk}-3(h_{(3)})_{jk}+H(h_{(2)})_{jk}-2L_{jk}\rho_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hess}_{jk}(H)+2H(L^{2})_{jk}-2(L^{3})_{jk}+L_{j}^{r}(h_{(2)})_{kr}+L_{k}^{l}(h_{(2)})_{jl}

\displaystyle-3H(h_{(2)})_{jk}+2L_{jk}\rho_{0}^{\prime}-3(h_{(3)})_{jk}-2HR_{0jk0}

on
M
. The four remaining contributions to
\nabla_{\partial_{s}}(R)_{0jk0}
for
s=0
equal

\displaystyle-\Gamma_{0j}^{l}R_{0lk0}-\Gamma_{0k}^{l}R_{0jl0}-\Gamma_{00}^{0}R_{0jk0}-\Gamma_{00}^{0}R_{0jk0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}g^{rl}g_{jr}^{\prime}R_{0lk0}-\frac{1}{2}g^{rl}g_{kr}^{\prime}R_{0jl0}-g^{00}g_{00}^{\prime}R_{0jk0}

\displaystyle=-L_{j}^{l}R_{0lk0}-L_{k}^{l}R_{0jl0}+2HR_{0jk0}

\displaystyle=-\mathring{L}_{j}^{l}R_{0lk0}-\mathring{L}_{k}^{l}R_{0jl0}.

Hence

\displaystyle\nabla_{\partial_{s}}(R)_{0jk0}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hess}_{jk}(H)+2H(L^{2})_{jk}-2(L^{3})_{jk}

\displaystyle+L_{j}^{r}((L\mathring{L})_{kr}-R_{0kr0})+L_{k}^{l}((L\mathring{L})_{jl}-R_{0jl0})-3H((L\mathring{L})_{jk}-R_{0jk0})

\displaystyle-\mathring{L}_{j}^{l}R_{0lk0}-\mathring{L}_{k}^{l}R_{0jl0}+2L_{jk}\rho_{0}^{\prime}-3(h_{(3)})_{jk}-2HR_{0jk0}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hess}_{jk}(H)-L_{j}^{r}R_{0kr0}-L_{k}^{l}R_{0jl0}-3H(L\mathring{L})_{jk}+3HR_{0jk0}

\displaystyle-\mathring{L}_{j}^{l}R_{0lk0}-\mathring{L}_{k}^{l}R_{0jl0}+2L_{jk}\rho_{0}^{\prime}-3(h_{(3)})_{jk}-2HR_{0jk0}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hess}_{jk}(H)-2\mathring{L}_{j}^{r}R_{0kr0}-2\mathring{L}_{k}^{r}R_{0jr0}-3H(L\mathring{L})_{jk}-HR_{0jk0}

\displaystyle+2L_{jk}\rho_{0}^{\prime}-3(h_{(3)})_{jk}

by the formula for
h_{(2)}
. This proves (
13.2.1
).
∎

Formula (
13.2.1
) was given in
[
G17
, (2.4)]
and the formula
(
13.2.2
) for the cubic coefficient was displayed in
[
GG19
, (2.11)]
.
28
28

28

In our
conventions,
R
and
L
have opposite signs as in
[
G17
,
GG19
]
.

The proof of Proposition
13.2.1
shows that
h_{(2)}
depends only on
\rho_{0}
. Similarly,
h_{(3)}

only depends on
\rho_{0}
and
\rho_{0}^{\prime}
. More generally, it is easy to see that
h_{(k)}

only depends on
\partial_{s}^{j}(\rho)|_{0}
for
j\leq k-2
.

Corollary 13.2.2
.

Formula (
13.2.1
) for the cubic term
h_{(3)}
of the expansion of
g

in adapted coordinates yields

(13.2.7)

\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)})=2nH,\quad\operatorname{tr}(h_{(2)})=|\mathring{L}|^{2}-\operatorname{Ric}_{00}

and

(13.2.8)

3\operatorname{tr}(h_{(3)})=-\nabla_{\partial_{s}}(\operatorname{Ric})_{00}-4\mathring{L}^{ik}R_{0ik0}+\Delta H-H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-3H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+2nH\rho_{0}^{\prime}.

If
g_{+}=r^{-2}g=r^{-2}(dr^{2}+h_{r})
is a Poincaré-Einstein metric in normal
form relative to
h=h_{0}
, then
h_{(1)}=0
and
h_{(2)}=-{\sf P}^{h}
. Comparing this
result with (
13.2.1
), implies that
R^{g}_{0ij0}={\sf P}^{h}_{ij}
. Hence

\operatorname{Ric}^{g}_{00}={\sf J}^{h}
.

We illustrate the above results in some simple model cases.

Example 13.2.3
.

Let
S^{n}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
be defined by
|x|=1
. Let
g_{0}
be the Euclidean metric
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
. Then the function
\sigma=(1-|x|^{2})/2
yields the Poincaré metric

\sigma^{-2}g_{0}=\frac{4}{(1-|x|^{2})^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}dx_{i}^{2}

on the unit ball
|x|<1
. Now
\mathcal{N}=\operatorname{grad}(\sigma)=-\sum_{i}x_{i}\partial_{i}
. Hence the
normalized gradient field
\mathfrak{X}=\mathcal{N}/|\mathcal{N}|^{2}
is given by
\mathfrak{X}=-\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\sum_{i}x_{i}\partial_{i}
. It follows that the map

\eta:(-1/2,1/2)\times S^{n}\ni(s,x)\mapsto\sqrt{1-2s}x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}

defines the adapted coordinates. Note that
\eta^{*}(\sigma)=s
. Hence we obtain

\eta^{*}(g_{0})=\frac{1}{1-2s}ds^{2}+(1-2s)h,

where the round metric
h
on
S^{n}
is induced by
g
. In particular,
h_{s}=h-2sh
, i.e.,
h_{(1)}=-2h
and
h_{(2)}=0
The coefficient
h_{(1)}
is to be
interpreted as
2L
being defined by the unit normal field
-\partial_{r}
. The
vanishing of the quadratic and the cubic term is confirmed by the general formulas.
Note also that
\rho=1
. It follows that
v(s)=(1-2s)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}
and

\mathring{v}(s)=(1-2s)^{\frac{n}{2}}
. These results confirm the relation (
6.25
)
using
a=1-2s
. Moreover, the identity (
11.3
) in Conjecture
11.4
is trivially satisfied.

Example 13.2.4
.

Let
S^{n}\subset S^{n+1}
be an equatorial subsphere. Let
g
be the round metric on

S^{n+1}
. Then the height-function
\sigma=\mathcal{H}\in C^{\infty}(S^{n+1})
defines the
metric
\mathcal{H}^{-2}g
on both connected components of the complement of the zero locus
of
\mathcal{H}
. It is isometric to the Poincaré-metric on the unit ball (see Section

12
). The map

\eta:(-1,1)\times S^{n}\ni(s,x)\mapsto(\sqrt{1-s^{2}}x,s)\in S^{n+1}

defines the adapted coordinates. Note that
\eta^{*}(\sigma)=s
. Hence we obtain

\eta^{*}(g)=\frac{1}{1-s^{2}}ds^{2}+(1-s^{2})h,

where the round metric
h
on
S^{n}
is induced by
g
. In particular,
h_{(1)}=0

and
h_{(2)}=-h
. The coefficient
h_{(1)}
vanishes since
L=0
and the
coefficient
h_{(2)}
is to be interpreted as
-R_{0ij0}
. The vanishing of the
cubic term is confirmed by the general formula. Note that
\mathcal{H}
is an eigenfunction
of the Laplacian on
S^{n+1}
with eigenvalue
-(n+1)
and
{\sf J}=\frac{n+1}{2}
.
Hence
\rho=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}
and
\eta^{*}(\rho)=\frac{1}{2}s
. Finally, we have

v(s)=(1-s^{2})^{\frac{n-1}{2}}
and
\mathring{v}(s)=(1-s^{2})^{\frac{n}{2}}
.
These results confirm the relation (
6.25
) using
a=1-s^{2}
. Moreover,
the identity (
11.3
) in Conjecture
11.4
reduces to the trivial relation

\binom{\frac{n-1}{2}}{\frac{n+1}{4}}(-1)^{\frac{n+1}{4}}+\binom{\frac{n-1}{2}}{\frac{n-3}{4}}(-1)^{\frac{n-3}{4}}=0.

In the above two examples, either the curvature of the background metric or the
second fundamental form vanishes. We finish this section with the discussion of a
model case with non-trivial curvature and non-trivial second fundamental form.

Example 13.2.5
.

Let
\mathbb{H}^{n+1}
be the upper half-space with the hyperbolic metric
g_{+}=r^{-2}(dr^{2}+dx^{2})
. For
c>0
, we let
X=\{r\geq c\}
with boundary
M=\{r=c\}
and background metric
g=g_{+}
. The metric
g
restricts to
h=c^{-2}dx^{2}
on

M
. The defining function
\sigma=1-c/r
is smooth up to the boundary
M
. It
solves the singular Yamabe problem since
\sigma^{-2}g_{+}=(r-c)^{-2}(dr^{2}+dx^{2})
has scalar curvature
-n(n+1)
. The map

\eta:(0,1)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\ni(s,x)\mapsto\left(\frac{c}{1-s},x\right)\in X

defines the adapted coordinates. In fact,
\eta^{*}(\sigma)=s
. We obtain the normal form

\eta^{*}(g)=\frac{1}{(1-s)^{2}}ds^{2}+\frac{(1-s)^{2}}{c^{2}}dx^{2}

of
g
in adapted coordinates. In particular,
h_{(1)}=-2h
,
h_{(2)}=h
and

h_{(3)}=0
. These results fit with the general formulas in Proposition

13.2.1
since
L=-h
and
R_{0ij0}=-h_{ij}
. The vanishing
of
h_{(3)}
follows using
\mathring{L}=0
,
H=-1
and
{\sf P}_{00}=-1/2
. A
calculation using the formula
\Delta_{g_{+}}=r^{2}\partial_{r}^{2}-(n-1)r\partial_{r}+\Delta_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}
for the Laplacian of
g_{+}
yields

\rho=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{c}{2r}.

Hence
\eta^{*}(\rho)=1-s/2
. Note that
\mathcal{N}=\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)=-c\partial_{r}
,

|\mathcal{N}|^{2}=r^{-2}c^{2}
and
\eta^{*}(|\mathcal{N}|^{2})=(1-s)^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}1-2s\eta^{*}(\rho)
. Finally, we have
v(s)=(1-s)^{n-1}
and
\mathring{v}(s)=(1-s)^{n}
. By
{\sf J}=-\frac{n+1}{2}
, the identity (
11.3
) in Conjecture

11.4
reduces to the trivial relation

\binom{n-1}{\frac{n+1}{2}}(-1)^{\frac{n+1}{2}}-\binom{n-1}{\frac{n-3}{2}}(-1)^{\frac{n-3}{2}}=0.

Finally, these results confirm the relation (
6.25
) using
a=(1-s)^{2}
.

13.3.
Approximate solutions of the singular Yamabe problem. The residue formula

In the present section, we determine the first few terms in the expansion

\sigma_{F}=r+\sigma_{(2)}r^{2}+\dots
of a solution of the equation

(13.3.1)

\mathcal{S}(g,\sigma_{F})=1+O(r^{n+1}).

We also describe the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
in terms of a formal residue of the supercritical
term
\sigma_{n+2}
.

By (
3
), equation (
13.3.1
) takes the form

\displaystyle\partial_{r}(\sigma_{F})^{2}+h_{r}^{ij}\partial_{i}(\sigma_{F})\partial_{j}(\sigma_{F})

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{n+1}\sigma_{F}\left(\partial_{r}^{2}(\sigma_{F})+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{r}^{-1}h_{r}^{\prime})\partial_{r}(\sigma_{F})+\Delta_{h_{r}}(\sigma_{F})+\bar{{\sf J}}\sigma_{F}\right)=1+O(r^{n+1}).

Here and in the following, we use the bar notation for curvature quantities of
g
.

We shall formulate the results in terms of the volume coefficients
u_{k}
of the metric
g

in geodesic normal coordinates (see (
6.1
)) using

(13.3.2)

\frac{u^{\prime}(r)}{u(r)}=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{r}^{-1}h_{r}^{\prime}).

The following results describe the first three Taylor coefficients of a solution
\sigma_{F}
of the
singular Yamabe problem in general dimensions.

Lemma 13.3.1
.

It holds

\displaystyle\sigma_{(2)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n}u_{1},

\displaystyle\sigma_{(3)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{3(n-1)}u_{2}-\frac{1}{3n}u_{1}^{2}+\frac{1}{3(n-1)}\bar{{\sf J}}

and

\displaystyle\sigma_{(4)}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4(n-2)}u_{3}-\frac{9n^{2}-20n+7}{12n(n-1)(n-2)}u_{1}u_{2}+\frac{6n^{2}-11n+1}{24n^{2}(n-2)}u_{1}^{3}

(13.3.3)

\displaystyle+\frac{2n-1}{6n(n-1)(n-2)}u_{1}\bar{{\sf J}}+\frac{1}{4(n-2)}\bar{{\sf J}}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{4(n-2)}\Delta(\sigma_{(2)}).

Alternatively, the above formulas can be derived from the description of the solution
\sigma
in
[
GGHW19
, Appendix]
.
In fact, these formulas describe expansions of
\sigma
into power series of any defining function with coefficients that live
on the background space
X
. The calculation then requires expanding these coefficients into power series of the distance
function. We omit the details.

The volume coefficients
u_{j}
may be expressed in terms of the Taylor coefficients of
h_{r}
. Such
relations follow from (
13.3.2
) by Taylor expansion in
r
and resolving the resulting relations
for
u_{j}
. We find

\displaystyle u_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)}),

\displaystyle u_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}(\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)})^{2}+4\operatorname{tr}(h_{(2)})-2\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)}^{2}))

and

\displaystyle u_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{48}(\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)})^{3}+12\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)})\operatorname{tr}(h_{(2)})+24\operatorname{tr}(h_{(3)})-6\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)})\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)}^{2})

\displaystyle-24\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)}h_{(2)})+8\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)}^{3})).

These formulas are valid in general dimensions. The expressions for
h_{(j)}
(for
j\leq 3
) in
Proposition
13.2.1
imply

\displaystyle u_{1}

\displaystyle=nH,

\displaystyle 2u_{2}

\displaystyle=-\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00}-|L|^{2}+n^{2}H^{2}=\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00}+\operatorname{scal}-\overline{\operatorname{scal}}

(by the Gauss equation) or equivalently

(13.3.4)

2u_{2}=-\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00}-|\mathring{L}|^{2}+n(n-1)H^{2}.

Moreover, we find

(13.3.5)

6u_{3}=-\bar{\nabla}_{0}(\overline{\operatorname{Ric}})_{00}+2(L,\bar{{\mathcal{G}}})-3nH\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00}+2\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})-3nH|L|^{2}+n^{3}H^{3},

where
\bar{{\mathcal{G}}}_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\bar{R}_{0ij0}
, or equivalently

\displaystyle 6u_{3}

\displaystyle=-\bar{\nabla}_{0}(\overline{\operatorname{Ric}})_{00}+2(\mathring{L},\bar{{\mathcal{G}}})-(3n-2)H\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00}

\displaystyle+2\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})-3(n-2)H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+n(n-1)(n-2)H^{3}.

For these formulas for
u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}
, see also
[
GG19
, (2.14)]
.

Note that

(13.3.6)

-|L|^{2}+n^{2}H^{2}=\sum_{ij}\begin{vmatrix}L_{ii}&L_{ij}\\
L_{ji}&L_{jj}\end{vmatrix}=2\sigma_{2}(L)

and

(13.3.7)

2\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})-3nH|L|^{2}+n^{3}H^{3}=\sum_{ijk}\begin{vmatrix}L_{ii}&L_{ij}&L_{ik}\\
L_{ji}&L_{jj}&L_{jk}\\
L_{ki}&L_{kj}&L_{kk}\end{vmatrix}=6\sigma_{3}(L)

in an orthonormal basis. Here
\sigma_{k}(L)
denotes the
k
-th elementary symmetric polynomial
in the eigenvalues of the shape operator. The identities (
13.3.6
) and (
13.3.7
) are special
cases of Newton’s identities relating elementary symmetric polynomials to power series.

In these terms, we have

\displaystyle 2u_{2}

\displaystyle=-\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00}+2\sigma_{2}(L)

\displaystyle 6u_{3}

\displaystyle=-\bar{\nabla}_{0}(\overline{\operatorname{Ric}})_{00}+2(\mathring{L},\bar{{\mathcal{G}}})-(3n-2)H\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00}+6\sigma_{3}(L).

Note also that versions of the Gauss identity express the quantities
\sigma_{2}(L)
and
\sigma_{3}(L)

in terms of the curvatures of
g
and of the induced metric on
M

[
AGV
, Corollary 3.2]
.
Finally, we note that (
13.3.7
) vanishes in
n=2
- this identity is equivalent to
\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})=0
in
n=2
.

The above results imply

Lemma 13.3.2
.

It holds
2\sigma_{(2)}=H
and

(13.3.8)

6\sigma_{(3)}=-2\bar{{\sf P}}_{00}-\frac{2}{n-1}|\mathring{L}|^{2}=2({\sf J}-\iota^{*}\bar{{\sf J}})-\frac{1}{n-1}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-nH^{2}

for
n\geq 2
.

The second equality follows by combining the first equality with (
13.1.3
).

The results of Lemma
13.3.2
are contained in
[
G17
, (2.6) and Section 4]
. See also

[
GG19
, (2.16)–(2.19)]
.
29
29

29

These references use a different convention for
L
and
H
.

The above formulas for
v_{j}
are equivalent to the corresponding formulas in
[
AGV
, Theorem 3.4]
. We
also refer to
[
G04
, Theorem 9.22, Problem 9.1]
for the corresponding results in higher codimensions.
However, the methods of proof in these references are different.

For
n=1
, the above results easily imply

(13.3.9)

{\mathcal{B}}_{1}=(r^{-2}(\mathcal{S}(S_{2})-1))|_{0}=-2u_{2}-\bar{{\sf J}}_{0}=\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00}-\bar{{\sf J}}_{0}=0

using
\mathring{L}=0
(see also Remark
13.5.2
). This result is well-known
[
G17
]
.

It also is of interest to explicate the above formulas for flat backgrounds. In fact, it follows from the identity

(13.3.10)

u(r)=\det(\operatorname{Id}+rL)

(see
[
G04
, Section 3.4]
) for a flat background that the formulas for
\sigma_{(k)}
(for
k\leq 4
)
can be expressed in terms of
L
. Newton’s identities imply

\displaystyle u_{1}

\displaystyle=nH,

\displaystyle u_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(n(n-1)H^{2}-|\mathring{L}|^{2}),

\displaystyle u_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}(H^{3}n(n-1)(n-2)-3(n-2)H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+2\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3}))

and a direct calculation yields the following result.

Lemma 13.3.3
.

The expansion of a solution of the Yamabe problem for
M^{n}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}

has the form

\sigma_{F}=r+\frac{r^{2}}{2}H-\frac{r^{3}}{3(n-1)}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+r^{4}\sigma_{(4)}+\cdots

with the coefficient

\displaystyle\sigma_{(4)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{24(n-2)}\left(6\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})+\frac{7n-11}{n-1}H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+3\Delta(H)\right).

Note that the coefficients in formula (
13.3.1
) for
\sigma_{(4)}
have a simple pole in
n=2
. The following result
calculates the formal residue at
n=2
.

Corollary 13.3.4
.

It holds

\operatorname{Res}_{n=2}(\sigma_{(4)})=\frac{3}{4}u_{3}+\frac{1}{32}u_{1}^{3}-\frac{1}{8}u_{1}u_{2}+\frac{1}{4}u_{1}\bar{{\sf J}}+\frac{1}{4}\bar{{\sf J}}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{4}\Delta(\sigma_{(2)}).

For a flat background, we obtain

\operatorname{Res}_{n=2}(\sigma_{(4)})=\frac{1}{8}(H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+\Delta(H)).

Note that for a flat background it holds
u_{3}=0
in
n=2
. We also recall that
\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})=0
for
n=2
.
Alternatively, one can use formula
[
GG19
, (2.18)]
for
\sigma_{(4)}
to confirm this residue formula for
general backgrounds.

Corollary
13.3.4
and the following result are special cases of the residue formula in Lemma
13.3.9
.

Corollary 13.3.5
.

It holds

\operatorname{Res}_{n=2}(\sigma_{(4)})=-\frac{3}{8}{\mathcal{B}}_{2}.

Proof.

We recall that the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
is defined by

{\mathcal{B}}_{2}=(r^{-3}(\mathcal{S}(S_{3})-1))|_{0},

where
S_{3}=r+\sigma_{(2)}r^{2}+\sigma_{(3)}r^{3}
. A calculation yields

{\mathcal{B}}_{2}=-2u_{3}-\frac{1}{12}u_{1}^{3}+\frac{1}{3}u_{1}u_{2}-\frac{2}{3}u_{1}\bar{{\sf J}}-\frac{2}{3}\bar{{\sf J}}^{\prime}-\frac{2}{3}\Delta(\sigma_{(2)}).

We omit the details. We recall that the term
u_{3}
vanishes in
n=2
in the flat case but not in
the curved case.
∎

In Section
13.6
, we shall derive another formula for
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
from Theorem
6
.
Although the equivalence of both formulas is non-trivial, we leave the check of consistency to the reader.

Similarly, we may either directly evaluate the definition
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}=(r^{-4}(\mathcal{S}(S_{4})-1))|_{0}
or
use the residue formula (
13.3.13
) to derive a formula for
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
from the residue of

\sigma_{(5)}
at
n=3
. The results read as follows.

Lemma 13.3.6
.

It holds

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{3}

\displaystyle=-2u_{4}+\frac{1}{2}u_{1}u_{3}+\frac{1}{3}u_{2}^{2}-\frac{7}{18}u_{1}^{2}u_{2}+\frac{2}{27}u_{1}^{4}-\frac{1}{3}\bar{{\sf J}}u_{2}-\frac{5}{12}\bar{{\sf J}}^{\prime}u_{1}-\frac{1}{4}\bar{{\sf J}}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\Delta(\sigma_{(3)})-\frac{1}{3}u_{1}\Delta(\sigma_{(2)})-\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{\prime}(\sigma_{(2)})+|d\sigma_{(2)}|^{2},

where

6\sigma_{(2)}=u_{1}\quad\mbox{and}\quad 9\sigma_{(3)}=3u_{2}-u_{1}^{2}+\frac{3}{2}\bar{{\sf J}}

and
\Delta^{\prime}=(d/dt)|_{0}(\Delta_{h+2sL})
(see (
9.14
)). For a flat background, it holds
\bar{{\sf J}}=0
,
the term
u_{4}
vanishes, and we obtain

(13.3.11)

12{\mathcal{B}}_{3}=\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})+6H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})+|\mathring{L}|^{4}+3|dH|^{2}-6H\Delta(H)-3\Delta^{\prime}(H).

The above formula for
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
also follows from the following result through the residue formula

(13.3.12)

\operatorname{Res}_{n=3}(\sigma_{(5)})=-\frac{2}{5}{\mathcal{B}}_{3}.

The evaluation of that formula rests on the following result for the coefficient
\sigma_{(5)}
.

Lemma 13.3.7
.

In general dimensions, it holds

\displaystyle\sigma_{(5)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{n+1}{10(n-3)}|d\sigma_{(2)}|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{5(n-3)}\Delta^{\prime}(\sigma_{(2)})+\frac{1}{5(n-3)}\Delta(\sigma_{(3)})+\frac{3n-1}{20(n-3)(n-2)n}\Delta(\sigma_{(2)})u_{1}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{10(n-3)}\bar{{\sf J}}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{n-1}{4(n-3)(n-2)n}\bar{{\sf J}}^{\prime}u_{1}+\frac{2(3n-5)}{15(n-3)(n-1)^{2}}\bar{{\sf J}}u_{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{4n-3}{20(n-2)(n-1)n}\bar{{\sf J}}u_{1}^{2}+\frac{1}{30(n-1)^{2}}\bar{{\sf J}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{48n^{4}-247n^{3}+387n^{2}-179n+3}{60(n-3)(n-2)(n-1)n^{2}}u_{1}^{2}u_{2}-\frac{2(3n^{2}-11n+10)}{15(n-3)(n-1)^{2}}u_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{24n^{4}-110n^{3}+133n^{2}-24n-3}{120(n-3)(n-2)n^{3}}u_{1}^{4}-\frac{16n^{2}-53n+27}{20(n-3)(n-2)n}u_{1}u_{3}+\frac{4}{5(n-3)}u_{4}.

We omit the details of the proof. An alternative formula for
\sigma_{(5)}
is given in
[
GGHW19
, Appendix]
.
Here the same comments as after Lemma
13.3.1
apply.

Remark 13.3.8
.

Lemma
13.3.7
implies

\operatorname{Res}_{n=2}(\sigma_{(5)})=-\frac{1}{2}u_{1}\operatorname{Res}_{n=2}(\sigma_{(4)})=\frac{3}{4}H{\mathcal{B}}_{2}.

This relation extends the identities

\displaystyle\operatorname{Res}_{n=1}(\sigma_{(4)})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}u_{1}\operatorname{Res}_{n=1}(\sigma_{(3)}),

\displaystyle\operatorname{Res}_{n=1}(\sigma_{(3)})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}u_{1}\operatorname{Res}_{n=1}(\sigma_{(2)})=0.

More generally, we conjecture the factorization identities

\operatorname{Res}_{n=k-3}(\sigma_{(k)})=-\frac{1}{2}u_{1}\operatorname{Res}_{n=k-3}(\sigma_{(k-1)})

for
k\geq 4
.

Combining (
13.3.11
) with the variation formula
\Delta^{\prime}(u)=-2(\operatorname{Hess}(u),L)-3(du,dH)
(see
(
9.14
)) shows that

12{\mathcal{B}}_{3}=\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})+12|dH|^{2}+6(\operatorname{Hess}(H),\mathring{L})+|\mathring{L}|^{4}+6H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3}).

In Section
13.7
, we shall alternatively derive that result from Theorem
6
.

A direct proof that, for conformally flat backgrounds, Lemma
13.3.6
is equivalent to Lemma

13.7.10
will be given in a separate work.

Finally, we establish the residue formula for the singular Yamabe obstruction in full generality.

Lemma 13.3.9
.

It holds

(13.3.13)

\operatorname{Res}_{n=k-2}(\sigma_{(k)})=-\frac{k-1}{2k}{\mathcal{B}}_{k-2},\;k\geq 4.

Proof.

Let
S_{k}
be the Taylor polynomial
r+r^{2}\sigma_{(2)}+\cdots+r^{k}\sigma_{(k)}

of degree
k
. Then a calculation shows that

(13.3.14)

\mathcal{S}(S_{k})=\mathcal{S}(S_{k-1})+r^{k-1}\frac{2k(n-k+2)}{n+1}\sigma_{(k)}+O(r^{k}).

Assume that
S_{k-1}
is the
(k-1)
-th approximate solution of the singular Yamabe problem, i.e.,

\mathcal{S}(S_{k-1})=1+O(r^{k-1})
. Then
S_{k}
is the
k
-th approximate solution if in the expansion

\mathcal{S}(S_{k})=1+r^{k-1}\left(\frac{2k(n-k+2)}{n+1}\sigma_{(k)}+\cdots\right)+O(r^{k})

with an unknown coefficient
\sigma_{(k)}
the coefficient of
r^{k-1}
vanishes, i.e., if

\mathcal{S}(S_{k})=1+O(r^{k})
. This can be solved for
\sigma_{(k)}
if
k=2,3,\dots,n+1
.
30
30

30

This algorithm
yields Lemma
13.3.1
.
In the case
k=n+2
, we only have

\mathcal{S}(S_{n+1})=1+O(r^{n+1}),

and the restriction of the latter remainder is the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
. Now (
13.3.14
) implies

\frac{k-1}{2k}(r^{-k+1}(\mathcal{S}(S_{k-1})-1)|_{0}+\operatorname{Res}_{n=k-2}(\sigma_{(k)})=\frac{k-1}{2k}(r^{-k+1}(\mathcal{S}(S_{k})-1)|_{0}

if
S_{k-1}
is the
(k-1)
-th approximate solution of the singular Yamabe problem. Then the left-hand
side is well-defined and the right-hand side vanishes. This implies the assertion.
∎

13.4.
Renormalized volume coefficients (adapted coordinates)

Here we consider the first three coefficients in the expansion of
v(s)
as defined in (
6.10
)
in adapted coordinates. As usual we identify
\rho
with
\eta^{*}(\rho)
. We also recall
that derivatives with respect to
s
are denoted by a prime.

We first derive formulas for
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
from Proposition
13.2.1
.

Example 13.4.1
.

Note that

a^{-1/2}=(1-2s\rho)^{-1/2}=1+s\rho_{0}+s^{2}(\frac{3}{2}\rho_{0}^{2}+\rho^{\prime}_{0})+\cdots.

Hence the factorization
v(s)=a^{-1/2}\mathring{v}(s)
and the identities

\mathring{v}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathring{v}_{2}=\frac{1}{8}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(2)})-\frac{1}{4}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)}^{2}))

show that the expansion of
v(s)
starts with

\displaystyle 1+\frac{1}{2}(2\rho_{0}+\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)}))s

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left(\operatorname{tr}(h_{(2)})-\frac{1}{2}|h_{(1)}|^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)})^{2}+\rho_{0}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)})+3\rho_{0}^{2}+2\rho^{\prime}_{0}\right)s^{2}+\cdots.

Now (
13.2.3
) and
\rho_{0}=-H
(Lemma
13.5.1
) imply that the linear
coefficient equals
(n-1)H
. Hence
v_{1}=(n-1)H
. This result fits with the formula

[
GW17
, (4.6)]
for its integral. Moreover, using Lemma
13.5.1
, we
obtain

\frac{1}{2}\left(|\mathring{L}|^{2}-\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00}-2|L|^{2}+n^{2}H^{2}-2nH^{2}+3H^{2}+2\bar{{\sf P}}_{00}+2\frac{|\mathring{L}|^{2}}{n-1}\right)

for the quadratic coefficient. By
\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00}=(n-1)\bar{{\sf P}}_{00}+\bar{{\sf J}}_{0}
and

|L|^{2}=|\mathring{L}|^{2}+nH^{2}
, the latter sum simplifies to

(13.4.1)

v_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\left(-\frac{n-3}{n-1}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-(n-3)\bar{{\sf P}}_{00}+(n-1)(n-3)H^{2}-\bar{{\sf J}}_{0}\right).

In particular, (
13.4.1
) shows that
2v_{2}=-\bar{{\sf J}}_{0}
for
n=3
. This proves the relation (
11.3
) in
Conjecture
11.4
for
n=3
.

In the following examples, we demonstrate how the identity (
6.18
) can be used to calculate the
renormalized volume coefficients
v_{k}
(for
k=1,2,3
). This alternative method does not require
the calculation of composition of
L
-operators and does not use explicit formulas for the Taylor coefficients of
h_{s}
.

Example 13.4.2
.

The restriction of (
6.18
) to
s=0
implies
v_{1}=-(n-1)\rho_{0}
. Now the fact

\rho_{0}=\iota^{*}\rho=-H
(Lemma
13.5.1
) implies
v_{1}=(n-1)H
.

Example 13.4.3
.

We restrict the derivative of (
6.18
) in
s
to
s=0
. Then

2v_{2}-v_{1}^{2}=-(n\!-\!3)\rho^{\prime}_{0}-\bar{{\sf J}}_{0}-2(n\!-\!1)\rho^{2}_{0}.

Now we combine this result with the value of
v_{1}
(Example
13.4.2
) and Lemma
13.5.1

to conclude that

\displaystyle-2v_{2}

\displaystyle=\bar{{\sf J}}_{0}+(n\!-\!3)\left(\bar{{\sf P}}_{00}+\frac{1}{n\!-\!1}|\mathring{L}|^{2}\right)-(n\!-\!3)(n\!-\!1)H^{2}

(13.4.2)

\displaystyle=\bar{{\sf J}}_{0}+(n\!-\!3)\rho_{0}^{\prime}-(n\!-\!3)(n\!-\!1)H^{2}.

This result fits with (
13.4.1
) and with the formula
[
GW17
, (4.8)]
for its
integral. Using (
13.1.3
), we finally obtain

(13.4.3)

-2v_{2}={\sf J}+(n\!-\!2)\bar{{\sf P}}_{00}+\frac{2n\!-\!5}{2(n\!-\!1)}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-(n\!-\!2)(n\!-\!3/2)H^{2}.

In particular, for
n=2
, we have

(13.4.4)

-2v_{2}={\sf J}-\frac{1}{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}.

For closed
M
, the total integral of this quantity is a conformal invariant (by
Gauss-Bonnet). Note that
2v_{2}={\bf Q}_{2}
(by Example
13.9.1
) which confirms Theorem

10.9
for
n=2
.

Example 13.4.4
.

The equality of the coefficients of
s^{2}
in (
6.18
) yields the identity

3v_{3}-3v_{1}v_{2}+v_{1}^{3}=-\frac{n\!-\!5}{2}\rho^{\prime\prime}_{0}-\bar{{\sf J}^{\prime}}_{0}-4(n\!-\!2)\rho^{\prime}_{0}\rho_{0}-2\rho_{0}\bar{{\sf J}}_{0}-4(n\!-\!1)\rho_{0}^{3}.

Hence, using
\rho_{0}=-H
, we obtain

3v_{3}=3v_{1}v_{2}-v_{1}^{3}-\frac{n\!-\!5}{2}\rho^{\prime\prime}_{0}-\bar{{\sf J}}^{\prime}_{0}+4(n\!-\!2)\rho^{\prime}_{0}H+2H\bar{{\sf J}}_{0}+4(n\!-\!1)H^{3}.

Combining this formula with the formulas for
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
in Example
13.4.2
and Example

13.4.3
gives

6v_{3}=(n\!-\!5)(n\!-\!3)(n\!-\!1)H^{3}-(n\!-\!5)(3n\!-\!5)H\rho^{\prime}_{0}-(3n\!-\!7)H\bar{{\sf J}}_{0}-(n\!-\!5)\rho^{\prime\prime}_{0}-2\bar{{\sf J}}^{\prime}_{0}.

In particular, this formula implies

6v_{3}=-8H\bar{{\sf J}}_{0}-2\bar{{\sf J}}^{\prime}_{0}.

if
n=5
. This proves the relation (
11.3
) in Conjecture
11.4
for
n=5
. Finally, we note that

\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\rho)
corresponds to
\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}-2\rho_{0}\rho_{0}^{\prime}
(see Example
13.1.7
).
Hence we can rewrite the latter formula as

\displaystyle 6v_{3}

\displaystyle=(n\!-\!5)(n\!-\!3)(n\!-\!1)H^{3}-(3n\!-\!7)H\iota^{*}\bar{{\sf J}}

(13.4.5)

\displaystyle-(n\!-\!5)(3n\!-\!7)H\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\rho)-(n\!-\!5)\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\rho)-2\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{{\sf J}}).

In particular, for
n=3
we get

6v_{3}=-2H\iota^{*}\bar{{\sf J}}+4H\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\rho)+2\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\rho)-2\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\bar{{\sf J}}).

This quantity actually equals a multiple of
{\bf Q}_{3}
, up to a divergence term; for a discussion of
{\bf Q}_{3}

we refer to Example
13.9.2
. By
[
GW17
, Appendix B]
, the result (
13.4.4
) implies
that
31
31

31

There seems to be a misprint in the contribution of the term
H\iota^{*}{\sf J}
.

-\iota^{*}L(-n+1)L(-n+2)L(-n+3)(1)=6(n-1)(n-2)(n-3)v_{3},

up to a divergence term. Therefore, the result confirms the formula for
c_{3}
in
Theorem
10.11
. In order to express the sum in (
13.4.4
) in terms of
standard curvature terms, it remains to determine
\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{k}(\rho)
for

k=1,2
. Explicit formulas for these terms will be derived in Section
13.5
(Lemma
13.5.1
,
Lemma
13.5.3
). The case
k=2
was first treated in
[
GW21
, Lemma 6.8]
(see
Remark
13.5.4
). It is only here where we need the full information of Proposition

13.2.1
.

Of course, the renormalized volume coefficients
v_{k}
, which are defined in terms of adapted coordinates,
are to be distinguished from the renormalized volume coefficients
w_{k}
, which are defined in terms of
geodesic normal coordinates
[
G17
]
. By (
6
), the latter ones are defined by the
relation

w(r)=(1+\sigma_{(2)}r+\sigma_{(3)}r^{2}+\cdots)^{-(n+1)}u(r).

In particular, we find
w_{1}=-(n+1)\sigma_{(2)}+u_{1}=\frac{n-1}{2}H
. The above relation implies

w_{2}=6\sigma_{(2)}^{2}-3\sigma_{(3)}-3\sigma_{(2)}u_{1}+u_{2}

for
n=2
, and its evaluation yields

2w_{2}=-{\sf J}+\frac{1}{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}.

Note that
w_{1}=v_{1}
(for
n=1
) and
w_{2}=v_{2}
(for
n=2
). In general, the coefficients

w_{n}
and
v_{n}
differ by a non-trivial total divergence. In particular, we find

w_{3}=-20\sigma_{(2)}^{3}+20\sigma_{(2)}\sigma_{(3)}-4\sigma_{(4)}+10\sigma_{(2)}^{2}u_{1}-4\sigma_{(3)}u_{1}-4\sigma_{(2)}u_{2}+u_{3}

for
n=3
, and an evaluation yields

\displaystyle 6v_{3}

\displaystyle=6w_{3}+\Delta(H).

Explicit formulas for
w_{1},w_{2}
(in general dimensions) were derived in
[
G17
, (4.5)]
.
In Section
13.12
, these coefficients will be described in terms of
L
-operators.

13.5.
Low-order Taylor coefficients of
\rho

Assuming that
\sigma
satisfies the condition
{(\mathcal{SY})}
,
we derive formulas for the first few Taylor coefficients of
\rho
in the variable
s
.

We first use Lemma
13.3.2
to derive formulas for the restrictions of
\rho
and

\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\rho)
to
M
. The following result reproves part of
[
GW21
, Lemma 6.6]
.

Lemma 13.5.1
.

Let
n\geq 2
. Then
\iota^{*}\rho=-H
and

(13.5.1)

\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\rho)={\sf P}_{00}+\frac{|\mathring{L}|^{2}}{n-1}.

Proof.

We calculate in geodesic normal coordinates. We expand the defining relation

(13.5.2)

\Delta_{g}(\sigma)=-(n\!+\!1)\rho-\sigma{\sf J}

of
\rho
into a power series of
r
. The Laplacian takes the form
\partial_{r}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{r}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{r})\partial_{r}+\Delta_{h_{r}}
. Hence the restriction of (
13.5.2
) to
r=0
implies

2\sigma_{(2)}+nH=-(n+1)\rho_{0}
, and Lemma
13.3.2
yields the first assertion. Next,
we restrict the derivative of (
13.5.2
) in
r
to
r=0
. Then

6\sigma_{(3)}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{r}(\operatorname{tr}(h_{r}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{r}))|_{0}+\operatorname{tr}(h_{r}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{r})|_{0}\sigma_{(2)}=-(n\!+\!1)\partial_{r}(\rho)|_{0}-{\sf J}_{0}.

But (
13.2.1
) implies the identities

\operatorname{tr}(h_{r}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{r})|_{0}=2\operatorname{tr}(L)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\partial_{r}(\operatorname{tr}(h_{r}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{r}))|_{0}=-2|L|^{2}-2\operatorname{Ric}_{00}.

Hence the above relation transforms into

6\sigma_{(3)}-|L|^{2}-\operatorname{Ric}_{00}+2\operatorname{tr}(L)\sigma_{(2)}=-(n\!+\!1)\partial_{r}(\rho)|_{0}-{\sf J}_{0}.

We combine this result with (
13.1.2
) for
\operatorname{Ric}_{00}
, (
13.3.8
) for

\sigma_{(3)}
and
|L|^{2}=|\mathring{L}|^{2}+nH^{2}
to obtain

\partial_{r}(\rho)|_{0}=\iota^{*}({\sf J}^{g})-{\sf J}^{h}+\frac{|\mathring{L}|^{2}}{2(n\!-\!1)}+\frac{n}{2}H^{2}.

Now (
13.1.3
) implies the assertion.
∎

The equation (
13.5.1
) justifies the notation
\rho_{0}^{\prime}
in Proposition
13.2.1
.

Next, we provide an alternative proof of Lemma
13.5.1
. This illustrates the efficiency
of the differential equation for
\rho
(Lemma
6.3
), the resulting recursive formula
in Proposition
6.4
and the formula for the obstruction (Theorem
6.6
)
in low-order cases.

First of all, the restriction of (
6.20
) to
s=0
yields
n\rho(0)+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)})=0
. Using
h_{(1)}=2L
by (
13.2.3
), we get

\rho(0)=-H
. This reproves the first part of Lemma
13.5.1
. Next, the
formula (
6.22
) for
k=1
yields

(n-1)\rho^{\prime}_{0}-2\rho_{0}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}+\partial_{s}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}+{\sf J}_{0}=0

or, equivalently,

(13.5.3)

(n-1)\rho_{0}^{\prime}-\rho_{0}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)})+\operatorname{tr}(h_{(2)})-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)}^{2})+{\sf J}_{0}=0.

By (
13.2.3
) and
\operatorname{tr}(L^{2})=\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{2})+nH^{2}
, this gives

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(n-1)\rho_{0}^{\prime}+2nH^{2}+\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{2})-\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-2\operatorname{tr}(L^{2})+{\sf J}_{0}

\displaystyle=(n-1)\rho_{0}^{\prime}-\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-|\mathring{L}|^{2}+{\sf J}_{0}

\displaystyle=(n-1)\rho_{0}^{\prime}-(n-1){\sf P}_{00}-|\mathring{L}|^{2}.

This reproves the second part of Lemma
13.5.1
.

Remark 13.5.2
.

For
n=1
, Theorem
6.6
states that

{\mathcal{B}}_{1}=-\partial_{s}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}+2\rho_{0}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}-{\sf J}_{0}

or, equivalently,

{\mathcal{B}}_{1}=-\operatorname{tr}(h_{(2)})+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)}^{2})+\rho_{0}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)})-{\sf J}_{0}.

By
\mathring{L}=0
,
\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)})=2H
,
\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)}^{2})=4H^{2}
,
\operatorname{tr}(h_{(2)})=-\operatorname{Ric}_{00}

and
\rho_{0}=-H
, this formula simplifies to

{\mathcal{B}}_{1}=\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-{\sf J}_{0}=\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-K_{0}=0

using
\operatorname{Ric}=Kg
,
K
being the Gauss curvature of
g
.

We continue discussing the second-order derivative of
\rho
in general dimensions

n\geq 3
. The arguments are parallel to the discussion in Section
13.6
.
Proposition
6.4
for
k=2
gives

-(n\!-\!2)\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}=\partial_{s}^{2}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}-4\rho_{0}\partial_{s}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}-4\rho_{0}^{\prime}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}+2{\sf J}^{\prime}_{0}.

This formula is equivalent to

\displaystyle-(n\!-\!2)\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}

(13.5.4)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}(3h_{(3)}-3h_{(1)}h_{(2)}+h_{(1)}^{3})-4\rho_{0}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(2)})+2\rho_{0}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)}^{2})-2\rho_{0}^{\prime}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)})+2{\sf J}_{0}^{\prime}.

In order to make that formula explicit, we use Proposition
13.2.1
and in particular
Corollary
13.2.2
. We obtain

\displaystyle-(n\!-\!2)\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}=-\nabla_{\partial_{s}}(\operatorname{Ric})_{00}+2\partial_{s}({\sf J})-4\mathring{L}^{ij}R_{0ij0}+\Delta H-H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-3H|\mathring{L}|^{2}-2nH\rho_{0}^{\prime}

(13.5.5)

\displaystyle-6\operatorname{tr}(L^{2}\mathring{L})+6L^{ij}R_{0ij0}+8\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})+4H|\mathring{L}|^{2}-4H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-8H|L|^{2}.

Now, by the obvious relation

-\nabla_{\partial_{s}}(\operatorname{Ric})_{00}+2\partial_{s}({\sf J})=-\nabla_{\partial_{s}}(G)_{00}-(n-2)\partial_{s}({\sf J})

for the Einstein tensor
G=\operatorname{Ric}-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{scal}g
and the identity
(
13.6.5
), we get

\displaystyle-(n\!-\!2)\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\delta^{h}(\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{s},\cdot))-\mathring{L}^{ij}\operatorname{Ric}_{ij}+(n\!+\!1)H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-H\operatorname{scal}-(n\!-\!2){\sf J}_{0}^{\prime}-2nH\rho_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle-4\mathring{L}^{ij}R_{0ij0}+\Delta H-H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-3H|\mathring{L}|^{2}

\displaystyle-6\operatorname{tr}(L^{2}\mathring{L})+6L^{ij}R_{0ij0}+8\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})+4H|\mathring{L}|^{2}-4H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-8H|L|^{2}.

The decomposition (
13.6.2
) shows that

\displaystyle L^{ij}R_{0ij0}

\displaystyle=L^{ij}(W_{0ij0}+{\sf P}_{ij}+{\sf P}_{00}g_{ij})

\displaystyle=L^{ij}W_{0ij0}+L^{ij}{\sf P}_{ij}+nH{\sf P}_{00}

\displaystyle=L^{ij}W_{0ij0}+\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}_{ij}+H({\sf J}-{\sf P}_{00})+nH{\sf P}_{00}

\displaystyle=L^{ij}W_{0ij0}+\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}_{ij}+H{\sf J}+(n-1)H{\sf P}_{00}

\displaystyle=\mathring{L}^{ij}W_{0ij0}+\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}_{ij}+H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}.

Similarly, we find

\mathring{L}^{ij}R_{0ij0}=\mathring{L}^{ij}W_{0ij0}+\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}_{ij}.

The latter two results and the formula (
13.5.1
) for
\rho_{0}^{\prime}
imply

\displaystyle-(n\!-\!2)\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\delta^{h}(\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{s},\cdot))-\mathring{L}^{ij}\operatorname{Ric}_{ij}+(n\!+\!1)H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-H\operatorname{scal}-(n\!-\!2){\sf J}_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle-4\mathring{L}^{ij}W_{0ij0}-4\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}_{ij}+\Delta H-H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-3H|\mathring{L}|^{2}

\displaystyle-6\operatorname{tr}(L^{2}\mathring{L})+6\mathring{L}^{ij}W_{0ij0}+6\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}_{ij}+6H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}

\displaystyle+8\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})+4H|\mathring{L}|^{2}-4H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-8H|L|^{2}-2nH\left({\sf P}_{00}+\frac{|\mathring{L}|^{2}}{n\!-\!1}\right).

Simplification gives

\displaystyle-(n\!-\!2)\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\delta^{h}(\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{s},\cdot))+\Delta H+2\mathring{L}^{ij}W_{0ij0}+2\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}_{ij}-\mathring{L}^{ij}\operatorname{Ric}_{ij}

\displaystyle+(n\!+\!2)H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-2nH{\sf P}_{00}-H\operatorname{scal}

\displaystyle+H|\mathring{L}|^{2}-8H|L|^{2}-6\operatorname{tr}(L^{2}\mathring{L})+8\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})-\frac{2n}{n\!-\!1}H|\mathring{L}|^{2}-(n\!-\!2){\sf J}_{0}^{\prime}.

Further simplification using

|L|^{2}=|\mathring{L}|^{2}+nH^{2},\quad\operatorname{tr}(L^{2}\mathring{L})=2H|\mathring{L}|^{2}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})=\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})+3H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+nH^{3}

yields

\displaystyle-(n\!-\!2)\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\delta^{h}(\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{s},\cdot))+\Delta H+2(\mathring{L}^{ij}W_{0ij0}+\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3}))-(n\!-\!3)\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}^{g}_{ij}

(13.5.6)

\displaystyle+\frac{(n\!-\!2)(n\!+\!1)}{n\!-\!1}H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-\frac{n\!-\!2}{n\!-\!1}H\operatorname{scal}+\frac{3n\!-\!5}{n\!-\!1}H|\mathring{L}|^{2}-(n\!-\!2){\sf J}_{0}^{\prime}.

Next, we apply the basic identity (
13.1.1
). It implies that

(13.5.7)

\mathring{L}^{ij}W_{0ij0}+\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}(n-2)\mathring{L}^{ij}\mathcal{F}_{ij}.

Now, combining the formula (
13.6.3
) for
\Delta H
and (
13.5.7
) with (
13.5
), yields

\displaystyle-(n\!-\!2)\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{n\!-\!2}{n\!-\!1}\delta^{h}(\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{s},\cdot))+\frac{1}{n\!-\!1}\delta\delta(\mathring{L})+2(n\!-\!2)\mathring{L}^{ij}\mathcal{F}_{ij}-(n\!-\!3)\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}^{g}_{ij}

\displaystyle+\frac{(n\!-\!2)(n\!+\!1)}{n\!-\!1}H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-\frac{n\!-\!2}{n\!-\!1}H\operatorname{scal}+\frac{3n\!-\!5}{n\!-\!1}H|\mathring{L}|^{2}-(n\!-\!2){\sf J}_{0}^{\prime}.

Hence

\displaystyle\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{(n\!-\!1)(n\!-\!2)}\delta\delta(\mathring{L})-\frac{1}{n\!-\!1}\delta^{h}(\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{s},\cdot))-2\mathring{L}^{ij}\mathcal{F}_{ij}+\frac{n\!-\!3}{n\!-\!2}\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}^{g}_{ij}

\displaystyle-\frac{n\!+\!1}{n\!-\!1}H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}+\frac{1}{n\!-\!1}H\operatorname{scal}-\frac{3n\!-\!5}{(n\!-\!1)(n\!-\!2)}H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+{\sf J}_{0}^{\prime}.

Finally, we use the defining relation
\iota^{*}{\sf P}^{g}=\mathcal{F}+{\sf P}^{h}-H\mathring{L}-1/2hH^{2}
of
\mathcal{F}

to replace in this identity the Schouten tensor
{\sf P}^{g}
by the Schouten tensor
{\sf P}^{h}
. Thus,
we have proved the following result.

Lemma 13.5.3
.

For
n\geq 3
, it holds

\displaystyle\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{(n\!-\!1)(n\!-\!2)}\delta\delta(\mathring{L})-\frac{1}{n\!-\!1}\delta^{h}(\operatorname{Ric}^{g}(\partial_{s},\cdot))-\frac{n\!-\!1}{n\!-\!2}(\mathring{L},\mathcal{F})+\frac{n\!-\!3}{n\!-\!2}(\mathring{L},{\sf P}^{h})

(13.5.8)

\displaystyle-\frac{n\!+\!1}{n\!-\!1}H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}+\frac{1}{n\!-\!1}H\operatorname{scal}-\frac{n\!+\!1}{n\!-\!1}H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+{\sf J}_{0}^{\prime}.

In Section
13.9
, we shall connect this result with the holographic formula for
{\bf Q}_{3}
.

Remark 13.5.4
.

Lemma
13.5.3
is equivalent to
[
GW21
, Remark 6.9]
and
[
GW20
, Remark 3.11]
,
up to the sign of the term
(\mathring{L},{\sf P}^{h})
. In fact, the quoted result calculates the restriction of

\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\rho)
to
M
. By Example
13.1.7
, it corresponds to
\rho^{\prime\prime}_{0}+2H\rho_{0}^{\prime}
.
But (
13.5.3
) implies

\displaystyle\rho^{\prime\prime}_{0}+2H\rho_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\cdots)-\frac{n\!+\!1}{n\!-\!1}H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}+\frac{1}{n\!-\!1}H\operatorname{scal}-\frac{n\!+\!1}{n\!-\!1}H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+{\sf J}_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle+2H\left(\frac{1}{n\!-\!1}\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-\frac{1}{n\!-\!1}{\sf J}_{0}+\frac{|\mathring{L}|^{2}}{n\!-\!1}\right)+{\sf J}_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\cdots)-H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}+2H{\sf J}_{0}-H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+{\sf J}_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\cdots)-H((n\!-\!1){\sf P}_{00}+|\mathring{L}|^{2})+H{\sf J}_{0}+{\sf J}_{0}^{\prime},

where
(\cdots)
indicates the four terms in the first line of (
13.5.3
). This proves the claim. The
present alternative proof rests on the recursive formula in Proposition
6.4
and the explicit
formula for
h_{(3)}
in Proposition
13.2.1
.

Remark 13.5.5
.

The proof of Proposition
13.2.1
shows that the calculation of the coefficient
h_{(2)}
involves

h_{(1)}
and
\rho_{0}
. Once
h_{(2)}
has been determined, we calculated
\rho_{0}^{\prime}
using (
13.5.3
).
Similarly, the calculation of
h_{(3)}
in the proof of Lemma
13.2.1
involves the lower-order coefficients
of
h_{s}
and
\rho_{0}
,
\rho_{0}^{\prime}
. Once
h_{(3)}
has been determined, the recursive formula (
13.5
)
yields
\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}
. A continuation of that iterative process yields the higher-order coefficients, at least in principle.

We finish this section with some comments concerning the functions
\rho
and the
singular Yamabe obstructions in Examples
13.2.3
–
13.2.4
. In Example

13.2.3
, it holds
\mathring{v}^{\prime}/\mathring{v}=-n/(1-2s)
. Hence the
differential equation (
6.20
) reduces to

-s\rho^{\prime}+n\rho-n(1-2s\rho)/(1-2s)=0.

One readily checks that
\rho=1
is the unique solution with initial value
\rho(0)=1
. The vanishing of the obstruction is reproduced by the formula
(
6.24
). Indeed, we obtain

(n+1)!{\mathcal{B}}_{n}=2n\partial_{s}^{n}(1/(1-2s))|_{0}-4n^{2}\partial_{s}^{n-1}(1/(1-2s))|_{0}=2n2^{n}n!-4n^{2}2^{n-1}(n-1)!=0.

Similarly, in Example
13.2.4
, it holds
\mathring{v}^{\prime}/\mathring{v}=-ns/(1-s^{2})
and
the differential equation (
6.20
) reduces to

-s\rho^{\prime}+n\rho-ns(1-2s\rho)/(1-s^{2})+\tfrac{n+1}{2}s=0.

One easily checks that
\rho=\frac{1}{2}s
is the unique solution with the
initial value
\rho(0)=0
. Again, the vanishing of the obstruction is reproduced by

(n+1)!{\mathcal{B}}_{n}=2\partial_{s}^{n}(ns/(1-s^{2}))|_{0}-4\binom{n}{2}\partial_{s}^{n-2}(ns/(1-s^{2}))|_{0}=2nn!-4n\binom{n}{2}(n-2)!=0

for odd
n
and trivially for even
n
.

13.6.
The obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
for general backgrounds

In the present section, we prove the equivalence of both formulas for the singular
Yamabe obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
displayed in (
3
) and derive the second of these
formulas from Theorem
6
.

The Codazzi-Mainardi identity states that

(13.6.1)

\nabla^{h}_{Y}(L)(X,Z)-\nabla^{h}_{X}(L)(Y,Z)=R^{g}(X,Y,Z,N)

for
X,Y,Z\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
if
L(X,Y)=-h(\nabla^{g}_{X}(Y),N)
for some unit
normal vector field
N

[
B87
, Theorem 1.72]
. Now, we decompose the curvature
tensor
R
as

(13.6.2)

\displaystyle R(X,Y,Z,W)=W(X,Y,Z,W)

\displaystyle+{\sf P}(Y,Z)g(X,W)-{\sf P}(X,Z)g(Y,W)-{\sf P}(Y,W)g(X,Z)+{\sf P}(X,W)g(Y,Z),

where
W
is the trace-free Weyl tensor (see (
13.1.6
)), and take traces in
(
13.6.1
) in the arguments
X,Z
. Then

n\langle dH,Y\rangle-\langle\delta^{h}(L),Y\rangle=-(n-1){\sf P}(N,Y).

Hence

\delta^{h}(\mathring{L})-(n-1)dH=(n-1){\sf P}(N,\cdot)

(see
[Lemma 6.25.2]J1). Now, taking a further divergence, yields

(13.6.3)

\delta^{h}\delta^{h}(\mathring{L})-(n-1)\Delta H=(n-1)\delta^{h}({\sf P}(N,\cdot)).

For
n=2
, this proves the equivalence of both formulas in (
3
).

We continue by showing that the second formula for
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
in (
3
) is a special
case of Theorem
6
. First, we observe that the formula

-3{\mathcal{B}}_{2}=\partial_{s}^{2}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}-4\rho_{0}\partial_{s}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}-4\rho_{0}^{\prime}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}+2{\sf J}_{0}^{\prime}

in Theorem
6
is equivalent to

-3{\mathcal{B}}_{2}=\operatorname{tr}(3h_{(3)}-3h_{(1)}h_{(2)}+h_{(1)}^{3})-4\rho_{0}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(2)})+2\rho_{0}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)}^{2})-2\rho_{0}^{\prime}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)})+2{\sf J}_{0}^{\prime}.

In order to make that sum explicit, we use the results in Proposition
13.2.1
.
By Corollary
13.2.2
for
n=2
and
\rho_{0}=-H
, we obtain

(13.6.4)

\displaystyle-3{\mathcal{B}}_{2}

\displaystyle=-\nabla^{g}_{\partial_{s}}(\operatorname{Ric})_{00}+2\partial_{s}({\sf J})-4\mathring{L}^{ik}R_{0ik0}+\Delta H-H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-3H|\mathring{L}|^{2}

\displaystyle-6\operatorname{tr}(L^{2}\mathring{L})+6L^{ij}R_{0ij0}+8\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})+4H|\mathring{L}|^{2}-4H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-8H|L|^{2}-4H\rho_{0}^{\prime}.

Now we prove the identity

(13.6.5)

\nabla^{g}_{\partial_{s}}(G)_{00}=-\delta^{h}(\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{s},\cdot))+\mathring{L}^{ij}\operatorname{Ric}_{ij}-(n+1)H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}+H\operatorname{scal}

for the Einstein tensor
G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\operatorname{Ric}-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{scal}g
. Note that
G=\operatorname{Ric}-2{\sf J}g
in dimension
n=3
. It is well-known that the second Bianchi identity implies the
relation
2\delta^{g}(\operatorname{Ric})=d\operatorname{scal}
. Hence

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}_{\partial_{s}}(\operatorname{Ric})(\partial_{s},\partial_{s})

\displaystyle=\delta^{g}(\operatorname{Ric})(\partial_{s})-g^{ij}\nabla^{g}_{\partial_{i}}(\operatorname{Ric})(\partial_{j},\partial_{s})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle d\operatorname{scal},\partial_{s}\rangle

\displaystyle-g^{ij}\partial_{i}(\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{j},\partial_{s}))+g^{ij}\operatorname{Ric}(\nabla^{g}_{\partial_{i}}(\partial_{j}),\partial_{s})+g^{ij}\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{j},\nabla^{g}_{\partial_{i}}(\partial_{s}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle d\operatorname{scal},\partial_{s}\rangle

\displaystyle-h^{ij}\partial_{i}(\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{j},\partial_{s}))+h^{ij}\operatorname{Ric}(\nabla^{h}_{\partial_{i}}(\partial_{j})-L_{ij}\partial_{s},\partial_{s})+h^{ij}\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{j},\nabla^{g}_{\partial_{i}}(\partial_{s}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle d\operatorname{scal},\partial_{s}\rangle-\delta^{h}(\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{s},\cdot))-nH\operatorname{Ric}_{00}+h^{ij}\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{j},\nabla^{g}_{\partial_{i}}(\partial_{s}))

on
M
. Therefore, using
\nabla_{\partial_{i}}^{g}(\partial_{s})=L_{i}^{k}\partial_{k}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}_{\partial_{s}}(G)_{00}

\displaystyle=-\delta^{h}(\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{s},\cdot))-nH\operatorname{Ric}_{00}+h^{ij}L_{i}^{k}\operatorname{Ric}_{jk}

\displaystyle=-\delta^{h}(\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{s},\cdot))-nH\operatorname{Ric}_{00}+L^{ij}\operatorname{Ric}_{ij}.

This implies (
13.6.5
).

Next, we observe that the decomposition (
13.6.2
) yields

\displaystyle L^{ij}R_{0ij0}

\displaystyle=L^{ij}(W_{0ij0}+{\sf P}_{ij}+{\sf P}_{00}g_{ij})

\displaystyle=L^{ij}{\sf P}_{ij}+2H{\sf P}_{00}

\displaystyle=\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}_{ij}+H({\sf J}-{\sf P}_{00})+2H{\sf P}_{00}=\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}_{ij}+H{\sf J}+H{\sf P}_{00}

\displaystyle=\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}_{ij}+H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}

since the Weyl tensor
W
vanishes in dimension
3
. Similarly, we find

\mathring{L}^{ij}R_{0ij0}=\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}_{ij}.

These results and the formula (
13.5.1
) for
\rho_{0}^{\prime}
show that the sum
(
13.6.4
) simplifies to (recall that
n=2
)

\displaystyle\delta^{h}(\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{s},\cdot))-\mathring{L}^{ij}\operatorname{Ric}_{ij}+3H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-H\operatorname{scal}-4\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}_{ij}+\Delta H-H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-3H|\mathring{L}|^{2}

\displaystyle-6\operatorname{tr}(L^{2}\mathring{L})+6\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}_{ij}+6H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}+8\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})+4H|\mathring{L}|^{2}-4H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}-8H|L|^{2}

\displaystyle-4H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}+H\operatorname{scal}-4H|\mathring{L}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\delta^{h}(\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{s},\cdot))+\Delta H+\mathring{L}^{ij}\operatorname{Ric}_{ij},

up to terms that are at least quadratic in
L
. In order to deal with these terms, we note that

|L|^{2}=|\mathring{L}|^{2}+2H^{2},\quad\operatorname{tr}(L^{2}\mathring{L})=2H|\mathring{L}|^{2}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})=3H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+2H^{3}

using
\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})=0
. It follows that the remaining terms are

(-3-12+24+4-8-4)H|\mathring{L}|^{2}=H|\mathring{L}|^{2}
. Summarizing we obtain

-3{\mathcal{B}}_{2}=\delta^{h}(\operatorname{Ric}(\partial_{s},\cdot))+\Delta H+\mathring{L}^{ij}\operatorname{Ric}_{ij}+H|\mathring{L}|^{2}.

This proves the second formula for
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
in (
3
).

Remark 13.6.1
.

The identity (
13.6.5
) is equivalent to
[
GW21
, Lemma B.7]
or

[
GW20
, Lemma A.7]
. In addition to the derivation of the above formula
for the singular Yamabe obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
, in these references, this crucial identity is used to
prove a formula for the second normal derivative of
\rho
and a formula for

{\bf Q}_{3}
. The calculations lead to the same results as here. Our discussion of the second normal
derivative of
\rho
and the explicit formula for
{\bf Q}_{3}
is contained in Sections

13.5
and
13.9
. It uses the identity (
13.6.5
).

13.7.
The obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
for conformally flat backgrounds

We first evaluate Theorem
6
for the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
of a three-manifold
M\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
.
The resulting formula is equivalent to a formula in
[
GW21
]
(see Lemma
13.7.9
). This will imply a
formula for
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
in a conformally flat background.

Proposition 13.7.1
.

The Yamabe obstruction of a hypersurface
(M^{3},h)
in the four-dimensional flat space
{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
is given by the formula

(13.7.1)

12{\mathcal{B}}_{3}=\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})+12|dH|^{2}+6(\mathring{L},\operatorname{Hess}(H))-2|\mathring{L}|^{4}+6\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4})+6H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})

or, equivalently,

(13.7.2)

12{\mathcal{B}}_{3}=\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})+12|dH|^{2}+6(\mathring{L},\operatorname{Hess}(H))+|\mathring{L}|^{4}+6H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3}).

Here the Hessian, the Laplacian, scalar products, and traces are taken with respect to the metric
h
on
M
.

Newton’s identity

24\sigma_{4}(L)=\operatorname{tr}(L)^{4}-6\operatorname{tr}(L)^{2}|L|^{2}+3|L|^{4}+8\operatorname{tr}(L)\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})-6\operatorname{tr}(L^{4})

for the elementary symmetric polynomial
\sigma_{4}(L)
of the eigenvalues of
L
(or rather of the shape
operator) implies

\displaystyle 24\sigma_{4}(L)

\displaystyle=n(n-1)(n-2)(n-3)H^{4}-6(n-2)(n-3)H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+8(n-3)H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})

\displaystyle+3(|\mathring{L}|^{4}-2\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4})).

Since
\sigma_{4}(L)=0
in dimension
n=3
, we get

Corollary 13.7.2
.

|\mathring{L}|^{4}=2\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4})
for
n=3
.

Corollary
13.7.2
implies the equivalence of (
13.7.1
) and (
13.7.2
).

We derive formula (
13.7.1
) as a consequence of the identity

(13.7.3)

12{\mathcal{B}}_{3}=-\partial_{s}^{3}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}+6\rho_{0}\partial_{s}^{2}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}+12\rho_{0}^{\prime}\partial_{s}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}+6\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}-3\partial_{s}^{2}(\bar{{\sf J}})|_{0}

in Theorem
6
. Note that, for a flat background, the last term vanishes. By the relations

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)}),

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(2h_{(2)}-h_{(1)}^{2}),

\displaystyle\partial_{s}^{2}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}

\displaystyle=2!\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(3h_{(3)}-3h_{(1)}h_{(2)}+h_{(1)}^{3}),

\displaystyle\partial_{s}^{3}\left(\frac{\mathring{v}^{\prime}}{\mathring{v}}\right)|_{0}

\displaystyle=3!\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(4h_{(4)}-4h_{(1)}h_{(3)}-2h_{(2)}^{2}+4h_{(1)}^{2}h_{(2)}-h_{(1)}^{4}),

the identity (
13.7.3
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle 12{\mathcal{B}}_{3}

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{tr}(12h_{(4)}-12h_{(1)}h_{(3)}-6h_{(2)}^{2}+12h_{(1)}^{2}h_{(2)}-3h_{(1)}^{4})

\displaystyle+6\rho_{0}\operatorname{tr}(3h_{(3)}-3h_{(1)}h_{(2)}+h_{(1)}^{3})

\displaystyle+6\rho_{0}^{\prime}\operatorname{tr}(2h_{(2)}-h_{(1)}^{2})

(13.7.4)

\displaystyle+3\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)}).

In order to evaluate that sum, we use the formulas for
h_{(k)}
(
k\leq 3
) in
Proposition
13.2.1
and the formulas for the first two normal derivatives
of
\rho
in Section
13.5
. In addition, it remains to determine the
coefficient
\operatorname{tr}(h_{(4)})
. The following result even provides a closed formula for
the Taylor coefficient
h_{(4)}
of
h_{s}
(for a flat background).

Lemma 13.7.3
.

Let
n=3
. Then

\displaystyle 12h_{(4)}

\displaystyle=-9H\operatorname{Hess}(H)+L\operatorname{Hess}(H)+\operatorname{Hess}(H)L-6dH\otimes dH-\gamma

(13.7.5)

\displaystyle-\operatorname{Hess}(|\mathring{L}|^{2})+4\mathring{L}^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+15H^{2}({(\mathring{L}^{2})}_{\circ}+H\mathring{L})-H\mathring{L}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+3L\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime},

where

(13.7.6)

\gamma_{jk}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}2h^{lm}(\nabla_{j}(L)_{km}+\nabla_{k}(L)_{jm}-\nabla_{m}(L)_{jk})\partial_{l}(H)

and

(13.7.7)

\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}=-\Delta(H)-2\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})-2H|\mathring{L}|^{2}.

Proof.

We use the same notation as in the proof of Proposition
13.2.1
. We expand the curvature
components
R_{0jk}^{0}
of the metric

\eta^{*}(g)=a^{-1}ds^{2}+h+h_{(1)}s+h_{(2)}s^{2}+h_{(3)}s^{3}+h_{(4)}s^{4}+\cdots

into power series of
s
. We recall that
a=\eta^{*}(|\mathcal{N}|^{2})
. In order to simplify the notation, we
write
g
for the metric
\eta^{*}(g)
and identify
\eta^{*}(\rho)
with
\rho
. By assumption, it holds

a=1-2s\rho
. As usual, the
0
-components refer to
\partial_{s}
. Since
g
is flat, the components

R_{0jk}^{0}
vanish. Now, for the above metric, we find the Christoffel symbols

\Gamma_{ij}^{0}=-\frac{1}{2}g^{00}g_{ij}^{\prime},\quad\Gamma_{0j}^{0}=\frac{1}{2}g^{00}\partial_{j}(g_{00}),\quad\Gamma_{00}^{0}=\frac{1}{2}g^{00}g_{00}^{\prime},\quad\Gamma_{0k}^{l}=\frac{1}{2}g^{rl}g_{kr}^{\prime},

where
′
denotes the derivative in
s
. We recall that
g^{00}=a=1-2s\rho
(by assumption) and

g_{00}=a^{-1}=1+2s\rho+\cdots
. Hence

\displaystyle 0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}R_{0jk}^{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Gamma_{jk}^{l}g^{00}(g_{00})_{l}+\frac{1}{4}g^{00}g^{rl}g_{kr}^{\prime}g_{jl}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}((g^{00})^{\prime}g_{jk}^{\prime}+g^{00}g_{jk}^{\prime\prime})

(13.7.8)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}((g^{00})_{j}(g_{00})_{k}+g^{00}(g_{00})_{kj})-\frac{1}{4}(g^{00})^{2}g_{jk}^{\prime}g_{00}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{4}(g^{00})^{2}(g_{00})_{j}(g_{00})_{k}.

Now we display the Taylor expansions of all
6
terms in (
13.7
) up to order
s^{2}
.
Using these results, it is easy to see that the coefficients of
s
reproduce the result of
the earlier calculation of
h_{(3)}
in the proof of Proposition
13.2.1
(Remark
13.7.7
).

First, we observe that the Christoffel symbols
\Gamma_{jk}^{l}
for
g
restrict to the Christoffel
symbols
\Gamma_{jk}^{l}
for
h
. Moreover, we recall the general variation formula

\delta(\Gamma_{jk}^{l})=\frac{1}{2}g^{lm}(\nabla_{j}(\delta(g))_{km}+\nabla_{k}(\delta(g))_{jm}-\nabla_{m}(\delta(g))_{jk}).

Let
(\Gamma_{jk}^{l})^{\prime}=\delta(\Gamma_{jk}^{l})
denote the variation of the Christoffel symbols for the variation

g=h+2Ls+\cdots
. By
g_{00}=1+2s\rho+\cdots
and
\rho_{0}=-H
, it follows that the first term in (
13.2
)
contributes by

\displaystyle s^{2}\frac{1}{2}(\Gamma_{jk}^{l})^{\prime}(-2\partial_{l}(H))

\displaystyle=-s^{2}(\Gamma_{jk}^{l})^{\prime}\partial_{l}(H)

\displaystyle=-s^{2}h^{lm}(\nabla_{j}(L)_{km}+\nabla_{k}(L)_{jm}-\nabla_{m}(L)_{jk})\partial_{l}(H)

(13.7.9)

\displaystyle=-s^{2}\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{jk}.

The remaining contributions of the first term in (
13.2
) match with the contributions by the second part
of the fourth term to

(13.7.10)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}s[\operatorname{Hess}(2\rho_{0})]-\frac{1}{2}s^{2}\left[\operatorname{Hess}(2\rho_{0}^{\prime}+4\rho_{0}^{2})-2\rho_{0}\operatorname{Hess}(2\rho_{0})\right]+\cdots

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle 24h_{(4)}

\displaystyle=-4\operatorname{Hess}(\rho_{0}^{\prime})-8\operatorname{Hess}(\rho_{0}^{2})+8\rho_{0}\operatorname{Hess}(\rho_{0})-2\gamma

\displaystyle+\big[3h_{(1)}h_{(3)}+3h_{(3)}h_{(1)}+4h_{(2)}^{2}-5h_{(1)}^{2}h_{(2)}+h_{(1)}^{4}+2h_{(1)}^{3}\rho_{0}-8h_{(1)}h_{(2)}\rho_{0}-2h_{(1)}^{2}\rho_{0}^{\prime}\big]

\displaystyle+30h_{(3)}\rho_{0}+16h_{(2)}\rho_{0}^{\prime}+3h_{(1)}\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle+4d\rho_{0}\otimes d\rho_{0}.

Now we apply the known formulas for

(

\displaystyle 3h_{(1)}h_{(3)}+3h_{(3)}h_{(1)}+4h_{(2)}^{2}-5h_{(1)}^{2}h_{(2)}+h_{(1)}^{4}+2h_{(1)}^{3}\rho_{0}-8h_{(1)}h_{(2)}\rho_{0}-2h_{(1)}^{2}\rho_{0}^{\prime}

(13.7.18)

\displaystyle=2L\operatorname{Hess}(H)+2\operatorname{Hess}(H)L.

In fact, we calculate

\displaystyle 3h_{(1)}h_{(3)}+3h_{(3)}h_{(1)}

\displaystyle=2L\operatorname{Hess}(H)+2\operatorname{Hess}(H)L-6HL^{2}\mathring{L}+2L^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-6HL\mathring{L}L+2L^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}
