Moreover, we get

\displaystyle 4h_{(2)}^{2}-5h_{(1)}^{2}h_{(2)}+h_{(1)}^{4}

\displaystyle=4\mathring{L}^{4}+8H\mathring{L}^{3}+4H^{2}\mathring{L}^{2}-20\mathring{L}^{4}-60H\mathring{L}^{3}-60H^{2}\mathring{L}^{2}-20H^{3}\mathring{L}

\displaystyle+16\mathring{L}^{4}+64H\mathring{L}^{3}+96H^{2}\mathring{L}^{2}+64H^{3}\mathring{L}+16H^{4}\operatorname{Id}

\displaystyle=12H\mathring{L}^{3}+40H^{2}\mathring{L}^{2}+44H^{3}\mathring{L}+16H^{4}\operatorname{Id}.

Summing these identities proves (
13.7
).

The above results imply

\displaystyle 12h_{(4)}

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{Hess}(|\mathring{L}|^{2})-4\operatorname{Hess}(H^{2})+4H\operatorname{Hess}(H)+2dH\otimes dH-\gamma

\displaystyle+L\operatorname{Hess}(H)+\operatorname{Hess}(H)L+\alpha

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{Hess}(|\mathring{L}|^{2})+L\operatorname{Hess}(H)+\operatorname{Hess}(H)L-8H\operatorname{Hess}(H)-6dH\otimes dH-\gamma+\alpha,

where

\displaystyle\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}15h_{(3)}\rho_{0}+8h_{(2)}\rho_{0}^{\prime}+3/2h_{(1)}\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=-5H\operatorname{Hess}(H)+15H^{2}L\mathring{L}-5HL|\mathring{L}|^{2}+4L\mathring{L}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+3L\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=-5H\operatorname{Hess}(H)+15H^{2}\mathring{L}^{2}+15H^{3}\mathring{L}-5H\mathring{L}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-5H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}\operatorname{Id}+4\mathring{L}^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+4H\mathring{L}|\mathring{L}|^{2}

\displaystyle+3L\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=-5H\operatorname{Hess}(H)+4\mathring{L}^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+5H^{2}(3\mathring{L}^{2}-|\mathring{L}|^{2}\operatorname{Id})-H\mathring{L}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+15H^{3}\mathring{L}+3L\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}.

Summarizing the last two results implies the first assertion.

The formula for
\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}
in
n=3
is a direct consequence of the formula for
\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}
for general
n

(Lemma
13.5.3
) using
(\mathring{L},\mathcal{F})=\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})
and
\delta\delta(\mathring{L})=2\Delta(H)
(by Codazzi-Mainardi).
∎

Lemma
13.7.3
implies

Corollary 13.7.4
.

12\operatorname{tr}(h_{(4)})=-\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})-9H\Delta(H)+2(L,\operatorname{Hess}(H))-12|dH|^{2}+4|\mathring{L}|^{4}+9H\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}.

Proof.

It only remains to prove that

(13.7.19)

\operatorname{tr}(\gamma)=6(dH,dH).

But

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=2h^{jk}h^{lm}(\nabla_{j}(L)_{km}+\nabla_{k}(L)_{jm}-\nabla_{m}(L)_{jk})\partial_{l}(H)

\displaystyle=2h^{lm}(\delta(L)_{m}+\delta(L)_{m}-\nabla_{m}(\operatorname{tr}(L)))\partial_{l}(H)

\displaystyle=6h^{lm}\partial_{m}(H)\partial_{l}(H)

\displaystyle=6(dH,dH)

by
\delta(L)=3dH
(Codazzi-Mainardi). The proof is complete.
∎

We proceed with the evaluation of (
13.7
).

Lemma 13.7.5
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}(12h_{(1)}h_{(3)}+6h_{(2)}^{2}-12h_{(1)}^{2}h_{(2)}+3h_{(1)}^{4})

\displaystyle=8(L,\operatorname{Hess}(H))+6\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4})+8|\mathring{L}|^{4}+36H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})+126H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+144H^{4}.

Proof.

By the known formulas for the coefficients
h_{(k)}
for
k\leq 3
, we find

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}(12h_{(1)}h_{(3)}+6h_{(2)}^{2}-12h_{(1)}^{2}h_{(2)}+3h_{(1)}^{4})

\displaystyle=8\operatorname{tr}(L\operatorname{Hess}(H))-24H\operatorname{tr}(L^{2}\mathring{L})+8\operatorname{tr}(L^{2})|\mathring{L}|^{2}+6\operatorname{tr}(L^{2}\mathring{L}^{2})-48\operatorname{tr}(L^{3}\mathring{L})+48\operatorname{tr}(L^{4}).

The latter sum equals the sum of
8\operatorname{tr}(L\operatorname{Hess}(H))
and

\displaystyle-24H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3}+2H\mathring{L}^{2})+8\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{2}+2H\mathring{L}+H^{2}\operatorname{Id})|\mathring{L}|^{2}+6\operatorname{tr}((\mathring{L}^{2}+2H\mathring{L}+H^{2})\mathring{L}^{2})

\displaystyle-48\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4}+3H\mathring{L}^{3}+3H^{2}\mathring{L}^{2})+48\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4}+4H\mathring{L}^{3}+6H^{2}\mathring{L}^{2}+H^{4}\operatorname{Id}).

The result follows by simplification.
∎

The following result evaluates the lower-order terms in (
13.7
).

Lemma 13.7.6
.

\displaystyle 6\rho_{0}\operatorname{tr}(3h_{(3)}-3h_{(1)}h_{(2)}+h_{(1)}^{3})+6\rho_{0}^{\prime}\operatorname{tr}(2h_{(2)}-h_{(1)}^{2})+3\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}\operatorname{tr}(h_{(1)})

\displaystyle=-6H\Delta(H)-6|\mathring{L}|^{4}-12H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})-108H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-144H^{4}+18H\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}.

Proof.

By
3\operatorname{tr}(h_{(3)})=\Delta(H)
, the sum equals

\displaystyle-6H(\Delta(H)-6\operatorname{tr}(L^{2}\mathring{L})+8\operatorname{tr}(L^{3}))+6|\mathring{L}|^{2}\operatorname{tr}(L\mathring{L}-2L^{2})+18H\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=-6H\Delta(H)+36H\operatorname{tr}(L^{2}\mathring{L})-48H\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})-6|\mathring{L}|^{4}-36H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+18H\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=-6H\Delta(H)+36H(\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})+2H|\mathring{L}|^{2})-48(H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})+3H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+3H^{4})

\displaystyle-6|\mathring{L}|^{4}-36H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+18H\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}.

Simplification completes the proof.
∎

Now we summarize the above results. We obtain

\displaystyle 12{\mathcal{B}}_{3}

\displaystyle=\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})+9H\Delta(H)-2(L,\operatorname{Hess}(H))+12|dH|^{2}-4|\mathring{L}|^{4}-9H\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle+8(L,\operatorname{Hess}(H))+6\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4})+8|\mathring{L}|^{4}+36H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})+126H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+144H^{4}

\displaystyle-6H\Delta(H)-6|\mathring{L}|^{4}-12H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})-108H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-144H^{4}+18H\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})+12|dH|^{2}+6(\mathring{L},\operatorname{Hess}(H))-2|\mathring{L}|^{4}+6\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4})

\displaystyle+9H\Delta(H)+18H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+24H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})+9H\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}.

The relation
\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}=-\Delta(H)-2\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})-2H|\mathring{L}|^{2}
implies the assertion. This completes
the proof of (
13.7.1
).

Remark 13.7.7
.

The linear terms in the expansions (
13.7.10
)-(
13.7.15
) of Christoffel symbols show that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hess}(H)+\frac{1}{4}(2h_{(1)}h_{(2)}+2h_{(2)}h_{(1)}-h_{(1)}^{3}-2\rho_{0}h_{(1)}^{2})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}(6h_{(3)}-8\rho_{0}h_{(2)}-4\rho_{0}^{\prime}h_{(1)})

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}(4\rho_{0}h_{(2)}+4\rho_{0}^{\prime}h_{(1)}).

Hence

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hess}(H)+h_{(1)}h_{(2)}-\frac{1}{4}h_{(1)}^{3}+\frac{1}{2}Hh_{(1)}^{2}-3h_{(3)}-3Hh_{(2)}+\rho_{0}^{\prime}h_{(1)}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hess}(H)-3h_{(3)}-3HL\mathring{L}+2\rho_{0}^{\prime}L

using the formulas for
h_{(1)}
and
h_{(2)}
in Proposition
13.2.1
.
This reproduces the formula (
13.2.1
) for
h_{(3)}
for a flat background.

We round up this section with a discussion of the relation of the formula for
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
in Proposition
13.7.1

to alternative formulas in the literature. In
[
GW14b
, (6)]
and
[
GW21
, Proposition 4.18]
, it is stated
that for a conformally flat background
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
equals
{\mathfrak{B}}_{3}
, where

(13.7.20)

6{\mathfrak{B}}_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}|\nabla\mathring{L}|^{2}+2(\mathring{L},\Delta(\mathring{L}))+3/2(\delta(\mathring{L}),\delta(\mathring{L}))-2{\sf J}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+|\mathring{L}|^{4}

with
{\sf J}={\sf J}^{h}
. In the flat case, using
\delta(\mathring{L})=2dH
(Codazzi-Mainardi) and the Gauss identity

{\sf J}=\frac{3}{2}H^{2}-\frac{1}{4}|\mathring{L}|^{2},

this formula reads

(13.7.21)

6{\mathfrak{B}}_{3}=|\nabla\mathring{L}|^{2}+2(\mathring{L},\Delta(\mathring{L}))+6|dH|^{2}-3H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+3/2|\mathring{L}|^{4}.

Lemma 13.7.8

(
[
JO22
, Remark 12]
)
.

For
n=3
, it holds

(13.7.22)

\frac{1}{2}\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})=3(\mathring{L},\operatorname{Hess}(H))+|\nabla\mathring{L}|^{2}+3H\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})-|L|^{4}

and

\delta\delta(\mathring{L}^{2})=4(\mathring{L},\operatorname{Hess}(H))+|\nabla\mathring{L}|^{2}+2|dH|^{2}+3H\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})-|L|^{4}.

Hence we have the difference formula

(13.7.23)

\frac{1}{2}\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})-\delta\delta(\mathring{L}^{2})=-(\mathring{L},\operatorname{Hess}(H))-2|dH|^{2}.

As a consequence, we obtain

Lemma 13.7.9
.

{\mathfrak{B}}_{3}={\mathcal{B}}_{3}
.

Proof.

On the one hand, we apply the identity (
13.7.22
) to calculate

\displaystyle 12{\mathcal{B}}_{3}

\displaystyle=6(\mathring{L},\operatorname{Hess}(H))+2|\nabla\mathring{L}|^{2}+6H\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})-2|L|^{4}

\displaystyle+12|dH|^{2}+6(\mathring{L},\operatorname{Hess}(H))-2|\mathring{L}|^{4}+6\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4})+6H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})

\displaystyle=12(\mathring{L},\operatorname{Hess}(H))+2|\nabla L|^{2}+6|dH|^{2}

\displaystyle+6H\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})+6H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})-2|L|^{4}-2|\mathring{L}|^{4}+6\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4})

using the relation
|\nabla L|^{2}=|\nabla\mathring{L}|^{2}+3|dH|^{2}
. Hence

6{\mathcal{B}}_{3}=6(\mathring{L},\operatorname{Hess}(H))+|\nabla L|^{2}+3|dH|^{2}+3H\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})+3H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})-|L|^{4}-|\mathring{L}|^{4}+3\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4}).

On the other hand, we use the identity
\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})=2(\mathring{L},\Delta(\mathring{L}))+2|\nabla\mathring{L}|^{2}
and
(
13.7.22
) to find

\displaystyle 6{\mathfrak{B}}_{3}

\displaystyle=\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})-|\nabla\mathring{L}|^{2}+6|dH|^{2}-3H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+3/2|\mathring{L}|^{4}

\displaystyle=6(\mathring{L},\operatorname{Hess}(H))+|\nabla L|^{2}+3|dH|^{2}+6H\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})-3H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-2|L|^{4}+3/2|\mathring{L}|^{4}.

Hence the difference
6({\mathfrak{B}}_{3}-{\mathcal{B}}_{3})
equals

\displaystyle 3H\operatorname{tr}(L^{3})-3H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})-|L|^{4}+|\mathring{L}|^{4}+3/2|\mathring{L}|^{4}-3H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-3\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4})

\displaystyle=3H(3H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{2})+3H^{3})-6H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-9H^{4}-3H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+3(1/2|\mathring{L}|^{4}-\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4}))

\displaystyle=3((1/2|\mathring{L}|^{4}-\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4}))

\displaystyle=0

by Corollary
13.7.2
. This proves the assertion.
∎

Finally, we show that the formula for
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
established in Proposition
13.7.1
implies

Lemma 13.7.10

(
[
GGHW19
]
)
.

For a conformally flat background, it holds

(13.7.24)

6{\mathcal{B}}_{3}=3\delta\delta((\mathring{L}^{2})_{\circ})+3({\sf P},(\mathring{L}^{2})_{\circ})+|\mathring{L}|^{4}.

Note that both
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
and the right-hand side of (
13.7.24
) are conformally
invariant. In fact, the operator
T:b\mapsto\delta\delta(b)+({\sf P},b)
acting
on trace-free symmetric bilinear forms
b
on
M^{3}
is well-known to be conformally
invariant in the sense that
e^{4\varphi}\hat{T}(b)=T(b)
. In (
13.7.24
),
the operator
T
acts on the trace-free part
(\mathring{L}^{2})_{\circ}
of
\mathring{L}^{2}
. The
conformal invariance
\hat{\mathring{L}}^{2}=\mathring{L}^{2}
implies the conformal invariance of the
trace-free part of
\mathring{L}^{2}
(Section
13.1.2
). This shows the claimed conformal
invariance. In particular, the right-hand side of (
13.7.24
) has the same
conformal transformation law as
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
.

In more explicit terms, formula (
13.7.24
) reads

(13.7.25)

\displaystyle 6{\mathcal{B}}_{3}

\displaystyle=3\delta\delta(\mathring{L}^{2})-\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})+3({\sf P},\mathring{L}^{2})-|\mathring{L}|^{2}{\sf J}+|\mathring{L}|^{4},

and it suffices to verify (
13.7.25
) in the flat case.

Lemma 13.7.11
.

For a flat background, the formulas (
13.7.2
) and (
13.7.25
)
are equivalent.

Proof.

The assertion is equivalent to the vanishing of the sum

\displaystyle 6\delta\delta(\mathring{L}^{2})-2\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})+6({\sf P},\mathring{L}^{2})-2|\mathring{L}|^{2}{\sf J}+2|\mathring{L}|^{4}

\displaystyle-\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})-12|dH|^{2}-6(\mathring{L},\operatorname{Hess}(H))-|\mathring{L}|^{4}-6H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3}).

But the identity

(13.7.26)

\mathcal{F}=\iota^{*}\bar{{\sf P}}-{\sf P}+H\mathring{L}+\frac{1}{2}H^{2}h\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}\mathring{L}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}|\mathring{L}|^{2}h

for the Fialkov tensor
\mathcal{F}
(see (
13.1.1
)) and the Gauss identity
{\sf J}=3/2H^{2}-1/4|\mathring{L}|^{2}

(see (
13.1.3
)) imply

(13.7.27)

6(\mathring{L}^{2},{\sf P})-2|\mathring{L}|^{2}{\sf J}=6H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})-|\mathring{L}|^{4}.

Hence the above sum simplifies to

6\delta\delta(\mathring{L}^{2})-3\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})-12|dH|^{2}-6(\mathring{L},\operatorname{Hess}(H)).

By (
13.7.23
), this sum vanishes. The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 13.7.12
.

Alternatively, one may derive formula (
13.7.24
) for conformally flat backgrounds by direct
evaluation of the definition of the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
. For details, we refer to
[
JO22
]
.

13.8.
Variational aspects

Here we relate the obstructions
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
(for general backgrounds) and
{\mathcal{B}}_{3}
(for
conformally flat backgrounds) to singular Yamabe
energy functionals
. The
discussion illustrates the general results of
[
GW17
,
G17
]
and connects
with the classical literature.

We first consider the classical situation of a variation of the Willmore functional. For a closed surface
f:M^{2}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, we consider a normal variation
f_{t}:M^{2}\hookrightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
of
f
:

f_{t}(x)=f(x)+tu(x)N_{0},

where
u\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
N_{0}
is the unit normal of
M
. The variation field of
f_{t}
is

uN_{0}
. We set
M_{t}=f_{t}(M)
and let

(13.8.1)

\mathcal{W}_{2}(f)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\int_{f(M)}|\mathring{L}|^{2}dvol_{h},

where the metric
h
is induced by the Euclidean metric on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. We often identify
M
with
f(M)
. Set

{\textit{var}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})[u]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}(d/dt)|_{0}(\mathcal{W}_{2}(M_{t})).

In order to calculate that variation, we recall the well-known variation formulas

\displaystyle{\textit{var}}(h)[u]

\displaystyle=2uL,

\displaystyle{\textit{var}}(L)[u]

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{Hess}(u)+uL^{2},

\displaystyle 2{\textit{var}}(H)[u]

\displaystyle=-\Delta(u)-u|L|^{2}

and

{\textit{var}}(dvol_{h})[u]=2uHdvol_{h},

where
L^{2}
,
|L|^{2}
and
\Delta(u)
are defined by the metric
h
on
M
. First, we note that

{\textit{var}}(|\mathring{L}|^{2})[u]=2(\mathring{L},{\textit{var}}(\mathring{L})[u])-4u(L,\mathring{L}^{2});

the second term comes from raising
2
indices:
|\mathring{L}|^{2}=\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{2})=h^{ia}h^{jb}\mathring{L}_{ij}\mathring{L}_{kb}
.
Hence

\displaystyle{\textit{var}}(|\mathring{L}|^{2})[u]

\displaystyle=2(\mathring{L},{\textit{var}}(L)[u]-H{\textit{var}}(h)[u])-4u\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3}+H\mathring{L}^{2})

\displaystyle=-2((\mathring{L},\operatorname{Hess}(u)-uL^{2})+2Hu(\mathring{L},L))+4uH|\mathring{L}|^{2}

using
\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})=0
. Now partial integration gives

\displaystyle{\textit{var}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})[u]

\displaystyle=-\int_{M}u\left[2\delta\delta(\mathring{L})-2(\mathring{L},L^{2})+4H(\mathring{L},L)+4H|\mathring{L}|^{2}-2H|\mathring{L}|^{2}\right]dvol_{h};

the last term comes from the variation of the volume. Simplification yields

(13.8.2)

{\textit{var}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})[u]=-\int_{M}u(2\Delta(H)+2H|\mathring{L}|^{2})dvol_{h}

using
\delta\delta(\mathring{L})=\Delta(H)
and again
\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})=0
. This proves the classical result that in
a flat background, the Euler-Lagrange equation of the Willmore functional
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is

\Delta(H)+H|\mathring{L}|^{2}=0.

By
|\mathring{L}|^{2}=2(H^{2}-K)
, where
K
is the Gauss curvature, the Euler-Lagrange equation of
the Willmore functional
\mathcal{W}_{2}
reads

\Delta(H)+2H(H^{2}-K)=0.

This equation is known as the Willmore equation. It was already mentioned in
[
T26
]
and Schadow (1922).
We refer to
[
W93
, Section 7.4]
for more details.

The variation formula (
13.8.2
) implies the special case

(13.8.3)

{\textit{var}}({\mathcal{A}}_{2})[u]=\frac{3}{2}\int_{M^{2}}u{\mathcal{B}}_{2}dvol_{h}

of the variation formula

(13.8.4)

{\textit{var}}({\mathcal{A}}_{n})[u]=(n+2)(n-1)\int_{M}u{\mathcal{L}}_{n}dvol_{h}=\frac{(n+1)(n-1)}{2}\int_{M^{n}}u{\mathcal{B}}_{n}dvol_{h}

for
{\mathcal{A}}_{n}=\int_{M}v_{n}dvol_{h}
. The first equality in (
13.8.4
) was proved in

[
G17
, Theorem 3.1]
. The variational formula in terms of
{\mathcal{B}}_{n}
was
established in
[
GW17
, Section 5]
by different arguments. For the second
equality, we refer to (
3.8
). In fact, (
13.4.4
) shows that

{\mathcal{A}}_{2}=\int_{M^{2}}v_{2}dvol=\int_{M^{2}}\left(-\frac{1}{2}{\sf J}+\frac{1}{4}|\mathring{L}|^{2}\right)dvol_{h}.

Hence

{\textit{var}}({\mathcal{A}}_{2})[u]=\frac{1}{4}{\textit{var}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})[u]=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{M}u(\Delta(H)+H|\mathring{L}|^{2})dvol_{h}

by Gauss-Bonnet. On the other hand, we have

{\mathcal{B}}_{2}=-\frac{1}{3}(\Delta(H)+H|\mathring{L}|^{2}).

This implies (
13.8.3
).

These results generalize as follows to closed surfaces
M^{2}\hookrightarrow(X^{3},g)

in general backgrounds. For the following discussion, we also refer to
[
V13
, Section
5.1]
.

We consider normal variations with a variation field of the form
uN_{0}
with a unit
normal field
N_{0}
. The following formula is well-known (see
[
R93
, Section 3]
,

[
HP99
, Theorem 3.2]
,
[
V13
, Theorem 4.1]
). It can be proved by calculation
in geodesic normal coordinates. For
u=1
, it plays a central role in
[
G04
, Chapter
3]
. It holds

(13.8.5)

{\textit{var}}(L_{ij})[u]=-\operatorname{Hess}_{ij}(u)+u(L^{2})_{ij}-u\bar{R}_{0ij0}.

Hence

\displaystyle{\textit{var}}(|\mathring{L}|^{2})[u]

\displaystyle=2(\mathring{L},{\textit{var}}(L)[u]-H{\textit{var}}(h)[u])-4u\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3}+H\mathring{L}^{2})

\displaystyle=2(\mathring{L},-\operatorname{Hess}(u)+uL^{2})-2u\mathring{L}^{ij}R_{0ij0}-4Hu(\mathring{L},L)-4H|\mathring{L}|^{2}.

Now simplification and partial integration gives

{\textit{var}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})[u]=-\int_{M}u(2\delta\delta(\mathring{L})+2H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+2\mathring{L}^{ij}R_{0ij0})dvol_{h}.

Since the Weyl tensor vanishes in dimension
3
, we have
\mathring{L}^{ij}R_{0ij0}=\mathring{L}^{ij}({\sf P}_{ij}+{\sf P}_{00}h_{ij})=\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}_{ij}
. Thus the integrand
is given by letting the operator

(13.8.6)

b\mapsto\delta\delta(b)+(\iota^{*}({\sf P}),b)+H(\mathring{L},b)

act on
b=\mathring{L}
. The above operator is well-known to be conformally covariant on trace-free symmetric
bilinear forms (see
[
V13
, Section 5.1.4]
). This implies the conformal invariance of the integrand.

The above calculation shows that the Euler-Lagrange equation of the Willmore functional

\mathcal{W}_{2}
is

(13.8.7)

\delta\delta(\mathring{L})+H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+\mathring{L}^{ij}\bar{R}_{0ij0}=0.

By Codazzi-Mainardi,
\delta\delta(\mathring{L})
equals
\Delta(H)+\delta(\bar{{\sf P}}_{0})
. Thus
we obtain

\Delta(H)+\delta(\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{0})+H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+\mathring{L}^{ij}\bar{R}_{0ij0}=0.

We also observe that the left-hand side of (
13.8.7
) coincides with

\delta\delta(\mathring{L})+H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+(\mathring{L},\iota^{*}(\bar{{\sf P}}))=\delta\delta(\mathring{L})+H|\mathring{L}|^{2}+(\mathring{L},\iota^{*}(\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}))

since
\mathring{L}^{ij}\bar{R}_{0ij0}=\mathring{L}^{ij}\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{ij}
. In fact, since the Weyl tensor
vanishes in dimension
3
, it holds

\bar{R}_{0ij0}=\bar{{\sf P}}_{ij}+\bar{{\sf P}}_{00}h_{ij}

and we obtain

\mathring{L}^{ij}\bar{R}_{0ij0}=\mathring{L}^{ij}\bar{{\sf P}}_{ij}=\mathring{L}^{ij}\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{ij}.

It follows that the variation of the Willmore functional
\mathcal{W}_{2}
yields the Yamabe obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
:

{\textit{var}}({\mathcal{A}}_{2})[u]=-\frac{1}{4}{\textit{var}}(\mathcal{W}_{2})[u]=-\frac{3}{2}\int u{\mathcal{B}}_{2}dvol_{h}

confirming (
13.8.3
) for general backgrounds.

We continue with an analogous discussion of the variation of

(13.8.8)

\mathcal{W}_{3}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}\int_{M^{3}}\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})dvol_{h}

for variations of a closed three-manifold
M^{3}\hookrightarrow X^{4}
in a conformally flat background
(X,g)
.
We first determine the variation of
\mathcal{W}_{3}
for a general background metric
g
. Here we use the variation
formulas

\displaystyle{\textit{var}}(h)[u]

\displaystyle=2uL,

\displaystyle{\textit{var}}(L)[u]

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{Hess}(u)+uL^{2}-u\bar{R}_{0\cdot\cdot 0},

(13.8.9)

\displaystyle 3{\textit{var}}(H)[u]

\displaystyle=-\Delta(u)-u|L|^{2}-u\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00}

and

{\textit{var}}(dvol_{h})[u]=3uHdvol_{h}.

Note that the operator
\Delta(u)+u|L|^{2}+u\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00}
is the Jacobi operator
appearing in the second variation formula for the area of minimal surfaces
[
CM11
]
.

First, we observe that

{\textit{var}}(\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3}))[u]=3(\mathring{L}^{2},{\textit{var}}(\mathring{L})[u])-6u(L,\mathring{L}^{3});

the second term comes from raising
3
indices:
\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})=h^{ia}h^{jb}h^{kc}\mathring{L}_{ij}\mathring{L}_{kb}\mathring{L}_{ca}
.
Hence

\displaystyle{\textit{var}}(\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3}))[u]

\displaystyle=3(\mathring{L}^{2},{\textit{var}}(\mathring{L})[u])-6u(L,\mathring{L}^{3})

\displaystyle=3(\mathring{L}^{2},{\textit{var}}(L)[u]-{\textit{var}}(H)[u]h-H{\textit{var}}(h)[u])-6u(L,\mathring{L}^{3})

\displaystyle=3(\mathring{L}^{2},-\operatorname{Hess}(u)+uL^{2})-3u(\mathring{L}^{2},\bar{R}_{0\cdot\cdot 0})+(\Delta(u)+u|L|^{2}+u\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00})|\mathring{L}|^{2}

\displaystyle-6uH(\mathring{L}^{2},L)-6u(L,\mathring{L}^{3}).

Now partial integration and simplification yields

\displaystyle{\textit{var}}\big(\int_{M}\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})dvol_{h}\big)[u]

\displaystyle=\int_{M}u\big[-3\delta\delta(\mathring{L}^{2})+\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})-3\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4})+|\mathring{L}|^{4}-3H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})\big]dvol_{h}

(13.8.10)

\displaystyle+\int_{M}u(-3(\mathring{L}^{2},\bar{R}_{0\cdot\cdot 0})+|\mathring{L}^{2}|\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00})dvol_{h}.

Now, for a conformally flat background, we reformulate this variation formula in a conformally invariant way.
The following result is also covered in
[
GGHW19
, Section 4]
using a different method.

Lemma 13.8.1
.

Assume that
n=3
and that the Weyl tensor of
(X,g)
vanishes. Then

{\textit{var}}\left(\int_{M}\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})dvol_{h}\right)[u]=-3\int_{M}u\left(\delta\delta((\mathring{L}^{2})_{\circ})+({\sf P},(\mathring{L}^{2})_{\circ})+\frac{1}{3}|\mathring{L}|^{4}\right)dvol_{h}.

Proof.

By (
13.8
), the claim is equivalent to the identity

\displaystyle\delta\delta((\mathring{L}^{2})_{\circ})+({\sf P},(\mathring{L}^{2})_{\circ})+\frac{1}{3}|\mathring{L}|^{4}

\displaystyle=\delta\delta(\mathring{L}^{2})-\frac{1}{3}\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})+\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4})-\frac{1}{3}|\mathring{L}|^{4}+H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})+(\mathring{L}^{2},\bar{R}_{0\cdot\cdot 0})-\frac{1}{3}|\mathring{L}^{2}|\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00}.

Note that

\delta\delta((\mathring{L}^{2})_{\circ})=\delta\delta(\mathring{L}^{2})-\frac{1}{3}\Delta(|\mathring{L}|^{2})

and

\displaystyle({\sf P},(\mathring{L}^{2})_{\circ})=({\sf P},\mathring{L}^{2})-\frac{1}{3}({\sf P},|\mathring{L}|^{2}h)=(\operatorname{Ric},\mathring{L}^{2})-{\sf J}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-\frac{1}{3}{\sf J}|\mathring{L}|^{2}=(\operatorname{Ric},\mathring{L}^{2})-\frac{4}{3}{\sf J}|\mathring{L}|^{2}.

Moreover, it holds

\displaystyle(\mathring{L}^{2},\bar{R}_{0\cdot\cdot 0})-\frac{1}{3}|\mathring{L}^{2}|\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00}

\displaystyle=(\mathring{L}^{2},\bar{{\sf P}})+\bar{{\sf P}}_{00}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-\frac{1}{3}|\mathring{L}^{2}|\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{00}

\displaystyle=(\mathring{L}^{2},\bar{{\sf P}})+\frac{1}{3}\bar{{\sf P}}_{00}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\bar{{\sf J}}|\mathring{L}|^{2}

(by the vanishing of the Weyl tensor). Hence the claim reduces to

\displaystyle(\operatorname{Ric},\mathring{L}^{2})-\frac{4}{3}{\sf J}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+\frac{1}{3}|\mathring{L}|^{4}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4})-\frac{1}{3}|\mathring{L}|^{4}+H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})+(\bar{{\sf P}},\mathring{L}^{2})+\frac{1}{3}\bar{{\sf P}}_{00}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\bar{{\sf J}}|\mathring{L}|^{2}.

Now the Gauss equations

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ric}_{ij}=\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}_{ij}-\bar{R}_{0ij0}+3HL_{ij}-(L^{2})_{ij}\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\sf J}-\overline{{\sf J}}=-\bar{{\sf P}}_{00}-\frac{1}{4}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+\frac{3}{2}H^{2}

(see (
13.1.1
) and (
13.1.2
)) imply

\displaystyle(\operatorname{Ric},\mathring{L}^{2})-\frac{4}{3}{\sf J}|\mathring{L}|^{2}

\displaystyle=(\overline{\operatorname{Ric}},\mathring{L}^{2})-(\bar{{\sf P}},\mathring{L}^{2})-\bar{{\sf P}}_{00}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+3H(L,\mathring{L}^{2})-(L^{2},\mathring{L}^{2})

\displaystyle-\frac{4}{3}\left(\bar{{\sf J}}-\bar{{\sf P}}_{00}-\frac{1}{4}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+\frac{3}{2}H^{2}\right)|\mathring{L}|^{2}

\displaystyle=2(\bar{{\sf P}},\mathring{L}^{2})+\bar{{\sf J}}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-(\bar{{\sf P}},\mathring{L}^{2})-\bar{{\sf P}}_{00}|\mathring{L}|^{2}+\frac{4}{3}\bar{{\sf P}}_{00}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-\frac{4}{3}\bar{{\sf J}}|\mathring{L}|^{2}

\displaystyle+3H(L,\mathring{L}^{2})-(L^{2},\mathring{L}^{2})+\frac{1}{3}|\mathring{L}|^{4}-2H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}

\displaystyle=(\bar{{\sf P}},\mathring{L}^{2})+\frac{1}{3}\bar{{\sf P}}_{00}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-\frac{1}{3}\bar{{\sf J}}|\mathring{L}|^{2}

\displaystyle+3H(L,\mathring{L}^{2})-(L^{2},\mathring{L}^{2})+\frac{1}{3}|\mathring{L}|^{4}-2H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}.

Hence it suffices to prove that

3H(L,\mathring{L}^{2})-(L^{2},\mathring{L}^{2})+\frac{2}{3}|\mathring{L}|^{4}-2H^{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}=\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4})-\frac{1}{3}|\mathring{L}|^{4}+H\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3}).

By simplification, this identity is equivalent to

\frac{2}{3}|\mathring{L}|^{4}-\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4})=\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4})-\frac{1}{3}|\mathring{L}|^{4},

i.e.,
|\mathring{L}|^{4}=2\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{4})
(see Corollary
13.7.2
). The proof is complete.
∎

In terms of

(13.8.11)

{\mathcal{A}}_{3}=\int_{M^{3}}v_{3}dvol_{h}=-\frac{2}{3}\int_{M^{3}}\operatorname{tr}(\mathring{L}^{3})dvol_{h}=-\frac{2}{3}\mathcal{W}_{3}

we obtain

Corollary 13.8.2
.

{\textit{var}}({\mathcal{A}}_{3})[u]=4\int_{M^{3}}u{\mathcal{B}}_{3}dvol_{h}.

Proof.

Combine (
13.7.24
) with Lemma
13.8.1
.
∎

This is a special case of (
13.8
).

Note that the second equality in (
13.8.11
) follows by combining (
13.5.7
) with
(
13.9.5
) and (
13.9.7
).

Another proof of Corollary
13.8.2
(even for general backgrounds) has been given in
[
GGHW19
]
using a method
that rests on a certain distributional calculus. Finally, the above classical style arguments have been extended to the general
case in
[
JO22
]
.

13.9.
Low-order extrinsic
Q
-curvatures

Here we discuss the low-order extrinsic
Q
-curvatures
{\bf Q}_{2}
and
{\bf Q}_{3}
from the perspective of
their holographic formulas.

Example 13.9.1
.

We consider
{\bf Q}_{2}
in general dimensions. The holographic formula
(
11.3
) states that

-{\bf Q}_{2}=\frac{1}{n-3}(2v_{2}+(n-2)(v{\sf J})_{0})+\frac{1}{n-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}\left(\frac{n}{2}-1\right)(v_{1})

for even
n
. Using
v_{1}=(n-1)H
and the formula (
13.4.3
) for
v_{2}
as well as

{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\lambda)=-\lambda H
(Lemma
13.11.1
), we obtain

-{\bf Q}_{2}={\sf J}_{0}-\rho_{0}^{\prime}+\frac{n}{2}H^{2}={\sf J}_{0}-{\sf P}_{00}-\frac{|\mathring{L}|^{2}}{n-1}+\frac{n}{2}H^{2}.

In particular, we see that the holographic formula makes sense for all
n\geq 2
. By
the hypersurface Gauss identity (
13.1.3
), the above formula simplifies to

(13.9.1)

-{\bf Q}_{2}={\sf J}^{h}-\frac{|\mathring{L}|^{2}}{2(n-1)}.

Note that this result fits with the formula

(13.9.2)

{\bf P}_{2}=\Delta_{h}-\left(\frac{n}{2}-1\right)\left({\sf J}^{h}-\frac{|\mathring{L}|^{2}}{2(n-1)}\right)

[
GW21
, Proposition 8.5]
. Independently, the latter formula for
{\bf P}_{2}

will be derived below from the solution operator
{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\lambda)
(Lemma
13.11.2

and the discussion following it). We also recall that
{\bf Q}_{2}=-Q_{2}
in the
Poincaré-Einstein case. Finally, we note that the formula for
{\bf Q}_{2}
is singular
for
n=1
and
\operatorname{Res}_{n=1}({\bf Q}_{2})=\frac{1}{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}=0
.

Example 13.9.2
.

The holographic formula (
11.3
) for
{\bf Q}_{3}
states that

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}{\bf Q}_{3}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{n-5}(6v_{3}+(n\!-\!3)(v{\sf J})_{1})+\tfrac{1}{n-3}{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(\tfrac{n-3}{2})(4v_{2}+(n\!-\!1)(v{\sf J})_{0})+\tfrac{1}{n-1}{\mathcal{T}}_{2}^{*}(\tfrac{n-3}{2})(2v_{1})

for even
n
. As a byproduct of the following discussion, we will see that the
fractions in that formula do not prevent its validity in odd dimensions. First, we
note that the above formula is equivalent to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}{\bf Q}_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n\!-\!5}(6v_{3}+(n\!-\!3){\sf J}^{\prime}_{0}+(n\!-\!3)v_{1}{\sf J}_{0})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n\!-\!3}{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}\left(\frac{n-3}{2}\right)(4v_{2}+(n\!-\!1){\sf J}_{0})+\frac{1}{n\!-\!1}{\mathcal{T}}_{2}^{*}\left(\frac{n-3}{2}\right)(2v_{1}).

Now the formulas

\displaystyle v_{1}

\displaystyle=(n\!-\!1)H

\displaystyle 2v_{2}

\displaystyle=(n\!-\!3)(n\!-\!1)H^{2}-\iota^{*}({\sf J})-(n\!-\!3)\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\rho)

(by (
13.4.3
))

\displaystyle 6v_{3}

\displaystyle=(n\!-\!5)(n\!-\!3)(n\!-\!1)H^{3}-(3n\!-\!7)H\iota^{*}({\sf J})-(n\!-\!5)(3n\!-\!7)H\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\rho)

\displaystyle-(n\!-\!5)\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\rho)-2\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}({\sf J})

(by (
13.4.4
))

show that the fractions are reduced. Indeed, it holds

\displaystyle 6v_{3}+(n\!-\!3){\sf J}^{\prime}_{0}+(n\!-\!3)v_{1}{\sf J}_{0}

\displaystyle=0\qquad\mbox{if $n=5$},

\displaystyle 4v_{2}+(n\!-\!1){\sf J}_{0}

\displaystyle=0\qquad\mbox{if $n=3$}

(these are the relations mentioned in Remark
11.5
). Then we obtain

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}{\bf Q}_{3}

\displaystyle=(n\!-\!3)(n\!-\!1)H^{3}-(3n\!-\!7)H\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\rho)+\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}({\sf J})-\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\rho)+(n\!-\!2)H\iota^{*}({\sf J})

(13.9.3)

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}\left(\frac{n\!-\!3}{2}\right)(\iota^{*}({\sf J})-2\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\rho)+2(n\!-\!1)H^{2})+{\mathcal{T}}_{2}^{*}\left(\frac{n\!-\!3}{2}\right)(2H).

A calculation using Lemma
13.5.1
, Lemmas
13.11.1
–
13.11.2
and the hypersurface Gauss
identity (
13.1.3
) shows that

(13.9.4)

\frac{1}{4}{\bf Q}_{3}=\Delta H+H\iota^{*}{\sf J}+\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}({\sf J})-(n\!-\!1)H\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\rho)-\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\rho).

In particular, for
n=3
, we find

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}{\bf Q}_{3}=\Delta H+H\iota^{*}({\sf J})+\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}({\sf J})-2H\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}(\rho)-\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\rho).

By comparison with Example
13.4.4
, we see that

12v_{3}=-{\bf Q}_{3}+4\Delta H

and consequently

(13.9.5)

12\int_{M^{3}}v_{3}dvol_{h}=-\int_{M^{3}}{\bf Q}_{3}dvol_{h}.

This confirms Theorem
10.9
for
n=3
.

Remark 13.9.3
.

Formula (
13.9.4
) also follows from the last display in the proof of
[
GW21
, Proposition 8.5]

or
[
GW20
, Proposition 5.4]
which evaluates the composition of three Laplace-Robin operators.

We continue with the discussion of the holographic formula for
{\bf Q}_{3}
in general
dimensions
n\geq 3
. In fact, we prove that an evaluation of (
13.9.4
) shows
that the explicit formula (
13.5.3
) for
\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}
is equivalent to a simple
formula for
{\bf Q}_{3}
.

Proposition 13.9.4
.

Assume that
n\geq 3
. Then the formula (
13.5.3
) for
\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}
is equivalent to

(13.9.6)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}{\bf Q}_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n\!-\!2}(\delta\delta(\mathring{L})-(n\!-\!3)(\mathring{L},{\sf P}^{h})+(n\!-\!1)(\mathring{L},\mathcal{F}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n\!-\!2}(\delta\delta(\mathring{L})-2(n\!-\!2)(\mathring{L},{\sf P}^{h})+(n\!-\!1)(\mathring{L},{\sf P}^{g})+(n\!-\!1)H|\mathring{L}|^{2}).

Note that (
13.9.6
) fits with the explicit formula for
{\bf P}_{3}
in Proposition
13.10.1
.

The proof of Proposition
13.9.4
will show that the displayed formula for
{\bf Q}_{3}
follows by combining
the holographic formula for
{\bf Q}_{3}
(in the form (
13.9.4
)) with the formulas for the first two normal
derivatives of
\rho
.

Note also that in dimension
n=3
, the above formula reads

(13.9.7)

{\bf Q}_{3}=4\delta\delta(\mathring{L})+8(\mathring{L},\mathcal{F}).

It immediately follows from that expression that the integral
\int_{M^{3}}{\bf Q}_{3}dvol_{h}
is conformally
invariant as a functional of
g
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the equivalence of (
13.5.3
) and the first identity. For this,
we make explicit the equality of (
13.9.4
) and (
13.9.6
). In terms of adapted coordinates,
it states the equality

\Delta H+H{\sf J}_{0}+{\sf J}_{0}^{\prime}-(n\!-\!1)H\rho_{0}^{\prime}-\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}-2H\rho_{0}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{n\!-\!2}\delta\delta(\mathring{L})-\frac{n\!-\!3}{n\!-\!2}\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}^{h}_{ij}+\frac{n\!-\!1}{n\!-\!2}\mathring{L}^{ij}\mathcal{F}_{ij}.

Here we used that
\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}(\rho)
corresponds to
\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}-2\rho_{0}\rho_{0}^{\prime}=\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}+2H\rho_{0}^{\prime}
(see Example
13.1.7
). By the identity (
13.6.3
) for
\Delta H
,
this relation is equivalent to

\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}=\frac{1}{n\!-\!1}\delta\delta(\mathring{L})-\frac{1}{n\!-\!2}\delta\delta(\mathring{L})+\frac{n\!-\!3}{n\!-\!2}\mathring{L}^{ij}{\sf P}^{h}_{ij}-\frac{n\!-\!1}{n\!-\!2}\mathring{L}^{ij}\mathcal{F}_{ij}+H{\sf J}_{0}-(n+1)H\rho_{0}^{\prime}+{\sf J}_{0}^{\prime}.

Now

H{\sf J}_{0}-(n\!+\!1)H\rho_{0}^{\prime}=-\frac{n\!+\!1}{n\!-\!1}H\operatorname{Ric}_{00}+\frac{1}{n\!-\!1}H\operatorname{scal}-\frac{n\!+\!1}{n\!-\!1}H|\mathring{L}|^{2}

shows that this expression for
\rho_{0}^{\prime\prime}
coincides with the one given in (
13.5.3
).
The proof is complete.
∎

The above formula for
{\bf Q}_{3}
is singular for
n=2
. But the second formula in (
13.9.6
) shows that

(13.9.8)

\operatorname{Res}_{n=2}({\bf Q}_{3})=4(\delta\delta(\mathring{L})+(\mathring{L},{\sf P}^{g})+H|\mathring{L}|^{2}),

up to the term
-8(n-2)(\mathring{L},{\sf P}^{h})=-8(\mathring{L},\operatorname{Ric}^{h})
(which vanishes in dimension
n=2
by

\operatorname{Ric}^{h}=Kh
,
K
being the Gauss curvature). The right-hand side of (
13.9.8
) is proportional
to the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
. From that perspective, its conformal invariance follows from the conformal
covariance of
{\bf P}_{3}
. The above argument to derive
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
from the constant term of
{\bf P}_{3}
is
due to
[
GW21
, Remark 8.6]
. For the general relation between the singular Yamabe obstruction and
the super-critical
Q
-curvature
Q_{n+1}
, we refer to Theorem
11.6
.

13.10.
The pair
({\bf P}_{3},{\bf Q}_{3})

The following explicit formula for
{\bf P}_{3}
was first proven in
[
GW21
, Proposition 8.5]

by evaluation of the relevant composition of three Laplace-Robin
operators.
33
33

33

See also the identical
[
GW20
, Proposition 5.4]
.

Proposition 13.10.1
.

For
n\geq 3
, the operator

{\bf P}_{3}=8\delta(\mathring{L}d)+\frac{n\!-\!3}{2}\frac{4}{n\!-\!2}(\delta\delta(\mathring{L})-(n\!-\!3)(\mathring{L},{\sf P}^{h})+(n\!-\!1)(\mathring{L},\mathcal{F}))

is conformally covariant:

e^{\frac{n+3}{2}\varphi}\circ{\bf P}_{3}(\hat{g})={\bf P}_{3}(g)\circ e^{\frac{n-3}{2}\varphi},\;\varphi\in C^{\infty}(X).

The formula for the leading term of
{\bf P}_{3}
will be derived in Lemma
13.11.6
.

Proof.

We first calculate

\displaystyle e^{\frac{n+3}{2}\varphi}\hat{\delta}(\hat{\mathring{L}}d)(e^{-\frac{n-3}{2}\varphi}u)=\delta(e^{\frac{n-3}{2}\varphi}\mathring{L}d)(e^{-\frac{n-3}{2}\varphi}u)+\frac{n\!-\!3}{2}e^{\frac{n-3}{2}\varphi}i_{\operatorname{grad}(\varphi)}(\mathring{L}d)(e^{-\frac{n-3}{2}\varphi}u)

\displaystyle=\delta(\mathring{L}d)(u)-\frac{n\!-\!3}{2}\delta(\mathring{L}d\varphi\cdot u)+\frac{n\!-\!3}{2}i_{\operatorname{grad}(\varphi)}(\mathring{L}du)-\left(\frac{n\!-\!3}{2}\right)^{2}(d\varphi,\mathring{L}d\varphi)u

\displaystyle=\delta(\mathring{L}d)(u)-\frac{n\!-\!3}{2}\delta(\mathring{L}d\varphi)u-\left(\frac{n\!-\!3}{2}\right)^{2}(d\varphi,\mathring{L}d\varphi)u

using
\hat{\mathring{L}}=e^{-\varphi}\mathring{L}
for the endomorphism
\mathring{L}
corresponding to the
trace-free second fundamental form and the transformation law
e^{(a+2)\varphi}\hat{\delta}e^{-a\varphi}=\delta+(n\!-\!2\!-\!a)i_{\operatorname{grad}(\varphi)}
on

\Omega^{1}(M)
. Next, the term with the Schouten tensor yields

(\mathring{L},{\sf P}^{h})-(\mathring{L},\operatorname{Hess}(\varphi))+(\mathring{L},d\varphi\otimes\varphi).

Finally, it holds

\displaystyle e^{3\varphi}\hat{\delta}\hat{\delta}(\hat{\mathring{L}})=\delta(e^{\varphi}\hat{\delta}(e^{\varphi}\mathring{L}))+(n\!-\!3)i_{\operatorname{grad}(\varphi)}e^{\varphi}\hat{\delta}(e^{\varphi}\mathring{L})

\displaystyle=\delta\delta(\mathring{L})+(n\!-\!1)\delta(\mathring{L}d\varphi)+(n\!-\!3)i_{\operatorname{grad}(\varphi)}\delta(\mathring{L})+(n\!-\!3)(n\!-\!1)i_{\operatorname{grad}(\varphi)}i_{\operatorname{grad}(\varphi)}(\mathring{L})

using
\hat{\mathring{L}}=e^{\varphi}\mathring{L}
and the transformation law

e^{(a+2)\varphi}\hat{\delta}(e^{-a\varphi}b)=\delta(b)+(n\!-\!2\!-\!a)i_{\operatorname{grad}(\varphi)}(b)-\operatorname{tr}(b)d\varphi

on symmetric bilinear forms
b
. Now simplification proves the claim.
∎

Remark 13.10.2
.

The operator
\frac{n-2}{4}{\bf P}_{3}
differs from the tractor calculus operator

D_{M}^{A}L_{AB}D_{X}^{B}

by the contribution of
(\mathring{L},\mathcal{F})

[
G03
, Section 6.3]
. Here
L_{AB}
is
a tractor calculus version of the second fundamental form. This identification again
implies its conformal covariance.

Proposition
13.10.1
yields an explicit formula for
{\bf Q}_{3}
(see
(
13.9.2
)). As a direct cross-check of that formula, we note that in
dimension
n=3

\displaystyle e^{3\varphi}\hat{{\bf Q}}_{3}

\displaystyle=4e^{3\varphi}\hat{\delta}\hat{\delta}(\hat{\mathring{L}})+8e^{3\varphi}(\hat{\mathring{L}},\hat{\mathcal{F}})_{\hat{h}}

\displaystyle=4\delta e^{\varphi}\hat{\delta}(e^{\varphi}\mathring{L})+8(\mathring{L},\mathcal{F})_{h}

\displaystyle=4(\delta\delta(\mathring{L})+2\delta(\mathring{L}d)(\varphi))+8(\mathring{L},\mathcal{F})_{h}

\displaystyle={\bf Q}_{3}+{\bf P}_{3}(\varphi).

This also confirms Theorem
10.5
for
n=3
.

Similar arguments provide an elementary proof of the conformal invariance of the obstruction
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
.

13.11.
Low-order solution operators

Under the assumption
{(\mathcal{SY})}
, we make the low-order solution operators

{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\lambda)
and
{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\lambda)
explicit in general dimensions. For that
purpose, we use the ansatz
u=s^{\lambda}f+s^{\lambda+1}{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\lambda)f+s^{\lambda+2}{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\lambda)f+\dots
for an approximate solution of the equation

-\Delta_{\sigma^{-2}g}(u)=\lambda(n-\lambda)u
in adapted coordinates.

Lemma 13.11.1
.

{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\lambda)=-\lambda H
.

Proof.

By the assumption
{(\mathcal{SY})}
, the coefficients in (
9.5
) expand as

a=1-2s\rho+O(s^{n+1})=1+2sH+\cdots
(using
\iota^{*}(\rho)=-H
) and

\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{s})=2\operatorname{tr}(L)+\cdots
. Now we let the operator (
9.5
)
act on
u
. The expansion of the result starts with

s^{2}\partial_{s}^{2}(s^{\lambda})f-(n-1)s\partial_{s}(s^{\lambda})f\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}-\lambda(n-\lambda)s^{\lambda}f.

The next term in the expansion reads

\displaystyle s^{2}\partial_{s}^{2}(s^{\lambda+1}){\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\lambda)(f)+2s^{3}H\partial_{s}^{2}(s^{\lambda})f+s^{2}\operatorname{tr}(L)\partial_{s}(s^{\lambda})f

\displaystyle-(n-1)s\partial_{s}(s^{\lambda+1}){\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\lambda)(f)-2(n-1)s^{2}H\partial_{s}(s^{\lambda})f+Hs^{2}\partial_{s}(s^{\lambda})f

\displaystyle=(\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!1)(\lambda\!+\!1)s^{\lambda+1}{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\lambda)(f)+(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!1)\lambda Hs^{\lambda+1}f.

This result equals
-\lambda(n-\lambda)s^{\lambda+1}{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\lambda)(f)
iff

{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\lambda)(f)=-\lambda H
.
∎

It is worth emphasizing that
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\lambda)
is regular in
\lambda
. In fact, this
property does not hold for general asymptotically hyperbolic metrics
[
Gu05
, Section 4]
.

As a consequence of Lemma
13.11.1
, we find

\displaystyle{\bf D}_{1}^{res}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!1)(\iota^{*}\partial_{s}+(v_{1}+{\mathcal{T}}_{1}^{*}(\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!1))\iota^{*})

(13.11.1)

\displaystyle=(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!1)(\iota^{*}\partial_{s}-\lambda H\iota^{*}).

This formula obviously confirms Theorem
8.1
for the operator

\iota^{*}L(\lambda)
.

The formula for
{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\lambda)
is a bit more complicated.

Lemma 13.11.2
.

Assume that
n\geq 2
. Then

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{-\Delta_{h}+\lambda{\sf J}^{h}}{2(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!2)}+\frac{\lambda}{2}{\sf P}_{00}-\frac{\lambda}{2(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!2)}\left(\frac{n\!-\!1\!-\!2\lambda}{n\!-\!1}-\frac{1}{2(n\!-\!1)}\right)|\mathring{L}|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{\lambda}{2(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!2)}\left((\lambda\!+\!1)(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!3)-\frac{n}{2}\right)H^{2}.

Proof.

By
{(\mathcal{SY})}
, the coefficients in (
9.5
) expand as

a=1-2s\rho+\cdots=1+2sH-2s^{2}\left({\sf P}_{00}+\frac{|\mathring{L}|^{2}}{n-1}\right)+\cdots,

up to a remainder in
O(s^{n+1})
, and

\operatorname{tr}(h_{s}^{-1}h^{\prime}_{s})=2\operatorname{tr}(L)-s(2\operatorname{Ric}_{00}+2|\mathring{L}|^{2}+4nH^{2})+\cdots.

Hence the equality of the coefficients of
s^{\lambda+2}
in the expansion of the
eigenequation
\Delta_{s^{-2}\eta^{*}(g)}u=-\lambda(n-\lambda)u
yields the
relation

\displaystyle 2(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!2){\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\lambda)(f)+(\lambda\!+\!1)(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!3)H{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\lambda)(f)

\displaystyle=\left[-2\lambda(\lambda\!-\!1)\rho_{0}^{\prime}-\lambda(\operatorname{Ric}_{00}+|\mathring{L}|^{2})+2(n\!-\!1)\lambda\rho_{0}^{\prime}-2\lambda\rho_{0}^{\prime}+\Delta\right](f)=0.

This condition for
{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\lambda)
simplifies to

\displaystyle 2(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!2){\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\lambda)(f)-\lambda(\lambda\!+\!1)(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!3)H^{2}f

\displaystyle=\lambda\left[-2(n\!-\!1\!-\!\lambda)\rho_{0}^{\prime}+\operatorname{Ric}_{00}+|\mathring{L}|^{2}\right]f-\Delta f

\displaystyle=\lambda\left[-2(n\!-\!1\!-\!\lambda)\left({\sf P}_{00}+\frac{|\mathring{L}|^{2}}{n-1}\right)+((n\!-\!1){\sf P}_{00}+{\sf J}_{0})+|\mathring{L}|^{2}\right]f-\Delta f

\displaystyle=-\lambda\left((n\!-\!1\!-\!2\lambda){\sf P}_{00}+\frac{n\!-\!1\!-\!2\lambda}{n\!-\!1}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-{\sf J}_{0}\right)f-\Delta f

using Lemma
13.5.1
. Finally, (
13.1.3
) implies

\displaystyle 2(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!2){\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\lambda)(f)

\displaystyle=-\lambda\left((n\!-\!2\!-\!2\lambda){\sf P}_{00}-{\sf J}^{h}+\left(\frac{n\!-\!1\!-\!2\lambda}{n\!-\!1}-\frac{1}{2(n\!-\!1)}\right)|\mathring{L}|^{2}+\frac{n}{2}H^{2}\right)f

\displaystyle+\lambda(\lambda\!+\!1)(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!3)H^{2}f-\Delta f.

This completes the proof.
∎

In particular, Lemma
13.11.2
implies

-4\operatorname{Res}_{\frac{n}{2}-1}({\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\lambda))=\Delta_{h}-\left(\frac{n}{2}-1\right)\left({\sf J}^{h}-\frac{|\mathring{L}|^{2}}{2(n-1)}\right)={\bf P}_{2}(g).

This is a special case of Theorem
9.3
. It follows that

-{\bf Q}_{2}(g)={\sf J}^{h}-\frac{1}{2(n-1)}|\mathring{L}|^{2}.

For
n=2
, Lemma
13.11.2
implies

{\mathcal{T}}_{2}(0)(1)=\frac{1}{4}{\sf J}^{h}-\frac{1}{8}|\mathring{L}|^{2}.

For the function
{\bf Q}_{2}(\lambda)
defined in (
7.2
), it follows that

{\bf Q}_{2}(0)=-{\sf J}^{h}+\frac{1}{2}|\mathring{L}|^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}{\bf Q}_{2}
.
This confirms (
10.9
) for
n=2
.

The above results yield an explicit formula for
{\bf D}_{2}^{res}(\lambda)
.

Lemma 13.11.3
.

\displaystyle{\bf D}_{2}^{res}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!3)\Big[(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!2)\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{2}-2(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!2)(\lambda\!-\!1)H\iota^{*}\partial_{s}

\displaystyle-\Big[\Delta_{h}+\lambda{\sf J}^{h}-\lambda(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!2){\sf P}_{00}-\lambda\left(\frac{2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!2}{2(n\!-\!1)}+\frac{2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!1}{2(n\!-\!1)}\right)|\mathring{L}|^{2}

(13.11.2)

\displaystyle-(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!2)(\lambda\!-\!1/2)\lambda H^{2}\Big]\iota^{*}\Big].

We omit the details of the calculation.

This result fits with the formula for
\iota^{*}L(\lambda-1)L(\lambda)
in
[
GW21
, Lemma 7.9]
,
i.e.,

{\bf D}_{2}^{res}(\lambda)\circ\eta^{*}=\iota^{*}L(\lambda-1)L(\lambda).

In order to see this, it only remains to express the normal derivatives in the
variable
s
by iterated gradients. But
\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}
corresponds to

\iota^{*}\partial_{s}
and
\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}
corresponds to
\iota^{*}(\partial_{s}^{2}+2H\partial_{s})
(see Example
13.1.7
).

Note that the prefactor in (
13.11.3
) implies that
{\bf D}_{2}^{res}(-\frac{n-3}{2})=0
.
The conformally covariant term in brackets (for
\lambda=-(n-3)/2
and up to the contribution by
|\mathring{L}|^{2}
)
has been used in
[
C18
, Theorem 1.1]
as a boundary operator associated to the Paneitz operator on
X
.
For general
\lambda
, it appears in
[
C18
, Proposition 3.3]
(up to the term containing
|\mathring{L}|^{2}
).
Concerning the classification of such boundary operators, we refer to
[
C18
, Remark 3.9]
.

Lemma
13.11.3
immediately shows that
{\bf D}_{2}^{res}(-\frac{n}{2}+1)={\bf P}_{2}(g)\iota^{*}
. In addition, we find the remarkable identity

(13.11.3)

{\bf D}_{2}^{res}\left(\frac{-n+1}{2}\right)\circ\eta^{*}=2\iota^{*}P_{2}(g),

where
P_{2}(g)
is the Yamabe operator of
g
. In fact, we calculate

\displaystyle{\bf D}_{2}^{res}\left(\frac{-n+1}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=2\left(\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{2}+(n\!+\!1)H\iota^{*}\partial_{s}+\Delta_{h}\iota^{*}-\frac{n\!-\!1}{2}\left[{\sf J}^{h}+{\sf P}_{00}-\frac{n}{2}H^{2}+\frac{1}{2(n\!-\!1)}|\mathring{L}|^{2}\right]\iota^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=2\left(\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{2}+(n\!+\!1)H\iota^{*}\partial_{s}+\Delta_{h}\iota^{*}-\frac{n\!-\!1}{2}\iota^{*}{\sf J}^{g}\right)

using (
13.1.3
). Hence

\displaystyle{\bf D}_{2}^{res}\left(\frac{-n\!+\!1}{2}\right)\circ\eta^{*}

\displaystyle=2\left(\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}+(n\!-\!1)H\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}+\Delta_{h}\iota^{*}-\frac{n\!-\!1}{2}\iota^{*}{\sf J}^{g}\right)

\displaystyle=2\iota^{*}\left(\Delta_{g}-\frac{n\!-\!1}{2}{\sf J}^{g}\right)=2\iota^{*}P_{2}(g)

using
\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}^{2}=(\iota^{*}\partial_{s}^{2}+2H\iota^{*}\partial_{s})\circ\eta^{*}
(Example

13.1.7
) and the identity (
13.1.10
).

For Poincaré-Einstein metrics, the relation (
13.11.3
) is one of the
identities in a second set of so-called factorization identities
[
J13
, Theorem 3.2]
. It is an open
problem whether the higher-order residue families in the general case continue to satisfy such identities.

Finally, we note that Lemma
13.11.3
implies that

(13.11.4)

{\bf Q}^{res}_{2}(0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\bf D}_{2}^{res}(0)(1)=0.

This is a special case of the following conjecture.

Conjecture 13.11.4
.

{\bf Q}_{N}^{res}(0)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(0)(1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}0
for
N\geq 1
.

This vanishing result is well-known for residue families of even order in the Poincaré-Einstein case

[
BJ10
, Theorem 1.6.6]
.

A conformally covariant second-order family of differential operators which
interpolates between the GJMS operators
\iota^{*}P_{2}(g)
and
P_{2}(h)\iota^{*}
(with

h=\iota^{*}(g))
was given in
[
J09
, Theorem 6.4.1]
. This result suggests restating
the above formula for
{\bf D}_{2}^{res}(\lambda)
in the perhaps more enlightening
form

\displaystyle\iota^{*}L(\lambda-1)L(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!3)\Big[(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!2)\iota^{*}P_{2}(g)-(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!1){\bf P}_{2}(g)\iota^{*}

\displaystyle-(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!1)(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!2)H\iota^{*}\nabla_{\mathcal{N}}

(13.11.5)

\displaystyle+2\left(\lambda\!+\!\frac{n\!-\!1}{2}\right)\left(\lambda\!+\!\frac{n\!-\!2}{2}\right)({\bf Q}_{2}(g)+\iota^{*}Q_{2}(g)+\lambda H^{2})\iota^{*}\Big],

where

P_{2}(g)=\Delta_{g}-\frac{n\!-\!1}{2}{\sf J}^{g}=\Delta_{g}-\frac{n\!-\!1}{2}Q_{2}(g)

is the Yamabe operator of
g
and

{\bf P}_{2}(g)=\Delta_{h}-\left(\frac{n}{2}-1\right)\left({\sf J}^{h}-\frac{|\mathring{L}|^{2}}{2(n-1)}\right)=\Delta_{h}+\left(\frac{n}{2}-1\right){\bf Q}_{2}(g)

is the extrinsic Yamabe operator on
M
(see (
13.9.2
)).
34
34

34

Note that we use the
conventions
{\bf P}_{2}(g)(1)=\frac{n-2}{2}{\bf Q}_{2}(g)
on
M
and
P_{2}(g)(1)=-\frac{n-1}{2}Q_{2}(g)
on
X
.
In order to prove that formula, it is
enough to relate the cubic polynomial in square brackets to the corresponding cubic polynomial in (
13.11.3
).
It is easy to relate the terms with derivatives. Moreover, the coincidence of the zeroth order terms
follows from the Gauss identity - we omit the calculation.

(
13.11
) immediately shows that
{\bf D}_{2}^{res}(g;-\frac{n}{2}+1)={\bf P}_{2}(g)\iota^{*}
and

{\bf D}_{2}^{res}\left(g;\frac{-n+1}{2}\right)\circ\eta^{*}=2\iota^{*}P_{2}(g).

The formula (
13.11
) leads to a simple formula for the
Q
-curvature polynomial

{\bf Q}_{2}^{res}(g;\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\bf D}_{2}^{res}(g;\lambda)(1).

Lemma 13.11.5
.

It holds

\displaystyle{\bf Q}_{2}^{res}(g;\lambda)=(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!3)\lambda

(13.11.6)

\displaystyle\times\left[(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!2)\iota^{*}Q_{2}(g)+(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!1){\bf Q}_{2}(g)+2\left(\lambda\!+\!\frac{n\!-\!1}{2}\right)\left(\lambda\!+\!\frac{n\!-\!2}{2}\right)H^{2}\right].

This result clearly implies (
13.11.4
). It suggests considering the quadratic polynomial in brackets
as the actual interesting object. In the critical dimension
n=2
, we also find
\dot{{\bf D}}_{2}^{res}(0)(1)=\dot{{\bf Q}}_{2}^{res}(0)=-{\bf Q}_{2}
.

One should compare Lemma
13.11.5
with
[
J09
, (6.4.11)]
.

Note that in the Poincaré-Einstein case (i.e., if
g=r^{2}g_{+}
), it holds
\iota^{*}Q_{2}(g)=\iota^{*}{\sf J}^{g}={\sf J}^{h}=Q_{2}(h)
,
{\bf Q}_{2}(g)=-Q_{2}(h)
(Remark
10.3
) and
H=0
. Hence (
13.11.5
)
reduces to

(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!3)\lambda\left[(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!2)Q_{2}(h)-(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!1){\bf Q}_{2}(h)\right]=(2\lambda\!+\!n\!-\!3)\lambda Q_{2}(h)

(see also Remark
7.5
).

It is an open problem whether for
N\geq 3
the
Q
-curvature polynomial

{\bf Q}_{N}^{res}(\lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def}}}{{=}}{\bf D}_{N}^{res}(\lambda)(1)
similarly can be reduced to a lower degree
polynomial and whether these polynomials admit a recursive description as in the Poincaré-Einstein case

[
J13
,
J14
]
.

Next, we determine the leading part of
{\bf P}_{3}
from the leading part of the solution
operator
{\mathcal{T}}_{3}(\lambda)
.
{\bf P}_{3}
is an operator of second-order. Let
\operatorname{LT}({\bf P}_{3})

denote its terms that contain one or two derivatives. The same notation will be
used for other second-order operators.

Lemma 13.11.6
.

It holds

\operatorname{LT}(\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=\frac{n-3}{2}}({\mathcal{T}}_{3}(\lambda))=\frac{1}{3}\delta(\mathring{L}d).

Hence

\operatorname{LT}({\bf P}_{3})=8\delta(\mathring{L}d).

Proof.

The leading part of the solution operator
{\mathcal{T}}_{3}(\lambda)
is determined by the equation

\displaystyle 3(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!3)\operatorname{LT}({\mathcal{T}}_{3}(\lambda))+2(\lambda\!+\!1)(\lambda\!+\!2)H\operatorname{LT}({\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\lambda))+(\lambda+2)\operatorname{tr}(L)\operatorname{LT}({\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\lambda))

\displaystyle+(\lambda+2)H\operatorname{LT}({\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\lambda))-2(n\!-\!1)(\lambda+2)H\operatorname{LT}({\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\lambda))-(dH,d\cdot)_{h}

\displaystyle+\operatorname{LT}(\Delta{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\lambda))+\Delta_{h}^{\prime}=0,

where
\Delta_{h_{s}}=\Delta_{h}+s\Delta_{h}^{\prime}+\frac{s^{2}}{2!}\Delta_{h}^{\prime\prime}+\cdots
. Simplification yields

\displaystyle-3(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!3)\operatorname{LT}({\mathcal{T}}_{3}(\lambda))

\displaystyle=(\lambda\!+\!2)(2\lambda\!-\!n\!+\!5)H\operatorname{LT}({\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\lambda))-(dH,d\cdot)_{h}+\operatorname{LT}(\Delta{\mathcal{T}}_{1}(\lambda))+\Delta^{\prime}.

But the first variation
\Delta^{\prime}
of the Laplacian with respect to the
variation
h_{s}
of
h
is given by (
9.14
). Using
\operatorname{LT}({\mathcal{T}}_{2}(\frac{n-3}{2}))=\frac{1}{2}\Delta
(Lemma
13.11.2
),
it follows that

-6\operatorname{LT}(\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=\frac{n-3}{2}}({\mathcal{T}}_{3}(\lambda))

equals

\displaystyle\frac{n\!+\!1}{2}H\Delta-(dH,d\cdot)_{h}-\frac{n\!-\!3}{2}\operatorname{LT}(\Delta\circ H)-2H\Delta+(n\!-\!2)(dH,d\cdot)_{h}-2\delta(\mathring{L}d)

\displaystyle=-(dH,d\cdot)_{h}-(n\!-\!3)(dH,d\cdot)_{h}+(n\!-\!2)(dH,d\cdot)_{h}-2\delta(\mathring{L}d)

\displaystyle=-2\delta(\mathring{L}d).

This completes the proof.
∎

Similar arguments prove

Lemma 13.11.7
.

It holds

\operatorname{LT}(\operatorname{Res}_{\lambda=\frac{n-5}{2}}({\mathcal{T}}_{5}(\lambda))=\frac{1}{30}(\Delta\delta(\mathring{L}d)+\delta(\mathring{L})\Delta).

Hence

\operatorname{LT}({\bf P}_{5})=192(\Delta\delta(\mathring{L}d)+\delta(\mathring{L}d)\Delta).

These results are special cases of Proposition
9.10
.

13.12.
Low order cases of Theorem
10.17

The following result illustrates Theorem
10.17
for
k=1,2
.

Lemma 13.12.1
.

Assume that
\sigma
satisfies
{(\mathcal{SY})}
. Then

\iota^{*}L(-n\!+\!1)\left(\left(\frac{r}{\sigma}\right)^{n-1}\right)=-\frac{(n\!-\!1)^{2}}{2}H

for
n\geq 1
and

\displaystyle\iota^{*}L(-n\!+\!1)L(-n\!+\!2)\left(\left(\frac{r}{\sigma}\right)^{n-2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{(n\!-\!1)(n\!-\!2)}{6}\left(-\frac{n\!-\!5}{n\!-\!1}|\mathring{L}|^{2}-2(n\!-\!2)\iota^{*}{\sf J}^{g}+2(n\!-\!5){\sf J}^{h}+\frac{(n\!-\!2)(n\!-\!3)}{2}H^{2}\right)

for
n\geq 2
. Here
L(\lambda)
is short for
L(g,\sigma;\lambda)
.

By
[
G00
, (4.5)]
, it holds
w_{1}=\frac{n-1}{2}H
and the right-hand side of the second identity
equals
2(n\!-\!1)(n\!-\!2)w_{2}
. Hence

w_{1}=-\frac{1}{n-1}\iota^{*}L(-n\!+\!1)\left(\left(\frac{r}{\sigma}\right)^{n-1}\right)

for
n\geq 2
and

w_{2}=\frac{1}{2(n\!-\!1)(n\!-\!2)}\iota^{*}L(-n\!+\!1)L(-n\!+\!2)\left(\left(\frac{r}{\sigma}\right)^{n-2}\right)

for
n\geq 3
.

Proof.

We expand in geodesic normal coordinates. First, we note that

\left(\frac{r}{\sigma}\right)^{n-1}=1-(n\!-\!1)\sigma_{(2)}r+\cdots

and recall that
\sigma_{(2)}=\frac{1}{2}H
. Now we calculate

\displaystyle\iota^{*}L(-n\!+\!1)(1)=-(n\!-\!1)^{2}H\quad\mbox{and}\quad\iota^{*}L(-n\!+\!1)(Hr)=-(n\!-\!1)H

using
\rho_{0}=-H
. This proves the first identity. Similarly, we find

\left(\frac{r}{\sigma}\right)^{n-2}=1-(n\!-\!2)\sigma_{(2)}r+\left(-(n\!-\!2)\sigma_{(3)}+\binom{n\!-\!1}{2}\sigma_{(2)}^{2}\right)r^{2}+\cdots.

Now
\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)=\partial_{r}+Hr\partial_{r}+\cdots
and we obtain

\displaystyle\iota^{*}L(-n\!+\!1)L(-n\!+\!2)(1)

\displaystyle=-(n\!-\!1)\iota^{*}(\partial_{r}+(n\!-\!1)H)((-n\!+\!3)(\partial_{r}+Hr\partial_{r}-(n\!-\!2)\rho)-r(\Delta_{g}-(n\!-\!2){\sf J}^{g}))(1)

\displaystyle=-(n\!-\!1)(n\!-\!2)((n\!-\!3)\partial_{r}(\rho)|_{0}+{\sf J}^{g}|_{0}-(n\!-\!1)(n\!-\!3)H^{2}).

Note that this result coincides with
2(n\!-\!1)(n\!-\!2)v_{2}
, where
v_{2}
is as in (
13.4.3
).
Moreover, we get

\displaystyle\iota^{*}L(-n\!+\!1)L(-n\!+\!2)(\sigma_{(3)}r^{2})

\displaystyle=(n\!-\!1)(n\!-\!2)(-2(n-3)\sigma_{(3)}-2\sigma_{(3)})

\displaystyle=2(n\!-\!1)(n\!-\!2)\sigma_{(3)}

and

\iota^{*}L(-n\!+\!1)L(-n\!+\!2)(\sigma_{(2)}^{2}r^{2})=2(n\!-\!1)(n\!-\!2)\sigma_{(2)}^{2}.

Finally, we obtain

\iota^{*}L(-n\!+\!1)L(-n\!+\!2)(\sigma_{(2)}r)=\frac{1}{2}(n\!-\!1)(n\!-\!2)(2n\!-\!3)H^{2}

using

\Delta_{g}(\sigma_{(2)}r)=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}(h_{r}^{-1}h_{r}^{\prime})\sigma_{(2)}+r\Delta_{h}(\sigma_{(2)})=nH\sigma_{(2)}+O(r).

We omit the details. Note that only in the latter calculation the contribution
Hr\partial_{r}
in
\operatorname{grad}_{g}(\sigma)

plays a role. Now, using Lemma
13.1.1
, Lemma
13.3.2
and Lemma
13.5.1
, these results imply
the second assertion.
∎
